Lecture 1: Introduction to Spring Framework

Source: "Spring Boot & Spring Framework Full Course | Spring Boot Full Course #1" · 43 min · Code Army · First lecture of the series

Why learn more than Spring Boot

Spring Boot alone = syntax without internals. Every Spring Boot app runs on:

  • Spring Core (IoC / DI).
  • Spring MVC, for web apps.
  • Other modules: Spring Data, Spring Security.

A Java full-stack engineer needs all of these. Series covers Spring Core, Spring MVC, the full Framework and industry practice — enough for any Spring interview and own projects.

Client–server architecture

  1. You type a URL (www.amazon.in) in a browser.
  2. Amazon's server (a computer hosting its app) receives it.
  3. The server entertains the request and sends a response.
  4. The browser renders it.

Whoever requests = client; whoever responds = server. Request travels as an HTTP request (or HTTPS); answer is an HTTP response.

Who can be a client, and what a server does

A client is anything that sends a request to a server:

  • A browser.
  • A mobile app (Android/iOS).
  • A frontend app (e.g. React).
  • Postman (API-calling tool).
  • Another server — in microservices, when Order service calls Payment/Notification over HTTP, Order is the client.

Client's job: send an HTTP request to store, fetch, update or delete.

A server entertains the request: receives, processes, responds. Processing may be:

  • Storing data in a DB.
  • Fetching information.
  • Updating/deleting records.
  • Authenticating the user (e.g. login).
In one line: client asks, server responds. The role depends on who starts the request, not the kind of machine.

HTTP: the rulebook between client and server

HTTP = HyperText Transfer Protocol — a protocol (rulebook) giving both sides an agreed format. It defines:

  • The structure of a request.
  • The structure of a response.
  • Which method (operation) was called.
  • How data is sent: encrypted or not.
MethodPurposeHas body?SafeIdempotent
GETRetrieves onlyNormally noYesYes
POSTStores/creates (login, signup)YesNoNo
PUTUpdates by replacing the whole resourceYesNoYes
PATCHUpdates specific fieldsYesNoNot guaranteed
DELETEDeletesUsually noNoYes
Safe vs idempotent (added; commonly asked): Safe = doesn't change server state. Idempotent = N identical requests leave the same state as one. DELETE /users/5 twice → user 5 still deleted; POST /orders twice → two orders. HTTP is stateless: login state rides on cookies, sessions or tokens.

Structure of an HTTP request

Fetching courses: method GET, host www.example.com, endpoint /courses → URL www.example.com/courses. Server may authenticate, fetch courses from DB, respond.

Four parts:

  1. Method: GET, POST, PUT, DELETE, PATCH.
  2. URL (a.k.a. path / endpoint).
  3. Headers: key–value metadata, e.g. Accept: application/json ("I accept JSON back"); credentials/tokens too.
  4. Body: data for the server to store. GET has none.
GET /courses HTTP/1.1
Host: www.example.com
Accept: application/json

Login = POST to /login; Content-Type: application/json says the body is JSON, carrying email and password:

POST /login HTTP/1.1
Host: www.example.com
Content-Type: application/json
Content-Length: 51

{"email":"user@example.com","password":"secret123"}

Server reads the credentials from the body, authenticates, responds.

Accept vs Content-Type: Accept = format you want back; Content-Type = format of this message's body. Blank line separates headers from body. Credentials in a body only over HTTPS.

Structure of an HTTP response

Three parts:

  1. Status code — most important; success, failure or other.
  2. Headers: optional, e.g. Content-Type.
  3. Body: returned information.
HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Content-Type: application/json

{"message":"Login successful"}
Common status codes
ClassMeaningExamples
1xxInformational100 Continue
2xxSuccess200 OK, 201 Created, 204 No Content
3xxRedirection301 Moved Permanently, 304 Not Modified
4xxClient error400 Bad Request, 401 Unauthorized (not authenticated), 403 Forbidden (authenticated, not allowed), 404 Not Found
5xxServer error500 Internal Server Error, 502 Bad Gateway, 503 Service Unavailable

HTTP vs HTTPS

  • HTTP data is not encrypted — anyone in between can read it.
  • HTTPS (S = secure): request and response are encrypted.
  • HTTPS = HTTP over TLS; the server's certificate also proves its identity (added).
  • Default ports: 80 (HTTP), 443 (HTTPS).
Transcript slips:
  • "2001" for created → 201 Created.
  • 503 called "internal server error" → 503 is Service Unavailable; Internal Server Error is 500.
  • Credentials header is Authorization (Authorization: Bearer <token>), not "Authentication".

What core Java gives us: code that runs once

Core Java: a public class Main with public static void main:

  1. Save as Main.java (named after the class).
  2. javac Main.java → bytecode Main.class, platform-independent ("Write Once, Run Anywhere").
  3. Run on the JVM (a sandbox). Code does not run natively on the OS; the JVM is the OS process.

A request to a Java server is really interacting with compiled Java code on that server's JVM.

Code flow vs website flow

A Java program: start → run → stop → exit. A website (instagram.com) is always active.

while (true) { … } could keep core Java alive, but the real question remains: how do you map an incoming HTTP request to a method?

An endpoint is just a method call

First principles: Java is OO, so server work is a method inside some class:

  • GET /courses → courses() fetches all courses (maybe from DB).
  • /signup → signup() registers the user.

Hitting the endpoint is the method call. Core Java covers OOP but not:

  • How to read an HTTP request (URL, headers, body).
  • Who calls the method (endpoint → method mapping).
  • How to send an HTTP response.

Can core Java do networking? Yes: java.net

Since Java 1, java.net provides Socket and ServerSocket:

ServerSocket server = new ServerSocket(8080);   // listen on port 8080
  • Port. Many apps run at once (WhatsApp, Chrome, Spotify). IP address picks the machine; port number picks the app. Your Java server can listen on 8080.
  • localhost = your own machine, loopback IP 127.0.0.1; localhost:8080 = port 8080 on it.

So why a framework at all?

Why java.net isn't enough: the boilerplate problem

Java doesn't understand HTTP. To the JVM, GET /courses Host: … is a stream of bytes it can read but not interpret. Manually you must:

  1. Read the input stream via BufferedReader (bytes → characters).
  2. Parse the request (GET, /courses, host).
  3. Map endpoint → method: if (endpoint.equals("/courses")) callThisMethod(); else if (…) …, one per endpoint.
  4. Build the HTTP response per protocol — java.net doesn't understand responses either.
  5. Implement multithreading — with only the main thread, other requests get stuck.

Lots of boilerplate before any business logic.

Worked example: GET /courses with plain java.net

(Added; lecture only lists the steps.) Manual steps marked in comments:

import java.io.*;
import java.net.*;
import java.nio.charset.StandardCharsets;

public class RawHttpServer {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws IOException {
        try (ServerSocket server = new ServerSocket(8080)) {
            System.out.println("Listening on http://localhost:8080");
            while (true) {                                   // stay up forever
                Socket client = server.accept();             // blocks until someone connects
                new Thread(() -> handle(client)).start();    // manual multithreading
            }
        }
    }

    private static void handle(Socket client) {
        try (client;
             BufferedReader in = new BufferedReader(
                     new InputStreamReader(client.getInputStream(), StandardCharsets.UTF_8));
             OutputStream out = client.getOutputStream()) {

            String requestLine = in.readLine();              // "GET /courses HTTP/1.1"
            if (requestLine == null) return;
            String[] parts = requestLine.split(" ");         // manual parsing
            if (parts.length < 2) return;
            String method = parts[0], path = parts[1];
            String line;                                     // skip headers until blank line
            while ((line = in.readLine()) != null && !line.isEmpty()) { }

            int status;
            String body;
            if (method.equals("GET") && path.equals("/courses")) {  // manual routing
                status = 200;
                body = getCourses();
            } else {
                status = 404;
                body = "{\"error\":\"Not Found\"}";
            }

            byte[] bytes = body.getBytes(StandardCharsets.UTF_8);
            String head = "HTTP/1.1 " + status + (status == 200 ? " OK" : " Not Found") + "\r\n"
                    + "Content-Type: application/json\r\n"
                    + "Content-Length: " + bytes.length + "\r\n"
                    + "Connection: close\r\n\r\n";           // manual response building
            out.write(head.getBytes(StandardCharsets.US_ASCII));
            out.write(bytes);
        } catch (IOException e) {
            e.printStackTrace();
        }
    }

    private static String getCourses() {                     // the only business logic
        return "[\"Java\",\"Spring Boot\",\"LLD\"]";
    }
}

Run (Java 9+, for try (client; …)): javac RawHttpServer.java → java RawHttpServer → curl -i http://localhost:8080/courses.

HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Content-Type: application/json
Content-Length: 28
Connection: close

["Java","Spring Boot","LLD"]
Why this doesn't scale: ~50 lines, only getCourses() is business logic. Still ignores bodies, query strings, keep-alive, chunked encoding, malformed input; spawns unbounded threads. Every endpoint adds an if.

Servlets and the Servlet container (1997)

Servlets and the Servlet container (1997): the first Java package built for web development.

  • Servlet — a Java class running inside a servlet container; the container itself runs inside the JVM.
  • Servlet container — usually just "the server":
    • Tomcat (most used).
    • Jetty.
    • Undertow.

Tomcat does all the manual boilerplate:

  • Opens the port.
  • Deciphers incoming bytes into HTTP.
  • Calls the servlet mapped to the URL.
  • Builds the HTTP response from the servlet's output.
  • Handles multithreading internally.

You write only business logic inside servlets (the /courses method lives in one).

  • Tomcat stays up, listening — solves "always running" too.
  • Many servlets; which runs depends on the request.
  • Java's first solution for web apps.

Same endpoint as a servlet (added; syntax covered later):

import jakarta.servlet.annotation.WebServlet;
import jakarta.servlet.http.HttpServlet;
import jakarta.servlet.http.HttpServletRequest;
import jakarta.servlet.http.HttpServletResponse;
import java.io.IOException;

@WebServlet("/courses")                        // URL → servlet mapping
public class CoursesServlet extends HttpServlet {
    @Override
    protected void doGet(HttpServletRequest req, HttpServletResponse resp) throws IOException {
        resp.setStatus(HttpServletResponse.SC_OK);
        resp.setContentType("application/json");
        resp.getWriter().write("[\"Java\",\"Spring Boot\",\"LLD\"]");
    }
}

Deployed as a WAR to Tomcat 10.1+. @WebServlet needs Servlet 3.0+ (earlier: web.xml). Container manages lifecycle: init() → service() → destroy().

Why the Spring Framework came

Servlet code at enterprise scale becomes tightly coupled — interlinked objects, hard to scale.

Spring introduced Dependency Injection and Inversion of Control → loosely coupled, easy-to-scale apps. Both get dedicated lectures; the idea (added):

// Tightly coupled: the service builds its own dependency
class CourseService {
    private final MySqlCourseRepository repo = new MySqlCourseRepository();
}

// Loosely coupled: the dependency is handed in (Spring's IoC container does this)
class CourseService {
    private final CourseRepository repo;          // interface
    CourseService(CourseRepository repo) {        // constructor injection
        this.repo = repo;
    }
}
Why this matters: in version 2, swapping MySQL → Postgres, or a fake repo in tests, doesn't touch CourseService. The container creates objects (beans) and wires them.

Spring is an ecosystem, not one framework

Spring contains many frameworks — an ecosystem, not only for web apps. Per spring.io ("What Spring can do"):

  • GenAI applications.
  • Microservices.
  • Reactive applications.
  • Cloud applications.
  • Event-driven systems.
  • Web apps (this series' focus).
  • Serverless architectures.
  • Batch processing jobs.

Tagline: makes Java simple, modern and productive.

The Spring ecosystem, layer by layer

LayerWhat it gives you
Spring CoreDI, IoC, beans → loosely coupled systems. Every module rests on it.
Spring MVCWeb apps. Uses Servlets internally — not used directly, but needed for deep debugging.
Spring DataConnecting to a database.
Spring AOPAspect-Oriented Programming (explained later).
Spring SecurityLogin and authentication.
Spring AIModern addition for GenAI apps (1.0 GA in 2025).
Spring BootAutomation layer on top of all.
Precision note (added): Spring MVC (spring-webmvc) and Spring AOP are modules inside the Spring Framework. Spring Data, Security, Boot, Cloud and AI are separate projects built on it.

Spring Boot: the opinionated automation layer

Spring Boot did not replace or obsolete the Spring Framework:

  • Spring Boot isn't a skill. The skills are MVC, Data, AOP, Security.
  • It's an automation layer to start development quickly — you "boot up" fast.
  • Opinionated: sets up the project by assuming configurations. Keep defaults or change them.
  • Plain Spring MVC needs many manual configurations; Boot removes that.
  • Changing defaults requires knowing the internal architecture (MVC, Data, AOP, Security) — you can't learn only Boot; Spring Core is shared by all.

Main features (expanded beyond the lecture):

  • Auto-configuration from the classpath.
  • Starter dependencies, e.g. spring-boot-starter-web.
  • Embedded server (Tomcat default) → java -jar app.jar.
  • External configuration: application.properties / application.yml.
  • Production features via Actuator.

Same endpoint in Spring Boot (added preview; next lecture writes real code):

import java.util.List;
import org.springframework.boot.SpringApplication;
import org.springframework.boot.autoconfigure.SpringBootApplication;
import org.springframework.web.bind.annotation.GetMapping;
import org.springframework.web.bind.annotation.RestController;

@SpringBootApplication                         // auto-configuration + component scanning
public class DemoApplication {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        SpringApplication.run(DemoApplication.class, args);   // starts embedded Tomcat on 8080
    }
}

@RestController
class CourseController {
    @GetMapping("/courses")                    // GET /courses → this method
    public List<String> getCourses() {
        return List.of("Java", "Spring Boot", "LLD");        // converted to JSON automatically
    }
}

Needs spring-boot-starter-web; Boot 3.x requires Java 17+. curl http://localhost:8080/courses → ["Java","Spring Boot","LLD"].

java.net vs Servlet vs Spring Boot

Who does the work at each stage
Concernjava.net socketsServletSpring Boot
Understands HTTPNo, raw bytesYesYes (built on servlets)
Listen on a portYouContainerEmbedded container, auto-configured
Thread managementYouContainerContainer
URL → code mappingif/else chains@WebServlet / web.xml@GetMapping on a method
JSON conversionYouYouAutomatic (Jackson)
Server setupYour own codeInstall Tomcat, deploy WARBuilt in; java -jar
CouplingPoorTight at scaleLoose, via DI
IntroducedJDK 1.0 (1996)1997Spring 1.0 in 2004; Boot 1.0 in 2014

Spring Data: JDBC → JPA → Hibernate → Spring Data JPA

How Java connects to a database, historically:

  1. JDBC (Java Database Connectivity) — write exact SQL (SELECT … FROM table …) in Demo.java; DB returns results.
  2. JPA (Java Persistence API) — "no hand-written SQL": call methods that talk to the DB. The DB still only understands SQL, so JDBC runs underneath. JPA is not an implementation — it's a rulebook.
  3. Hibernate implements JPA, using JDBC internally.
  4. Spring Data JPA brings this into Spring; Hibernate implements it underneath.
Learning order: Spring Data JPA needs Hibernate; Hibernate needs JDBC. The series covers the whole chain. 
// JDBC: SQL and mapping by hand
String sql = "SELECT id, title FROM courses WHERE id = ?";
try (Connection con = DriverManager.getConnection(url, user, pass);
     PreparedStatement ps = con.prepareStatement(sql)) {
    ps.setLong(1, 42L);
    try (ResultSet rs = ps.executeQuery()) {
        if (rs.next()) {
            course = new Course(rs.getLong("id"), rs.getString("title"));
        }
    }
}

// Spring Data JPA: declare an interface; the SQL is generated
public interface CourseRepository extends JpaRepository<Course, Long> {
    List<Course> findByTitleContaining(String keyword);   // query derived from the method name
}

JDBC vs JPA vs Hibernate vs Spring Data JPA

JDBCJPAHibernateSpring Data JPA
TypeLow-level APISpecificationORM, JPA implementationAbstraction over JPA
You writeSQL + row mappingEntities, JPQLEntities (SQL generated)Repository interfaces
Uses underneathDB driverNeeds a providerJDBCA JPA provider (Hibernate by default in Boot)
BoilerplateMostLessLessLeast

The complete Spring architecture

  • Client sends a request to a Spring Boot application.
  • App is built from modules (MVC, Security, AOP, Data), all sharing Spring Core.
  • DB access: Spring Data JPA → Hibernate → JDBC.
  • Spring MVC uses servlets.

Order of one request (added; DispatcherServlet comes in later lectures):

  1. Tomcat accepts, parses, assigns a thread.
  2. Security filters authenticate.
  3. DispatcherServlet (Spring MVC's single front-controller servlet) routes to the controller method.
  4. Controller calls the service.
  5. Service calls the repository → Hibernate → JDBC → DB.
  6. Return value → JSON via Jackson → response.

Monolith vs microservices

"Spring microservices" is not a module — it's an architecture design. A Spring Boot app can be:

  • Monolithic — one app handles all endpoints.
  • Microservices — small services (Order, Payment, User…) that call each other; splitting covered later.
MonolithMicroservices
DeploymentOne unitMany independent units
CommunicationIn-process method callsNetwork calls (HTTP, messaging)
ScalingWhole appEach service separately
ComplexitySimpler to build/test/debug earlyDistributed problems: latency, partial failures, tracing
FitsSmall teams, early productsLarge systems/teams needing independent releases

Spring Cloud = microservice tooling: service discovery, API gateway, centralized config (added).

Spring Framework vs Spring Boot

AspectSpring FrameworkSpring Boot
What it isCore: IoC/DI plus modules (MVC, AOP, …)Opinionated automation layer on Spring
ConfigurationMostly manual (Java config / XML)Auto-configured defaults, overridable
DependenciesPick and version-match eachStarters with managed, compatible versions
ServerWAR to an external serverEmbedded Tomcat/Jetty/Undertow, java -jar
Setup timeSlowerFast (the whole point)

Q: "Does Spring Boot replace Spring?"

No. Boot is built on Spring Core and modules and only automates configuration; every Boot app is a Spring app. Override defaults via properties (server.port=9090), your own beans, or excluding an auto-configuration — which requires knowing the modules.

Q: "Why Spring if Servlets already worked?"

Servlets solved networking/HTTP, not application structure. Large servlet apps (plus heavyweight J2EE EJB 2.x) became tightly coupled and hard to test; Spring's IoC/DI made them modular. Origin: Rod Johnson's 2002 J2EE design book; Spring 1.0 in 2004 (added).

Q: "Can a server be a client?"

Yes — roles per request. Order service is a server to the frontend and a client to Payment.

Q: "How does a servlet container serve many users at once?"

Thread-per-request from a pool; Tomcat default max 200 (server.tomcat.threads.max). A servlet is usually a single instance shared across threads → instance fields must be thread-safe. Java 21 + Boot 3.2+: spring.threads.virtual.enabled=true gives virtual threads.

Corrections & worth knowing

  • Servlets predate J2EE. Lecture says servlets came in 1997 "as a Java Enterprise Edition package". Servlet 1.0 was 1997; J2EE 1.2 (1999) later included it. Today: Jakarta EE.
  • javax.* → jakarta.*. Since Jakarta EE 9 (Servlet 5.0) — so Boot 3+, Framework 6+, Tomcat 10+ — packages are jakarta.servlet, jakarta.persistence. Old javax.servlet code → compile errors or ClassNotFoundException.
  • "Spring JPA" = Spring Data JPA. JPA is now Jakarta Persistence. Hibernate is Boot's default provider; EclipseLink an alternative.
  • Boot isn't tied to Spring MVC. Lecture says Boot "is based on Spring MVC"; Boot configures MVC only when spring-boot-starter-web is present. Also works for non-web (batch, CLI) and reactive apps with Spring WebFlux (no Servlet API, Netty by default).
  • Tomcat is not a full application server. Three kinds:
    • Web server (Nginx): static content, proxying.
    • Servlet container (Tomcat, Jetty, Undertow): runs servlets.
    • Application server (WildFly, WebLogic): full Jakarta EE incl. EJB, JMS, JTA.
    Spring gets those extras from libraries and runs fine on Tomcat.
  • POST retries. Non-idempotent → a retried payment could charge twice. Fix: idempotency key sent by client; server ignores duplicates.
  • Other JDK options. JDK 6+ com.sun.net.httpserver.HttpServer; JDK 18 jwebserver for static files. Not for production; not in the lecture.
  • HTTP versions. HTTP/1.1 and HTTP/2 over TCP; HTTP/3 over QUIC (UDP). Same request/response concepts.

Lecture 2: Your First Spring Boot Application (Hello World)

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #2 · 31 min · Code Army · First hands-on lecture

The goal: "Hello World" in the browser

Type a URL, see Hello World. No Spring syntax taught yet — intentional: feel how simple starting is and how Boot configures everything automatically. The "how" comes in later lectures.

  • Client: your browser (Chrome).
  • Server: your Spring Boot app on the same computer.
  • Same machine → browser hits localhost, port 8080.
  • Request GET /hello → response string Hello World.

IP addresses and the real client–server flow

Every networked device has an IP address ("what is my IP", e.g. whatismyip.com, shows IPv4 and IPv6). Data must be sent to a machine's IP.

Lecture 1's "request goes directly to www.example.com" was simplified — the network only routes to IPs. Real flow:

  1. Client asks DNS (Domain Name System), which resolves a domain name to an IP address.
  2. DNS returns the server's IP.
  3. Client calls the server using that IP (e.g. GET /hello, IP as host).
  4. Server responds.

localhost and 127.0.0.1

Client and server on one computer → host 127.0.0.1, a fixed IP meaning "myself"; localhost is its name. In localhost:8080/hello: host localhost, port 8080, endpoint /hello.

Port numbers: which application gets the data

IP gets a packet to the right machine, which runs many apps (Chrome, WhatsApp, Spotify, your Spring Boot app…). Which one gets it?

Definition: the port number. One IP has many ports; each listening application is bound to its own port. Spring Boot listens on 8080 by default — hence the port in the URL.

Q: "We never type a port for real websites. How does the server know?"

Some ports are defined by standard:

ProtocolDefault port
HTTP80
HTTPS443

Typing https://www.example.com → the browser attaches port 443 automatically.

Reverse proxy: mapping 443 to 8080

Request arrives on 443, but the Spring Boot backend listens on 8080 (default, unchanged). A reverse proxy sits between:

  • Receives requests on public port 443.
  • Maps them to 8080 — every HTTPS request reaches the app.
  • Part of the server side; proxy + app together = "the server".
What a reverse proxy really does (added): forwards the request — not an HTTP redirect; the browser never sees 8080. Examples: Nginx, HAProxy, cloud load balancers. Usually also terminate TLS (decrypt HTTPS, plain HTTP to app), load-balance across instances, serve static files. App on 8080 also avoids the admin rights Linux requires for ports below 1024.

Before any code: "This site can't be reached"

localhost:8080/hello gives "This site can't be reached" — nothing listens on 8080 yet.

Requirements

  • An IDE — IntelliJ IDEA (most popular for Java); Eclipse/NetBeans work too.
  • Java — Spring Boot 3 and 4 need Java 17+.

The problem: building a Java web app from scratch

Before any business code, you'd need:

  1. A folder structure (loose files don't scale to many classes).
  2. Lots of configuration.
  3. Downloading dependencies.
  4. Configuring the Spring Boot version.
  5. Configuring the Java version.
  6. A package structure.

All configuration work — hence Spring Initializr.

What a dependency (library) is

To connect to MySQL you use a ready-made MySQL connector — a library. An external library your code depends on = a dependency.

Official MySQL driver: MySQL Connector/J (added). Maven (downloads dependencies) is next lecture.

Spring Initializr: a ready-made project skeleton

start.spring.io: pick options → Generate → zip with the whole project skeleton (Boot config, Java and Boot versions, folder structure). Used for every project in the series.

Options chosen in the lecture
OptionChoicesPickedWhy / what it means

Why the Spring Web description matters: Tomcat (Lecture 1's servlet container) comes embedded in the app — no separate server install.

SNAPSHOT vs RC vs stable

LabelMeaningUse it?
SNAPSHOTWork in progress, can change dailyNo: likely bugs
M1, M2… (added)Milestone: early previewNo
RCRelease Candidate: almost final, not releasedNo: don't risk it
No suffix (e.g. 4.0.6)GA / stableYes, newest stable

JAR vs WAR

JAR (Java ARchive)WAR (Web ARchive)
ServerEmbedded in the app (Tomcat default)External: install Tomcat, deploy WAR into it
How to runjava -jar app.jarCopy into server's deploy folder
Used byModern Boot apps, containers / cloudOlder web apps, shared app servers

Detail covered later. "Embedded server" = Lecture 1's point that Boot ships its own Tomcat.

The generated project

Generate → demo.zip → unzip → IntelliJ Open the demo folder (first import takes a moment).

demo/
├── .mvn/                       Maven wrapper files
├── mvnw, mvnw.cmd              Maven wrapper scripts (no global Maven install needed)
├── pom.xml                     Maven config: dependencies, versions, build
└── src/
    ├── main/
    │   ├── java/
    │   │   └── com/example/demo/
    │   │       └── DemoApplication.java      ← entry point (main method)
    │   └── resources/
    │       └── application.properties        ← configuration (e.g. server.port)
    └── test/
        └── java/com/example/demo/
            └── DemoApplicationTests.java

.mvn, pom.xml don't make sense yet. src/main/java/com/example/demo/DemoApplication.java has the familiar public static void main — execution starts there.

package com.example.demo;

import org.springframework.boot.SpringApplication;
import org.springframework.boot.autoconfigure.SpringBootApplication;

@SpringBootApplication
public class DemoApplication {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        SpringApplication.run(DemoApplication.class, args);
    }
}

@SpringBootApplication and SpringApplication.run aren't explained yet; the point is the skeleton is ready.

Step 1: print "Hello World" on the console

Add System.out.println("Hello World"); in main → Run DemoApplication → logs + Hello World. Plain Java so far, but proves the project runs.

Tomcat is already running: the Whitelabel Error Page

Now localhost:8080/hello (or localhost:8080) shows something: running the app started the embedded server.

Tomcat initialized with port 8080 (http)
Starting service [Tomcat]
Tomcat started on port 8080 (http) with context path '/'

(Abridged: message part only; logger names vary by version.) Tomcat got 8080 and is listening for network calls. Browser shows:

Whitelabel Error Page
This application has no explicit mapping for /error, so you are seeing this as a fallback.
... There was an unexpected error (type=Not Found, status=404).

No endpoint mapped and no custom error fallback → Spring Boot's default page. Proof the server runs: "Tomcat started on port 8080".

Step 2: the first endpoint, HelloController

In com.example.demo, create class HelloController annotated @RestController.

Controller (for now): the gateway where you write API endpoints — which method runs for /hello, /orders… Exactly Lecture 1's problem: mapping a URL / endpoint to a Java method.

Plain Java method: must return a string, so public String hello() { return "Hello World"; } (not void).

You could call it yourself from main:

HelloController controller = new HelloController();
String s = controller.hello();
System.out.println(s);          // Hello World

That prints to the console — normal Java. Goal: run the method when a browser hits /hello. Add @GetMapping("/hello") (GET method + endpoint) and remove the manual call.

package com.example.demo;

import org.springframework.web.bind.annotation.GetMapping;
import org.springframework.web.bind.annotation.RestController;

@RestController                          // "this class holds API endpoints"
public class HelloController {

    @GetMapping("/hello")                // GET /hello  →  hello()
    public String hello() {
        return "Hello World";            // becomes the HTTP response body
    }
}

Rerun: nothing on console, Tomcat on 8080, http://localhost:8080/hello shows Hello World.

How little it took: Initializr skeleton, untouched structure, one class, one method, two annotations:
  • @RestController — this class is a controller (endpoint gateway).
  • @GetMapping("/hello") — maps GET /hello to hello().
Browser hits /hello → hello() returns "Hello World" → displayed.
Leading slash: lecture writes @GetMapping("hello"); works because Spring adds a missing /. "/hello" is the convention.

Returning HTML instead of plain text

Return "<h1>Hello World</h1>" → browser renders a big heading.

Why it renders (added): @RestController writes the String straight into the body. Browser sends Accept: text/html,… → Spring responds Content-Type: text/html. Fine for a demo; real pages use a template engine (Thymeleaf). REST APIs return JSON, auto-produced from returned objects.

Changing the port: application.properties

In src/main/resources/application.properties:

server.port=9090

Log: Tomcat started on port 9090. localhost:8080/hello → can't be reached; localhost:9090/hello → Hello World. Point: the Tomcat server listens on the port, not "the Spring Boot app" by itself.

Precision (added): embedded Tomcat runs inside the same JVM process, started by Boot. To the OS your process owns the port; Tomcat is the component accepting connections.

Step 3: a second endpoint, /bye

A controller can have many endpoints:

@GetMapping("/bye")
public String greetBye() {       // method name need not match the endpoint
    return "Bye";
}

Rerun (needed after every change). /hello → Hello World, /bye → Bye. Methods are called based on the endpoint; method name and endpoint are independent.

No more manual restarts (added): Spring Boot DevTools auto-restarts on class changes; dev only, excluded from production builds.

Questions still unanswered

Hello World in ~10 minutes — the lecture's motive: Boot's speed and auto-configuration. Open questions:

  • Who started the Tomcat server?
  • Did we install Tomcat? No — it came via the Spring Web dependency ("uses Tomcat as the default embedded container").
  • How did /hello map to hello()? Nothing in main calls it.
  • What are @RestController, @GetMapping, @SpringBootApplication? What does SpringApplication.run do?
Preview of the answer (added; each gets its own lecture):
  1. SpringApplication.run creates the application context (IoC container).
  2. Component scanning (part of @SpringBootApplication) finds HelloController etc. in the same package and sub-packages, creating beans.
  3. Auto-configuration sees Spring MVC + Tomcat on the classpath (via Spring Web), starts embedded Tomcat, registers the DispatcherServlet.
  4. Spring MVC reads @GetMappings into a URL → method table; on GET /hello the DispatcherServlet calls hello().

Q: "If Spring Boot configures everything, why learn Spring MVC and Core?"

  • Boot can't be understood alone. Explaining @GetMapping/@RestController needs Spring MVC; its ideology needs Spring Core.
  • Debugging. If /bye doesn't show "Bye", you can't debug without knowing what annotations and controllers do and how an endpoint gets hit when main calls nothing. Stepping into SpringApplication.run would make no sense.

Hence the full Spring architecture in this series.

Corrections & worth knowing

  • "What is my IP" shows your public IP (usually the router's). Home devices sit behind NAT with private IPs (192.168.x.x, 10.x.x.x); inbound traffic reaches the router, not your laptop, unless port-forwarded. Local IP: ipconfig (Windows) / ip addr (Linux).
  • Client apps don't "run on a port" like servers. Lecture says Chrome, WhatsApp, Spotify each run on a port. Servers listen on fixed ports (8080, 443, 3306); clients get a temporary ephemeral source port per outgoing connection, where replies return. Either way IP + port identifies the endpoint.
  • One listener per port. A second app on 8080 fails: "Port 8080 was already in use". Fix with server.port or stop the other process; server.port=0 picks a random free port.
  • Reverse proxy forwards, doesn't redirect. Lecture says "redirected" to 8080; a redirect would send the browser to a new URL. Proxy forwards internally; client never sees 8080.
  • DNS results are cached (browser, OS, resolvers) — not looked up per request. localhost resolves locally (hosts file), no DNS.
  • Boot 4 web starter. Initializr still says "Spring Web", but the Boot 4 pom.xml lists spring-boot-starter-webmvc (+ spring-boot-starter-webmvc-test) where Boot 3 used spring-boot-starter-web / spring-boot-starter-test (confirmed in Lecture 3). Same behaviour: Spring MVC + embedded Tomcat.
  • Other ways to create the project. IntelliJ Ultimate's Initializr wizard, VS Code's Spring Initializr extension, or curl https://start.spring.io/starter.zip -d dependencies=web -o demo.zip — same service.
  • Running without an IDE. ./mvnw spring-boot:run (mvnw.cmd on Windows). ./mvnw package then java -jar target/demo-0.0.1-SNAPSHOT.jar. Project version defaults to 0.0.1-SNAPSHOT (same "work in progress" meaning).
  • Keep controllers under the main class's package. Scanning starts at DemoApplication's package (com.example.demo); a controller in com.other isn't found → 404. Classic "my endpoint doesn't work" bug.

Lecture 3: Maven in Depth

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #3 · 1h28m · Code Army · JARs, dependencies, pom.xml, repositories, lifecycle, archetypes

Why a build tool at all

A Java project needs a management tool — mainly Maven or Gradle. Series uses Maven: widely used and easy to understand. Starts with JAR files.

The problem: sharing a project of many class files

Demo.java → compiler → Demo.class → any JVM. Real projects have many classes (Order.java, User.java, Payment.java…); even compiled together you get one .class per class.

Sharing with a friend:

ApproachProblem
Email every .class fileTerrible — each downloaded and run separately.
Zip a folder of classesWorks but ugly: with hundreds of files some get missed, folder structure may not be preserved, extra resources (images, static, properties files) left out.

Java needed a standard way to share multi-file projects → the JAR.

JAR: Java ARchive

Definition:
A JAR (Java ARchive) is a zip file acting as a package, containing:
  • Many compiled .class files.
  • Resources (images, properties files).
  • Your folder / package structure.
Others integrate it into their apps. A JAR always holds compiled code.

Two advantages:

  1. Share your own code — e.g. package a calculator library as calculator.jar; a friend calls its classes.
  2. Use third-party libraries (more important) — download the MySQL connector JAR instead of writing one; Postgres has its own. Spring Boot and Spring MVC also arrive as JARs (dependencies).

Library vs application

LibraryApplication
Runnable?No — maybe no main; classes for othersYes — has main, runs as a whole
Used howPut inside an applicationRun directly
ExamplesJava built-in libraries, MySQL connector, calculator libraryYour Spring Boot app

A library used inside an app = a dependency (Maven / Spring Boot term).

Q: "When I build a JAR, are my third-party JARs inside it?"

Depends on library vs application:

  • Library JAR (my-library.jar using MySQL connector): only your code, a non-executable JAR. Users learn its required libraries from a special file — Maven's pom.xml — and Maven fetches them automatically.
  • Application JAR (Spring Boot my-application.jar with Main.java): executable, runs independently; dependencies like MySQL connector are inside it.
Precision note (added): an app JAR isn't automatically fat. Plain Maven package of any project gives a thin JAR (your classes only), runnable via java -jar only if the manifest names a Main-Class. Boot's spring-boot-maven-plugin (in <build>) repackages it into an executable fat JAR: dependencies in BOOT-INF/lib, a launcher as entry point. Published library JARs carry their pom in META-INF/maven/…, and the repository stores the .pom beside the JAR — that's how Maven learns their dependencies.

The classpath: how the JVM finds a class

Only your code is compiled; third-party JARs are already compiled. When your code calls a library class, how does the JVM find it?

Definition: the classpath = list of directories and JARs where the JVM searches for classes. Lecture's version: own compiled code first, then the JARs' bytecode.

More precisely (added): class loaders load JDK classes first (bootstrap, platform loaders), then search the application classpath in listed order; first match wins. Maven lists target/classes before dependency JARs, so your code is found first.

Why manual dependency management breaks down

Manually: search "mysql connector jar download" → mysql.com → version 9.7.0, your OS → download. Fine for one JAR, not in practice:

  1. Manual effort — real apps need hundreds of JARs.
  2. Version mismatch — Spring Framework 7 works with Boot 4, Spring 6 won't; Spring 7 + a Boot 3 JAR breaks. You'd manage it all.
  3. Transitive dependencies — Boot's JAR depends on JARs that depend on others; you'd need to know and download all.
  4. Sharing a library — your JAR carries no info about what it needs; you'd add a text file of libraries and exact versions, and the friend still does the manual work.

Maven: a project management tool

Maven's promise: "You focus on business logic. Just tell me which dependencies you need, and I'll download them. If they depend on other dependencies (transitive), I'll download those too, without you even telling me."

Definition: Maven is a project management (and build) tool, an Apache project, handling the complications of apps/libraries using many third-party libraries.

Four jobs:

  1. Standard folder structure — one fixed layout common to everyone, so teams don't each invent their own.
  2. Compiles your code — every class in one click.
  3. Creates your JAR — compile and package are lifecycle phases, alongside clean, test, etc.
  4. Downloads dependencies, including transitive ones.

Getting Maven

  • Download from the Apache Maven site ("maven download") and install.
  • Not needed for IDE work — IntelliJ ships with Maven preinstalled.
Transcript slip: lecture says install Maven by "setting up the classpath". For the CLI, add Maven's bin to the PATH env variable, with JAVA_HOME pointing to a JDK. The classpath is the JVM's class-search list, unrelated.

Creating a Maven project in IntelliJ

  1. New Project, name maven-demo, choose location.
  2. Build system: IntelliJ (built-in), Maven, Gradle → Maven.
  3. JDK 23 (preselected). Tick Add sample code.
  4. Advanced settings: GroupId org.example, ArtifactId maven-demo (defaults).
  5. Create.
Why not IntelliJ's built-in build system: teammates on Maven or Gradle would have different folder structures and build systems (how code is built, compiled, packaged, and how dependencies download). Maven as the standard makes IDE choice (IntelliJ, Eclipse, NetBeans) irrelevant.

Sample code deleted, leaving a clean Main class with main.

Maven's standard folder structure

maven-demo/
├── .idea/                IntelliJ's internal files (ignore)
├── .mvn/                 Maven internal files (ignore)
├── .gitignore
├── pom.xml               ← the most important Maven file
├── src/
│   ├── main/
│   │   ├── java/         .java source files → org/example/Main.java
│   │   └── resources/    non-Java files: text files, images, properties
│   └── test/
│       └── java/         test classes, same package layout as main
└── (External Libraries)  IDE view of downloaded JARs; now only the JDK (OpenJDK 23)
  • src: the whole application.
  • main: real code / business logic. test: unit tests (JUnit, Mockito, used later).
  • main/java: all .java files. main/resources: all non-Java files.
  • External Libraries: downloaded JARs; now only the JDK's.
Don't be confused by the dots: org.example under java is two folders (org → example), shown on one line because org has one child. Add org.example2 and they split into org → example, example2. Your files go under org.example (Demo.java, Demo2.java); add packages like org.example.controller for HelloController. Boot apps typically have a controller package.

The Spring Boot project's structure, side by side

Regenerate the Lecture 2 project if needed from start.spring.io:

  • Maven, Java, any non-SNAPSHOT Boot 4.x (4.0.7 or 4.1.0 now).
  • Default metadata, Jar, Properties, Java 21.
  • Spring Web dependency.

Versus the empty Maven project:

  • Same src/main/java, src/main/resources, src/test/java, pom.xml.
  • Package com.example.demo = folders com → example → demo, from groupId com.example + artifactId demo. Contains DemoApplication (generated) and HelloController (Lecture 2).
  • resources has static/ (images, static resources), templates/ and application.properties (port changed there).
  • test/java mirrors packages with DemoApplicationTests; a HelloControllerTest goes in the same package under test. Writing tests comes later.
  • A target/ folder appears if you ran the app; deleted for now, explained next.

Note (added): package names needn't match groupId/artifactId — just a generator convention. IntelliJ quickstart used groupId alone (org.example); Initializr uses groupId.artifactId.

Compiling: the target folder

Run compiles then runs. In a Maven project, compiling (and packaging) is Maven's job.

  1. Open the Maven tool window ("m" icon; enable in settings if hidden): Lifecycle, Plugins, Repositories.
  2. Lifecycle has clean, validate, compile, test… Click compile.
  3. BUILD SUCCESS; target/ appears with classes/, generated-sources/, maven-status/.
  4. target/classes mirrors the package structure: org/example/controller, Demo.class, Demo2.class, Main.class.

Main.class looks readable only because the IDE decompiles it. Delete target; compile/run recreates it.

Q: "Does the green Run button use Maven?"

No — IntelliJ's own build system. Knowing it's a Maven project, IntelliJ outputs to the same place Maven would (target/classes), so it looks identical. (Added:) force Maven via Settings → Build Tools → Maven → Runner → "Delegate IDE build/run actions to Maven".

Creating a JAR: package

Maven tool window → package. Log: "Building jar: …maven-demo-1.0-SNAPSHOT.jar", placed in target/ beside classes/ (naming rule below). One shareable package — though with Maven you won't share manually. Three jobs done; the fourth (dependencies) needs pom.xml.

pom.xml: Project Object Model

POM = Project Object Model: complete project information — name, version, third-party libraries, plugins. Maven's most important file; small when empty, grows with config and dependencies.

<?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?>
<project xmlns="http://maven.apache.org/POM/4.0.0"
         xmlns:xsi="http://www.w3.org/2001/XMLSchema-instance"
         xsi:schemaLocation="http://maven.apache.org/POM/4.0.0 http://maven.apache.org/xsd/maven-4.0.0.xsd">
    <modelVersion>4.0.0</modelVersion>

    <groupId>org.example</groupId>
    <artifactId>maven-demo</artifactId>
    <version>1.0-SNAPSHOT</version>

    <properties>
        <maven.compiler.source>23</maven.compiler.source>
        <maven.compiler.target>23</maven.compiler.target>
        <project.build.sourceEncoding>UTF-8</project.build.sourceEncoding>
    </properties>
</project>
PartMeaning
<project>Root tag of every pom.
xmlns, xsi, schemaLocationThe pom's schema (allowed tags). An invented <abc></abc> → "invalid content was found".
<modelVersion>POM model version (4.0.0), not Maven's version.
groupId, artifactId, versionGAV coordinates — together uniquely identify the project worldwide.
<properties>Key–value pairs: tag name = key, content = value.

GAV: groupId, artifactId, version

  • groupId must be unique worldwide so others can reference your library. Use your domain reversed: codearmy.in → in.codearmy, optionally extended (in.codearmy.java, in.codearmy.spring). Maven convention, not Spring. No domain? org.example for practice.
  • artifactId = project name: maven-demo.
  • version defaults to 1.0-SNAPSHOT (work in progress, possibly buggy). Drop -SNAPSHOT when complete and tested to mark a safe release (convention).

Properties: custom keys and ${…}

  • Generated: maven.compiler.source = 23, maven.compiler.target = 23, project.build.sourceEncoding = UTF-8.
  • Any key allowed: <java.version>23</java.version>, even <author.name>Aditya</author.name>.
  • Reuse with ${key}: <version>1.0-SNAPSHOT-${author.name}</version> + package → maven-demo-1.0-SNAPSHOT-Aditya.jar.
Worth knowing (added):
  • Expressions in <version> trigger a warning; only ${revision}, ${sha1}, ${changelist} are meant for it.
  • Java 9+: prefer <maven.compiler.release>23</maven.compiler.release> over source/target — also checks you only use that version's APIs.
  • java.version is special only in Spring Boot (parent pom maps it to compiler release).

<packaging>: jar or war; defaults to jar.

Downloading dependencies with Maven

<dependencies> lists external JARs; each <dependency> has its own GAV. Find coordinates on mvnrepository.com:

  1. Search "mysql connector" → MySQL Connector/J.
  2. Pick a version — lecture's rule: not the very latest (less tested, few users, likelier bugs). Older ones show higher usage (168, 193…), but don't go too far back (mismatch). Picked 9.5.0.
  3. Maven tab (vs Gradle, SBT, Mill, Ivy, Grape…) → copy snippet into <dependencies>, drop the comment line.
  4. Not downloaded yet (External Libraries shows only OpenJDK) → click the Maven reload icon ("m" with arrows) or reload from the tool window.
<dependencies>
    <dependency>
        <groupId>com.mysql</groupId>
        <artifactId>mysql-connector-j</artifactId>
        <version>9.5.0</version>
        <scope>compile</scope>
    </dependency>
</dependencies>

External Libraries now shows mysql-connector-j and protobuf-java (Google Protocol Buffers). The tool window's Dependencies node shows protobuf-java under the connector — a transitive dependency fetched unasked. Fast because already cached locally; first time is slower.

Adding Hibernate (org.hibernate.orm : hibernate-core, 7.3.6.Final, slightly behind newest) pulls in many JARs (Jakarta, GlassFish, Eclipse…), several levels deep — you asked only for Hibernate.

Dependency scope

<scope> is optional (often omitted in older snippets): the phase needing the dependency. Default compile; test = test phase only. Full list (added):

ScopeAvailable atIn the final app?Typical example
compile (default)Compile, test, runYesSpring, Hibernate
runtimeTest, run, not compileYesJDBC drivers like mysql-connector-j (Initializr uses this scope; code only uses the JDBC API)
testTest compile and run onlyNoJUnit, Mockito, spring-boot-starter-…-test
providedCompile and testNo: server provides itServlet API for a WAR on Tomcat, Lombok
system / importSpecial cases—import brings in a BOM inside dependencyManagement

The Spring Boot project's pom.xml

Bigger, because Boot adds:

  • Same schema, modelVersion, GAV, plus a special <parent> tag (below).
  • Empty tags (url, licenses, developers, scm) — ignore.
  • <properties> with java.version for reuse.
  • Non-empty <dependencies>: spring-boot-starter-webmvc (web apps with Spring MVC) and spring-boot-starter-webmvc-test (tests), each with transitive dependencies.
  • <build><plugins>: Maven needs plugins for lifecycle work (compile, package, verify, install). Boot's is spring-boot-maven-plugin. More later.

Initializr's value: needed dependencies are pre-listed, Maven syncs them into External Libraries.

Parent POM and the Super POM

POMs support inheritance. The top is the Super POM, like Java's Object. maven-demo has no <parent>, so its parent is the Super POM by default (like a class extending nothing still extends Object).

Why? A pom needs far more config than you see; the Super POM holds it and yours reuses it, staying light.

Right-click pom → Maven → Show Effective POM (maven-demo-effective-pom.xml) contains:

  • Everything in your pom: GAV, properties, MySQL and Hibernate dependencies.
  • Things you never wrote: <repositories>, <pluginRepositories>, <build> settings (source, output, resource dirs, test resources), <pluginManagement>…

Write only what you need; override inherited settings by declaring the same tag (e.g. <repositories>), like a subclass overriding a method.

The Boot pom's parent is org.springframework.boot : spring-boot-starter-parent; Ctrl/Cmd-click it → its parent is spring-boot-dependencies, which has none, so its parent is the Super POM. Multi-level, as in Java: each level declares some things and overrides some.

Super POM vs effective POM

Super POMEffective POM
What it isBuilt-in parent of all POMsFinal merged POM Maven actually uses
ContainsMaven defaults: central repo, standard dirs, default plugin versionsYour pom + all parents + Super POM (+ active profiles, settings), merged with overrides
Where to see itInside Maven's own JARIntelliJ "Show Effective POM", or mvn help:effective-pom
Precision note: lecture calls the effective POM "the Super POM, the parent of my pom". It's the merge result — hence your MySQL and Hibernate dependencies appear in it. Lecture corrects itself later: Super POM = parent of all; effective POM = what Maven finally uses.

Q: "Why does the Spring Boot pom have no dependency versions?"

(Added; follows from the parent chain.) spring-boot-dependencies is a BOM (Bill of Materials): its <dependencyManagement> pins tested, compatible versions of hundreds of libraries (Spring, Jackson, Tomcat, Hibernate, MySQL Connector…). You write only groupId + artifactId — solving the "version mismatch" problem. In Boot, don't hand-pick versions for managed libraries; the mvnrepository method is for plain Maven, and a hard-coded version can break the tested combination.

Repositories: where Maven downloads from

Effective POM's <repositories> names the central repository, https://repo.maven.apache.org/maven2. Maven Central holds thousands of JARs; give a GAV, Maven downloads it.

Two kinds of repository:

  1. Maven Central — remote.
  2. Local repository — hidden .m2 in your home directory (~/.m2 Mac/Linux, C:\Users\<you>\.m2 Windows); repository/ inside stores all downloads.

Local repository's two purposes:

  • Cache — check ~/.m2/repository first; if missing, download from central, show under External Libraries, store in .m2. First build slow, later fast (noticeable with 100–200 dependencies).
  • Your own JARs — installed via the install phase (below).

Naming rule: artifactId-version.jar

  • JAR file name is always artifactId-version.jar: maven-demo-1.0-SNAPSHOT.jar, mysql-connector-j-9.5.0.jar.
  • Hence third-party libraries are often called artifacts ("downloading artifacts").
  • In .m2/repository, folders follow coordinates: com/mysql/mysql-connector-j/9.5.0/. Mac: show hidden folders with Cmd+Shift+. or cd ~/.m2/repository.

Other remote repositories (company-wide)

Third kind: other remote repositories. A company may run a private remote repository (e.g. Sonatype Nexus, JFrog Artifactory, added):

  1. Private artifacts — internal libraries shouldn't be public on Central.
  2. Safety — Central is open; some artifacts may be vulnerable, buggy or threats. Company repo holds only tried-and-tested ones.
<repositories>
    <repository>
        <id>company-repo</id>
        <url>https://repo.example.com/maven</url>
    </repository>
</repositories>

Lecture adds then comments it out (URL doesn't exist → build error).

Correction: adding a repository doesn't replace central: lecture says Maven then fetches "from here instead of central". A <repository> with a new id is added; central (id central, from the Super POM) is still consulted. To route all downloads via a company repo, configure a <mirror> with <mirrorOf>*</mirrorOf> in ~/.m2/settings.xml, or redefine id central. Credentials belong in settings.xml, never the pom.

Demo: deleting the local repository. Delete ~/.m2/repository → every project breaks (External Libraries back to just OpenJDK). Sync may leave "unresolved dependency" errors → use Reload All Maven Projects. Everything re-downloads (slowly) and re-caches.

The Maven lifecycle

Maven handles the project end to end: cleaning, validating, compiling, testing, packaging, deploying. Tool window lists clean, validate, compile, test, package, verify, install, site, deploy.

Three lifecycles:

LifecycleMain phasesPurpose
cleancleanDelete old build output (target/)
default (the important one)validate → compile → test → package → verify → install → deployBuild, test, publish
sitesiteDocs and reports
Key rule: phases run in order: a phase runs every earlier phase in its lifecycle first. validate only validates; compile validates then compiles; deploy runs validate → compile → test → package → verify → install → deploy. More hidden intermediate phases exist.
  • validate — project structure: pom exists, correct, readable, required details present.
  • compile — .java → .class in target/classes.
  • test — runs your test cases (JUnit, Mockito…) from src/test, e.g. HelloControllerTest; after compile.
  • package — builds the JAR in target/ from compiled code, only after tests pass (no tests → just builds).
  • verify — extra checks via plugins in <plugins>: integration tests, code quality (extra spaces, unused variables), coverage. Teams can require these before install.
  • install — JAR into the local repository (~/.m2/repository); only verified code gets there.
  • deploy — uploads to a remote repository (usually company-wide) so others depend on your artifact like Maven Central.
Maven deploy ≠ deploying code: git push to GitHub/GitLab sends whole source code toward production. Maven deploy only publishes the JAR / artifact to an artifact repository; it runs nothing.
  • clean — independent; deletes target/ and locally built artifacts in the project. JARs installed in .m2 are not touched.
  • site — docs and reports; ran long (downloading plugins), produced target/site with CSS, images, dependency summary. Rarely needed. Day to day: default and clean.

Running Maven from the terminal

mvn compile validates then compiles. "mvn: command not found" → install for CLI:

  • Mac: brew install maven.
  • Windows: choco install maven (Chocolatey).
  • Or download from the Maven site and add to PATH.

Then mvn compile, mvn install, mvn deploy…

Combining phases across lifecycles:

  • mvn clean compile — clean, then compile afresh.
  • mvn clean install — most famous: delete target, then validate → compile → test → package → verify → install (test/verify effectively skipped here with nothing to do).

Installed at ~/.m2/repository/org/example/maven-demo/1.0-SNAPSHOT/maven-demo-1.0-SNAPSHOT.jar: path = groupId (as folders) / artifactId / version /, file artifactId-version.jar.

Clarification (lecture got tangled): lecture wonders why the path has org/example but no maven-demo package, mixing Java packages with repo folders. Unrelated: repo path is always groupId/artifactId/version/ (org/example/maven-demo/1.0-SNAPSHOT/); Java packages matter only inside the JAR.
Worth knowing (added):
  • Maven Wrapper from Initializr: ./mvnw clean install (mvnw.cmd on Windows) downloads the project's pinned Maven — no global install, same Maven for everyone.
  • Phases run plugin goals: compile → maven-compiler-plugin, test → maven-surefire-plugin, package → maven-jar-plugin, install → maven-install-plugin, deploy → maven-deploy-plugin. Hence <plugins> matters.
  • Flags: -DskipTests skips tests; -o offline; -U forces re-checking SNAPSHOTs. mvn dependency:tree prints the transitive tree.
  • More phases exist: clean has pre-clean, post-clean; site has pre-site, post-site, site-deploy; default has ~23, incl. process-resources, test-compile.

Maven archetypes: ready-made templates

New Project → Maven Archetype: a ready-made template.

  • Catalogs: Internal (small, built into the IDE); Maven Central (much larger, incl. Spring Boot web starter and Spring JPA — like Initializr).
  • maven-archetype-quickstart (version 1.1) generated:
    • A pom with a JUnit test dependency.
    • App.java printing Hello World.
    • AppTest.java with unit tests.
  • A Spring Boot starter archetype from Central generated:
    • A pom (Apache license header) with Boot starter dependencies and Hibernate Validator.
    • Some plugins.
    • Sample classes like HelloConsumer and a hello implementation.
    Keep needed dependencies, remove the rest; naming conventions differ from the series'.

Usable, but Spring Initializr remains the way to start Boot projects. (Added:) CLI: mvn archetype:generate.

Transcript slips: quickstart's dependency is JUnit only (lecture says "basically Mockito"; Mockito is separate). Lecture ties the Spring archetype's plugins to the "validate stage", then corrects to "verify stage"; in fact plugins bind to any phase — compile, test, package all run via plugins.

Maven vs Gradle

MavenGradle
Build filepom.xml (XML, declarative)build.gradle (Groovy) or build.gradle.kts (Kotlin DSL)
ModelFixed lifecycle and conventionsFlexible task graph
SpeedGoodOften faster (incremental builds, build cache, daemon)
Learning curveEasy, predictableSteeper; scripts are code
Where usedMost enterprise Java / Spring projectsAndroid (default), large multi-module builds
SharedBoth use Maven repositories and GAV; both offered by Spring Initializr

Q: "Two dependencies need different versions of the same library?"

(Added; common interview follow-up.) Maven keeps one version per artifact by "nearest wins" (closest to your project in the tree); at equal depth, first declared wins. May silently pick a version one library can't use → runtime NoSuchMethodError / ClassNotFoundException. Tools:

  • mvn dependency:tree to see what was picked.
  • <exclusions> to drop an unwanted transitive dependency.
  • Declare the wanted version directly, or pin it in <dependencyManagement> / a BOM (Boot's BOM already does).

Corrections & worth knowing

  • .m2 is in your home directory, not "root level". Lecture says root on Mac, "somewhere under Users" on Windows. It's ~/.m2 (/Users/<you>/.m2) on Mac, C:\Users\<you>\.m2 on Windows; changeable via <localRepository> in settings.xml.
  • "Don't take the latest version" needs nuance. Avoid SNAPSHOT, milestone, RC. Among stable releases, newer patches carry security and bug fixes — prefer the latest patch of a line (newest 9.5.x). mvnrepository usage counts show popularity, not quality.
  • Boot 4 starters confirmed. Generated pom lists spring-boot-starter-webmvc and spring-boot-starter-webmvc-test — Boot 4 names for Boot 3's spring-boot-starter-web / spring-boot-starter-test. Settles Lecture 2's open note.
  • SNAPSHOTs behave differently. Maven re-checks remotes for newer SNAPSHOT builds (daily by default); releases are immutable, downloaded once. Never depend on SNAPSHOTs in a release.
  • Don't commit target/ — generated output (Initializr's .gitignore excludes it). Do commit pom.xml, mvnw, .mvn/wrapper.
  • WAR packaging = web archive for an external servlet container; JAR is Boot's default with embedded server (Lecture 2).

Lecture 4: Dependency Injection & Inversion of Control (Plain Java)

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #4 · 39 min · Code Army · The Spring Core ideas, shown without Spring

Why Spring Core ideas at all?

We already write reasonably clean OOP Java without Spring — so why Dependency Injection (DI) and Inversion of Control (IoC)? Not about Spring MVC/web: these ideas apply even to console apps and make code clean and loosely coupled.

Plan: no Spring at all — problem and solutions in plain Java; next lecture shows Spring's way.

Setup: new Maven project core-demo (groupId in.codearmy, JDK 23, sample code), no dependencies, pom untouched; generated code replaced with a quick "Hello" check.

The problem: OrderService builds its own EmailService

Deliberately simple scenario: an order service places an order, then notifies the user.

public class EmailService {
    public void sendNotification() {
        System.out.println("Email notification sent");
    }
}

public class OrderService {
    EmailService notification = new EmailService();   // creates its own dependency

    public void placeOrder() {
        System.out.println("Order placed");           // stands in for real business logic
        notification.sendNotification();
    }
}

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        OrderService order = new OrderService();
        order.placeOrder();
    }
}
Order placed
Email notification sent

Works: OrderService, EmailService, and Main calling OrderService.

What a dependency is

Definition: placeOrder() can't work unless an EmailService object exists → OrderService depends on EmailService; EmailService is a dependency of OrderService.

Same idea as Lecture 3's pom dependencies (code depends on a library JAR) — here one class depends on another class's object.

Why this is tight coupling

Core problem: tight coupling — a design issue, not a Spring one. In one line: OrderService creates its own dependency.

Analogy: tight vs loose coupling: "Delhi to Chandigarh, at this time, in this bus, with this driver, from this company" = tightly coupled — I only needed to commute but pinned every detail. Caring only that I get there = loosely coupled.

OrderService only needed "send a notification", but it:

  1. Bound itself to a concrete class (non-abstract, all methods implemented). Email only; switching to SMS/pop-up means editing OrderService.
  2. Creates the object itself — both uses and builds its dependency (multiple jobs). Revisited after fixing point 1.

Step 1: code to an interface

Program to interfaces, not concrete classes. Editing OrderService for each new SmsService/PopupNotificationService breaks the Open/Closed Principle (O in SOLID: extend, don't modify). Plain good design — but Spring's whole ideology is built on it.

Classes go in a notification package (interface, EmailService, SmsService, PopupNotificationService); OrderService and Main stay top-level.

public interface NotificationService {
    void sendNotification();
}

public class EmailService implements NotificationService {
    @Override
    public void sendNotification() {
        System.out.println("Email notification sent");
    }
}

public class SmsService implements NotificationService {
    @Override
    public void sendNotification() {
        System.out.println("SMS notification sent");
    }
}

public class PopupNotificationService implements NotificationService {
    @Override
    public void sendNotification() {
        System.out.println("Popup notification sent");
    }
}

In OrderService: NotificationService notification = new EmailService();. Swap to new SmsService() → "SMS notification sent" (same for pop-up) — basic polymorphism.

Why the interface alone doesn't fix it

The variable's type is an interface, but the object is still created concretely inside OrderService.

  • Creating an object isn't the problem; where it's created is. OrderService's job is orders (placeOrder, add, delete) — why build notification objects?
  • SRP breaks (S in SOLID: one reason to change). Order logic plus object creation (a factory's job). It should use a notification, not create one.
  • OCP still breaks — each new notification type still changes OrderService.
Dependencies are normal; self-creating them is the problem: real projects have hundreds (even hundreds of thousands) of interdependent files — a dependency tree (A → B → C) is fine. The problem is depending on something and also constructing it. Creating objects is not business logic (see the LLD / clean-code playlist).

Step 2: Dependency Injection

OrderService: "I won't create it — hand me a ready-made object." Simplest way: ask in the constructor:

public class OrderService {
    private NotificationService notification;             // instance variable, interface type

    public OrderService(NotificationService notification) {
        this.notification = notification;                 // whatever is passed in
    }

    public void placeOrder() {
        System.out.println("Order placed");
        notification.sendNotification();                  // calls the passed-in object's method
    }
}

Main now errors (it called the no-arg constructor). Main creates OrderService, so it passes the object:

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        NotificationService notification = new EmailService();   // or new SmsService(), new PopupNotificationService()
        OrderService order = new OrderService(notification);    // inject it
        order.placeOrder();
    }
}
Order placed
Email notification sent

Switch to SMS by changing only Main. OrderService and the notification classes (interface + 3 implementations) are untouched.

Why this fixes both principles:
  • OCP holds — new type needs no change to OrderService.
  • SRP holds — only orders; it delegates notification, doesn't create it.
  • OrderService doesn't know which notification it gets — any NotificationService: "give me any notification and I'll call sendNotification."
Definition: Dependency Injection: the dependency is no longer created by OrderService but supplied from outside (here Main): "Don't create it; I'll inject it." Main creates e.g. PopupNotificationService, passes it via the constructor; OrderService stores and calls it in placeOrder.
DI: don't let a class create what it depends on; provide it from outside. "Outside" can be anywhere; here Main is the driver that wires everything, keeping other classes independent.

Benefit: easy unit testing with a fake

Unit test = one class in isolation. Tests go in src/test/java, same package, e.g. OrderServiceTest (Lecture 3).

  • Old design (new EmailService() inside): testing placeOrder sends a real email — welded to EmailService though you only test order logic.
  • DI design: pass a fake implementing the same interface — accepted unchanged (interface reference holds child object).
public class FakeEmailService implements NotificationService {
    @Override
    public void sendNotification() {
        System.out.println("Dummy email sent");   // no real email, no cost
    }
}

// in Main (for the demo)
OrderService order = new OrderService(new FakeEmailService());
order.placeOrder();
Order placed
Dummy email sent

Why pay for real emails while testing? The fake tests OrderService alone.

Worth knowing (added): the lecture wires FakeEmailService in Main for the demo. Really, fakes live in src/test/java, passed by the test — usually generated by Mockito: mock(NotificationService.class), then verify(mock).sendNotification(). Fakes, stubs, mocks = test doubles; all need DI to be pluggable.

Next problem: Main becomes a wiring nightmare

With UserService, PaymentService and dozens more, Main creates and wires every object one by one — very complicated.

Spring Core fixes this: it manages and injects dependencies; even Main needs almost nothing (looks magical at first).

The DI mantra

One-liners from the lecture:
  • "Don't create your own dependencies, get your dependencies."
  • "A class should ask for what it needs, and not build everything itself."

DI is not a Spring concept. Spring automates it; this lecture did DI without Spring.

Types of dependency injection

  1. Constructor injection — dependency is a constructor parameter.
  2. Setter injection — a setter assigns it (IntelliJ Generate → Setter, choose notification).
  3. Field injection — framework sets the field directly; covered with Spring (not practical in plain Java, see note).
public class OrderService {
    private NotificationService notification;

    public OrderService() { }                                     // overloaded empty constructor

    public OrderService(NotificationService notification) {       // constructor injection
        this.notification = notification;
    }

    public void setNotification(NotificationService notification) { // setter injection
        this.notification = notification;
    }

    public void placeOrder() {
        System.out.println("Order placed");
        notification.sendNotification();
    }
}

// Main, using setter injection
OrderService order = new OrderService();
order.setNotification(new EmailService());
order.placeOrder();          // Order placed / Email notification sent

Constructor vs setter injection

Added (not in lecture) — standard interview question
Constructor injectionSetter injection
Object always complete?Yes — can't exist without dependenciesNo — new OrderService() + placeOrder() without setter → NullPointerException
ImmutabilityField can be finalField must be mutable
Best forMandatory dependenciesOptional / changed later
Signals a problemLong constructor = class does too much (SRP)Hides dependency count
Circular dependenciesFail fast at startupResolvable — often hides a design flaw
Spring's recommendationPreferredFor optional dependencies
Precision note: field injection without Spring (added): lecture says it "isn't possible" — it is (public field, or reflection on a private one), just not sensible. Spring (@Autowired on a field) uses reflection to write private fields. Hence discouraged: hidden dependency, can't be final, can't build the class in a plain unit test without the framework.

Inversion of Control: what "control" means

IoC is tightly related to DI; often used interchangeably but distinct. "Control" here = who creates the dependency.

  • Initial design: Main creates OrderService, which creates EmailService — control inside OrderService.
  • After DI: Main creates both and provides EmailService to OrderService — control flows from outside in.
Definition: Inversion of Control: control of creating EmailService moved from inside OrderService to outside — control is inverted.

IoC vs DI

Inversion of ControlDependency Injection
What it isAn idea / principleAn approach / technique
Says"Don't control creation and flow yourself; let something outside do it""Pass a class its dependencies through the constructor, setter or field"
RelationDI is a way to achieve IoC. IoC = goal, DI = implementation
Transcript slip: "dependency inversion" should be "dependency injection": the lecture says "inversion" several times meaning injection. Different things:
  • Dependency Injection — technique: supply dependencies from outside.
  • Dependency Inversion Principle (DIP) — D in SOLID: high-level modules shouldn't depend on low-level modules; both should depend on abstractions. Step 1 applied it (OrderService → NotificationService interface).
DIP says depend on an interface; DI supplies the concrete object. Complementary, not synonyms.
Worth knowing: IoC is broader than DI (added): IoC = the framework calls your code ("Hollywood Principle": don't call us, we'll call you). DI inverts object creation; others invert flow:
  • Template Method — base class calls your overridden steps.
  • Event listeners and callbacks.
  • Servlets: Tomcat calls doGet (Lecture 1).
  • @GetMapping methods: Spring calls hello() (Lecture 2).
Service Locator (class asks a registry) also inverts creation but hides dependencies, so DI is preferred.

Where Spring fits: the IoC container

Main now injects, wires and configures everything — better than self-creation, but it gets complicated. The Spring Framework's IoC container does exactly what Main does; right now Main is our container.

JobNow, done by MainLater, done by the Spring IoC container
Create objectsnew EmailService(), new OrderService(…)Container instantiates them
Manage objectsImplicitly decides creation/destructionContainer controls the lifecycle
Connect objects togetherPasses notification into constructor/setter (wiring)Container wires dependencies automatically
  • In Spring code new becomes almost negligible — the container creates most objects and maintains relationships (~90% of the time you don't).
  • Not magic — later lectures show how the container works and how we interact with it.
Definition: bean: an object whose lifecycle the Spring IoC container manages. Every bean is an object, but not every object is a bean.

Anticipated question: "Isn't Main wiring everything just moving the problem?"

Partly, deliberately. All construction in one place = composition root (added term): other classes stay free of new for collaborators; one place to change wiring. Cost: it grows with the app. Spring's container is an automated composition root — declare beans, it builds the object graph from their constructors.

Corrections & worth knowing

  • Make injected fields private final (constructor injection). Lecture's field is package-private and mutable; final = set exactly once, never null after construction.
  • DI removes new for collaborators, not everywhere. Value objects, collections, DTOs via new are fine; the rule targets services you'd swap or fake.
  • Loose coupling has a cost. A single never-changing implementation behind an interface = indirection without benefit. Abstract where variation/testing needs it (LLD "gold plating / over-engineering" anti-pattern).
  • Factory vs DI. A NotificationFactory still leaves OrderService pulling its dependency, coupled to the factory. DI pushes it in — OrderService knows nothing of how it was made.
  • Terminology. notification is correctly an instance variable (per-object). In Spring: typically private final NotificationService notificationService + single constructor, injected without annotation (Spring 4.3+).

Lecture 5: Spring Core — IoC Container, Beans & Annotation-Based Configuration

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #5 · 1h34m · Code Army · ApplicationContext, @Component, @Autowired, @Primary/@Qualifier, @Bean

From principle to framework

Lecture 4: DI/IoC as principles. Now: how Spring Core implements them — the IoC container and beans. All of Spring is based on Spring Core, so this is key.

Setup: empty Maven project spring-core-demo (groupId in.codearmy, JDK 23), boilerplate removed, pom.xml with no dependencies.

Recap: manual DI with OrderService and PaymentService

E-commerce: payment before the order is placed.

public class PaymentService {
    public void pay() {
        System.out.println("Payment done");
    }
}

public class OrderService {
    private PaymentService paymentService;

    public OrderService(PaymentService paymentService) {   // dependency comes from outside
        this.paymentService = paymentService;
    }

    public void placeOrder() {
        paymentService.pay();
        System.out.println("Order placed");
    }
}

// Main: we create, wire and call everything ourselves
PaymentService service = new PaymentService();
OrderService order = new OrderService(service);
order.placeOrder();          // Payment done / Order placed
  • Declaring private PaymentService paymentService; and calling pay() unassigned fails — null reference.
  • = new PaymentService() inside OrderService hard-codes it and breaks SRP.
  • So it comes via the constructor; Main creates and injects.

Goal: stop doing this manually — the IoC container creates PaymentService, creates OrderService, and wires them. Right now Main plays the container.

The dependency you need: spring-context

  • Spring Boot? No — that's for auto-configuration.
  • Spring MVC? No — console app, not web.
  • Just spring-context — the Spring Core dependency providing the IoC container.

Search mvnrepository.com for "spring context". Series targets Spring Boot 4 → Spring Framework 7; pick a slightly older, widely used 7.x. Paste into <dependencies>, reload Maven.

<dependencies>
    <dependency>
        <groupId>org.springframework</groupId>
        <artifactId>spring-context</artifactId>
        <version>7.0.0</version>   <!-- any stable 7.x release -->
    </dependency>
</dependencies>

External Libraries gains spring-core, spring-beans, spring-aop, spring-expression, spring-context, Commons Logging, Micrometer (observation), JSpecify, etc. — the Maven Dependencies view shows these are spring-context's transitive dependencies.

Beans, and the two ways to configure them

Definition: bean: an object inside the Spring IoC container. The container creates it, wires/injects its dependencies, and owns its whole lifecycle. Every bean is an object, but not every object is a bean — objects we create ourselves aren't.
ApproachWhere it appears
Annotation-based configuration (this lecture)Modern code — what you'll mostly see
XML-based configuration (next lecture)Mostly legacy; more complicated (maintaining XML)

(Added:) also pure Java-based config — @Configuration classes with @Bean methods; appears later as part of the "annotation" approach.

Prerequisite: Java Reflection and the Class object

Spring relies heavily on the Reflection API (full video promised later; minimum here):

class Student {
    private String name;
    private int age;

    public Student() { }

    public void getAttendance() { }
    public void print() { }
}

Student s1 = new Student();            // s1: a reference to a Student OBJECT
Class<Student> c1 = Student.class;     // c1: an object describing the Student CLASS
  • Like the Object class, Java has a special class Class (generic: Class<Student>).
  • c1 is not a Student (no c1.name); it holds the class's metadata:
    • Class name (Student).
    • Fields (name, age) and types.
    • Constructors.
    • Methods (getAttendance, print).
    • Private/public members.
    • Which annotations the class carries.
Why Spring needs this: to create/manage objects itself, Spring needs the blueprint (constructors, fields, annotations) via reflection — hence passing AppConfig.class, OrderService.class.

Step 1: mark classes with @Component

Tell Spring which classes to manage:

@Component
public class OrderService { ... }

@Component
public class PaymentService { ... }

@Component = "Spring, you create and manage this class's beans" — a marker. Not enough alone: the container must also be started.

Step 2: the IoC container is ApplicationContext
