  • IoC container = ApplicationContext, an interface (extends other interfaces).
  • No new ApplicationContext() — needs an implementation; several exist.
  • Annotation-based config: AnnotationConfigApplicationContext.
  • new AnnotationConfigApplicationContext(...) = start a Spring container using annotation-based configuration.

The container needs rules (which classes, how to configure) — in a separate class whose reflection (AppConfig.class) goes to the constructor.

Step 3: the configuration class, @Configuration + @ComponentScan

@Configuration                          // "this is a configuration class"
@ComponentScan("in.codearmy")           // "scan this package for @Component classes"
public class AppConfig {
    // empty for now; @Bean methods come later
}

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        ApplicationContext context =
                new AnnotationConfigApplicationContext(AppConfig.class);

        OrderService order = context.getBean(OrderService.class);   // no 'new'
        order.placeOrder();

        PaymentService payment = context.getBean(PaymentService.class);
        payment.pay();
    }
}
  • @Configuration marks AppConfig as a configuration class.
  • @ComponentScan finds every @Component class in the given package (in.codearmy: OrderService, PaymentService, Main, AppConfig) and manages its beans.
  • Class name is arbitrary; the @Configuration annotation matters.
  • Container reads AppConfig's metadata/annotations, scans, creates beans for both @Component classes.
  • context.getBean(OrderService.class) fetches the bean — no new, we only use it.

First check with the constructor dependency temporarily removed (independent classes) — both beans exist:

Order placed
Payment done

How @ComponentScan searches

  • Sub-packages included at any depth (in.codearmy.random, in.codearmy.random.random2…).
  • Only @Component classes (other stereotypes later). Remove @Component from PaymentService → getBean(PaymentService.class) fails: "No qualifying bean of type PaymentService available".
  • No package given? Defaults to the annotated class's package (AppConfig's) + sub-packages. A package outside it (e.g. top-level random) isn't scanned — name a common parent.

Step 4: automatic wiring with @Autowired

Restore the constructor dependency. The container has both beans, so it passes the PaymentService bean into the OrderService constructor — what Main did by hand.

@Component
public class OrderService {
    private final PaymentService paymentService;

    @Autowired                                   // "inject this through the constructor"
    public OrderService(PaymentService paymentService) {
        this.paymentService = paymentService;
    }

    public void placeOrder() {
        paymentService.pay();
        System.out.println("Order placed");
    }
}

// Main
OrderService order = context.getBean(OrderService.class);
order.placeOrder();          // Payment done / Order placed

@Autowired = "wire this automatically". The container calls the constructor while creating OrderService, passing the PaymentService bean.

Three types of injection in Spring

// 1. Constructor injection (recommended)
@Component
public class OrderService {
    private final PaymentService paymentService;
    public OrderService(PaymentService paymentService) {   // @Autowired optional: single constructor
        this.paymentService = paymentService;
    }
}

// 2. Setter injection: called by Spring after the object is created
@Component
public class OrderService {
    private PaymentService paymentService;
    @Autowired
    public void setPaymentService(PaymentService paymentService) {
        this.paymentService = paymentService;
    }
}

// 3. Field injection: Spring sets the field directly (via reflection)
@Component
public class OrderService {
    @Autowired
    private PaymentService paymentService;     // IntelliJ: "Field injection is not recommended"
}
  • Setter: not called during construction — Spring creates both objects, then calls the @Autowired setter.
  • Field: impossible-ish in plain Java (Lecture 4), Spring does it; IntelliJ warns "Field injection is not recommended".
  • Single constructor → no @Autowired needed in modern Spring. With multiple constructors, mark one with @Autowired (later).
  • Setter and field injection always need @Autowired, else the dependency stays null.

Why constructor injection is recommended

  1. Wired at creation — never half-built, no later linking.
  2. Field can be final (initialized in constructor) — nobody swaps it later. Impossible with field/setter injection (happens after construction).
  3. Easy to unit test without Spring: new OrderService(fakePayment).
    • Field injection on a private field: no way to assign → new OrderService().placeOrder() throws NullPointerException at paymentService.pay().
    • A setter allows it, with more hassle.
    • For a fake payment, make PaymentService an interface (UPI, card, fake), like Lecture 4's NotificationService.

What happens inside: the container's steps

  1. Start the container: new AnnotationConfigApplicationContext(AppConfig.class) — rules from AppConfig's metadata.
  2. Read AppConfig — which components to scan, which package (in.codearmy), later much more.
  3. Process @ComponentScan.
  4. Find @Component classes: OrderService, PaymentService.
  5. Create bean definitions before any object — metadata per class:
    • Bean name, e.g. paymentService.
    • Bean's class.
    • Scope (later lecture).
    • Dependencies: none for PaymentService; PaymentService for OrderService.
    BeanDefinition is a real Spring interface, used internally.
  6. Create objects: roughly new PaymentService(), then new OrderService(payment) with the already-made one — linked.
  7. App uses beans: context.getBean(OrderService.class).placeOrder() works, dependency already injected.

Anticipated question: "Why bean definitions first?"

Spring manages, not just creates: to wire beans that depend on each other correctly, it needs complete knowledge of every class before creating any object.

(Added:) this also lets you hook in before objects exist: a BeanFactoryPostProcessor can modify bean definitions (e.g. resolve ${…} placeholders) pre-instantiation. Creation order comes from dependencies, not discovery order — dependencies are created first, on demand.

Anticipated question: "What errors can wiring produce?"

SituationResult
No wiring (no constructor, no @Autowired), then pay()NullPointerException: paymentService is null
OrderService needs PaymentService, which has no @ComponentFails at startup: IntelliJ "Could not autowire. No beans of 'PaymentService' type found"; runtime "No qualifying bean… expected at least 1 bean which qualifies as autowire candidate" (UnsatisfiedDependencyException wrapping NoSuchBeanDefinitionException)
Two beans match the type"expected single matching bean but found 2" (NoUniqueBeanDefinitionException) — next section

Loose coupling with an interface: multiple candidate beans

OrderService is still tied to concrete PaymentService (Lecture 4's problem); payment could be card or UPI. PaymentService becomes an interface in a payment package (IntelliJ Refactor → Move updates imports to in.codearmy.payment).

public interface PaymentService {
    void pay();
}

@Component
public class CardPayment implements PaymentService {
    @Override
    public void pay() { System.out.println("Paying via card"); }
}

@Component
public class UpiPayment implements PaymentService {
    @Override
    public void pay() { System.out.println("Paying via UPI"); }
}
  • @Component on the interface is meaningless (can't instantiate). No implementation annotated → no bean → "Could not autowire".
  • Only on CardPayment: it is a PaymentService → injected → "Paying via card / Order placed". Move to UpiPayment → "Paying via UPI". OrderService unchanged.
  • On both: cardPayment and upiPayment beans; OrderService needs one → "No qualifying bean of type PaymentService: expected single matching bean but found 2".

Resolving ambiguity: @Primary and @Qualifier

// Option 1: @Primary, the default winner
@Component
@Primary
public class UpiPayment implements PaymentService { ... }       // → "Paying via UPI"

// Option 2: @Qualifier at the injection point, naming the bean
@Component
public class OrderService {
    private final PaymentService paymentService;
    public OrderService(@Qualifier("cardPayment") PaymentService paymentService) {
        this.paymentService = paymentService;                   // → "Paying via card"
    }
}
  • @Primary on one candidate: "when confused, prefer this".
  • @Qualifier("name") at the injection point (constructor param, setter param, or field) names the winner.
  • Default bean name (@Component): class name in camelCase — cardPayment, upiPayment.
  • Custom names: lecture puts @Qualifier("cp") on CardPayment, @Qualifier("up") on UpiPayment, injects @Qualifier("up") → "Paying via UPI". Works, but see note.
Precision note: qualifier vs bean name: @Qualifier("cp") on a class doesn't rename the bean (still cardPayment); it adds a qualifier value. At an injection point, @Qualifier("x") matches qualifier x, falling back to bean name — so the lecture's plain @Qualifier (no value) on both classes was unnecessary; @Qualifier("cardPayment") already works. To truly rename: @Component("cp"), or @Bean("cp") / @Bean(name = "cp").
@Primary@Qualifier
Placed onThe bean (class or @Bean method)Injection point (optionally also the bean, to give it a qualifier)
DecidesGlobal default for that typeChoice for this specific injection
Precedence@Qualifier wins over @Primary when both apply
Use whenOne implementation is the usual choiceDifferent consumers need different implementations

(Added:) inject List<PaymentService> or Map<String, PaymentService> for all implementations (Strategy-style lookup by name). With no qualifier, Spring finally tries matching the parameter name to a bean name.

When @Component can't work

Two cases where adding @Component fails.

Case 1: constructor needs values Spring can't know.

@Component                      // error straight away
public class User {
    private String name;
    private int age;

    public User(String name, int age) {
        this.name = name;
        this.age = age;
    }
    // getters and setters ...
}

new User(?, ?) — Spring won't invent values. OrderService worked because its constructor needed a bean; String/int aren't beans. You can still create User yourself, just not hand it to Spring this way.

(Added:) @Value("Aditya") on constructor params or properties-file values can supply them (later lectures).

Case 2: third-party library class — compiled .class files in a JAR are read-only; no @Component possible. Demo:

  1. Separate plain Maven project spring-core-demo-2 (groupId in.strikes), no Spring, one class: 
    public class CartService {
    public void addToCart() {
        System.out.println("Added to cart");
    }
}
  2. Maven install: validate, compile, test (none), package (JAR in target/), verify (no plugins), install (JAR into local ~/.m2; Lecture 3).
  3. Add as a dependency in spring-core-demo's pom (Maven checks local repo first): 
    <dependency>
    <groupId>in.strikes</groupId>
    <artifactId>spring-core-demo-2</artifactId>
    <version>1.0-SNAPSHOT</version>
</dependency>
  4. After reload, External Libraries shows in.strikes.CartService.class — decompiled, readable, not editable.
  5. new CartService().addToCart() works in Main, but @Component is impossible.

The fix: @Bean methods in the configuration class

Spring: "If I can't create the object, you create it, give it to me, and I'll put it in my IoC container and manage it from then on." Only creation failed — so write a method in the @Configuration class returning the object, annotated @Bean.

@Configuration
@ComponentScan("in.codearmy")
public class AppConfig {

    @Bean
    public User createUser() {
        return new User("Aditya", 28);          // we supply the constructor values
    }

    @Bean
    public CartService createCartService() {    // third-party class: no annotation needed on it
        return new CartService();
    }
}

// Main
User user = context.getBean(User.class);
System.out.println(user.getName());             // Aditya

CartService cart = context.getBean(CartService.class);
cart.addToCart();                               // Added to cart
  • @Bean: while reading config, Spring calls every @Bean method and stores the returned object in the container as a bean.
  • Method name is arbitrary.
  • Both are now managed — available via getBean and injectable anywhere.

@Component vs @Bean

@Component@Bean
Placed onA class (class-level)A method (method-level), usually in a @Configuration class
Who creates the objectSpring (calls the constructor)You write creation code; Spring calls your method
Discovered by@ComponentScanProcessing the configuration class
Default bean nameClass name in camelCase (cardPayment)The method name (createCardPayment)
Use whenOwn classes with simple, bean-only dependenciesCan't annotate the class (third-party/read-only), or complex creation (constructor values, builders, conditional setup)

Both create beans; use them together.

Dependencies between @Bean methods

@Bean works for own classes too. With @Component removed from OrderService, CardPayment, UpiPayment, all are declared as @Beans:

@Bean
public PaymentService createCardPayment() {          // return type can be the interface
    return new CardPayment();
}

@Bean
public OrderService createOrderService(PaymentService paymentService) {
    return new OrderService(paymentService);         // Spring supplies the parameter
}

The @Bean method takes PaymentService as a parameter; Spring passes a matching bean (CardPayment) — still constructor-style injection, no @Autowired. Container holds user, cart, cardPayment, orderService; prints "Paying via card / Order placed".

If OrderService had no such constructor (e.g. field injection), two options:

// (a) wire it by hand inside the @Bean method: needs a setter
@Bean
public OrderService createOrderService() {
    PaymentService payment = createCardPayment();
    OrderService order = new OrderService();
    order.setPaymentService(payment);
    return order;
}

// (b) or put @Autowired on OrderService's setter and just return the object;
//     Spring still processes @Autowired on objects returned from @Bean methods
@Bean
public OrderService createOrderService() {
    return new OrderService();                  // Spring calls the @Autowired setter afterwards
}

(b) = "I create it; you wire it." The lecture then reverts to the constructor version.

@Primary and @Qualifier with @Bean

Add createUpiPayment() → two PaymentService beans → "Could not autowire. There is more than one bean". Same fixes:

  • @Primary on createCardPayment() → "Paying via card".
  • @Qualifier("createUpiPayment") on the createOrderService parameter → "Paying via UPI". Bean name = method name, not class name.
  • Custom qualifiers likewise: @Qualifier("cp") / @Qualifier("upi") on methods, @Qualifier("upi") at the parameter.

Anticipated question: "Both @Component and a @Bean method?"

Lecture re-enables @Component on CardPayment, keeps its @Bean method, uses @Qualifier("cp"); prints "Paying via card", no error. It concludes only one bean is created because @Bean "gets priority" over scanning.

Correction: there are actually two beans (verified on Spring 7.0.5): scanning registers cardPayment; the @Bean method registers createCardPayment (@Qualifier("cp") doesn't rename). Different names = two separate CardPayment beans; no error only because @Qualifier("cp") matches just the @Bean one. Without it: "found 2".
Overriding happens only with the same bean name (e.g. @Bean("cardPayment")) — later definition replaces earlier. Plain Spring allows it by default; Spring Boot disables bean-definition overriding by default (since Boot 2.1) and fails at startup unless spring.main.allow-bean-definition-overriding=true. "Explicit wins over scanned" holds only in that same-name case.

Anticipated question: "Why a separate AppConfig instead of main?"

Possible, but Lecture 4's main-as-driver got complicated — exactly the problem. main is the entry point and should stay simple. Name is arbitrary; @Configuration marks it.

Correction: what removing @Configuration actually does (verified on Spring 7.0.5): the lecture says @ComponentScan then won't run but @Bean methods would. Actually a class passed to new AnnotationConfigApplicationContext(AppConfig.class) is processed as config even without @Configuration ("lite mode") — @ComponentScan and @Bean both honoured.
  • @Configuration adds "full mode": a CGLIB proxy subclass, so one @Bean method calling another (createCardPayment() inside createOrderService()) returns the same singleton bean.
  • Lite mode has no proxy — the call reruns the method, creating a second, unmanaged instance.
Keep @Configuration on config classes.

ApplicationContext vs BeanFactory

Early Spring used BeanFactory (interface) for the container. Today ApplicationContext extends it (via ListableBeanFactory and HierarchicalBeanFactory), so it has everything BeanFactory has.

Correction: super vs sub is inverted in the lecture: it calls ApplicationContext the "super interface". Reverse: BeanFactory is the root (super) interface; ApplicationContext is a sub-interface adding features ("ApplicationContext extends BeanFactory" is correct). BeanFactory isn't deprecated — old standalone implementations like XmlBeanFactory were deprecated, then removed.
What ApplicationContext adds (added)
BeanFactoryApplicationContext
Singleton creationLazy: on first getBeanEager: all singletons at startup — wiring errors surface immediately
Post-processors (@Autowired handling, AOP)Registered manuallyDetected and registered automatically
ExtrasBasic DI onlyEvents (ApplicationEvent), i18n (MessageSource), resource loading, environment and profiles
UseLow-level / memory-constrainedPractically always; what Spring Boot creates

Corrections & worth knowing

  • Beans are singletons by default — two getBean(OrderService.class) calls return the same object (bean definition's "scope"; own lecture later).
  • Close the context — AnnotationConfigApplicationContext is AutoCloseable: try (var context = new AnnotationConfigApplicationContext(AppConfig.class)) { … } so destroy callbacks run.
  • Scanning doesn't load every class — reads .class metadata via a bytecode reader (ASM), loads/reflects only classes that become beans; keeps startup fast.
  • Default-name edge case — follows java.beans.Introspector.decapitalize: CardPayment → cardPayment, but two leading capitals keep the name (URLService stays URLService).
  • getBean(Type) with two matches also throws NoUniqueBeanDefinitionException; use getBean("name", Type.class) or @Primary.
  • You rarely call getBean — a "pull" (service-locator style) used here to peek. Real beans get injected; only the entry point touches the context, and in Boot SpringApplication.run does that (Lecture 2).
  • @SpringBootApplication bundles what we wrote by hand: @Configuration (via @SpringBootConfiguration), @ComponentScan of its own package, @EnableAutoConfiguration — why Lecture 2's controller had to be in the main class's package or below.

Lecture 6: Circular Dependency, Bean Scopes & Eager vs Lazy Initialization

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #6 · 1h22m · Code Army · Three linked Spring Core topics

Normal dependencies: Spring works out the order

OrderService needs PaymentService, which needs PaymentGateway (all @Component with @Autowired dependencies). The container sees the chain and creates beans in reverse: gateway, then payment service (gateway injected), then order service.

The problem: circular dependency

Definition: A needs B to be created and B needs A = circular dependency. No starting point. E.g. OrderService ↔ PaymentService.

Setup: a new project and a @Configuration fact

Maven project circular-dependency-demo, spring-context 7.0.7 (Spring 7 pairs with Boot 4). Main starts AnnotationConfigApplicationContext(AppConfig.class); AppConfig has @Configuration + plain @ComponentScan (same package, so no package needed).

Worth knowing: the configuration class is itself a bean: Spring calls AppConfig's non-static @Bean methods, so it needs an AppConfig object we never created. Ctrl-click @Configuration: it's meta-annotated @Component — the container creates/manages it; special because it declares beans and scan rules. (Verified on Spring 7.0.5: actually a CGLIB subclass, AppConfig$$SpringCGLIB$$0 — Lecture 5's "full mode" proxy.)

Reproducing it with constructor injection

@Component
public class OrderService {
    private PaymentService paymentService;

    public OrderService(PaymentService paymentService) {
        this.paymentService = paymentService;
    }

    public void placeOrder() {
        paymentService.pay();
        System.out.println("Order placed");
    }

    public void getOrderDetails() {
        System.out.println("Order details");
    }
}

@Component
public class PaymentService {
    private OrderService orderService;

    public PaymentService(OrderService orderService) {   // now depends back on OrderService
        this.orderService = orderService;
    }

    public void pay() {
        System.out.println("Payment done");
        orderService.getOrderDetails();                   // calls back into OrderService
    }
}

// Main
OrderService order = context.getBean(OrderService.class);
order.placeOrder();

Intended flow: placeOrder → pay ("Payment done") → getOrderDetails ("Order details") → "Order placed". But the beans can't be created: OrderService's constructor needs PaymentService, whose constructor needs OrderService:

BeanCurrentlyInCreationException: Error creating bean with name 'orderService':
Requested bean is currently in creation: Is there an unresolvable circular reference?
"Currently in creation": creating orderService required paymentService, which asked for orderService — already mid-creation. Spring detects it and suggests the cause.

It's a design problem, not a Spring problem

Cycles come from badly designed code; plain Java hits them too. Plain classes A, B in a simple package, no @Component:

  • Constructor params (A(B b), B(A a)): can't write new A(new B(new A(…))) — no start.
  • "Hack" default constructors building each other (A() { this.b = new B(); }, B() { this.a = new A(); }): new A() prints "A created, B created, A created…" until StackOverflowError. (@Components on OrderService/PaymentService removed for this demo, else Spring's error comes first.)

Spring's workaround: field or setter injection

The constructors cause the cycle. Switch both to field injection (setter behaves the same) and it runs:

@Component
public class OrderService {
    @Autowired
    private PaymentService paymentService;
    ...
}

@Component
public class PaymentService {
    @Autowired
    private OrderService orderService;
    ...
}
Payment done
Order details
Order placed
Side question: how does Spring set a private field with no setter/constructor? Reflection — the bean definition gives full metadata, and Field.setAccessible(true) allows writing private members. That's how field injection works at all.

How field or setter injection breaks the cycle

They separate object creation from dependency injection: objects are built via (default) constructors that don't need the other bean; dependencies are needed only after creation.

Normally Spring fully completes each bean (create + inject) before the next. With a cycle:

  1. Create OrderService — partial (dependency not injected).
  2. Needs PaymentService; none in container → create it (partial).
  3. PaymentService needs OrderService: exists partially, Spring injects a reference to it anyway → PaymentService complete.
  4. Inject complete PaymentService into OrderService → complete.
Terminology (added): lecture's "reference concept" = Spring's early singleton reference. DefaultSingletonBeanRegistry keeps a three-level cache:
  1. singletonObjects: fully built beans.
  2. earlySingletonObjects: early references to partly built beans.
  3. singletonFactories: factories producing an early reference, possibly an AOP proxy.
Works only for singletons with non-constructor injection — with constructor injection no object exists until the constructor finishes.

Don't resolve a circular dependency, remove it

  • Not good practice — making it "work" hides a design flaw.
  • Spring Boot refuses it by default: since Boot 2.6, spring.main.allow-circular-references defaults to false — startup fails even with field/setter injection unless true. Plain Spring Core still allows it.
  • Other "resolutions": a different scope, @Lazy (later), @PostConstruct (lifecycle lecture). Right move: refactor so the cycle never arises.
Transcript slip: lecture says 2.6 and later "2.4" — it's Spring Boot 2.6 that prohibited circular references by default.
Why it's bad design: in scalable enterprise apps cycles should not exist. Entangled OrderService/PaymentService means responsibilities weren't separated — Single Responsibility Principle failed. Fix the design, don't hunt for workarounds.

Refactoring the cycle away

A calls B, B calls back into A — wrong.

  • OrderService delegates pay() before placing an order and expects a result (real code: success flag, then "Order placed") — correct.
  • PaymentService talks to a gateway, logs "Payment done", then calls orderService.getOrderDetails() — not its responsibility.
  • Move that call into OrderService, after payment succeeds.
@Component
public class OrderService {
    private final PaymentService paymentService;

    public OrderService(PaymentService paymentService) {
        this.paymentService = paymentService;
    }

    public void placeOrder() {
        paymentService.pay();
        getOrderDetails();                 // moved here: OrderService's own job
        System.out.println("Order placed");
    }

    public void getOrderDetails() {
        System.out.println("Order details");
    }
}

@Component
public class PaymentService {              // no OrderService field any more
    public void pay() {
        System.out.println("Payment done");
    }
}

Same output, but dependency is now linear (nothing depends back); constructor injection works again.

(Added:) other standard refactors:

  • Extract shared logic into a third class both depend on.
  • Invert one direction with an interface (DIP).
  • Replace the callback with an event: PaymentService publishes PaymentCompletedEvent, an order listener reacts.

Bean scopes: singleton

Project bean-scope-demo: OrderService is a dependency-free @Component whose constructor prints "Order service created".

OrderService order  = context.getBean(OrderService.class);
OrderService order2 = context.getBean(OrderService.class);
System.out.println(order == order2);      // == compares references
Order service created
true

Constructor ran once; both point to the same object.

Definition: singleton scope (the default): one object per bean definition (per container). Every getBean and every injection returns it — if @Components A and B both take OrderService in their constructors, they get the same instance (created first).

@Scope("singleton") changes nothing — it's the default.

Bean scopes: prototype

@Component
@Scope("prototype")          // or @Scope(ConfigurableBeanFactory.SCOPE_PROTOTYPE)
public class OrderService {
    public OrderService() { System.out.println("Order service created"); }
    public void placeOrder() { System.out.println("Order placed"); }
}
Definition: prototype scope: a new object every time one is requested (getBean or injection) — like new per request.

Two getBean calls + A and B injecting → "Order service created" four times; order == order2 is false.

  • Prototype beans aren't fully managed — created and handed over, lifecycle not tracked; destroy callbacks not called (bean-lifecycle lecture).
  • Prototype beans are created lazily. With nobody requesting OrderService, a singleton is still created at startup; a prototype isn't (nothing printed). So the diagram is slightly off: no prototype instance "waits" in the container.
Singleton (default)Prototype
InstancesOne per bean definition per containerOne per request / injection
CreatedEagerly at startup (by default)Lazily, only when requested
Lifecycle managedFully, including destroy callbacksCreation and init only, no destroy
getBean == getBeantruefalse
SuitsStateless: services, managers, repositoriesStateful objects needing own data

Spring singleton is not the GoF Singleton

  • Singleton design pattern (LLD): a class can ever have only one instance in the JVM (per class loader).
  • Spring's singleton: one object per bean definition in this container:
    • You can still new OrderService() (order3) — just unmanaged.
    • Two @Bean methods returning OrderService (getOrder(), getOrder2(); @Component removed) = two bean definitions → "Order service created" twice. Each is singleton within its definition: getBean("getOrder") always returns the same one.

Then anything asking by type (A, B, getBean(OrderService.class)) fails: NoUniqueBeanDefinitionException "expected 1, found 2: getOrder, getOrder2" — needs @Primary/@Qualifier (Lecture 5).

The lecturer's emphasis: "Singleton doesn't mean only one object exists in the IoC container. It means one object per bean definition." Often skipped; a common interview trap.
Precision note (added): for @Bean methods, scope comes from @Scope on the method, not on the returned class. The lecture's class-level @Scope("singleton") only "worked" because singleton is the default.

When to use which scope

  • Singleton for stateless classes (no per-caller data): OrderService handles many orders but holds no state. Services/managers (OrderManager, PaymentManager) are ideal.
  • Prototype for stateful classes: a singleton User ("Aditya", 28) would be returned to everyone, but the app needs many users.
Worth knowing (added):
  • Singletons are shared across threads — concurrent web requests share service beans, so mutable instance fields cause race conditions. Keep them stateless or thread-safe.
  • Plain data objects usually aren't beans — User/Order come from new, JSON deserialization or JPA, not the container. Prototype suits stateful helpers needing injection (per-task builder, non-thread-safe client).
  • Prototype-inside-singleton trap: injection happens once, so the singleton keeps the same "prototype" forever (lecture's A/B got one each). For fresh per use: ObjectProvider<OrderService> + getObject(), @Lookup methods, or a scoped proxy.

Other scopes (web only)

ScopeOne instance per…
requestHTTP request
sessionUser HTTP session
applicationWeb application (ServletContext) while it runs
websocket (added)WebSocket session

Web apps only (Spring MVC / Boot), later. application resembles singleton; (added) but it's per ServletContext, shared across all Spring contexts in the web app and exposed as a servlet-context attribute, while singleton is per ApplicationContext.

Bean initialization: eager

Project bean-initialization-demo: OrderService, PaymentService are @Components printing "… created"; Main only starts the context → both lines print immediately.

Definition: eager initialization: beans created as soon as the application context starts, used or not. Default for singletons.

Bean initialization: lazy, with @Lazy

@Component
@Lazy                        // don't create at startup; create when first needed
public class OrderService { ... }
  • @Lazy on both → context start prints nothing (container empty).
  • getBean(OrderService.class) prints "Order service created" then; same for PaymentService.
Definition: lazy initialization: bean created only when first needed — getBean, or as a dependency of a bean being created.

The rules: singleton can be lazy; prototype is always lazy

  • No scope = singleton, eager by default; @Lazy makes it lazy.
  • Prototype is always lazy, never eager; prints nothing at startup; @Lazy on it is redundant.
  • Why? Prototype gives a new object per request — a pre-built one would never be handed out. Singleton returns the same one, so building it up front makes sense.
ScopeDefault initCan change?
SingletonEagerYes: @Lazy makes it lazy
PrototypeLazyNo: always lazy

Lazy meets dependencies

OrderService now depends on PaymentService via constructor. No annotations → both eager: "Payment service created", then "Order service created" (payment first).

@Lazy onWhat's created at startupWhy
OrderService class onlyOnly PaymentServiceNobody asks for OrderService; PaymentService is eager. On getBean(OrderService), Spring creates it and injects the existing PaymentService.
PaymentService class onlyBothEager OrderService needs it; lazy = "not until needed", and now it is.
Both classesNothingEverything waits for getBean.

@Lazy at the injection point: a proxy

@Lazy can also go at the injection point, like @Qualifier:

@Component
public class OrderService {
    private final PaymentService paymentService;

    public OrderService(@Lazy PaymentService paymentService) {   // inject a proxy, not the real bean
        this.paymentService = paymentService;
        System.out.println("Order service created");
    }

    public void placeOrder() {
        paymentService.pay();            // first real use: the real PaymentService is resolved now
        System.out.println("Order placed");
    }
}
  • Create OrderService without its dependency: Spring injects a proxy (Proxy pattern — looks/behaves like PaymentService but isn't). OrderService is fully created.
  • On pay() via the proxy, Spring resolves the real PaymentService (creating it if lazy) and forwards the call.
  • PaymentService not lazy → still created at startup; proxy points to it on first use.
  • PaymentService also @Lazy → only OrderService created at startup.

@Lazy on PaymentService and at the injection point; Main calls getBean(OrderService), prints a marker, then placeOrder():

Order service created
Payment service not started yet
Payment service created
Payment successful
Order placed
Correction: the proxy is not swapped out (verified on Spring 7.0.5): lecture says after placeOrder() the proxy is "removed and the real object put in". Actually the field keeps the proxy forever: paymentService.getClass() is still PaymentService$$SpringCGLIB$$0; each call delegates to the target fetched from the container. Same effect, but matters for debugging (getClass(), == show the proxy) and when the target's scope changes.

Why is eager the default? Fail fast

Fail fast: broken wiring (missing bean, multiple candidates, circular dependency) is reported at startup, before users arrive. With @Lazy, failure can happen in production, mid-request. Use @Lazy for a genuinely heavy bean you're confident in. Only singletons can be eager or lazy; prototypes always lazy.

Global lazy mode:

# application.properties
spring.main.lazy-initialization=true     # default false: every bean becomes lazy
@Component
@Lazy(false)                 // opt this one bean back into eager creation
public class StartupCriticalService { ... }

@Lazy(false) only makes sense with global lazy mode on (else eager is default).

Correction: spring.main.lazy-initialization is a Spring Boot property (Boot 2.2+), read by SpringApplication — not a Spring Framework one. Plain Spring Core has no application.properties processing for it; use per-bean @Lazy or @ComponentScan(lazyInit = true).

Solving a circular dependency with @Lazy

Back to the constructor cycle (OrderService(PaymentService), PaymentService(OrderService), pay() calls orderService.getOrderDetails()) → BeanCurrentlyInCreationException. Fix:

  1. @Lazy on the PaymentService class — alone not enough (each still needs the other to be created).
  2. Plus @Lazy on OrderService's constructor parameter — OrderService gets a proxy; cycle broken at startup.
  3. On placeOrder(), the proxy triggers real PaymentService creation; its constructor gets the existing OrderService; call proceeds.
Payment successful
Order details
Order placed
Precision note (verified on Spring 7.0.5): the class-level @Lazy isn't what breaks the cycle — @Lazy on the one constructor parameter alone suffices. In the test, OrderService got a proxy, then eager PaymentService was built at startup with the real OrderService. Class-level @Lazy only defers real PaymentService creation. This resolves but doesn't remove the cycle; refactoring is the real fix.

Ways to break a circular dependency

ApproachHowVerdict
Refactor (move responsibility, extract a class, use events)Remove the back-referencePreferred — fixes the real SRP problem
Field or setter injectionEarly singleton referencesWorks in plain Spring; blocked by default in Boot 2.6+
@Lazy at an injection pointInject a proxy, resolve on first useWorks with constructor injection; hides the flaw
ObjectProvider<T> / @PostConstruct wiringLook the bean up after constructionWorks; also a workaround
spring.main.allow-circular-references=trueRe-enable Boot's old behaviourLast resort for legacy code

Homework / suggested extensions

Practice questions mixing @Lazy, singleton and prototype: predict what's created at startup, in what order, how many instances, before running.

Corrections & worth knowing

  • Prototypes don't participate in circular-reference resolution — early references are singleton-only; a cycle involving a prototype always fails, even with field injection.
  • Scope constants: prefer ConfigurableBeanFactory.SCOPE_PROTOTYPE / SCOPE_SINGLETON over string literals; in web apps @RequestScope, @SessionScope.
  • @Lazy works on @Bean methods and @Configuration classes (on a config class: all its @Beans).
  • Lazy trades startup time for first-request latency — global lazy suits dev, tests, serverless cold starts more than latency-sensitive production.
  • A BeanFactory alone is lazy (Lecture 5); eager singletons are an ApplicationContext behaviour.

Lecture 7: Spring Bean Lifecycle

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #7 · 1h27m · Code Army · From container start to bean destruction; aware interfaces, init/destroy callbacks

Why the lifecycle matters

One of the most important Spring topics, especially for interviews: how the container handles its beans internally, phase by phase (ends with a summary).

  • Without Spring: you create/manage objects; garbage collection destroys unreferenced ones (never called by you).
  • With Spring: container creates, manages, destroys beans ("destroy" = remove from container; not covered before).
  • Main walkthrough: singleton, eager bean (default); then differences for lazy singletons and prototypes (small).

Setup

Maven project bean-lifecycle-demo:

  • spring-context 7.0.7.
  • AppConfig: @Configuration + plain @ComponentScan (own package).
  • Main: new AnnotationConfigApplicationContext(AppConfig.class) — passes AppConfig's metadata via reflection, not an object.
  • OrderService depends on PaymentService via constructor (single constructor, no @Autowired). placeOrder() prints "Order placed", calls pay() ("Payment done").
OrderService order = context.getBean(OrderService.class);
order.placeOrder();          // Order placed / Payment done

Step 1: the IoC container starts

Nothing is managed until the container is up: new AnnotationConfigApplicationContext(…) starts it; ApplicationContext is the interface to it.

Step 2: read the configuration

Which beans, which packages — from the config class's metadata, via reflection:

  • @ComponentScan present, and its package (default: AppConfig's own, in.strikes).
  • @Configuration present → config class; may declare @Bean methods to manage.
The config class is itself a managed bean: non-static @Bean methods need an AppConfig object; @Configuration is meta-annotated @Component, so the container creates/manages it (this lifecycle applies). Proof: plain method demo() on AppConfig → context.getBean(AppConfig.class).demo() prints "demo".

Scanning reflects over each class (AppConfig, Main, OrderService, PaymentService) and picks @Component ones: OrderService, PaymentService.

Step 3: read / create bean definitions

Knowing each bean's metadata first lets Spring inject and manage properly. For OrderService:

What a bean definition records
FieldExample for OrderService
Bean nameorderService by default (first letter lower-case); @Component("orderBean") renames
Bean classOrderService
Scopesingleton (default)
Lazy?false (singletons eager by default)
DependenciespaymentService
Anticipated question: "All definitions first, or one bean at a time?" All definitions first, then objects — definitions say what to create now vs later (prototype and lazy = true wait for first use), so only eager singletons are instantiated.

Step 4: instantiate objects

  • Not random — dependency-aware, starting with whichever class the scan produced first.
  • PaymentService first (no dependencies) → just create it.
  • OrderService first → constructor reveals the need → create PaymentService first, then OrderService.

Step 5: inject dependencies

  • Constructor injection: steps 4 and 5 merge — dependency passed at creation.
  • Field/setter injection: separate. With @Autowired on a (private OK, reflection) field, OrderService is created first; during injection Spring creates PaymentService and wires it.

Container holds wired AppConfig, OrderService, PaymentService. Usable? Not yet — most material says "instantiate, inject, use" (maybe @PostConstruct); the in-depth steps follow.

Step 6: Aware interfaces are called

A bean may need info about itself or its container (bean name, which ApplicationContext). Spring's Aware interfaces provide it — rare in business logic, but a lifecycle step.

@Component("userBean")
public class UserService implements BeanNameAware, ApplicationContextAware {

    public UserService() {
        System.out.println("User service constructor called");
    }

    @Override
    public void setBeanName(String name) {                       // from BeanNameAware
        System.out.println("Bean name is " + name);
    }

    @Override
    public void setApplicationContext(ApplicationContext ctx) throws BeansException {  // from ApplicationContextAware
        System.out.println("Application context name is " + ctx.getClass());
    }
}
User service constructor called
Bean name is userBean
Application context name is class org.springframework.context.annotation.AnnotationConfigApplicationContext
  • Each Aware interface is a functional interface (one method); IntelliJ: hover error → Implement methods.
  • Spring calls them after instantiation and DI — callback methods ("the call comes back from Spring"). Non-Aware beans skip this step.
  • XML config would print an XML-based context class.
Why setBeanName, not getBeanName: Spring calls it and sets (passes in) the name for you to keep. Calling userService.setBeanName("userBean2") yourself just prints "Bean name is userBean2" — does not rename; still userBean in the container.

When useful? ~99% of business logic: never. Niche cases:

  • Logging — bean name in each leveled log line (info, error) shows its source.
  • Multiple containers (e.g. XML + annotation side by side; uncommon) — debug the right one.
Worth knowing (added): others: BeanClassLoaderAware, BeanFactoryAware, EnvironmentAware, ResourceLoaderAware, ApplicationEventPublisherAware. Modern code prefers injecting what you need (constructor param ApplicationContext ctx or Environment env) without tying to an Aware interface.

Step 7: initialization callbacks

Important (unlike Aware). After creation and DI, but before use, Spring offers a hook to initialize. E.g. a CartService @Component with a Map<Integer, String>; before addToCart() you might:

  • Fill the map, or flush/clear it (maybe pre-filled via an injected dependency).
  • Clear or invalidate a cache.
  • Pre-load an expensive resource (large file) so the first call isn't slow.

Three ways:

1. InitializingBean (old style)

@Component
public class CartService implements InitializingBean {
    private Map<Integer, String> mp;

    public CartService() {
        mp = new HashMap<>();
        System.out.println("Cart service constructor called");
    }

    @Override
    public void afterPropertiesSet() {          // Spring calls this after DI (and after Aware callbacks)
        mp.put(1, "Aditya");
        mp.put(2, "Rohit");
        System.out.println("Bean is ready");
    }

    public String getValue(int key) { return mp.get(key); }
    public void addToCart() { System.out.println("Added to cart"); }
}

// Main
CartService cart = context.getBean(CartService.class);
System.out.println(cart.getValue(1));
Cart service constructor called
Bean is ready
Aditya

(@Component removed from UserService for clean output.)

2. initMethod on @Bean

For a bean from an @Bean method, name your own method:

// AppConfig
@Bean(initMethod = "start")
public CartService getCartBean() {
    return new CartService();
}

// CartService: any public method; no interface, no annotation
public void start() {
    mp.put(1, "Aditya");
    mp.put(2, "Rohit");
    System.out.println("Bean is ready");
}

Without it, the map stays empty and getValue(1) prints null. For @Components use InitializingBean/@PostConstruct; initMethod is still used for @Bean methods, especially for classes you can't edit.

3. @PostConstruct (the one used today)

@Component
public class CartService {
    ...
    @PostConstruct                               // jakarta.annotation.PostConstruct
    public void start2() {
        mp.put(1, "Aditya");
        mp.put(2, "Rohit");
        System.out.println("Bean is ready");
    }
}

No interface, override or config — just an annotation on any method. Initial error: it's not in spring-context but the Jakarta Annotations library (auto-included in Spring Boot). Bare Spring Core adds it:

<dependency>
    <groupId>jakarta.annotation</groupId>
    <artifactId>jakarta.annotation-api</artifactId>
    <version>3.0.0</version>
</dependency>

Reload, import jakarta.annotation.PostConstruct, run: "Aditya".

Why @PostConstruct won (added reasoning): standard Jakarta Annotations (JSR-250) — no Spring API coupling (unlike InitializingBean), and easy to spot in a class.

Aware interfaces + @PostConstruct in CartService:

Cart service constructor called
Bean name is cartService
Application context name is class ...AnnotationConfigApplicationContext
Bean is ready
Aditya

With both @PostConstruct and initMethod = "start" declared, "Bean is ready" printed twice — both callbacks run.

Anticipated question: "Why not initialize in the constructor?"

Classic interview question — filling the map in the constructor does work here. Why @PostConstruct?

  1. Dependencies may not exist yet. Constructor runs at creation, before DI and Aware steps; field/setter-injected dependencies are still null. By @PostConstruct, all are injected.
  2. Keep the constructor light. Expensive file loads/cache warming slow creation; do heavy work in the init callback.
Constructor@PostConstruct
RunsAt instantiation (step 4)After DI and Aware callbacks (step 7)
Field / setter dependenciesNot yet injected (null)Injected
Good forAssigning fields, cheap setupValidation, cache warm-up, loading resources, using dependencies

Step 8: the bean is ready to use

Now fully usable: cart.getValue(1), cart.addToCart(), for as long as the app needs it.

Step 9: destruction callbacks

"About to destroy this; do last work." Mirrors initialization:

InitializationDestructionStatus today
InitializingBean.afterPropertiesSet()DisposableBean.destroy()Old; couples class to Spring
@Bean(initMethod = "…")@Bean(destroyMethod = "…")For @Bean-defined beans
@PostConstruct@PreDestroyThe usual choice

Do the reverse of init:

  • Empty the map.
  • Clear or invalidate the cache.
  • Close expensive resources (file, connection, thread pool).
// 1. DisposableBean
@Component
public class CartService implements DisposableBean {
    @Override
    public void destroy() {
        mp.clear();
        System.out.println("Bean is getting destroyed");
    }
}

// 2. destroyMethod on @Bean
@Bean(initMethod = "start", destroyMethod = "stop")
public CartService getCartBean() { return new CartService(); }

// 3. @PreDestroy
@PreDestroy
public void stop() {
    mp.clear();
    System.out.println("Bean is getting destroyed");
}

Triggering destruction: normally the program just exits ("Process finished with exit code 0") without shutting beans down — so close the context:

ConfigurableApplicationContext context =
        new AnnotationConfigApplicationContext(AppConfig.class);
CartService cart = context.getBean(CartService.class);
System.out.println(cart.getValue(1));
context.close();             // triggers destruction callbacks, then destroys singletons
  • ApplicationContext has no close(); it's on child interface ConfigurableApplicationContext.
  • Run now ends with "Bean is getting destroyed" for any of the three styles.
Worth knowing (added):
  • context.registerShutdownHook() closes the context on JVM exit (incl. Ctrl+C); Boot does this for you.
  • try-with-resources (try (var ctx = new AnnotationConfigApplicationContext(…))) also closes it.
  • @Bean destroyMethod defaults to "(inferred)": a public no-arg close()/shutdown() is called automatically — why data sources close without config.

Step 10: the bean is destroyed

After destroy callbacks, the bean is removed from the container; with context.close(), the container shuts down.

The full singleton lifecycle

Worth adding: BeanPostProcessor and exact callback order (verified on Spring 7.0.5): the lecture omits what every lifecycle interview expects: BeanPostProcessor, whose two methods wrap the init phase for every bean:
  • postProcessBeforeInitialization — before init callbacks. @PostConstruct itself is run by built-in CommonAnnotationBeanPostProcessor.
  • postProcessAfterInitialization — after them; where Spring wraps beans in AOP proxies (@Transactional, @Async…).
A bean with every callback printed: 
constructor
setBeanName               (BeanNameAware)
setApplicationContext     (ApplicationContextAware)
postProcessBeforeInitialization
@PostConstruct
afterPropertiesSet        (InitializingBean)
initMethod
postProcessAfterInitialization
-- bean in use; context.close() --
@PreDestroy
destroy                   (DisposableBean)
destroyMethod
Combined styles run annotation, then interface, then custom method (init and destroy). Singletons are destroyed in reverse dependency order — a bean before the beans it depends on.

What changes for a lazy singleton

With @Lazy on CartService, steps 1–3 are unchanged, then everything stops until first needed (getBean or as a dependency). No getBean → nothing printed; stuck after "read bean definition". Once requested, steps 4–10 run as for eager; context.close() still triggers @PreDestroy.

What changes for a prototype

  • Always lazy — steps 4+ only on request.
  • Instantiation, DI, Aware and init callbacks run normally per new instance.
  • Then handed over to the client; Spring stops managing it — now an object, not a bean; handling it is your job.
  • No destruction callbacks: with @Scope("prototype") on CartService, constructor, Aware and "Bean is ready" print, but @PreDestroy is never called, even with context.close().
Why Spring drops prototypes: to avoid memory leaks: tracking every getBean-created prototype for later destruction would keep references, so GC could never reclaim them and the heap would fill. They're destroyed like any Java object, by garbage collection once unreachable; context.close() does not destroy prototypes.
Consequence: a prototype holding an expensive resource must be released by you (try-with-resources or finally) — GC reclaims memory, not file handles or connections.
Lifecycle stepEager singletonLazy singletonPrototype
Bean definition at startupYesYesYes
Instantiate / DI / Aware / initAt startupOn first requestOn every request
Kept in containerYesYesNo: handed to client
Destruction callbacks on close()YesYes (if created)No: GC only

Anticipated question: "Can @PostConstruct resolve a circular dependency?"

Yes — trick question, and again a hack. @Components A(B) and B(A) fail with BeanCurrentlyInCreationException. Fix: B gets a plain setter (no @Autowired), A hands itself over in @PostConstruct:

@Component
public class A {
    private B b;

    public A(B b) {                 // A still receives B via constructor
        this.b = b;
    }

    @PostConstruct
    public void setB() {
        b.setA(this);               // after A is fully built, give B a reference to A
    }
}

@Component
public class B {
    private A a;                    // no constructor dependency, no @Autowired

    public void setA(A a) {
        this.a = a;
    }
}

Spring creates B, then A with B; A's @PostConstruct wires A into B. Starts without error.

Why this matters in Spring Boot: plain Spring Core resolves such cycles via field injection, but since Spring Boot 2.6 that's prohibited (Lecture 6) — you'd need @PostConstruct wiring or @Lazy. Still: don't resolve cycles, refactor them away.

Corrections & worth knowing

  • GC uses reachability, not "is anyone referring to it". Objects are collected when unreachable from GC roots (stack variables, static fields…); two objects referencing only each other are still collected.
  • Init callbacks run for @Bean objects too — @PostConstruct/@PreDestroy in the returned class are honoured; initMethod/destroyMethod mainly for classes you can't annotate.
  • Exceptions in init callbacks fail startup (eager beans) — Lecture 6's fail-fast: a broken cache load or config validation in @PostConstruct stops the app before traffic.
  • Don't rely on AOP inside @PostConstruct — the AOP proxy isn't applied until postProcessAfterInitialization, so @Transactional on a method called from it won't start a transaction. For fully-started-app work: ContextRefreshedEvent / ApplicationReadyEvent (Boot), or SmartInitializingSingleton.
  • Destroy callbacks run only on graceful shutdown (close(), shutdown hook, SIGTERM). Crash or kill -9 skips them — never put critical persistence only in @PreDestroy.

Lecture 8: XML-Based Configuration

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #8 · 1h11m · Code Army · The legacy way to configure the IoC container

Why look at XML at all?

So far: annotation config (@Component, @Bean, @ComponentScan, @Autowired). XML is older, common in legacy code.

  • Essential? No — the series and Spring Boot use annotations.
  • Helps for: legacy or hybrid (XML + annotation) systems; shows how much config a REST API once took → why Boot's auto-configuration matters.

Prerequisite: Lectures 5–7. Goal: understand concepts, not memorize syntax.

Key idea: the container only needs configuration metadata

Container's job is unchanged (Lecture 7 lifecycle); it needs configuration metadata (which beans, how to build), from either source:

Emphasis: the container needs only configuration metadata; XML or annotations, it doesn't care.

Recap: the annotation version

Project xml-based-config-demo, spring-context 7.0.7:

@Component
public class OrderService {
    public void placeOrder() { System.out.println("Order placed"); }
}

@Configuration
@ComponentScan
public class AppConfig { }

ApplicationContext context = new AnnotationConfigApplicationContext(AppConfig.class);
OrderService order = context.getBean(OrderService.class);
order.placeOrder();          // Order placed

For XML: remove @Component and AppConfig; OrderService becomes a plain class.

Step 1: ClassPathXmlApplicationContext and beans.xml

ApplicationContext context = new ClassPathXmlApplicationContext("beans.xml");
  • AnnotationConfigApplicationContext reads annotations; ClassPathXmlApplicationContext reads XML from the classpath.
  • Takes an XML file name (any; beans.xml) instead of a config class.
  • Location: classpath — in Maven, src/main/resources/beans.xml.

Standard header (declaration + <beans> root + schema) — never hand-written; copy from Spring docs ("XML metadata for Spring 7"):

<?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?>
<beans xmlns="http://www.springframework.org/schema/beans"
       xmlns:xsi="http://www.w3.org/2001/XMLSchema-instance"
       xsi:schemaLocation="http://www.springframework.org/schema/beans
           https://www.springframework.org/schema/beans/spring-beans.xsd">

    <!-- bean definitions here -->

</beans>

The schema defines which tags are allowed.

Step 2: define a bean with <bean>

<bean id="orderService" class="in.strikes.OrderService"/>
  • id — unique identifier; by convention camelCase class name.
  • class — fully-qualified class name (in.strikes.OrderService).
  • No body → self-closing tag.

XML equivalent of:

@Bean
public OrderService getOrderServiceBean() {
    return new OrderService();
}

@Bean infers id (method name) and class; XML spells out both.

Proof: constructor prints "Order service created" at startup — singleton and eager by default. First run: FileNotFoundException (code passed "bean.xml") — names must match.

Three ways to fetch a bean

// 1. By type
OrderService o1 = context.getBean(OrderService.class);

// 2. By id (or name): returns Object, so a cast is needed
OrderService o2 = (OrderService) context.getBean("orderService");

// 3. By id AND type: no cast, no ambiguity  ← best
OrderService o3 = context.getBean("orderService", OrderService.class);
ApproachFails when
By typeTwo beans of same class: NoUniqueBeanDefinitionException
By idWrong id: NoSuchBeanDefinitionException; needs a cast
By id + typeOnly if id doesn't exist. Recommended.
  • Two beans of one class OK (id="orderService", id="orderService2"), like two @Bean methods: constructor prints twice (singleton per definition, Lecture 6); by-type fetch fails.
  • Ids must be unique: duplicate → IDE warning ("bean name duplicated") + startup error.
Correction: beans without id do get a name (verified, Spring 7.0.5): lecture says none (since getBean("orderService") fails). Spring generates FQCN + #0 (in.strikes.OrderService#0), plain FQCN as alias of the first. Test: names=[demo.Order#0, …] aliases=[demo.Order]; getBean("demo.Order") worked. Only the camelCase default is missing. Always set id.

id vs name (aliases)

<bean id="orderService"
      name="orderServiceBean orderServiceBean2 orderServiceBean3"
      class="in.strikes.OrderService"/>

Fetchable by id or any name. Exactly one id, many names (aliases), separated by spaces, commas or semicolons.

PaymentService (pay() → "Payment done", id="paymentService") likewise; pay() + placeOrder() → "Payment done / Order placed".

Dependency injection in XML

OrderService depends on PaymentService (placeOrder() calls paymentService.pay()); Main needs only OrderService. Annotations: @Component on both. XML: injection declared in the bean definition.

Only constructor and setter injection in XML: no field injection — XML drives constructors or public setters, can't reach private fields. (Added:) with <context:annotation-config/> or component scanning in XML, field @Autowired works again — but that's annotation processing.

Constructor injection: <constructor-arg ref>

public class OrderService {
    private PaymentService paymentService;

    public OrderService(PaymentService paymentService) {
        this.paymentService = paymentService;
    }

    public void placeOrder() {
        paymentService.pay();
        System.out.println("Order placed");
    }
}
<bean id="paymentService" class="in.strikes.PaymentService"/>

<bean id="orderService" class="in.strikes.OrderService">
    <constructor-arg ref="paymentService"/>      <!-- ref = id of another bean -->
</bean>

Container creates paymentService, then effectively new OrderService(paymentService). ref = another bean; value = literal.

Literal constructor values: value, index, name

public class PaymentService {
    private String type;
    private int retryCount;

    public PaymentService(String type, int retryCount) {
        this.type = type;
        this.retryCount = retryCount;
    }

    public void pay() {
        System.out.println("Payment done. Type of payment is " + type
                + " with " + retryCount + " retry counts");
    }
}
<!-- by position (declaration order) -->
<bean id="paymentService" class="in.strikes.PaymentService">
    <constructor-arg value="UPI"/>
    <constructor-arg value="3"/>
</bean>

<!-- by index: tag order no longer matters -->
<constructor-arg index="1" value="3"/>
<constructor-arg index="0" value="UPI"/>

<!-- by parameter name -->
<constructor-arg name="type" value="UPI"/>
<constructor-arg name="retryCount" value="3"/>

Output: "Payment done. Type of payment is UPI with 3 retry counts" ("3" → int). IntelliJ's "Could not autowire. No beans of String type found" is false (ignores XML).

Worth knowing: name matching needs -parameters (verified, Spring 7.0.5): without it, names aren't in bytecode (Spring 6.1 dropped the debug-info fallback) → declaration order used; retryCount before type failed: "Could not convert … 'UPI' to int". <maven.compiler.parameters>true</maven.compiler.parameters> fixed it. Boot's parent POM (maybe IntelliJ) enables it, hiding the issue. index always works.

Setter injection: <property>

public class OrderService {
    private PaymentService paymentService;

    public void setPaymentService(PaymentService paymentService) {   // no @Autowired
        this.paymentService = paymentService;
    }
    ...
}
<bean id="orderService" class="in.strikes.OrderService">
    <property name="paymentService" ref="paymentService"/>
</bean>
  • As in Lecture 7: beans created first, then setter called.
  • ref = id of bean to inject.
  • name = property name → setter by convention: paymentService → setPaymentService(…).

Proof it's the setter: rename to setPaymentServiceBean → XML needs name="paymentServiceBean" though the field is unchanged (JavaBeans naming).

Multiple implementations: no @Primary needed

PaymentService → interface (payment package): UpiPaymentService ("Paying via UPI"), CardPaymentService ("Paying via card"); injected into OrderService's constructor.

<bean id="upiPaymentService"  class="in.strikes.payment.UpiPaymentService"/>
<bean id="cardPaymentService" class="in.strikes.payment.CardPaymentService"/>

<bean id="orderService" class="in.strikes.OrderService">
    <constructor-arg ref="cardPaymentService"/>     <!-- → "Paying via card" -->
</bean>

Annotations need @Primary/@Qualifier; XML ref names the exact bean (ref upiPaymentService → "Paying via UPI"; same for <property ref>). primary="true" exists, relevant only when wiring by type.

Worth knowing (added):
  • XML autowiring: autowire="byType", "byName", "constructor" on <bean>, or default-autowire on <beans>. autowire-candidate="false" excludes a bean from by-type matching.
  • Autowiring brings back annotation-style ambiguity → primary="true" matters.

Scope: scope="prototype"

<bean id="userService" class="in.strikes.UserService" scope="prototype"/>

"User service created" prints at startup as singleton; as prototype only on context.getBean(UserService.class). = @Scope("prototype") (Lecture 6).

Precision note: a prototype appears at startup if a singleton depends on it only because the eager singleton needs one instance then; still on-demand.

Injecting collections: <list>, <set>, <map>

// String version first: <constructor-arg value="Aditya"/> → getUsername() prints Aditya
public class UserService {
    private List<String> userNames;
    public UserService(List<String> userNames) { this.userNames = userNames; }
    public List<String> getUserNames() { return userNames; }
}
<bean id="userService" class="in.strikes.UserService">
    <constructor-arg name="userNames">
        <list>
            <value>Aditya</value>
            <value>Rohit</value>
            <value>Rohan</value>
        </list>
    </constructor-arg>
</bean>

<!-- a Set: same shape with <set> -->
<!-- a Map: -->
<map>
    <entry key="1" value="Aditya"/>
    <entry key="2" value="Rohit"/>
</map>

Prints [Aditya, Rohit, Rohan] (first run failed: getter still returned String). Tags: <list>, <set>, <map>/<entry> (Java's key–value term), <props> for Properties (added). Parameter type must match (<map> → Map).

Lifecycle callbacks: init-method and destroy-method

public class UserService {
    public UserService() { System.out.println("User service created"); }
    public void init()    { System.out.println("Post construct phase"); }
    public void cleanUp() { System.out.println("Pre destroy phase"); }
}
<bean id="userService" class="in.strikes.UserService"
      init-method="init" destroy-method="cleanUp"/>
ClassPathXmlApplicationContext context = new ClassPathXmlApplicationContext("beans.xml");
UserService user = context.getBean(UserService.class);
context.close();       // ApplicationContext has no close(); use the concrete class (or ConfigurableApplicationContext)
User service created
Post construct phase
Pre destroy phase
  • Any method names; = @Bean(initMethod, destroyMethod) or @PostConstruct/@PreDestroy.
  • No context.close() → destroy never runs.
  • scope="prototype" → cleanUp not called (container doesn't manage prototypes after handing out, Lecture 7).

Splitting XML across files: <import>

Large XML gets split: beans.xml (payment, order), beans2.xml (user), parent appConfig.xml imports both. Each needs the header.

<!-- appConfig.xml -->
<beans ...same header...>
    <import resource="beans.xml"/>
    <import resource="beans2.xml"/>
</beans>

// Main
new ClassPathXmlApplicationContext("appConfig.xml");

Hybrid XML + annotations

Migrations from XML are gradual → hybrid setups, still one container: XML context + component scanning in XML picks up @Component classes too.

<!-- needs xmlns:context and the spring-context.xsd schema in the header -->
<context:component-scan base-package="in.strikes"/>

(Added:) reverse: @ImportResource("classpath:beans.xml") on a config class loads legacy XML (common in Boot apps).

The IoC container doesn't care: XML or @Configuration, all bean definitions.

Annotation vs XML

ConceptAnnotationXML
ContainerAnnotationConfigApplicationContext(AppConfig.class)ClassPathXmlApplicationContext("beans.xml")
Declare a bean@Component / @Bean<bean id="…" class="…"/>
Default namecamelCase class name / method nameFQCN + #0 (set id!)
Aliases@Bean({"a", "b"})name="a b"
Constructor DIConstructor parameter (@Autowired optional)<constructor-arg ref/value/index/name>
Setter DI@Autowired on setter<property name ref/value>
Field DI@Autowired on fieldNot possible in pure XML
Choosing among candidates@Primary / @QualifierReference exact id (primary="true" exists)
Scope@Scope("prototype")scope="prototype"
Lazy@Lazylazy-init="true" (added)
Init / destroy@PostConstruct / @PreDestroyinit-method / destroy-method
Scanning@ComponentScan<context:component-scan/>
Split config@Import<import resource/>

Pros and cons of XML config

AdvantagesDisadvantages
Plain Java classes, no @Autowired/@Component clutter; wiring in one place.One file grows huge, must be split.
Rewire without recompiling (added).Verbose, harder to read.
Wires third-party classes you can't annotate (like @Bean).(Added:) no compile-time checks — typos found at startup; IDE refactors can miss string refs.

Corrections & worth knowing

  • Namespace shortcuts: p: and c: — <bean id="o" class="…" p:paymentService-ref="paymentService"/>, c:type="UPI". Common in older code.
  • Other XML contexts: FileSystemXmlApplicationContext (file path, not classpath); GenericXmlApplicationContext (flexible modern variant).
  • Inner beans: <bean> nested in <constructor-arg>/<property> = anonymous, used only there, no id.
  • Same pitfalls: circular constructor deps fail, prototype destroy skipped, singleton per definition (Lectures 5–7).

Lecture 9: How Spring Boot Manages Beans — @SpringBootApplication & Auto-Configuration

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #9 · 1h · Code Army · From Spring Core's manual setup to Spring Boot's auto-configuration

From Spring Core to Spring Boot

Lectures 5–8: Spring Core only, no auto-configuration. Now: Spring Boot's container and auto-configuration, so you focus on business logic.

  • Boot is still annotation-based; @Component, @Primary, @Qualifier… and the lifecycle work as before.
  • What disappears: the manual setup.

Prerequisite: Lectures 5–7.

Recap: Spring Core's manual steps

spring-core-demo (only spring-context): OrderService gets PaymentService via its single constructor; placeOrder() calls pay() then prints "Order placed".

@Configuration
@ComponentScan("in.strikes")
public class AppConfig {
    @Bean
    public UserService getUserServiceBean() { return new UserService(); }
}

ApplicationContext context = new AnnotationConfigApplicationContext(AppConfig.class);
OrderService order = context.getBean(OrderService.class);
order.placeOrder();          // Payment done / Order placed

First run failed ("no context"): config class not passed. Manual steps:

  1. Create the container yourself (choose annotation/XML), keep a reference.
  2. @Component on every managed class.
  3. Write a config class: @Configuration + @ComponentScan + package, plus @Bean methods.
  4. Pass it to the container.
  5. context.getBean(…) to call a bean.

Simple for Core (container holds OrderService, PaymentService, UserService), but Spring MVC web apps (servlets, later) need far more. Boot automates it.

Spring Boot does not mean "web app": it can sit on Spring MVC but is independent of it; it automates config of whatever Spring features you use. Here: a plain console (non-web) Boot app.

Creating a Spring Boot project: two ways

1. Plain Maven project + spring-boot-starter

spring-boot-core-demo: add "spring-boot-starter" (not -web/-test) instead of spring-context, e.g. 4.0.5.

<dependency>
    <groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
    <artifactId>spring-boot-starter</artifactId>
    <version>4.0.5</version>
</dependency>
  • IntelliJ flagged a known vulnerability; Ignore is OK for a demo only.
  • Starter = dependency bundle: spring-boot-starter-logging, spring-boot-autoconfigure, spring-core, spring-context… (transitive).

2. Spring Initializr (preferred)

start.spring.io (Lecture 2):

  • Java, Maven, Spring Boot 4.1.0.
  • Group in.strikes, artifact spring-boot-core-demo-2.
  • Jar, Properties, Java 21.
  • No dependencies — spring-boot-starter is always included.

Layout: src/main/java/in/strikes/springbootcoredemo2/SpringBootCoreDemo2Application.java (groupId + artifactId = package) and src/test.

Generated pom.xml: why no versions?

  • Two dependencies:
    • spring-boot-starter.
    • spring-boot-starter-test (tests; not needed here).
  • Neither has a <version>.
  • Reason: <parent> spring-boot-starter-parent → parent spring-boot-dependencies → Super POM (Lecture 3).
Why the parent POM matters: many deps (starter-web, MySQL driver, Hibernate, JDBC…); only certain versions work together — hand-matching 10–12 libraries is hectic.
spring-boot-starter-parent holds tested, compatible versions: add Boot deps without a version; what the child omits comes from the parent.

Hand-made projects: add <parent>. (Added:) already have a parent? Import spring-boot-dependencies as a BOM (<dependencyManagement>, <type>pom</type><scope>import</scope>). Parent also sets Java version, -parameters (Lecture 8), plugin config.

Same app in Spring Boot: no config class, no container setup

Copy OrderService, PaymentService (still @Component) into the Initializr package. Main class:

@SpringBootApplication
public class SpringBootCoreDemo2Application {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        ApplicationContext context =
                SpringApplication.run(SpringBootCoreDemo2Application.class, args);

        OrderService orderService = context.getBean(OrderService.class);
        orderService.placeOrder();          // Payment done / Order placed
    }
}
  • No new AnnotationConfigApplicationContext: SpringApplication.run starts the container and returns a ConfigurableApplicationContext (sub-interface of ApplicationContext).
  • No config class, no @ComponentScan — yet both beans created and wired.
Don't use getBean in Boot: let Boot drive. Web: an endpoint like /order (Lecture 2). Non-web: CommandLineRunner / ApplicationRunner (next lecture).

Inside @SpringBootApplication: three annotations in one

Composed annotation carrying:

  • Meta-annotations @Target, @Retention, @Documented, @Inherited (ignore; separate Java video).
  • Three that matter:
    • @SpringBootConfiguration.
    • @EnableAutoConfiguration.
    • @ComponentScan (with exclude filters).

Equivalent to writing all three yourself.

@SpringBootConfiguration: the main class is the config class

  • Replaces AppConfig: Boot's @Configuration (meta-annotated with it → ultimately @Component). Marks the primary configuration class; others still allowed.
  • On the main class → main class is a config class; @Bean methods allowed there:
@SpringBootApplication
public class SpringBootCoreDemo2Application {

    @Bean
    public UserService getUserServiceBean() {      // legal: the main class is a config class
        return new UserService();
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) { ... }
}

@ComponentScan: Boot's package convention

  • Already present via @SpringBootApplication.
  • No package given → convention: scan the main class's package (in.strikes.springbootcoredemo2) and sub-packages.
  • Sibling/outside packages (e.g. in.strikes.newpackage) are not scanned — classic "bean/endpoint not found" bug (Lecture 2). Same rule as Core.

Boot is opinionated: follow conventions, or override:

@SpringBootApplication(scanBasePackages = "in.strikes")   // scan the parent package instead

@EnableAutoConfiguration: "look at my project and create what seems important"

Two ways so far to register beans:

  1. @Component on a class, found by scanning.
  2. @Bean on a config-class method — for classes you can't annotate (third-party) or complex creation.

Either way you declare them. Boot adds a third way: auto-configuration classes.

// written by Spring Boot (or a library author), not by you
@AutoConfiguration
@ConditionalOnClass(WebClassA.class)          // only if this class is on the classpath
public class SomeAutoConfiguration {

    @Bean
    @ConditionalOnMissingBean                 // only if the user didn't define one
    public WebClassA getWebClassA() {
        return new WebClassA();
    }
}
  • Config classes marked @AutoConfiguration, shipped inside Boot or a library; you don't write them. Their beans are created automatically because @EnableAutoConfiguration is on (via @SpringBootApplication).

Why Boot creates beans you didn't ask for

A Maven dependency brings classes, but no beans. E.g. spring-boot-starter brings a JsonParser interface with implementations; to manage one:

@Bean
public JsonParser getJsonParserBean() {
    return new BasicJsonParser();          // an interface can't be instantiated; pick an implementation
}

spring-boot-starter-web needs many beans (embedded Tomcat, MVC infrastructure, JSON conversion) — manual in plain Spring MVC. Boot ships auto-configs for standard libraries (web MVC, data/JDBC, Hibernate, Jackson, testing, Mockito…): add the dependency, beans appear.

Demo: adding the web starter starts Tomcat

  1. Console app: "Payment done / Order placed"; logs "Starting SpringBootCoreDemo2Application… No active profile set… Started…", no Tomcat.
  2. Add only this (no version, thanks to parent), reload Maven: 
    <dependency>
    <groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
    <artifactId>spring-boot-starter-web</artifactId>
</dependency>
    Tree now includes Jackson and embedded Tomcat.
  3. Run again, no code change: "Tomcat started on port 8080" — undeclared beans via @EnableAutoConfiguration.
Version note (Boot 4): Initializr uses spring-boot-starter-webmvc (Lecture 3); old -web still works and pulls Tomcat. Prefer webmvc.

Conditions: when an auto-configuration applies

AnnotationMeaning
@AutoConfiguration"Spring Boot, create the beans in this class yourself."
@ConditionalOnClass(X.class)Only if X is on the classpath (its dependency present) — e.g. web beans only with the web starter.
@ConditionalOnMissingBeanOnly if the container doesn't already have one. Your own @Bean of that type → Boot backs off.
@BeanBean created when conditions pass.

Libraries can ship them too: a "payment gateway" JAR with an @AutoConfiguration class gets its beans created in any Boot app.

How auto-configuration is actually loaded (added)

Precision note: auto-configurations are not component-scanned (unlike the lecture's model); the scan excludes them (AutoConfigurationExcludeFilter). @EnableAutoConfiguration reads each JAR's
META-INF/spring/org.springframework.boot.autoconfigure.AutoConfiguration.imports
(Boot 2.7+/3+; older: META-INF/spring.factories). Listed classes are candidates; @Conditional… decides. A library's auto-config works only if registered in that file. Candidates run after your config → @ConditionalOnMissingBean sees your beans and backs off.

Summary: what Spring Boot removed

Spring Core (manual)Spring Boot
new AnnotationConfigApplicationContext(AppConfig.class)SpringApplication.run(Main.class, args) starts and returns the context
Separate @Configuration classMain class is the config class (@SpringBootConfiguration)
@ComponentScan("pkg")Built in: main class package + sub-packages
Hand-written beans for library infrastructureAuto-configured, conditionally
Hand-picked, version-matched dependenciesStarters + spring-boot-starter-parent manage versions
Deploy to an external server (web)Embedded Tomcat auto-configured

Unchanged: @Component, @Bean, @Primary, @Qualifier, scopes, lifecycle. Series now uses Boot (Spring MVC via Boot). Next: application.properties, startup code without getBean.

Anticipated question: "How does Spring Boot auto-configure everything?"

Favourite interview question. Good answer:

  1. @SpringBootApplication includes @EnableAutoConfiguration.
  2. It loads auto-configuration classes listed in AutoConfiguration.imports in Boot's and library JARs.
  3. Each is guarded by conditions: @ConditionalOnClass (library on classpath?), @ConditionalOnMissingBean (user already defined one?), @ConditionalOnProperty (property set?).
  4. Only matching configs contribute beans → a starter "just works" and your own bean overrides Boot's default.

Corrections & worth knowing

  • See what was auto-configured: --debug or debug=true in application.properties prints the condition evaluation report (matched / not matched and why). First tool for "why is/isn't this bean here?".
  • Turn one off: @SpringBootApplication(exclude = DataSourceAutoConfiguration.class) or spring.autoconfigure.exclude=….
  • Context type depends on classpath: SpringApplication detects plain / servlet / reactive; web starter → servlet web-server context starting Tomcat.
  • One @SpringBootConfiguration per app; @SpringBootTest searches upward from the test's package to find it.
  • Hand-set versions override the parent: a <version> on a Boot-managed dependency beats the BOM and can break compatibility. Leave versions to the parent.

Lecture 10: application.properties, @Value, @ConfigurationProperties & Startup Runners

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #10 · 36 min · Code Army · Externalized configuration and the right way to run code at startup

Two topics that finish the Boot core

  1. application.properties: supplying values to code from outside it.
  2. Starting your own logic. getBean + call is bad practice; Boot should be a black box driven by annotations/auto-config. There's a proper hook.
