Setup: Initializr project demo, same choices as Lecture 9:

  • Java, Maven, Boot 4.1.0.
  • Package in.strikes.demo.
  • Jar, Properties, Java 21.
  • No dependencies (pom: spring-boot-starter + -test).

"Hello World" in main confirms it runs.

Starting point: hard-coded values

@Component
public class PaymentGateway {
    private String type;          // e.g. Paytm, Razorpay
    private int retryCount;
    // getters and setters
}

// main (the getBean way, for now)
ApplicationContext context = SpringApplication.run(DemoApplication.class, args);
PaymentGateway paymentGateway = context.getBean(PaymentGateway.class);
paymentGateway.setType("Paytm");
paymentGateway.setRetryCount(5);
System.out.println(paymentGateway.getType());         // Paytm
System.out.println(paymentGateway.getRetryCount());   // 5

Switching Paytm → Razorpay = edit, recompile, redeploy. Don't hard-code; use a config file outside the Java code.

What application.properties is

  • In src/main/resources; Initializr adds spring.application.name=demo.
  • Non-Java file of key=value pairs (like a map).
  • A configuration file (plain data), not a @Configuration class. Boot loads it automatically; values injectable anywhere.

Other external sources:

  • application.yml (Initializr: "Properties" or "YAML").
  • Environment variables.
  • Command-line arguments.
  • Java system properties.
# application.properties
spring.application.name=demo
payment-gateway.type=Paytm
payment-gateway.retry-count=5
Precision note: "change the file and the value changes": not "whenever the code compiles" — Boot reads it at startup; changes apply on restart. A copy inside the JAR needs a rebuild. Real no-recompile benefit: overriding from outside the JAR (external file, env var, CLI arg — see precedence), or one build with per-environment config.

Injecting one value: @Value("${…}")

@Component
public class PaymentGateway {
    private String type;
    private int retryCount;

    public PaymentGateway(@Value("${payment-gateway.type}") String type,
                          @Value("${payment-gateway.retry-count}") int retryCount) {
        this.type = type;
        this.retryCount = retryCount;
    }
    // getters ...
}
  • @Value("${key}") — curly braces; parentheses gave UnsatisfiedDependencyException / TypeMismatchException / NumberFormatException.
  • Setter calls removed; output still Paytm, 5 ("5" → int).
  • Works on constructor params, setters, fields like @Autowired: @Value("${payment-gateway.type}") private String type;.
  • It is DI from the property file: @Autowired injects beans (e.g. OrderService), @Value plain values (String — not primitive — int).

Missing key: "Injection of autowired dependencies failed" (could not resolve placeholder) — it's autowiring. Fix: default after a colon:

@Value("${payment-gateway.type:Razorpay}")        // Razorpay if the key is absent
@Value("${payment-gateway.retry-count:3}")

Key wins if present. Also reads YAML, env vars, other sources.

Many values: @ConfigurationProperties

One @Value per setting is tedious and error-prone. @ConfigurationProperties binds all keys under a prefix onto fields.

# application.properties
payment-property.type=Paytm
payment-property.retry-count=5
payment-property.enabled=true
payment-property.timeout=3000
@Component
@ConfigurationProperties(prefix = "payment-property")
public class PaymentProperties {
    private String type;
    private int retryCount;       // bound from retry-count
    private boolean enabled;
    private int timeout;
    // getters and setters (binding uses the setters)
}
  • Each payment-property. key → field of the same name.
  • Prefix must be canonical (kebab-case): paymentProperty rejected ("prefix must be in canonical form"); payment-property works.
  • Relaxed binding: retry-count → retryCount. camelCase in Java, kebab-case in the file.
  • Boolean field isEnabled mismatched; enabled + getter isEnabled() follows JavaBeans.

Standard pattern: config-only class injected into its user; PaymentGateway delegates:

@Component
public class PaymentGateway {
    private final PaymentProperties paymentProperties;

    public PaymentGateway(PaymentProperties paymentProperties) {   // constructor injection
        this.paymentProperties = paymentProperties;
    }

    public String getType()      { return paymentProperties.getType(); }
    public int getRetryCount()   { return paymentProperties.getRetryCount(); }
    public boolean isEnabled()   { return paymentProperties.isEnabled(); }
    public int getTimeout()      { return paymentProperties.getTimeout(); }

    public void print() {
        System.out.println(getType());
        System.out.println(getRetryCount());
        System.out.println(isEnabled());
        System.out.println(getTimeout());
    }
}
Paytm
5
true
3000

Business code talks to the config's user, not the raw holder — common enterprise pattern.

@Value vs @ConfigurationProperties

@Value@ConfigurationProperties
BindsOne key per annotationAll keys under a prefix, onto a class
Best forOne or two valuesGroups of related settings
Defaults${key:default}Field initializers (private int timeout = 3000;)
Relaxed binding (kebab ↔ camel)Limited: write key as it appearsYes
Type safety and validation (added)Per fieldWhole object; @Validated + @NotNull, @Min…
SpEL (added)Yes: #{…}No

Starting your code properly: ApplicationRunner / CommandLineRunner

getBean(PaymentGateway.class) + print() = touching the container directly. Web: an endpoint (localhost:8080/print, Lecture 2). Console: a method that runs automatically at startup.

@Component
public class DemoRunner implements ApplicationRunner {
    private final PaymentGateway paymentGateway;

    public DemoRunner(PaymentGateway paymentGateway) {      // no @Autowired needed
        this.paymentGateway = paymentGateway;
    }

    @Override
    public void run(ApplicationArguments args) {
        paymentGateway.print();                             // runs automatically at startup
    }
}

@SpringBootApplication
public class DemoApplication {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        SpringApplication.run(DemoApplication.class, args);   // no context, no getBean
    }
}
  • ApplicationRunner: functional interface, one method run. On a @Component, Boot calls run() automatically at startup.
  • main shrinks to one line; output still Paytm 5 true 3000.

CommandLineRunner: same job, raw arguments:

@Component
public class DemoRunner implements CommandLineRunner {
    ...
    @Override
    public void run(String... args) {       // varargs: zero, one or many strings
        paymentGateway.print();
    }
}
ApplicationRunnerCommandLineRunner
run parameterApplicationArguments, parsedString... args, raw
Argument handlinggetOptionNames(), containsOption("x"), getOptionValues("x") for --x=value, plus getNonOptionArgs()Parse strings yourself
When it runsAfter context fully started (all singletons created), before SpringApplication.run returns

Args = main(String[] args)'s CLI args; rarely used here.

Running from the terminal

mvn spring-boot:run

Builds and runs (output after "BUILD SUCCESS"). Needs Maven (Lecture 3) or ./mvnw spring-boot:run; via spring-boot-maven-plugin.

Worth knowing: passing values at launch (added): 
java -jar target/demo-0.0.1-SNAPSHOT.jar --payment-property.type=Razorpay
./mvnw spring-boot:run -Dspring-boot.run.arguments="--payment-property.type=Razorpay"
PAYMENT_PROPERTY_TYPE=Razorpay java -jar target/demo-0.0.1-SNAPSHOT.jar     # env var (relaxed binding)
Each overrides payment-property.type without touching code or JAR — real "configure without recompiling". --x=value also appears in ApplicationArguments.

Property source precedence (added)

Same key in several places → fixed order, higher wins (simplified):

PrioritySource
1 (highest)Command-line arguments (--key=value)
2Java system properties (-Dkey=value)
3OS environment variables (KEY_NAME)
4Profile-specific files (application-prod.properties)
5application.properties / .yml outside the JAR (working dir or ./config/)
6 (lowest)application.properties / .yml inside the JAR

Profiles (spring.profiles.active=prod): one build, dev/test/prod settings. Env vars use relaxed binding: PAYMENT_PROPERTY_RETRY_COUNT → payment-property.retry-count.

The same config in YAML

# application.yml (equivalent to the properties above)
payment-property:
  type: Paytm
  retry-count: 5
  enabled: true
  timeout: 3000

Nests by indentation; good for deep/list config, whitespace-sensitive. One format per project; if both, .properties wins.

Corrections & worth knowing

  • Without @Component: @EnableConfigurationProperties(PaymentProperties.class) on a config class or @ConfigurationPropertiesScan on main — often preferred.
  • Immutable records: @ConfigurationProperties("payment-property") public record PaymentProperties(String type, int retryCount, boolean enabled, int timeout) {} binds via constructor — no setters, unmodifiable.
  • IDE auto-complete: optional spring-boot-configuration-processor generates metadata so IntelliJ suggests/validates payment-property.*.
  • Several runners: order with @Order(1), @Order(2)…; an exception in a runner fails startup.
  • No secrets in committed application.properties: passwords/API keys go in env vars or a secrets manager, referenced as ${DB_PASSWORD}.

Lecture 11: CRUD Mini-Project, Part 1 — Architecture, Entity & the First Endpoint

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #11 · 1h37m · Code Army · Client + Spring Boot server + MySQL; Controller–Service–Repository

Why a CRUD project matters

Boot + database + CRUD (Create, Read, Update, Delete) — the backbone of every backend (posting on Instagram/YouTube = create, scrolling reels = read, editing/removing a post = update/delete). First time client, server and database work together.

Parts (JPA writing SQL) feel magical by design; JDBC, Hibernate, Spring MVC internals come later.

The finished app (preview)

Postman = client; MySQL student_crud_db, table student. Part 1: only create, with a stubbed repository; rest next lecture.

RequestEffect
POST localhost:8080/api/students + JSON body (name, email, rollNumber, age, subject)Inserts a row, returns saved student
GET /api/students/1Returns student 1
GET /api/studentsList of all students
PUT /api/students/1 + full JSON bodyUpdates student 1 (e.g. age 28 → 29, subject Spring Boot → Spring Framework)
DELETE /api/students/1Deletes row permanently: "Student deleted permanently"
PATCH /api/students/2/soft-deleteSoft delete: is_deleted = 1; row stays, hidden from reads

Console shows each request's SQL (insert, select, update…) — generated by Spring Data JPA, never written.

The big picture: client, server, database

HTTP methods vs CRUD operations
CRUD operationHTTP methodMeaning
CreatePOSTCreate a new resource
ReadGETRetrieve data (no body)
UpdatePUTUpdate an existing resource
DeleteDELETERemove a resource
(Partial update)PATCHChange part of a resource; used here for soft delete
  • Client: frontend (React/JS), browser or mobile; here Postman (GET, POST, PUT, PATCH, DELETE, HEAD, OPTIONS…).
  • Host: localhost:8080 (Lectures 1–2); deployed, a real domain like codearmy.in.
  • Endpoint: part after host, e.g. /api/students. A URL maps to a method in a class; /api/teachers → different method (404 here).
  • Request parts: method (POST), host, endpoint, headers, JSON body.
  • Headers: Accept: application/json (response format accepted), Content-Type: application/json (body format sent). Postman adds defaults.

Requirements

  • Postman (Windows / Mac).
  • MySQL server (relational; PostgreSQL/Oracle equally fine; later projects use Postgres, H2). Set a password on install.
  • GUI client instead of terminal:
    • DBeaver (used here): free, open source, cross-platform.
    • MySQL Workbench: official, more complex.
    • Sequel Ace: Mac only.

Project setup: three dependencies

start.spring.io:

  • Java, Maven, Boot 4.1.0.
  • Group in.strikes, artifact crud-spring-boot-demo.
  • Jar, Properties, Java 21.
Initializr dependencyStarter in pomWhy
Spring Webspring-boot-starter-webmvcControllers + embedded Tomcat
Spring Data JPAspring-boot-starter-data-jpaDB access without writing SQL. JPA = Jakarta Persistence API (formerly Java Persistence API), Hibernate underneath
MySQL Drivermysql-connector-j (runtime scope)JDBC driver for MySQL

pom also has unused data-jpa-test and webmvc-test.

First run fails: "Failed to configure a DataSource"

APPLICATION FAILED TO START
Failed to configure a DataSource: 'url' attribute is not specified
and no embedded datasource could be configured.
  • Auto-configuration (Lecture 9) sees JPA + MySQL driver and tries to create a DataSource, which needs URL, username, password (in application.properties) — none yet.

Temporary workaround (real fix next lecture): exclude it:

@SpringBootApplication(exclude = DataSourceAutoConfiguration.class)
public class CrudSpringBootDemoApplication {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        SpringApplication.run(CrudSpringBootDemoApplication.class, args);
    }
}

App starts: "Tomcat started on port 8080". localhost:8080 shows the Whitelabel Error Page (no endpoint yet).

Worth knowing (added): proper fix: spring.datasource.url/username/password (next part). DataSourceAutoConfiguration's package is version-dependent (Boot 4 split auto-configs per technology) — let the IDE import it.

Why not put everything in one StudentService?

One class could:

  1. Listen to the endpoint.
  2. Convert incoming JSON.
  3. Apply business logic.
  4. Store data in the DB.
  5. Send the response.

Too many jobs: one class, one responsibility (SRP), delegate to injected deps → layers.

Controller – Service – Repository architecture

LayerClassSingle responsibility
ControllerStudentControllerHTTP: map endpoint to method, receive params and body (JSON → object), return response
ServiceStudentServiceBusiness logic/validation (e.g. CS subject only, roll number in range); coordinates controller and repository
RepositoryStudentRepositoryDB access only
EntityStudentThe data: class mapped to a DB table
  • Pattern for every Boot app in the series. Spring MVC actually runs before the controller (later).
  • A DTO (data transfer object) will later sit between JSON and entity.
Anticipated question: "The service just passes the request on. Why have it?" Validation and business rules belong there, not in controller (HTTP) or repository (DB); as the app grows it combines repositories or calls other services.

Package structure

in.strikes.crudspringbootdemo
├── CrudSpringBootDemoApplication.java     (main class stays at the top)
├── controller/   StudentController
├── service/      StudentService
├── repository/   StudentRepository
└── entity/       Student                  (sometimes called "model")

Group by layer; sub-packages under main's package are scanned (Lecture 9).

The entity: Student

JSON fields map onto a class with the same fields: name, email, age (number), rollNumber, subject.

{
  "id": 1,
  "name": "Aditya",
  "email": "aditya@gmail.com",
  "age": 28,
  "rollNumber": 1001,
  "subject": "Spring Framework"
}
package in.strikes.crudspringbootdemo.entity;

import jakarta.persistence.Entity;
import jakarta.persistence.Id;

@Entity                                 // "map this class to a database table"
public class Student {

    @Id                                 // primary key
    private Long id;                    // Long: ids can grow large

    private String name;
    private String email;
    private Integer age;
    private int rollNumber;
    private String subject;

    // getters and setters for all fields (Generate → Getter and Setter)
}
  • @Entity alone errors: "Persistent entity 'Student' should have a primary key".
  • Existing field as key? name, age, subject repeat; email (and maybe rollNumber per college) unique. Good practice: separate id — private Long id with @Id.
An entity is not a Spring bean: no @Component meta-annotation; created with new or by JPA loading rows, managed by JPA (persistence context), not the IoC container.

What JPA saves you from: SQL by hand

In DBeaver: create DB student_crud_db, write SQL manually:

CREATE TABLE student (
    id          INT AUTO_INCREMENT PRIMARY KEY,
    name        VARCHAR(255),
    age         INT,
    email       VARCHAR(255),
    roll_number INT,
    subject     VARCHAR(255)
);

INSERT INTO student VALUES (1, 'Aditya', 28, 'aditya@gmail.com', 1001, 'Spring Framework');

Same fields live in JSON, Student.java and the table. Must the repository hand-write the INSERT? No — Spring Data generates SQL from method calls (even CREATE TABLE, from the entity). SQL knowledge still matters, just isn't required.

Definition: ORM (Object-Relational Mapping): one Student object = one row (s1 Aditya, 28…; s2 Rohit, 29…). Class → table, field → column, object → row. Hibernate (JPA implementation) converts, using JDBC underneath.
Correction: lecture says "Object Relation Model"; standard term is Object-Relational Mapping.

Designing the REST endpoints

Common prefix /api/students; each endpoint unique by method + path:

OperationMethodEndpointBody?
CreatePOST/api/studentsYes: student JSON
Read oneGET/api/students/{id}No
Read allGET/api/studentsNo
UpdatePUT/api/students/{id}Yes: full JSON, any field(s) via one endpoint
DeleteDELETE/api/students/{id}No
  • POST/GET /api/students share a path, differ in method → fine; same method + path twice confuses Spring.
  • One full-body update beats updateName, updateEmail… endpoints.
  • Temporary alternative: actions in path (/api/students/create, /get/{id}, /getAll, /update, /delete/{id}); lecturer prefers method + path.
Worth knowing: REST naming (added): URLs are nouns (resources), HTTP method is the verb: POST /api/students, not /create. Verbs in paths are an anti-pattern (duplicate the method). Plural names (/students), ids as segments (/students/1).

The controller: the first endpoint

package in.strikes.crudspringbootdemo.controller;

@RestController                          // controller whose return values become the HTTP body
@RequestMapping("/api/students")         // common prefix for every method in this class
public class StudentController {

    private final StudentService studentService;

    public StudentController(StudentService studentService) {   // constructor injection
        this.studentService = studentService;
    }

    @PostMapping("/create")              // POST /api/students/create  (temporary style)
    public ResponseEntity<Student> createStudent(@RequestBody Student student) {
        System.out.println("Inside student controller");
        Student createdStudent = studentService.createStudent(student);
        System.out.println("Exiting student controller");
        return ResponseEntity
                .status(HttpStatus.CREATED)          // 201
                .body(createdStudent);
    }

    // readStudent, updateStudent, deleteStudent: next lecture
}
  • @RestController: REST calls in, JSON out; meta-annotated @Controller → @Component → a bean (specialized vs generic @Component). Plain @Controller later.
  • @RequestMapping("/api/students") at class level: prefix for all endpoints.
  • @PostMapping("/create"): HTTP POST on prefix + /create; unique combo → routed here. Internals later.
  • @RequestBody Student student: JSON → Student automatically via Jackson (keys matched to fields). No manual mapping.

Proof: a void version printed Aditya, aditya@gmail.com; Postman got no body.

Worth knowing: what Jackson needs (added): creates the object via the no-arg constructor, fills via setters (or fields), matching keys by name — rollNumber must match exactly. Unknown keys ignored by Boot defaults. JPA also requires a no-arg constructor.

The service and the (stub) repository

@Service                                  // specialized @Component: "business logic lives here"
public class StudentService {

    private final StudentRepository studentRepository;

    public StudentService(StudentRepository studentRepository) {
        this.studentRepository = studentRepository;
    }

    public Student createStudent(Student studentRequest) {
        System.out.println("Inside student service");
        // business logic / validation would go here
        Student studentResponse = studentRepository.saveStudent(studentRequest);
        System.out.println("Exiting student service");
        return studentResponse;
    }
}

@Component                                // for now; real JPA repository next lecture
public class StudentRepository {

    public Student saveStudent(Student studentRequest) {
        System.out.println("Inside student repository");
        // no DB yet: return a dummy student
        Student s1 = new Student();       // plain 'new': entities aren't beans
        s1.setName("Aditya");
        s1.setAge(28);
        s1.setEmail("aditya@gmail.com");
        s1.setRollNumber(1001);
        s1.setSubject("Spring Framework");
        System.out.println("Exiting student repository");
        return s1;
    }
}
  • @Service: a @Component signalling business logic.
  • Without @Component on StudentRepository: "Could not autowire. No beans of 'StudentRepository' type found" (Lecture 5).
  • All layers wired by constructor injection, no @Autowired (single constructor).

Console after one POST (down and back up):

Inside student controller
Inside student service
Inside student repository
Exiting student repository
Exiting student service
Exiting student controller

The response: JSON body and status code

  • String return ("Student created") → raw text; Student return → JSON with id: null — Jackson serialises it (reverse of @RequestBody).
  • Response = body, status code, headers. Default status 200 OK.

To choose status, return ResponseEntity<Student> — generic wrapper extending HttpEntity with status, headers, body.

return ResponseEntity.ok(createdStudent);                        // 200 OK

return ResponseEntity.status(201).body(createdStudent);          // by number
return ResponseEntity.status(HttpStatus.CREATED).body(createdStudent);   // by enum → 201 Created
  • Creating a resource → 201 Created, not 200.
  • HttpStatus enum: OK (200), CREATED (201), ACCEPTED (202), UNAUTHORIZED (401), NOT_FOUND (404)… valueOf accepts 100–999.
  • Postman now shows 201 Created + JSON.
Transcript slips / corrections:
  • Lecture says "2001"; it's 201 Created.
  • ResponseEntity.created(…) takes a URI → Location header (e.g. /api/students/1); hence passing a Student failed. ResponseEntity.created(URI.create("/api/students/" + id)).body(created) is most REST-correct.
  • Invalid code throws at runtime. Standard HTTP codes: 3 digits (100–599).

Where things stand

Done: architecture, packages, Student entity, create endpoint through three layers returning 201. Not yet:

  • Real DB access (repository returns a dummy; DataSource excluded).
  • Read, update, delete endpoints.

Next lecture. Interviews ask how your project works inside — anyone can generate one with ChatGPT.

Corrections & worth knowing

  • @RestController = @Controller + @ResponseBody — return values written to the body as JSON, not treated as view (HTML template) names.
  • Prefer @Repository: specialized @Component that also translates DB exceptions into DataAccessException. With Spring Data JPA you usually write an interface extending JpaRepository.
  • Generated ids: with DB AUTO_INCREMENT, add @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY) next to @Id; client shouldn't send id on create.
  • Soft vs hard delete: hard removes the row; soft flips a flag (is_deleted) so data can be restored, audited, referenced. Cost: every read must filter deleted rows.
  • Returning entities leaks internal fields, couples API to tables → DTOs later.

Lecture 12: CRUD Mini-Project, Part 2 — JpaRepository, Connecting MySQL & Read / Update / Delete

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #12 · 59m · Code Army · continues the crud-spring-boot-demo project from Lecture 11

Where we left off

Lecture 11's /create ran through controller, service, stub repository but stored nothing. Now:

  1. Replace the stub with a Spring Data JPA repository.
  2. Connect to MySQL.
  3. Let JPA create the table.
  4. Add read-one, read-all, update, delete endpoints.

The idea: call methods, not SQL

Call ready-made Spring Data JPA methods that run the SQL.

OperationMethodSQL it roughly runs
Createsave(entity)INSERT INTO student …
Read allfindAll()SELECT * FROM student
Read onefindById(id)SELECT * FROM student WHERE id = ?
Updatesave(entity) again, no separate methodUPDATE student SET … WHERE id = ?
DeletedeleteById(id)DELETE FROM student WHERE id = ?
Exists?existsById(id) → booleanSELECT COUNT(*) … WHERE id = ?
  • JPA = Jakarta Persistence API. Boot 2.x imports javax.persistence.*; Boot 3+ jakarta.persistence.*.
  • Also saveAll (bulk), saveAndFlush, saveAllAndFlush (later).
  • save: insert or update? Decided by the primary key (see demo).

The repository becomes an interface extending JpaRepository

package in.strikes.crudspringbootdemo.repository;

import in.strikes.crudspringbootdemo.entity.Student;
import org.springframework.data.jpa.repository.JpaRepository;

public interface StudentRepository extends JpaRepository<Student, Long> {
    // empty — all CRUD methods are inherited
}
  • Generics: managed entity (Student) and its primary key type (Long, matching @Id private Long id).
  • Service calls the inherited method: 
    public Student createStudent(Student studentRequest) {
    return studentRepository.save(studentRequest);   // returns the saved entity
}

Why an interface, not a class?

A class would need its own saveStudent with a hand-written INSERT; an interface extending JpaRepository inherits all methods.

Where does save come from?

save is declared in CrudRepository (Ctrl-click).

Anticipated question: "Who implements all these abstract methods?"

Normally class StudentRepoImpl implements StudentRepository overriding save, flush, findAll… Here nothing is implemented, yet it works.

Answer: Spring Data JPA provides the implementation at runtime: given entity and id type, it generates the implementation bean and injects it into StudentService. "This is the magic of Spring Data JPA."

Do we need @Repository?

  • @Repository: specialised @Component (like @Service, @Controller).
  • Not needed on a Spring Data interface — Spring Data supplies the implementation.
  • Useful for your own repository class (complex queries, joins); often you can just declare new methods in the interface instead (later).

Setting up the database

Checking MySQL from the terminal

mysql --version              # is MySQL installed?
mysql -u root -p             # connect as root; -p = prompt for the password
mysql> SHOW DATABASES;
mysql> USE student_crud_db;
mysql> SHOW TABLES;          # Empty set (the old table was dropped)
mysql> exit

Connecting DBeaver

  1. New Database Connection → MySQL → Next.
  2. Host localhost, Database empty (we create it), username root (from install), install-time password.
  3. URL: jdbc:mysql://localhost:3306/ — copy for later. MySQL default port 3306 (like Tomcat's 8080; ports: Lecture 2).
  4. Test Connection → "Connected" → Finish.

CREATE DATABASE student_crud_db; — the only SQL we write; JPA does the rest. Password 123456: localhost only.

Connecting the app: application.properties

Remove the Lecture 11 exclusion:

@SpringBootApplication                     // exclude = DataSourceAutoConfiguration.class removed
public class CrudSpringBootDemoApplication { … }

Then tell Boot how to reach the DB:

# where the database is: jdbc:mysql://host:port/database
spring.datasource.url=jdbc:mysql://localhost:3306/student_crud_db
spring.datasource.username=root
spring.datasource.password=123456

# let Hibernate create/alter tables from the @Entity classes
spring.jpa.hibernate.ddl-auto=update

# print the SQL Hibernate runs, nicely formatted
spring.jpa.show-sql=true
spring.jpa.properties.hibernate.format_sql=true
  • spring.datasource.url = JDBC URL:
    • jdbc:mysql:// protocol signature.
    • Host: localhost, or the server address if deployed elsewhere.
    • Port 3306 + database name.
    Needed by the DataSource auto-configuration (excluded in Lecture 11).
  • username / password: same as DBeaver.
  • spring.jpa.hibernate.ddl-auto=update: "Hibernate, own the tables." Student → table student, fields → columns (id, name, age, email, roll_number, subject). update also alters on entity changes. DDL = Data Definition Language.
  • show-sql defaults false; format_sql pretty-prints.

"Do you need to memorise these keys? Not at all. I don't either."

JPA vs Hibernate (corrected): not "JPA implements Hibernate": JPA is a specification (@Entity, EntityManager), Hibernate an implementation (Boot's default). Spring Data JPA repositories call JPA; Hibernate executes over JDBC.
spring.jpa.hibernate.ddl-auto values (the lecture defers these; added)
ValueWhat Hibernate does at startupUse for
noneNothingProduction; Boot's default for real (non-embedded) DBs
validateChecks tables match entities, fails if notProduction, with migrations
updateCreates missing tables/columns; never dropsLocal development
createDrops and recreates schema. Data lostTests, demos
create-dropLike create, drops schema on shutdownTests; default for embedded DBs like H2

Demo run: the table creates itself

  1. Start: logs show JDBC URL, MySQL driver, Hibernate dialect, then "Tomcat started on port 8080".
  2. DBeaver: student table with id, age, email, name, roll_number, subject — no CREATE TABLE written.
  3. No CREATE TABLE logged on restart (table existed; update creates only what's missing). Drop + restart →
Hibernate:
    create table student (
        id bigint not null,
        age integer,
        email varchar(255),
        name varchar(255),
        roll_number integer not null,
        subject varchar(255),
        primary key (id)
    )

All derived from Student.java:

  • @Id → primary key (id).
  • Long → bigint; String → varchar(255).
  • rollNumber → roll_number (Boot's default camelCase → snake_case naming).
  • not null since int is primitive; Integer age is nullable.

Demo run: create through the real database

  1. POST /api/students/create with Aditya's JSON (incl. "id": 1) → 201 Created + saved object.
  2. Logs: two statements — select … where id=?, then insert into student (…) values (?,?,?,?,?,?) (? = bind parameters). Row in DBeaver.
  3. POST id 2, Rohit, 29, roll 102, "MERN" → second row, again select then insert.

Extra SELECT: save does insert and update, so it checks the id first ("by reading the logs").

Precision note: when save selects first (verified, Boot 4.1.0 / Hibernate 7, H2):
  • save asks: is this entity new? For wrapper ids like Long, new = id == null.
  • New: em.persist() → plain INSERT, no select.
  • Id present: em.merge() → SELECT by id, then INSERT if absent or UPDATE if present. Hence the demo's select-then-insert (JSON had "id": 1).
  • No @GeneratedValue here: POST without id fails — JpaSystemException: Identifier of entity 'Student' must be manually assigned before calling 'persist()'. Client must invent ids (fragile).
  • Usual fix: @Id @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY) + MySQL AUTO_INCREMENT → id-less POST does one insert … values (?, default).
  • POST with a non-existent id throws ObjectOptimisticLockingFailureException in Hibernate 7 (merge treats it as a deleted row). With generated ids, create should ignore/reject client id.

Read one: GET /api/students/get/{id}

Path variable vs query parameter

Don't hard-code (@GetMapping("/get/1")); two ways to send it:

Path variableQuery parameter
URL/api/students/get/1/api/students/get?id=1&name=Aditya
Annotation@PathVariable@RequestParam (later)
Typical useIdentifying which resourceFiltering, sorting, paging, optional values
PostmanPart of the pathAuto-filled in Params tab

This lecture: path variable.

Controller

@GetMapping("/get/{id}")                               // {id} = placeholder in the URL
public ResponseEntity<Student> getStudent(@PathVariable Long id) {
    Student studentResponse = studentService.getStudent(id);
    if (studentResponse == null) {
        return ResponseEntity.notFound().build();      // 404, empty body
    }
    return ResponseEntity.ok(studentResponse);         // 200 + JSON
}
  • @GetMapping: GET counterpart of @PostMapping.
  • @PathVariable Long id: reads {id}, converts to Long — path analogue of @RequestBody.
  • ResponseEntity.ok(x) = ResponseEntity.status(HttpStatus.OK).body(x).

Service: findById returns an Optional

public Student getStudent(Long id) {
    Optional<Student> studentResponse = studentRepository.findById(id);
    if (studentResponse.isPresent()) {
        return studentResponse.get();      // unwrap the Student
    }
    return null;
}
  • Student s = studentRepository.findById(id); won't compile: findById returns Optional<Student>.
  • Record may not exist (id 3). Instead of null (NPE risk): Optional, a box that may or may not contain a value; isPresent() checks, get() unwraps.

Demo run and bugs found

  1. First: 404 even for id 1 — mapping lacked {id} (@GetMapping("/get")).
  2. With /get/{id}: id 1 → Aditya (log: select … where id=?); id 2 → Rohit.
  3. Id 3 gave 200 OK, empty body — wrong. Null check: ResponseEntity.status(HttpStatus.NOT_FOUND).body(null) → simplified ResponseEntity.notFound().build() → 404.

GET sends no body (URL, method, headers only); Postman's Body tab is ignored.

Read all: GET /api/students/getAll

// controller
@GetMapping("/getAll")
public ResponseEntity<List<Student>> getAllStudents() {
    List<Student> studentList = studentService.getAllStudents();
    if (studentList.isEmpty()) {
        return ResponseEntity.notFound().build();      // see correction below
    }
    return ResponseEntity.ok(studentList);
}

// service
public List<Student> getAllStudents() {
    return studentRepository.findAll();                // SELECT all rows
}
  • List<Student> → JSON array (Aditya, Rohit); controller decides status.

Update: PUT /api/students/update/{id}

Needs two inputs: which student (path id) and new data (JSON body; PUT carries one). Example: Rohit (id 2), age 29 → 28, roll 102 → 105.

// controller
@PutMapping("/update/{id}")
public ResponseEntity<Student> updateStudent(@PathVariable Long id,
                                             @RequestBody Student studentRequest) {
    Student studentResponse = studentService.updateStudent(id, studentRequest);
    if (studentResponse == null) {
        return ResponseEntity.notFound().build();
    }
    return ResponseEntity.ok(studentResponse);
}

// service
public Student updateStudent(Long id, Student studentRequest) {
    Optional<Student> existingStudent = studentRepository.findById(id);
    if (existingStudent.isEmpty()) {
        return null;                                    // controller turns this into 404
    }
    Student studentToSave = existingStudent.get();
    studentToSave.setName(studentRequest.getName());
    studentToSave.setEmail(studentRequest.getEmail());
    studentToSave.setAge(studentRequest.getAge());
    studentToSave.setRollNumber(studentRequest.getRollNumber());
    studentToSave.setSubject(studentRequest.getSubject());
    // id deliberately NOT copied from the request
    return studentRepository.save(studentToSave);       // id exists → UPDATE
}

Steps:

  1. Load existing row via findById; missing (isEmpty(), opposite of isPresent()) → null → 404.
  2. Copy five fields onto the loaded entity. Id not copied — record keeps its original id whatever the body says (good practice).
  3. save: existing id → UPDATE (in logs).

Demo: first 404 (misspelled "update"); then PUT /update/2 → age 28, roll 105.

Why the loaded entity, not the request object: saving studentRequest trusts the client's id → may update the wrong row or create one. Loading by path id makes the URL decide which row changes.

Cleaner (later): return the Optional to the controller, no null passing.

Delete: DELETE /api/students/delete/{id}

// controller
@DeleteMapping("/delete/{id}")
public ResponseEntity<String> deleteStudent(@PathVariable Long id) {
    boolean isDeleted = studentService.deleteStudent(id);
    if (!isDeleted) {
        return ResponseEntity.notFound().build();       // 404
    }
    return ResponseEntity.ok("Record deleted");         // 200 + message
}

// service
public boolean deleteStudent(Long id) {
    boolean isStudent = studentRepository.existsById(id);
    if (!isStudent) {
        return false;
    }
    studentRepository.deleteById(id);                   // returns void
    return true;
}
  • Check with existsById; deleteById returns void, so service returns true itself.
  • ResponseEntity<Boolean> changed to String "Record deleted" (readable).

Demo:

  • DELETE /delete/3 (absent) → 404, empty body.
  • DELETE /delete/2 → 200 "Record deleted"; Rohit's row gone.

Full request flow

The finished endpoints (lecture's action-in-path style)
OperationMappingInputRepository callSuccess / failure
Create@PostMapping("/create")@RequestBodysave201
Read one@GetMapping("/get/{id}")@PathVariablefindById200 / 404
Read all@GetMapping("/getAll")—findAll200 / 404 on empty
Update@PutMapping("/update/{id}")@PathVariable + @RequestBodyfindById + save200 / 404
Delete@DeleteMapping("/delete/{id}")@PathVariableexistsById + deleteById200 / 404

No SQL: five methods give full CRUD; delete-all/update-all equally easy.

Next: soft delete

  • Delete is permanent so far. Most apps prefer soft delete: is_deleted 0 → 1; row stays but is treated as non-existent (can't be fetched/updated).
  • Why keep it?
    • Recover user data later.
    • Logs/analytics (e.g. counting active users).
  • Every read/update must skip deleted rows → standard methods (findById…) aren't enough; next lecture writes those queries.

Gaps worth raising in an interview

  • Check-then-act race: existsById→deleteById or findById→save are two round trips; another request can change the row between. Use @Transactional; for concurrent updates, optimistic locking via a @Version column.
  • Extra queries: delete runs count(*), then select + delete (deleteById loads first; verified). findById(id).map(s -> { repo.delete(s); return true; }).orElse(false) saves one query.
  • No validation: negative age, blank name, duplicate email saved as-is. Service-layer validation promised in Lecture 11; Bean Validation (@Valid, @NotBlank) later.
  • null as "not found" spreads null checks everywhere. Alternatives:
    • Return Optional to controller: return service.find(id).map(ResponseEntity::ok).orElse(ResponseEntity.notFound().build());
    • Throw custom StudentNotFoundException, handled by @RestControllerAdvice.
  • No pagination: findAll() loads every row; use findAll(Pageable).
  • Entity as API contract: same Student is request, response and table; DTOs separate them.
  • Credentials in application.properties: use spring.datasource.password=${DB_PASSWORD} from the environment; never commit real passwords.

Corrections & worth knowing

  • Runtime repository = JDK dynamic proxy (jdk.proxy2.$Proxy98, verified Boot 4.1.0) delegating to SimpleJpaRepository. Auto-config enables repository scanning (= @EnableJpaRepositories) for Repository sub-interfaces under main's package — no annotation needed.
  • findAll() returns List only via ListCrudRepository: since Spring Data 3 (Boot 3) JpaRepository extends it (an ArrayList, verified). Plain CrudRepository.findAll() returns Iterable.
  • deleteById on a missing id is silent in Spring Data 3+ (verified: selects, nothing, no exception). Older versions threw EmptyResultDataAccessException. Explicit existence check produces the 404.
  • Empty collection → 200 OK + [], not 404 (the collection exists); 404 is for a missing single resource.
  • Delete responses: 200 + message works; many APIs return 204 No Content (ResponseEntity.noContent().build()).
  • PUT vs PATCH: update copies all fields, so omitted ones become null — correct PUT semantics (replace whole resource). For one field, use PATCH copying only non-null fields.
  • @PathVariable name matching needs -parameters (enabled by spring-boot-starter-parent). Without it (Spring 6.1+), write @PathVariable("id") — same issue as XML constructor-arg name (Lecture 8).
  • In a @Transactional method, save after modifying a loaded entity is optional: dirty checking writes changes at commit. Calling it is harmless and clearer.
  • Dialect auto-detected: logs show MySQLDialect; Hibernate 6+ detects it from the connection, so spring.jpa.database-platform isn't needed.
  • REST style: /create, /get/{id}, /getAll, /update/{id}, /delete/{id} put verbs in paths. Lecture 11's resource style: POST /api/students, GET /api/students/{id}, GET /api/students, PUT /api/students/{id}, DELETE /api/students/{id}.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"2001 Created" → 201 Created.
"spring.datasource.datasource" → spring.datasource.url.
"Tomcat on port 880" → 8080.
"spring jpa show query" → spring.jpa.show-sql.
"findBy" → findById.
ResponseEntity<Student> once typed as "struct".
"public Student get updateStudent" → public Student updateStudent.
studentRequest.setRollNumber(…) fixed on screen to studentRequest.getRollNumber().
"an interface's methods must all be overridden" applies to a class implementing it; an interface extending an interface overrides nothing.

Lecture 13: Soft Delete vs Hard Delete, Derived Query Methods, Auto-Increment IDs & @RequestParam

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #13 · 46m · Code Army · continues the CRUD project from Lectures 11–12

The problem: delete erases history

DB holds id 1, Aditya, 1001; deleteById(1) runs a DELETE and the row is gone = hard delete. Risky in enterprise apps: can't pull records of all users (incl. those who left); logs, audits, analytics lose data; old accounts can't be recovered. Rarely discussed, yet a widely used industry practice.

The idea: soft delete

Mark the row instead: column deleted, default 0 (active); delete flips it to 1.

idnameroll_numberdeletedMeaning
1Aditya10010Active
2Rohit1021Deleted, for the app

A soft-deleted row takes part in no CRUD operation — physically present, treated as non-existent: GET id 2 → 404 Not Found; update → "record doesn't exist"; get all omits Rohit; soft-delete again → already deleted.

Hard delete vs soft delete
Hard deleteSoft delete
What happensRemoved from DB (DELETE)Marked deleted (UPDATE … SET deleted = 1)
Recoverable?NoYes, flip the flag back
History / audit / analyticsLostKept
CostNoneEvery query must filter deleted = false; table keeps growing
WhenData never needed, or must be erasedDefault in most real apps
Why storage isn't the argument: "space is not that big a factor; data is". Deleted rows still serve logs, recovery, business reports — soft delete preferred, hard delete for rare, deliberate cases.

Adding the flag to the entity

@Entity
public class Student {
    …
    private boolean deleted;          // false = active, true = soft-deleted

    public boolean isDeleted() { return deleted; }            // IDE-generated getter for boolean
    public void setDeleted(boolean deleted) { this.deleted = deleted; }
}
  • ddl-auto=update adds the column on restart (lecturer dropped the table first for clarity).
  • MySQL: deleted bit not null — bit stores Java boolean; not null since primitive.
  • Row inserted by hand in DBeaver gets deleted = 0 — correct.

Why the flag changes every query

findById(1) was SELECT * FROM student WHERE id = 1; now must be:

SELECT * FROM student WHERE id = 1 AND deleted = false;   -- read one
SELECT * FROM student WHERE deleted = false;              -- read all

findById, findAll, existsById don't know about deleted — they'd still return Rohit; no built-in "by id and not deleted". save needs no change.

Derived query methods: SQL from a method name

No need to implement the repository: Spring Data JPA builds the query from the method name — just declare it; implementation provided at runtime, like findById. Parsing details later.

public interface StudentRepository extends JpaRepository<Student, Long> {

    Optional<Student> findByIdAndDeletedIsFalse(Long id);   // WHERE id = ? AND deleted = false

    List<Student> findByDeletedIsFalse();                   // WHERE deleted = false
}
  • Declared, never defined: "Defining it is JPA's responsibility."
  • You choose the return type: Optional<Student> (maybe one), List<Student> (many).
  • IntelliJ autocompletes from entity fields: after findByIdAnd… → Deleted, Age, Subject, Email…; after a property → IsFalse, IsAfter, IsBefore, Between, Containing, Empty. None pre-exist; generated from the name.

Generated SQL (verified on Boot 4.1.0):

findByIdAndDeletedIsFalse(2)  →  select … from student s1_0 where s1_0.id=? and s1_0.deleted=false
findByDeletedIsFalse()        →  select … from student s1_0 where s1_0.deleted=false

The naming rule

First "read all" attempt findAllAndDeletedIsFalse() → app failed to start:

Error creating bean with name 'studentRepository' …
Cannot create query for method [StudentRepository.findAllAndDeletedIsFalse()];
No property 'findAll' found for type 'Student'

A derived query must contain By (separates action from criteria); without it, findAll was read as a Student field. Names are checked at repository-bean creation → bad name = startup failure, not runtime surprise.

  • Pattern: findBy + field + optional condition, more fields joined by And / Or.
  • One criterion → no And: findByDeletedIsFalse.
  • Use entity Java field names (deleted, rollNumber), not column names (roll_number).
Worth knowing (added, verified):
  • findAllByDeletedFalse() is valid — text between find and By is ignored. The failure was the missing By, not All.
  • DeletedFalse ≡ DeletedIsFalse; Is is optional filler.
  • Other prefixes: existsBy…, countBy…, deleteBy…. E.g. existsByIdAndDeletedFalse(id) → select id … where id=? and deleted=false fetch first ? rows only.
  • Long names get unreadable; complex queries use @Query (JPQL) — later lecture.

Updating each operation for soft delete

All changes in StudentService (business logic).

OperationBeforeAfter
Createsave(request)request.setDeleted(false), then save
Read onefindByIdfindByIdAndDeletedIsFalse
Read allfindAllfindByDeletedIsFalse
UpdatefindById + copy + savefindByIdAndDeletedIsFalse + copy + setDeleted(false) + save
Hard deleteexistsById + deleteByIdUnchanged, by choice
Soft delete—New: findByIdAndDeletedIsFalse + setDeleted(true) + save

Create: never trust the client with the flag

public Student createStudent(Student studentRequest) {
    studentRequest.setDeleted(false);          // ignore whatever the client sent
    return studentRepository.save(studentRequest);
}

Student doubles as request body, so a client could POST "deleted": true → record deleted from birth. "I shouldn't give the client that much power." Deletion is an internal decision, only via the soft-delete API. Demo: POST Aditya with "deleted": true → saved and returned false.

Read one and read all

public Student getStudent(Long id) {
    Optional<Student> studentResponse = studentRepository.findByIdAndDeletedIsFalse(id);
    return studentResponse.isPresent() ? studentResponse.get() : null;   // null → 404 in controller
}

public List<Student> getAllStudents() {
    return studentRepository.findByDeletedIsFalse();
}

Update

Load via findByIdAndDeletedIsFalse (soft-deleted = not found); setDeleted(false) before save, "so nobody sends deleted true in an update either".

Hard delete: a design choice

Should hard delete hit already soft-deleted records? Business decision:

  • Option A (lecture): removes any record — rare, deliberate: "when I perform it, I know what I'm doing." Keep existsById (ignores flag).
  • Option B: soft-deleted rows invisible to hard delete too → existsByIdAndDeletedIsFalse.

The soft-delete endpoint: PATCH

  • "A kind of delete" → DELETE would work.
  • But nothing is deleted: one field changes (deleted 0 → 1) — exactly PATCH's purpose (partial modification).
// controller
@PatchMapping("/delete-soft/{id}")
public ResponseEntity<String> deleteStudentSoftly(@PathVariable Long id) {
    boolean isDeleted = studentService.deleteStudentSoftly(id);
    if (!isDeleted) {
        return ResponseEntity.notFound().build();
    }
    return ResponseEntity.ok("Record deleted");
}

// service
public boolean deleteStudentSoftly(Long id) {
    Optional<Student> existingStudent = studentRepository.findByIdAndDeletedIsFalse(id);
    if (existingStudent.isEmpty()) {
        return false;                          // missing OR already soft-deleted
    }
    Student studentToSave = existingStudent.get();
    studentToSave.setDeleted(true);
    studentRepository.save(studentToSave);     // UPDATE … SET deleted = 1
    return true;
}

Three steps: (1) get the record only if not deleted; (2) set deleted = true; (3) save — id exists, so UPDATE.

Demo run: soft delete end to end

  1. POST Aditya (id 1), Rohit (id 2); both deleted = 0.
  2. PATCH /delete-soft/2 → 200 "Record deleted". Logs: derived select … where id=? and deleted=false, then update. DBeaver: Rohit deleted = 1.
  3. GET /get/2 → 404; GET /get/1 → Aditya.
  4. GET /getAll → only Aditya.
  5. POST Rahul (id 3, "Django"): first try 405 Method Not Allowed (URL was a GET-only endpoint), fixed to /create. PATCH /delete-soft/3 → deleted = 1.
  6. DELETE /delete/3 → "Record deleted", row physically gone. Hard delete works on soft-deleted records, as designed.

The client-supplied id bug, and @GeneratedValue

POST bodies carried an id — the client chose the PK. POSTing new student Rohan with "id": 2 (Rohit's) → 201 Created, but Rohit's row overwritten with Rohan's data.

Why a create became an update: save does insert and update (Lecture 12). With an id, it SELECTs first; found Rohit → UPDATE. Create silently modified data. Fix: stop clients sending ids; let the DB generate them. 
@Id
@GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY)    // DB auto-increment
private Long id;
  • IDENTITY = DB assigns id via MySQL AUTO_INCREMENT.
  • Catch: existing table unchanged — id still bigint not null without auto_increment (column change = DDL). Dropped table + restart → create table student (id bigint not null auto_increment, …).
  • POST bodies now have no id; Rohit = 1, Aditya = 2 automatically. No accidental overwrite via create, "unless I do it myself".
Precision note (added, verified on Boot 4.1.0 / Hibernate 7):
  • ddl-auto=update never modifies existing columns, only adds missing tables/columns → adding @GeneratedValue later needs manual ALTER TABLE or recreate.
  • @GeneratedValue alone doesn't stop a client sending an id; Hibernate 7 still takes the merge path:
    • Id exists → row updated (same overwrite bug).
    • Id doesn't exist → ObjectOptimisticLockingFailureException, a 500.
    Robust fix: also studentRequest.setId(null) in createStudent (like setDeleted(false)), or a DTO with no id field.

Path variables vs request (query) parameters

Path: /get/1. Request parameter: /get?id=1, chained with & (/get?id=1&name=Aditya); Postman fills its Params tab automatically. Travel in the request URL.

When to prefer request parameters: with several fields, path segments get ambiguous (/get/1/Aditya — which is id, which name?). Named params (?id=1&name=Aditya) are self-describing. 
@GetMapping("/get")                                    // no {id} in the path any more
public ResponseEntity<Student> getStudent(@RequestParam Long id) { … }

@PutMapping("/update")
public ResponseEntity<Student> updateStudent(@RequestParam Long id,
                                             @RequestBody Student studentRequest) { … }

@DeleteMapping("/delete")
public ResponseEntity<String> deleteStudent(@RequestParam Long id) { … }

@PatchMapping("/delete-soft")
public ResponseEntity<String> deleteStudentSoftly(@RequestParam Long id) { … }
  • @RequestParam Long id: take request param id into this variable. URL param name must match the variable name, or use @RequestParam("id").
  • Demo: GET /get?id=1 → Aditya; ?id=2 → second record. GET /get/1 now 404 (no mapping matches).
@PathVariable vs @RequestParam
@PathVariable@RequestParam
URL/students/1/students?id=1
MappingNeeds {id} in pathPlain path
Missing valueNo match → 404400 Bad Request (required by default)
Optional valuesAwkwardrequired = false or defaultValue = "…"
Typical use (added)Identify one resource: GET /students/1Filter / sort / page: GET /students?subject=MERN&page=0

Homework

More endpoints (update-all, delete-all); real service validations ("roll number between 1001 and 9999"); practise multi-field derived query methods and inspect the SQL.

Next: internals — Servlets (briefly; nobody writes raw servlets today), then Spring MVC, which explains Boot's web layer.

Interview gaps

  • Forgetting the filter. Every query needs deleted = false; one miss leaks deleted data. Centralise in Hibernate:
    • @SQLRestriction("deleted = false") on the entity (Hibernate 6.3+, replaces deprecated @Where) + @SQLDelete(sql = "UPDATE student SET deleted = true WHERE id = ?") so deleteById soft-deletes.
    • Or Hibernate 6.4+ @SoftDelete (both).
    • Trade-off: native query needed to see deleted rows.
  • Unique constraints. Unique email of a soft-deleted user blocks re-sign-up. Include deleted in the unique key, or rewrite email on deletion.
  • Who and when. Boolean loses deletion time; use deleted_at TIMESTAMP NULL (null = active), often + deleted_by → "restore within 30 days", purge jobs.
  • Legal erasure. GDPR right to erasure may require real deletion/anonymisation — soft delete isn't universal.
  • Index the flag. Nearly every query filters on it; index (deleted, …) or partial index (PostgreSQL).
  • Restore endpoint. Undo is the main benefit; no PATCH /restore yet.
  • Race conditions. Find-then-save = two statements. Single @Modifying @Query("update Student s set s.deleted = true where s.id = :id and s.deleted = false") returning row count is atomic; 0 → 404.

Corrections & worth knowing

  • Client needn't know it's a soft delete. Lecture picks PATCH since one field changes internally. Many APIs keep DELETE /students/{id} as the public contract and soft-delete behind it — to the client the resource is gone (GET → 404). PATCH fits when the client explicitly edits a status ({"status": "ARCHIVED"}). Both defensible; justify your choice.
  • Ignore the flag on input — and id too. setDeleted(false) in create is right; in update it's redundant (fields copied onto an entity already loaded with deleted = false). General fix: request DTOs without server-owned fields.
  • Derived queries validated at startup. Typo (findByRollNo vs field rollNumber) → PropertyReferenceException, app stops. A feature: early errors.
  • boolean vs Boolean. Primitive → NOT NULL column, defaults false — right for a flag; wrapper allows a meaningless null state. Getter isDeleted(); Jackson still maps to JSON "deleted".
  • Column type by DB: MySQL bit (lecture log), H2 boolean (verified), PostgreSQL boolean; Hibernate picks per dialect.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"200 Created" → 201 Created.
"404 Method Not Allowed" → 405.
"saves the student into memory" → into the database.
"GeneratedType.IDENTITY" → GenerationType.IDENTITY.
"bigin" → bigint.
Endpoint written as "delete-soft" and "delete-softly"; final is /delete-soft.

Lecture 14: Java Servlets & Tomcat — The Foundation Under Spring MVC (with a Servlet CRUD App)

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #14 · 1h46m · Code Army · Servlets, servlet container, external vs embedded Tomcat, WAR deployment, servlet lifecycle

Why learn servlets in the Spring Boot era?

Nobody writes raw servlets for new projects, but Spring MVC (hence every Boot web app) is built on the Servlet API — Java's first web technology. To understand Spring internals, know: what a servlet is, what Tomcat is, external vs embedded Tomcat.

Lecture builds a small CRUD app with plain servlets on standalone Tomcat, Postman as client.

Recap: client–server

Client (frontend, mobile, Postman) sends an HTTP request; server (deployed backend) returns an HTTP response — here Tomcat running our servlets, normally a Boot / MVC app.

The problem: a web app with core Java alone

Yes and no. java.net's ServerSocket/Socket can listen on a port (localhost:8080) and accept connections, but give only a byte stream — no HTTP understanding.

A request like GET localhost:8080/hello carries:

Part of an HTTP requestExample
MethodGET, POST, PUT, PATCH, DELETE
Host and portlocalhost:8080, or codearmy.in when deployed
Endpoint (path)/hello, /login, /courses
HeadersContent-Type: application/json
Query parameters / path variables?id=1
BodyJSON for POST/PUT; none for GET

Goal: map each endpoint to a Java method (/hello → hello(), /login → login()), result back to client. A raw socket doesn't give method/path, so you'd parse text yourself:

// what "routing" looks like when you parse raw HTTP yourself
if (endpoint.equals("/hello") && method.equals("GET")) {
    callHelloMethod();
}

Everything you'd hand-build with java.net

(1) read raw HTTP text from the socket; (2) parse URL/path; (3) headers; (4) query params; (5) read/parse body; (6) identify HTTP method; (7) map to the right Java method; (8) pass request data in; (9) build response text; (10) add status code; (11) add response headers; (12) send it back; (13) manage threads for concurrent users (else everyone waits), keep server listening.

The real failure mode: none of these 13 steps is business logic — server plumbing repeated per app. Focus should be hello(), not parsing bytes. Boot lets you write only the mapped method; this lecture shows the layer enabling that.

The fix: a servlet container

Move all plumbing (listening, parsing, responses, threading) into a separate container; our code = business methods only. That's a servlet container; most famous: Apache Tomcat, often just "the server".

Tomcat and servlets, defined

  • Tomcat = Java web server running Java web apps; to the OS a long-running process listening on a port (8080 default). Parses each request, picks the handling class/method, calls it.
  • Servlet = special Java class we write; Tomcat creates/manages its objects and calls its methods. Many servlets per container, each mapped to a URL (/hello → Servlet A, /login → Servlet B), via XML or annotations.
Where Spring's ideas came from
SpringServlet world
ContainerIoC container (ApplicationContext)Tomcat (servlet container)
Managed objectsBeansServlets
Who creates themThe containerThe container
Lifecycle hooks@PostConstruct / @PreDestroyinit() / destroy()
Dependency injectionYesNo — wire with new

Both: Java classes managed by a container. "The idea of an IoC container managing beans came from servlets."

The request and response objects

Java understands objects, not HTTP text. Per request Tomcat:

  1. Parses into an HttpServletRequest (getters: getParameter(), getHeader(), getMethod(), getRequestURI(), getReader() for body).
  2. Creates an empty HttpServletResponse (setStatus(), setContentType(), setHeader(), getWriter() for body).
  3. Passes both to the servlet method.
  4. Servlet reads request, fills response, returns nothing — handlers are void.
  5. Tomcat turns the filled object into an HTTP response.
Why handlers are void: servlet gets a reference to Tomcat's response object; whatever it sets changes that same object. (Java passes references by value → both point to one object.)

External vs embedded Tomcat, and WAR files

  • Today (Boot): spring-boot-starter-web (spring-boot-starter-webmvc in Boot 4) brings embedded Tomcat — a library in your app; run main(), packaged as JAR.
  • Earlier: download Tomcat separately (external), package app as WAR, deploy into Tomcat which runs it.
  • WAR (Web Application Archive) ≈ JAR: compiled classes, config, servlet classes, static HTML/CSS/JS, standard WEB-INF/ layout.

Setting up an external Tomcat

  1. Apache Tomcat site → Core → zip (Windows) or tar.gz (Mac/Linux), extract. Use Tomcat 10.1.x (as lecture).
  2. Folders: bin/, conf/, lib/, logs/, temp/, webapps/… conf/server.xml has <Connector port="8080" …> — why it listens on 8080.
  3. Before start, localhost:8080 → "This site can't be reached".
  4. Mac/Linux: chmod +x bin/*.sh, then bin/catalina.sh run. Windows: bin\catalina.bat run or bin\startup.bat.
  5. Log: "Server startup in [292] milliseconds"; localhost:8080 shows the welcome page.

Project setup: a WAR with the Servlet API

<packaging>war</packaging>                 <!-- default is jar (Maven lecture) -->

<dependencies>
    <dependency>
        <groupId>jakarta.servlet</groupId>
        <artifactId>jakarta.servlet-api</artifactId>
        <version>6.0.0</version>               <!-- matches Tomcat 10.1; see note -->
        <scope>provided</scope>
    </dependency>
</dependencies>

<build>
    <finalName>crud-app</finalName>         <!-- WAR name = URL context path -->
</build>
  • Plain Maven project servlet-crud-demo, no Spring; only servlet API (mvnrepository.com, "jakarta servlet").
  • finalName → crud-app.war instead of artifactId-version.war; the name ends up in the URL.
Match Servlet API to Tomcat version:
  • Lecture adds jakarta.servlet-api 6.1.0 but runs Tomcat 10.1.
  • Tomcat 10.1 = Servlet 6.0; Tomcat 11 = 6.1. Compiling against a newer API works only while you call nothing new.
  • Pair: Tomcat 10.1 ↔ 6.0.0, Tomcat 11 ↔ 6.1.0.
  • Tomcat ≤9 uses javax.servlet, 10+ uses jakarta.servlet — not interchangeable.

The User model and in-memory "database"

No real DB (JDBC boilerplate not yet covered); users in a HashMap. Layout mirrors Boot layers minus repository:

in.strikes
├── model/     User          (id, name, email, mobile + getters/setters + all-args constructor)
├── servlet/   UserServlet   (like a controller)
└── service/   UserService   (map-backed storage)

User: plain POJO (Integer id, String name, String email, String mobile), no annotations, unmanaged.

Writing the servlet

@WebServlet("/users")                          // Tomcat: send /users requests here
public class UserServlet extends HttpServlet {

    private UserService userService = new UserService();   // no DI → manual, tightly coupled

    @Override protected void doPost(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response) …
    @Override protected void doGet(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response) …
    @Override protected void doPut(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response) …
    @Override protected void doDelete(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response) …
}
  • Mapping: old way web.xml listing servlet + URL; @WebServlet("/users") does the same, preferred (like Spring annotations).
  • extends HttpServlet — abstract class with init, doGet, doHead, doPost, doPut, doDelete, getLastModified…; override what you need. doPatch shows in the lecture's IDE only because it compiled against Servlet 6.1 (added there, Tomcat 11); under Tomcat 10.1 (6.0) HttpServlet doesn't dispatch PATCH.
One endpoint per servlet class, one method per HTTP verb: Spring maps per method (@GetMapping("/get/{id}")); a servlet's URL belongs to the class. POST, GET (one/all), PUT, DELETE all hit /users; the HTTP method picks doXxx. Read-one and read-all share doGet, which checks for an id param.
OperationRequestHandler
CreatePOST /users?id=1&name=Aditya&email=…&mobile=…doPost
Read oneGET /users?id=1doGet (id present)
Read allGET /usersdoGet (no id)
UpdatePUT /users?id=…doPut (homework)
DeleteDELETE /users?id=…doDelete (homework)

Why create uses query params, not a JSON body

  • Boot: Jackson converts JSON body to User (@RequestBody).
  • Plain servlet: no JSON conversion — read request.getReader() (BufferedReader) + a JSON library yourself.
  • Demo sends fields as query params: POST /users?id=1&name=Aditya&email=aditya@gmail.com&mobile=9876543210.

doPost: read, validate, store, respond

@Override
protected void doPost(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response) throws IOException {
    String idParam = request.getParameter("id");        // parameters always arrive as String
    String name    = request.getParameter("name");
    String email   = request.getParameter("email");
    String mobile  = request.getParameter("mobile");

    if (idParam == null || name == null || email == null || mobile == null) {
        response.setStatus(400);
        response.setContentType("application/json");
        response.getWriter().write("{\"message\": \"Some fields are missing\"}");
        return;
    }
    User user = new User(Integer.parseInt(idParam), name, email, mobile);   // String → Integer by hand
    User createdUser = userService.createUser(user);

    response.setStatus(201);                                 // Created
    response.setContentType("application/json");
    response.getWriter().write("{\"message\": \"User added successfully\"}");
}
  • request.getParameter("id") = what Tomcat saves us: no hunting ?/& in raw text.
  • Params are always String → Integer.parseInt.
  • Response built field by field: status, content type, body via getWriter().write(…); JSON hand-written with escaped quotes (\").
  • getWriter() throws IOException → throws IOException.
Transcript slips / bugs fixed above:
  • Lecture's check id == null && email == null && name == null && mobile == null rejects only if all missing; should be || ("if any is null"), as intended.
  • It parsed id before checking: Integer.parseInt(null) → NumberFormatException → 500 not 400. Check the raw string first.
  • "2001" then "200" for created → 201.
  • "User added successfully" first written in the 400 branch, then swapped to "Some fields are missing".

doGet: one handler for read-one and read-all

@Override
protected void doGet(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response) throws IOException {
    String idParam = request.getParameter("id");

    if (idParam == null) {                                         // GET /users → all users
        List<User> users = userService.getAllUsers();
        response.setStatus(200);
        response.setContentType("application/json");
        response.getWriter().write(usersToJson(users));
        return;                                                    // forgotten at first → 500
    }
    User userResp = userService.getUserById(Integer.parseInt(idParam));
    if (userResp == null) {
        response.setStatus(404);                                   // no body needed
        return;
    }
    response.setStatus(200);
    response.setContentType("application/json");
    response.getWriter().write(userToJson(userResp));
}

Manual JSON needs two helpers:

private String userToJson(User user) {
    return "{\"id\": " + user.getId()
         + ", \"name\": \"" + user.getName() + "\""
         + ", \"email\": \"" + user.getEmail() + "\""
         + ", \"mobile\": \"" + user.getMobile() + "\"}";
}

private String usersToJson(List<User> users) {
    StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder("[");
    for (int i = 0; i < users.size(); i++) {           // index loop, so we know the last element
        sb.append(userToJson(users.get(i)));
        if (i < users.size() - 1) sb.append(",");       // no trailing comma, or the JSON is invalid
    }
    return sb.append("]").toString();
}
  • JSON array [ {…}, {…} ]: reuse userToJson, commas between items, none after last (invalid JSON).
  • For-each can't tell the last item → index loop.
  • Boot: just return user;, Jackson does the rest.

UserService

public class UserService {
    private Map<Integer, User> userDb;

    public UserService() {
        userDb = new HashMap<>();                 // initialise fields in the constructor
    }

    public User createUser(User userRequest) {
        userDb.put(userRequest.getId(), userRequest);
        return userRequest;
    }

    public User getUserById(Integer id) {
        return userDb.getOrDefault(id, null);
    }

    public List<User> getAllUsers() {
        List<User> usersResponse = new ArrayList<>();
        for (User user : userDb.values()) {
            usersResponse.add(user);
        }
        return usersResponse;                      // or: new ArrayList<>(userDb.values())
    }
}
  • First try return (List<User>) userDb.values(); → ClassCastException: values() is a Collection view, not a List; must copy.
  • UserServlet builds UserService with new — no @Autowired, tightly coupled. "This is exactly why we moved to the Spring Framework."

Deploying and testing

  1. Maven → Lifecycle → package → target/crud-app.war.
  2. Copy into Tomcat webapps/; Tomcat unpacks to webapps/crud-app/ (META-INF/, WEB-INF/classes/in/strikes/… with compiled model, service, servlet).
  3. Restart Tomcat (bin/catalina.sh run). Unlike Boot, you can't run main() in the IDE — no embedded server.
  4. POST http://localhost:8080/users?… → 404. URL needs the WAR name: http://localhost:8080/crud-app/users?… → "User added successfully". Rohit (id 2) likewise.
  5. GET /crud-app/users?id=1 → Aditya JSON (200); ?id=2 → Rohit.
  6. GET /crud-app/users → ClassCastException, fixed as above. Each fix = repackage, replace WAR, restart; in-memory map → every restart wiped users, re-POST needed.
  7. Then 500 Internal Server Error: read-all branch lacked return, fell through to Integer.parseInt(null). Added return, redeployed → 200 with full list.

Verified (added), embedded Tomcat 10.1: POST all fields 201; missing fields 400; GET ?id=1 200, ?id=5 404; GET /crud-app/users 200 JSON array; PUT without doPut 405 Method Not Allowed; /users without context path 404.

Why the URL needs /crud-app

  • External Tomcat hosts many apps (each WAR in webapps/); each gets a context path (URL prefix) from the WAR name — hence short finalName crud-app.
  • App → many servlets, each own path; servlet → many doXxx, one endpoint.
  • /crud-app/users → app crud-app → UserServlet → handler for the HTTP method.

Why scope is provided

  • jakarta.servlet-api supplies HttpServlet, HttpServletRequest, HttpServletResponse… — needed to compile (package runs compile first).
  • Tomcat already ships them (it creates/calls servlets), so the WAR shouldn't bundle them.
  • provided = on compile classpath, excluded from package, runtime provides it. A second copy invites class-loading conflicts (Tomcat's copy vs yours).

Servlet lifecycle

Tomcat manages creation/destruction like IoC does for beans, minus DI. Three callbacks:

MethodWhenSpring analogueUse for
init()Once, right after constructor@PostConstructLoading expensive resources, warming caches
service(req, res)Every request—Dispatching to doGet/doPost…
destroy()Once, on shutdown/undeploy@PreDestroyClosing resources, flushing caches
  • Lazy by default: created on first request to its URL, not startup (configurable, below).
  • service() normally not overridden: HttpServlet.service() checks the method and calls doGet, doPost, doPut, doDelete, doOptions… (+ doPatch from 6.1). Our doGet wins via polymorphism.

Demo run: HelloServlet

@WebServlet("/hello")
public class HelloServlet extends HttpServlet {
    public HelloServlet()    { System.out.println("HelloServlet constructor called"); }
    @Override public void init()    { System.out.println("init method called"); }
    @Override protected void doGet(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response) throws IOException {
        response.setStatus(200);
        response.setContentType("text/plain");        // plain text, not JSON
        response.getWriter().write("Hello");
    }
    @Override public void destroy() { System.out.println("destroy method called"); }
}
  1. Repackage, replace WAR, restart Tomcat.
  2. Prints appear in Tomcat's terminal, not IntelliJ (code runs in Tomcat).
  3. First GET /crud-app/hello → 200 "Hello"; log: "HelloServlet constructor called", "init method called".
  4. Second terminal: bin/shutdown.sh (Windows bin\shutdown.bat) → "destroy method called" before stop.

Verified (added): Tomcat 10.1, two consecutive GET /hello ran on different threads (http-nio-exec-1, exec-2), same instance; constructor + init once, before first request.

From many servlets to one front controller

Growing apps repeat chores per servlet (input, responses, validation, logging, routing). Put one central servlet in front:

  1. Tomcat sends every request to it.
  2. It does common work: logging, validation, reading request, writing response.
  3. Forwards to the right lightweight handler.
The key link: Spring MVC uses servlets: its central servlet is DispatcherServlet; per-feature servlets become controllers (beans). Boot doesn't build the web layer; it boots Spring MVC with auto-config + embedded Tomcat. Next lecture: DispatcherServlet in depth.

Homework

  • Implement doPut (update map entry from query params) and doDelete (remove) to finish CRUD.
  • Extract repeated status / content-type / writer code into a helper.

Interview gaps

  • Servlets are singletons shared across threads. One instance per servlet, called concurrently from a thread pool (default maxThreads 200). The field HashMap is not thread-safe — concurrent POSTs can corrupt it. Use ConcurrentHashMap; keep request data in locals, not fields. Same rule for Spring singleton controllers.
  • Hand-built JSON is unsafe. " or \ in a name → invalid JSON, nothing escaped. Use Jackson ObjectMapper: mapper.readValue(request.getReader(), User.class), mapper.writeValue(response.getWriter(), user).
  • Create data belongs in the body. Query params leak into server logs, browser history, proxies; have length limits. Demo-only.
  • Character encoding. response.setCharacterEncoding("UTF-8") before getWriter(), else non-ASCII may garble.
  • Unchecked parse. ?id=abc → Integer.parseInt throws → 500. Catch NumberFormatException, return 400.
  • Slow redeploy cycle. Package/copy/restart per change. autoDeploy (on by default) hot-deploys WARs dropped in webapps/; IDE integrations automate the rest; embedded servers avoid it.

Corrections & worth knowing

  • Core Java does have an HTTP server: com.sun.net.httpserver.HttpServer since Java 6 (parses method, headers, body); JDK 18 added jwebserver. Fine for tiny tools; no servlet model, routing framework or production features. Point about raw java.net sockets stands.
  • HttpServletRequest / HttpServletResponse are interfaces, implemented by Tomcat. Lecture's getHostName/getEndpoint were illustrative; real: getServerName(), getRequestURI(), getHeader(), getParameter(), getReader().
  • HttpServlet is abstract with no abstract methods — nothing forced. Skipped doXxx → 405 Method Not Allowed (verified with PUT).
  • @WebServlet("/users") is exact match: /crud-app/users/1 → 404 (verified). For path segments map "/users/*" + request.getPathInfo() — why ids used query params.
  • Eager loading: @WebServlet(urlPatterns = "/users", loadOnStartup = 1) or <load-on-startup> in web.xml → create + init at deploy, no slow first request.
  • Lifecycle signatures: container calls init(ServletConfig), which stores config then calls the no-arg init() the lecture overrode. Real dispatcher: service(HttpServletRequest, HttpServletResponse), reached from generic service(ServletRequest, ServletResponse).
  • Filters handle repeated per-request chores: jakarta.servlet.Filter wraps servlets (logging, auth, CORS, encoding). Spring Security = filter chain in front of DispatcherServlet.
  • Tomcat is a servlet container, not a full app server. Internals: Catalina (servlet container), Coyote (HTTP connector), Jasper (JSP engine). Full Jakarta EE servers (WildFly, GlassFish) add EJB, JMS… Jetty, Undertow = alternative containers; Boot can embed any.
  • 404 is a client error (4xx, not found). "It's not an error, it's not-found" = not a server fault.
  • ROOT.war deploys at empty context path (no /crud-app). Boot's embedded Tomcat uses root context by default (server.servlet.context-path changes it).
Transcript slips to ignore:
"port 880" → 8080.
"We extend HttpServlet so its methods must be overridden" → optional.
"init is like pre-construct / destroy is like pre-construct" → init ≈ @PostConstruct, destroy ≈ @PreDestroy.
"HTTP request class" → HttpServletRequest (interface).
"user service" said for "user servlet" in places.
"BS catalina.run" → bin/catalina.sh run.
"web.jar" → webapps folder.

Lecture 15: Spring MVC Internals — DispatcherServlet, HandlerMapping, a Boot-less MVC App & JSP Views

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #15 · 1h15m · Code Army · the last "internals" lecture before the series moves fully to Spring Boot

Why this lecture closes the internals

Boot web apps sit on Spring MVC, which sits on servlets and Tomcat (Lecture 14). With MVC's architecture clear, "Spring Boot's entire internal architecture is done": Boot = auto-configuration layer on top of Spring MVC.

What Spring MVC is, and what Boot adds

  • Spring MVC = Spring's actual web framework (module Spring Web MVC, artifact spring-webmvc). Builds:
    • REST APIs returning JSON (series focus).
    • Server-rendered web apps returning HTML/JSP views (end of lecture).
  • Web annotations belong to Spring MVC, not Boot: @Controller, @RestController, @RequestMapping, @GetMapping, @PostMapping, @RequestBody…
  • Boot makes MVC easy. Lectures 11–13: only business logic (controller → service → repository), zero config. Plain MVC: start Tomcat, declare DispatcherServlet, pick dependencies yourself — done here to show what Boot hides.

The problem: who connects Tomcat to my controller?

Known: controller → service → repository → DB; @GetMapping("/hello") on HelloController.hello() handles GET /hello. But:

  • Tomcat, not the controller, listens on 8080.
  • Tomcat talks only to servlets; HelloController isn't one.
  • So who maps GET /hello to HelloController.hello()?

Step 1: the plain-servlet world, and its repetition

Recap (Lecture 14): Tomcat maps URLs to many servlets (UserServlet, OrderServlet, PaymentServlet); for /users it parses into HttpServletRequest, creates an empty HttpServletResponse, passes both to UserServlet, which fills the response. Every servlet repeats non-business chores: checking params, JSON ↔ Java conversion, building status/headers/body, exception handling.

Step 2: Spring's idea, one front servlet

Tomcat stays (it listens), but hands everything to a single servlet, the DispatcherServlet: does common chores once, routes to plain controller beans (UserController, OrderController, PaymentController). We write controllers, not servlets.

Why @RequestBody "just works": Postman sends {"id": 1, "name": "Aditya"}, controller declares @RequestBody User user → arrives filled. JSON-to-Java conversion happened before your method, in the dispatcher's machinery.

Inside the dispatcher: HandlerMapping

DispatcherServlet is only the entry point, delegating to internal components. First: HandlerMapping, a mapping table request → controller method:

What HandlerMapping conceptually holds
HTTP method + pathHandler (controller method)
GET /studentsStudentController.getAllStudents()
POST /studentsStudentController.createStudent()

How the table is built

  1. IoC creates all beans at startup: controllers (@Controller/@RestController meta-annotated with @Component), services, repositories.
  2. HandlerMapping scans controller beans for @GetMapping, @PostMapping, @PutMapping, @DeleteMapping, @RequestMapping…
  3. Builds the table.
  4. Per request, DispatcherServlet looks up the table and invokes the method.

IoC creates controllers; dispatcher calls them. Actual invocation goes via a HandlerAdapter, but the two names to remember: DispatcherServlet, HandlerMapping.

Where each piece of client data gets bound

DataExampleLecture's explanationMore precisely (added)
