Request body (JSON){"id":1,"name":"Aditya"}DispatcherServlet uses Jackson to map JSON keys to fieldsJackson HttpMessageConverter, invoked by the @RequestBody argument resolver; same converters write return values as JSON
Path variable/users/1HandlerMapping matches /users/{id} via a "handler method argument resolver"Mapping extracts URI template vars; HandlerMethodArgumentResolver (PathVariableMethodArgumentResolver) binds id, converts to Long
Query parameter/users?id=1Tomcat already parses into HttpServletRequestTomcat parses; RequestParamMethodArgumentResolver reads getParameter(), converts types for @RequestParam

Return path mirrors it: repository → service → controller → dispatcher, which writes the object as JSON into HttpServletResponse; Tomcat sends it. Servlet methods are void — response filled, not returned (Lecture 14).

The complete request flow

Worth adding: the full cast, for interviews:
  • HandlerMapping (RequestMappingHandlerMapping) finds the handler → HandlerExecutionChain (handler + HandlerInterceptors).
  • HandlerAdapter (RequestMappingHandlerAdapter) invokes it via HandlerMethodArgumentResolvers (params) and HandlerMethodReturnValueHandlers (results).
  • HttpMessageConverters: body ↔ objects (JSON via Jackson).
  • HandlerExceptionResolver: exceptions → responses; powers @ExceptionHandler, @ControllerAdvice.
  • ViewResolver: view name → template, for non-REST pages.

Building a Spring MVC app without Spring Boot

Tomcat now embedded (library, started from main()), no WAR deploy. Plain Maven project spring-mvc-demo; three deps Boot's starter would give:

DependencyWhyVersion used
org.springframework:spring-webmvcSpring MVC: DispatcherServlet, web annotations7.0.7
org.apache.tomcat.embed:tomcat-embed-coreEmbedded Tomcat (not tomcat-embed-jasper = JSP)11.0.22
com.fasterxml.jackson.core:jackson-databindJSON ↔ Java2.x (fewest listed vulnerabilities)
Version pairing:
  • Spring 7 targets Jakarta EE 11 / Servlet 6.1 → Tomcat 11. Spring 6 ↔ Tomcat 10.1.
  • Spring 7's primary JSON is Jackson 3 (tools.jackson.core:jackson-databind); Jackson 2 (com.fasterxml…) supported but deprecated. Verified Jackson 2.22 works with spring-webmvc 7.0.5.
  • Boot's starter picks compatible versions for you.

Application layers: same as Boot

  • entity/Student: POJO (Long id, String name, String email, getters/setters); no @Entity, no JPA.
  • repository/StudentRepository (@Repository): HashMap<Long, Student> studentDb, built in constructor; JPA-style names: save, findById (studentDb.get(id), null if absent), findAll (new ArrayList<>(studentDb.values())).
  • service/StudentService (@Service): constructor-injects repository; createStudent, getStudent, getAllStudents delegate.
  • controller/StudentController:
@RestController
@RequestMapping("/students")
public class StudentController {
    private final StudentService studentService;

    public StudentController(StudentService studentService) { this.studentService = studentService; }

    @PostMapping                                             // POST /students
    public ResponseEntity<Student> createStudent(@RequestBody Student studentRequest) {
        return ResponseEntity.ok(studentService.createStudent(studentRequest));
    }

    @GetMapping("/{id}")                                     // GET /students/1
    public ResponseEntity<Student> getStudent(@PathVariable("id") Long id) {
        Student studentResponse = studentService.getStudent(id);
        if (studentResponse == null) return ResponseEntity.notFound().build();
        return ResponseEntity.ok(studentResponse);
    }

    @GetMapping                                              // GET /students
    public ResponseEntity<List<Student>> getAllStudents() {
        return ResponseEntity.ok(studentService.getAllStudents());   // empty list is fine: 200 []
    }
}

Identical to Boot so far. Boot would now run main(); MVC needs two more pieces.

Missing piece 1: a configuration class

@Configuration
@ComponentScan(basePackages = "in.strikes")     // default would scan only in.strikes.config
@EnableWebMvc
public class WebConfig {
}
  • @Configuration + @ComponentScan = Spring Core (Lecture 5). Without basePackages, scan starts at in.strikes.config and misses controllers.
  • @EnableWebMvc switches on MVC machinery; without it @RequestBody, @PostMapping… aren't active.
Precision note (added): @EnableWebMvc imports DelegatingWebMvcConfiguration, registering infrastructure beans: RequestMappingHandlerMapping, RequestMappingHandlerAdapter, message converters (Jackson if on classpath), validators… Don't add @EnableWebMvc in Boot — signals full control; Boot's MVC auto-config backs off (static resources, converter customisation…). Customise via WebMvcConfigurer instead.

Missing piece 2: bootstrap in main()

public static void main(String[] args) throws LifecycleException {
    // 1. embedded Tomcat on port 8080
    Tomcat tomcat = new Tomcat();
    tomcat.setPort(8080);
    tomcat.getConnector();                               // creates the connector that listens on the port

    // 2. a web application ("context") inside Tomcat
    String contextPath = "";                             // root: no /crud-app prefix
    String baseDoc = new File("src/main/webapp").getAbsolutePath();   // folder must exist
    Context context = tomcat.addContext(contextPath, baseDoc);

    // 3. the Spring IoC container: a *web* application context
    AnnotationConfigWebApplicationContext springContext = new AnnotationConfigWebApplicationContext();
    springContext.register(WebConfig.class);

    // 4. the front controller, given the container so it can find controller beans
    DispatcherServlet dispatcherServlet = new DispatcherServlet(springContext);
    Tomcat.addServlet(context, "dispatcherServlet", dispatcherServlet);   // static method
    context.addServletMappingDecoded("/", "dispatcherServlet");            // every request → dispatcher

    // 5. start and keep running
    tomcat.start();
    System.out.println("Tomcat started on port 8080");
    tomcat.getServer().await();                           // block main thread, keep server up
}
  • Connector: getConnector() initialises the component listening on 8080.
  • Context = one web app. External Tomcat holds many apps in webapps/, each with a context path (/crud-app, Lecture 14). Either way Tomcat needs:
    • A context path: empty here → no URL prefix.
    • A physical base folder: empty src/main/webapp, absolute path.
  • Web application context: first try AnnotationConfigApplicationContext → new DispatcherServlet(…) refused; needs a WebApplicationContext: AnnotationConfigWebApplicationContext. No config-class constructor → register(WebConfig.class). "The IoC container is the same; only the implementation class differs" (annotation, XML, web).
  • Why dispatcher gets the container: HandlerMapping must scan controller beans for mapping annotations.
  • Register + map: Tomcat.addServlet(context, name, servlet); mapping "/" sends every request to it.
  • Start + wait: start() throws LifecycleException; getServer().await() keeps process alive. "Tomcat started" printed by hand (Boot logs it).

Demo run and bugs hit

  1. First run failed at the base-doc line (path built via System.getProperty(…)); new File("src/main/webapp").getAbsolutePath() fixed it → "Tomcat started on port 8080".
  2. POST localhost:8080/students with Aditya's JSON → 200; no /api, no app prefix (empty context path). Rohit added too.
  3. GET /students/1 failed; fix: @PathVariable("id") Long id. After restart + re-post (in-memory), /students/1, /2 and GET /students worked.
Correction: why @PathVariable Long id failed (verified on Spring 7.0.5):
  • Lecture's reason (Spring can't match 1 to Long since longs are 1L) is wrong. Real error:
    IllegalArgumentException: Name for argument of type [java.lang.Long] not specified, and parameter name information not available via reflection. Ensure that the compiler uses the '-parameters' flag.
  • Plain Maven doesn't compile with -parameters → Spring can't learn the name id; since Spring 6.1 no debug-info fallback.
  • spring-boot-starter-parent enables -parameters → bare form worked in the Boot CRUD app. Same issue as XML constructor-arg name (Lecture 8).
  • Fixes: @PathVariable("id"), or <maven.compiler.parameters>true</maven.compiler.parameters>.

Boot replaces the whole bootstrap with spring-boot-starter-web (-webmvc in Boot 4) + @SpringBootApplication: Tomcat self-starts on 8080; no WebConfig base packages, no main() wiring.

@Controller + @ResponseBody = @RestController

Plain @Controller + @ResponseBody per handler works identically. @RestController is meta-annotated with both, applying @ResponseBody to every method.

Why @ResponseBody exists: MVC predates REST. By default a @Controller String return = view name (page to render); @ResponseBody = write the return value into the HTTP body (JSON).

Precision note (added, verified):
  • @Controller returning ResponseEntity needs no @ResponseBody — handled as body by HttpEntityMethodProcessor. Result: 200 with JSON.
  • A plain object from @Controller without @ResponseBody → view lookup → 404 (no view).
  • Lecture's extra annotations harmless but unnecessary for ResponseEntity methods.

MVC = Model, View, Controller

LetterMeaningIn Spring MVC
ModelData from controller to viewModel object: model.addAttribute("message", …)
ViewPage shown to userHTML/JSP (or Thymeleaf) template
ControllerHandles HTTP requests@Controller classes with mapping methods

Views today are usually a separate frontend (React, Vue, plain JS); MVC mostly serves REST, name stayed. JSP mainly in legacy code, good to know.

Worked example: a JSP page with Spring MVC

Separate project jsp-demo. Dependencies:

spring-webmvc, tomcat-embed-core as before; no Jackson (no JSON); tomcat-embed-jasper (11.0.22) = Tomcat's JSP engine.

The controller returns view names

@Controller                                       // NOT @RestController: we return views
public class HelloController {

    @GetMapping("/")                              // home page
    public String showHomePage(Model model) {
        model.addAttribute("message", "Enter your name");
        return "home";                            // a VIEW NAME, not text: resolves to home.jsp
    }

    @PostMapping("/greet")                        // the form posts here
    public String greetUser(@RequestParam String name, Model model) {
        model.addAttribute("message", "Hello " + name);
        return "home";                            // same page, new message
    }
}

The view: src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/views/home.jsp

  • webapp/ holds static content + views, mirroring an external Tomcat app (META-INF, WEB-INF). First named hello.jsp, renamed home.jsp to match the view name.
  • HTML + Java expression language: <p>${message}</p> prints the model attribute.
  • <form action="/greet" method="post"> with <input type="text" name="name"> + submit.
  • Styles in webapp/assets/style.css.

Extra configuration for views

@Configuration
@ComponentScan(basePackages = "in.strikes")
@EnableWebMvc
public class WebConfig implements WebMvcConfigurer {

    @Override
    public void addResourceHandlers(ResourceHandlerRegistry registry) {      // serve CSS
        registry.addResourceHandler("/assets/**").addResourceLocations("/assets/");
    }

    @Bean
    public ViewResolver viewResolver() {
        InternalResourceViewResolver resolver = new InternalResourceViewResolver();
        resolver.setPrefix("/WEB-INF/views/");      // where views live
        resolver.setSuffix(".jsp");                 // "home" → /WEB-INF/views/home.jsp
        return resolver;
    }
}
  • ViewResolver: name → file (prefix + name + suffix). Not auto-configured without Boot → declared as bean.
  • Resource handler: else CSS doesn't load — dispatcher on "/" gets /assets/style.css; Spring must be told it's a static file.

main() changes vs REST demo:

  • tomcat.addWebapp(…) instead of addContext — applies Tomcat's default web setup incl. its JSP servlet.
  • context.addServletContainerInitializer(new JasperInitializer(), null) initialises the JSP engine.
  • One more line (lecture notes): since dispatcher on "/" would grab everything, .jsp requests go to the JSP servlet.

Demo run

  1. localhost:8080/: GET / → showHomePage; resolver turns "home" into home.jsp; shows "Enter your name" + form.
  2. Submit "Aditya": browser POSTs /greet with name=Aditya, @RequestParam String name receives it → "Hello Aditya". "Rohit" → "Hello Rohit".

Series milestone

Spring Core, servlets and MVC internals done — "congratulations", the foundation under Boot is understood. From now on everything is built in Spring Boot (no one wants this boilerplate each time); goal: fast business logic with Boot features.

Spring MVC vs Spring Boot

Plain Spring MVCSpring Boot (web)
What it isThe web framework itselfAuto-config + starters + embedded server on top of MVC
DependenciesPick spring-webmvc, Tomcat, Jackson + compatible versions yourselfOne starter, managed versions
ServerStart Tomcat in code, or deploy WAR externallyEmbedded Tomcat starts automatically
DispatcherServletCreate, register, map by handAuto-configured, mapped to /
IoC containerCreate AnnotationConfigWebApplicationContext, register configSpringApplication.run creates it
Component scan@ComponentScan(basePackages = …)Implied by @SpringBootApplication
View resolver / static files / JSONConfigure manuallyDefaults (/static, Jackson, Thymeleaf if present)
-parametersOff unless configuredOn via starter parent

Corrections & worth knowing

  • JSP = JavaServer Pages, not "Java Servlet Pages". JSPs are compiled into servlets by Jasper (likely the mix-up). JSP has limits with Boot executable JARs; Thymeleaf is today's usual template engine.
  • Form fields aren't query params. method="post" form sends name=Aditya in the body (application/x-www-form-urlencoded). @RequestParam still reads it: getParameter() merges query-string and form-body params.
  • Views under WEB-INF: container never serves WEB-INF files directly → home.jsp reachable only via the controller.
  • DispatcherServlet doesn't convert itself. JSON conversion, argument binding, exception handling are delegated to HttpMessageConverters, HandlerMethodArgumentResolvers, HandlerExceptionResolvers, orchestrated via HandlerAdapter. Dispatcher coordinates. These names answer "how does @RequestBody work?" precisely.
  • Interceptors vs filters. Servlet Filter runs in Tomcat before DispatcherServlet; Spring HandlerInterceptor runs inside it around the controller, with access to the chosen handler. Auth/logging can live in either; Spring Security uses filters.
  • Where Boot does it: DispatcherServletAutoConfiguration creates/registers the dispatcher; ServletWebServerFactoryAutoConfiguration starts embedded Tomcat; WebMvcAutoConfiguration supplies what WebConfig did (static resources from classpath:/static, message converters, view resolvers). Package names moved in Boot 4's modularisation.
  • Context hierarchy (legacy): classic web.xml apps often had a root WebApplicationContext (services, repositories) + a child per DispatcherServlet (controllers). Boot and this demo: single context.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"port 880" → 8080.
"Handler Mapping Resolver" / "Handler Method Resolver" → HandlerMethodArgumentResolver.
"@SpringBootApplication uses @EnableAutoConfiguration" is correct; "@SpringBoot annotation" → @SpringBootApplication.
"WebMvcConfigurer" is an interface you implement, as the lecture does.
JSP file called both hello.jsp and home.jsp; final is home.jsp.

Lecture 16: DTOs (Data Transfer Objects) & Request Validation

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #16 · 1h04m · Code Army · request/response DTOs, entity↔DTO mapping, Bean Validation with @Valid

Recap: the entity does everything

Client JSON (POST /api/students, {"id": 1, "name": "Aditya", …}) → controller maps it (Jackson, DispatcherServlet machinery, Lecture 15) straight into the Student entity → service → repository → row (class = table, fields = columns) → saved entity serialised back to JSON.

One class, four jobs: request model; object passed between layers; DB mapping; response model. Its only real job should be DB mapping. Fix: DTOs, Data Transfer Objects.

Setup

Fresh Initializr project (crud-dto-demo: Boot 4.1.0, Java 21, Spring Web, Spring Data JPA, MySQL Driver) rebuilds the layers once: controller/service/repository/entity packages; Student with @Id @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY); StudentRepository extends JpaRepository<Student, Long>; create endpoint @RequestBody Student → ResponseEntity<Student>.

Real work on the Lecture 13 CRUD project (renamed dto-demo): entity id, name, age, email, rollNumber, subject, deleted; endpoints create, get, getAll, update, delete, soft-delete. POST returns 201 with the entity.

The problem: why the entity everywhere breaks

ProblemWhat happens
Client can set internal fieldsRequest maps onto the whole entity → client can send "deleted": true or createdAt/updatedAt. Each needs a manual service override (setDeleted(false)…). Forget one and the client writes it to the DB.
Internal fields leak outResponse = whole entity → client sees deleted. Why should a React frontend know how soft delete works? Tomorrow: a password hash or other sensitive data.
DB schema = API contractSplitting name into firstName/lastName changes the JSON → every client breaks; must be told the API contract changed.
Validation piles into the serviceAll defensive checks land in business code.
The key idea: two worlds: internet clients = external world; past the controller = internal world. The entity is internal (what's stored). External world talks to separate classes, so both change independently.

The fix: DTOs

  • DTO = plain POJO (fields + getters/setters) with only the fields the client should send or receive.
  • Essentially a subset (sometimes superset) of the entity, shaped for one conversation.
  • Requests and responses differ (e.g. response adds a message) → separate request DTOs and response DTOs.

Worked example: create with DTOs

1. Request DTO: what the client may send

package in.strikes.demo.dto;

public class CreateStudentRequestDto {       // no id (DB generates it), no deleted, no timestamps
    private String name;
    private Integer age;
    private String email;
    private Integer rollNumber;
    private String subject;
    // getters and setters
}

Controller: createStudent(@RequestBody CreateStudentRequestDto studentRequestDto). Client can't set deleted — the field doesn't exist. API contract = exactly these five keys.

2. Who converts DTO ↔ entity?

  • Options: controller, service, repository. Lecture: service (business logic home), as private helpers.
  • As the project grows, move into dedicated mapper classes (mapper package) called by the service.

3. Response DTO: what the client gets back

public class CreateStudentResponseDto {
    private Long id;                // so a frontend can fetch the record later
    private String name;
    private Integer age;
    private String email;
    private Integer rollNumber;
    private String subject;
    private String message;         // extra field the entity doesn't have
    private LocalDateTime createdAt;
    private LocalDateTime updatedAt;
    // getters and setters
}

message just shows responses can carry extra data; a proper response envelope comes with exception handling later.

4. Service: map in, save, map out

public CreateStudentResponseDto createStudent(CreateStudentRequestDto studentRequestDto) {
    Student student = mapToEntity(studentRequestDto);
    student.setCreatedAt(LocalDateTime.now());
    student.setUpdatedAt(LocalDateTime.now());
    Student studentResponse = studentRepository.save(student);
    return mapToDto(studentResponse);
}

private Student mapToEntity(CreateStudentRequestDto dto) {
    Student student = new Student();
    student.setName(dto.getName());
    student.setAge(dto.getAge());
    student.setEmail(dto.getEmail());
    student.setRollNumber(dto.getRollNumber());
    student.setSubject(dto.getSubject());
    student.setDeleted(false);                 // server-owned field set here, not by the client
    return student;
}

private CreateStudentResponseDto mapToDto(Student student) {
    CreateStudentResponseDto responseDto = new CreateStudentResponseDto();
    responseDto.setId(student.getId());
    responseDto.setName(student.getName());
    responseDto.setAge(student.getAge());
    responseDto.setEmail(student.getEmail());
    responseDto.setRollNumber(student.getRollNumber());
    responseDto.setSubject(student.getSubject());
    responseDto.setCreatedAt(student.getCreatedAt());
    responseDto.setUpdatedAt(student.getUpdatedAt());
    responseDto.setMessage("Student saved successfully");
    return responseDto;                         // no "deleted" → never leaks
}
  • Controller returns ResponseEntity<CreateStudentResponseDto>.
  • Field-by-field setting risks omissions; Builder pattern will replace it later (Builder in the LLD notebook, Lecture 28).

Demo: POST Saurav (27, roll 108, React) → exactly id, name, age, email, rollNumber, subject, message; no deleted. "Now I'm in control of what the client sends and receives."

5. Adding createdAt / updatedAt: decoupling pays off

  • Entity gets private LocalDateTime createdAt and updatedAt (Java date-time type, not an epoch string).
  • Service sets both to LocalDateTime.now() on create; update refreshes only updatedAt.
  • Response DTO exposes them; request DTO doesn't accept them.
  • Client's request unchanged. Entity/table changed, external contract didn't — worlds decoupled.

After recreating the table, log shows created_at, updated_at datetime columns; POST response includes both; DBeaver row matches.

One DTO per operation: update

Classes renamed per operation: CreateStudentRequestDto, CreateStudentResponseDto (IntelliJ Refactor → Rename updates all usages). Accidental camelCase class names fixed to PascalCase.

Business rule: email can't change after creation (common). So the update DTO has no email field:

public class UpdateStudentRequestDto {     // name, age, rollNumber, subject; no email, no id
    private String name;
    private Integer age;
    private Integer rollNumber;
    private String subject;
    // getters and setters
}

public class UpdateStudentResponseDto {    // like the create response, but only updatedAt
    private Long id;
    private String name;
    private Integer age;
    private String email;
    private Integer rollNumber;
    private String subject;
    private String message;
    private LocalDateTime updatedAt;
    // getters and setters
}

In updateStudent:

  1. Load via findByIdAndDeletedIsFalse; null (404) if missing.
  2. Copy name, age, rollNumber, subject. Old setEmail(…) no longer compiles (no getEmail() on DTO) — type system enforces the rule.
  3. updatedAt = LocalDateTime.now(), save, mapToUpdateDto(savedStudent) (message "Student updated successfully").
Why separate create/update response DTOs even if alike: one StudentResponseDto would work, but separate ones let the update response gain/lose fields without touching anything else. Rule: what each endpoint returns is a business decision. Update response omits createdAt purely to show that freedom.

Demo: PUT /api/students/update?id=1 (request param, Lecture 13) with name "Saurabh", subject "MERN" → update DTO with fresh updatedAt; DB row changes.

Read endpoints reuse a response DTO

  • getStudent returns mapToDto(student) (CreateStudentResponseDto), not the entity.
  • getAllStudents maps via streams:
return studentList.stream()
        .map(this::mapToDto)
        .toList();                     // Java 16+; a for-loop works too

Delete and soft delete neither take nor return an entity → unchanged. GET /api/students/get?id=1 returns the DTO.

Validation: stop bad input at the door

DTOs limit which fields arrive, not what's in them. Client can send: blank/spaces-only name; email without @ or domain; age 4 ("how is a four-year-old using our app?"); missing roll number.

Checks could go in the service or a validator it calls, but these are malformed requests, not business rules. The service is "special" (business logic only) → reject bad input at the controller, before the service.

Dependency: spring-boot-starter-validation

  • Initializr: "Validation". Existing project: add to pom (lecture copied from mvnrepository).
  • Omit <version>: spring-boot-starter-parent manages compatible starter versions (Lecture 9) — no mixing a 4.0.6 starter into a 4.1.0 project.
  • No need to memorise starter names: Initializr adds them; pom shows real names (spring-boot-starter-data-jpa, spring-boot-starter-webmvc…).

Annotate the request DTO

public class CreateStudentRequestDto {

    @NotBlank(message = "Name cannot be null, empty or blank")
    @Size(min = 2, max = 50, message = "Student name must be within 2 to 50 characters long")
    private String name;

    @NotNull(message = "Age is required")
    @Min(value = 18, message = "Student must be at least 18 years old")
    private Integer age;                       // Integer, not int: see below

    @NotBlank(message = "Student email cannot be blank")
    @Email(message = "Student email must be valid")
    private String email;

    @NotNull(message = "Roll number is required")
    private Integer rollNumber;

    @NotBlank(message = "Subject is required")
    private String subject;
    // getters and setters
}

Trigger it with @Valid

@PostMapping("/create")
public ResponseEntity<CreateStudentResponseDto> createStudent(
        @Valid @RequestBody CreateStudentRequestDto studentRequestDto) { … }

Without @Valid the annotations do nothing — it tells Spring to run the constraints on this argument.

Common constraints (package jakarta.validation.constraints)
AnnotationRejectsTypical use
@NotNullnull onlyAny type: Integer, objects
@NotEmptynull, "" (and empty collections)Strings, lists; " " passes
@NotBlanknull, "", whitespace-onlyUsual choice for strings
@Size(min, max)Length/size out of rangeName length, list size
@Min / @MaxNumbers below/above a valueAge ≥ 18
@EmailMalformed emailEmail fields
@PositiveZero and negativesAmounts, counts
@Past / @FutureDates on wrong side of nowBirth date, expiry date
@Pattern(regexp) (added)Strings not matching regexPhone numbers, codes
Why Integer, not int: primitive int is never null — missing field silently = 0, so @NotNull is meaningless. Integer → missing = null, caught. Also @Min, @Email, @Size and most others treat null as valid → pair each with @NotNull/@NotBlank.

Demo run: validation fires, messages don't show

  1. POST /create with blank name + email → 400 Bad Request, Boot's default body: timestamp, status, error: "Bad Request", path. Service never called.
  2. Custom messages + restart → same generic body: client learns something's wrong, not what.
  3. Valid data (name "Aditya", age 19, proper email) → succeeds.

Boot's default error response omits validation messages; showing them needs custom exception handling (next lecture).

Entity vs DTO

EntityDTO
PurposeMaps to a DB tableCarries data across the API boundary
Annotations@Entity, @Id, JPA mappingsValidation (@NotBlank…), JSON (@JsonProperty…)
ShapeEvery column, incl. internalOnly what one operation needs
Lives inService, repository (internal)Controller boundary (external contract)
Changes whenSchema changesAPI contract changes (versioned, communicated)
Managed byJPA persistence contextNobody: plain object

Interview gaps

  • Validate the update DTO too. Only create was validated; UpdateStudentRequestDto needs constraints + @Valid on update.
  • Unknown fields silently ignored. Sending "email"/"deleted" to update gives no error: Boot's Jackson has FAIL_ON_UNKNOWN_PROPERTIES off. Safe (discarded) but surprising; enable spring.jackson.deserialization.fail-on-unknown-properties=true for strict contracts.
  • Hand-written mapping doesn't scale. Dozens of setters = error-prone. Options: builder (Lombok @Builder), static factory (StudentResponseDto.from(student)), MapStruct (compile-time, fast) or ModelMapper (reflection).
  • Manual timestamps get forgotten. Use JPA @PrePersist/@PreUpdate, Hibernate @CreationTimestamp/@UpdateTimestamp, or Spring Data auditing (@CreatedDate/@LastModifiedDate + @EnableJpaAuditing). Prefer Instant/OffsetDateTime over LocalDateTime across time zones.
  • Structural vs business validation. Annotations cover shape (blank, range, format). DB-dependent rules ("email unique", "roll number not taken") stay in the service; only a DB unique constraint makes them race-safe.

Corrections & worth knowing

  • The attack: mass assignment (over-posting). Binding requests straight to entities lets clients set deleted, role, balance… Listed in OWASP API Security Top 10; DTOs are the standard defence.
  • Returning entities: JPA pitfalls. Serialising entities with relationships can cause LazyInitializationException, N+1 queries, or infinite recursion on bidirectional links. Response DTOs avoid all (matters once relationships appear).
  • Java records make great DTOs (16+): public record CreateStudentRequest(@NotBlank String name, @NotNull @Min(18) Integer age) {} — immutable, no getter boilerplate, supported by Jackson and Bean Validation.
  • What @Valid really does. From Jakarta Bean Validation (jakarta.validation), implemented by Hibernate Validator (via the starter). Failure → MethodArgumentNotValidException → 400. Nested objects need @Valid on the field. Validating @RequestParam/@PathVariable or groups involves Spring's @Validated.
  • Seeing messages without custom handling: spring.web.error.include-binding-errors=always (and include-message) exposes them — handy in dev. Boot 4 moved these from server.error.* to spring.web.error.*; old server.error.include-message has no effect (verified on Boot 4.1.0). Proper fix: @RestControllerAdvice (next lecture).
  • @Email is lenient: accepts a@b (no TLD). Add @Pattern for stricter; only a confirmation email proves it's real.
  • ddl-auto=update would have added the columns. Lecture says new timestamp fields "won't be added by default" and drops the table; actually update adds missing columns (nullable), but never changes existing ones (e.g. auto-increment, Lecture 13). Dropping only gave a clean start.
  • Dates in JSON: Boot's Jackson serialises LocalDateTime as ISO-8601 ("2026-09-28T01:23:45.123"), not a timestamp array. MySQL: datetime(6).
Transcript slips to ignore:
"2001 Created" → 201.
"student.setSubject(studentRepository.getSubject())" → studentRequestDto.getSubject().
"epochs value" → an epoch timestamp (seconds or milliseconds since 1970-01-01 UTC).

Lecture 17: Exception Handling in Spring Boot — Status Codes, @RestControllerAdvice, Custom Exceptions & Error DTOs

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #17 · 1h27m · Code Army · continues the DTO/validation CRUD project of Lecture 16

Why exception handling matters in an API

In a backend, when something fails the client must get a proper response saying what went wrong. Important for interviews and real apps.

PartWhat it carries
Status codeThe meaning of the response: success/failure, whose fault, what kind. Most important
HeadersKey–value metadata
BodyThe data, as JSON
Professional vs beginner APIs: a beginner API returns 200 for everything, even 200 with {"message": "No data found"} — body says failure, status says success. A professional API picks the matching status and returns error bodies in one consistent format.

One request, many outcomes

GET localhost:8080/api/students/1 can end many ways:

CaseWhose faultStatusExample body
1. Student exists—200 OK{"id": 1, "name": "Aditya", "email": "ad@gmail.com"}
2. Student doesn't existClient asked for missing resource404 Not Found{"message": "Student with id 1 not found"}
3. Invalid request (id not a number, path vs param mix-up, bad body)Client400 Bad Request{"message": "Student id must be a number"}
4. DB/server down, unclassifiable bugServer500 Internal Server Error{"message": "Something went wrong"}

Lecturer first wrote 400 for "not found", corrected to 404. 400 = Bad Request. 500 = generic "couldn't pin it down", the most common in the wild.

Step 1: clean up the endpoints (REST convention)

Lecture 13 used verbs in paths (/create, /get, /getAll, /update, /delete). Method + path is already unique, so they go:

BeforeAfter
POST /api/students/create@PostMapping → POST /api/students
GET /api/students/get?id=1@GetMapping("/{id}") + @PathVariable → GET /api/students/1
GET /api/students/getAll@GetMapping → GET /api/students
PUT /api/students/update?id=1@PutMapping → PUT /api/students/{id}
DELETE /api/students/delete?id=1@DeleteMapping → DELETE /api/students/{id}
PATCH /api/students/delete-softKept. PATCH = "change part of a resource" generally, not only soft delete

Demo: POST /api/students with Rohit (29, roll 103, rohit@gmail.com, Node.js) → 201, new row.

The problem: the controller is full of if/else

  • getStudent: if (response == null) return notFound(); else return ok(…).
  • getAllStudents checks isEmpty() — shouldn't; an empty list is still success.
  • updateStudent repeats the null check; delete/softDelete check a boolean.

The service returns null/false to signal problems. GET /api/students/100 → bare 404, empty body.

Why this is wrong: the controller's job is fixed: take the request, hand it to the service, return the result. Deciding responses for edge cases is exception handling; scattering it as if/else makes controllers bulky and error bodies inconsistent. Fix: split into a happy flow (controller) and an exception flow (dedicated handler class).

Step 2: what Spring Boot does if you handle nothing

Experiment: the service calls Optional.get() directly:

return mapToDto(studentRepository.findById(id).get());   // throws NoSuchElementException if absent

Uncaught in service and controller. GET /api/students/100 now returns:

HTTP 500 Internal Server Error
{
  "timestamp": "…",
  "status": 500,
  "error": "Internal Server Error",
  "path": "/api/students/100"
}

No raw exception: Boot's default error handling built a proper response. The real cause, NoSuchElementException: No value present, shows only in the IntelliJ console — hidden for security (reveals internals). To show it while debugging (local only, never prod), adding "message": "No value present":

spring.web.error.include-message=always
Version note (verified on Boot 4.1.0): Boot 4 uses spring.web.error.include-message (as in lecture). Boot 2.x/3.x's server.error.include-message has no effect in Boot 4 (tested both). Most tutorials still show the old name.

How the default works: the /error endpoint

Unhandled exceptions are re-routed to /error (hence the separate original path), which serves the per-client format: JSON for Postman, Whitelabel Error Page (HTML) for a browser ("This application has no explicit mapping for /error, so you are seeing this as a fallback", with type, status 500, timestamp, message).

"If Boot already handles exceptions, why write our own?"

The default is generic (nearly always 500, same four fields): can't say "student with id 100 does not exist" or list failed rules — Lecture 16's messages ("Name cannot be null, empty or blank") arrive as a generic 400.

HTTP status code families

FamilyMeaningCodes to know
1xxInformational100 Continue, 101 Switching Protocols (added)
2xxSuccess200 OK; 201 Created (new resource, usual POST result); 204 No Content
3xxRedirection301 Moved Permanently, 302 Found, 304 Not Modified (added)
4xxClient-side error400 Bad Request; 404 Not Found; 409 Conflict (e.g. email already registered); 401/403 (added, below)
5xxServer-side error500 Internal Server Error; 503 Service Unavailable (added)
204 vs 404: 204 No Content is success ("done, nothing to return") — the professional reply to a successful DELETE. 404 Not Found is client failure ("target doesn't exist, nothing happened").

ResponseEntity gives control (without it Spring always replies 200):

  • Status + body: ResponseEntity.ok(x) = ResponseEntity.status(HttpStatus.OK).body(x).
  • Status + headers + body.
  • Status only: ResponseEntity.notFound().build(), noContent().build().

Step 3: the exception flow

@RestControllerAdvice: a helper for all controllers

package in.strikes.cruddemo.exception;

@RestControllerAdvice
public class GlobalExceptionHandler {
    // one method per exception type
}
  • @ControllerAdvice = helper for every controller; common behaviour written once. Main use: exception handling; also model attributes, data-binding setup. Connects to AOP (later).
  • @RestControllerAdvice = @ControllerAdvice + @ResponseBody, like @RestController = @Controller + @ResponseBody (Lecture 15). Use for JSON REST APIs; @ControllerAdvice for view-returning apps, or add @ResponseBody per method.

@ExceptionHandler: an app-wide catch block

@ExceptionHandler(RuntimeException.class)
public ResponseEntity<String> handleRuntimeException(RuntimeException ex) {
    return ResponseEntity.status(HttpStatus.INTERNAL_SERVER_ERROR)   // enum; value 500
                         .body(ex.getMessage());
}

@ExceptionHandler(Exception.class)                  // safety net: last fallback
public ResponseEntity<String> handleGenericException(Exception ex) {
    return ResponseEntity.status(HttpStatus.INTERNAL_SERVER_ERROR)
                         .body("Something went wrong, please try again later");
}
  • A thrown RuntimeException lands here instead of bubbling to the controller; GET /api/students/100 → 500 "No value present". Our handler replaced Boot's default.
  • Handlers form a hierarchy, like catch (ArithmeticException) → catch (RuntimeException) → catch (Exception). The Exception handler is the safety net.
  • Order doesn't matter (unlike catch): Spring picks the handler whose type is closest to the thrown class.

Replacing Optional juggling with orElseThrow

Intermediate attempt findById(id).orElse(null) + mapToDto(student) threw NullPointerException → RuntimeException handler → 500 "Cannot invoke …Student.getId() because student is null". Leaks package/class names; "works" but neither meaningful nor safe.

Clean version: throw a business exception saying exactly what happened:

public CreateStudentResponseDto getStudentById(Long id) {
    Student studentResponse = studentRepository.findById(id)
            .orElseThrow(() -> new ResourceNotFoundException("Student with id " + id + " not found"));
    return mapToDto(studentResponse);             // reaching this line means it exists
}

Step 4: custom exceptions

public class ResourceNotFoundException extends RuntimeException {
    public ResourceNotFoundException(String message) {
        super(message);                         // parent stores the message
    }
}

public class DuplicateResourceException extends RuntimeException {
    public DuplicateResourceException(String message) {
        super(message);
    }
}
  • Ours, not Java's. Extending RuntimeException = unchecked, no throws clauses.
  • Mostly a marker: the type carries meaning, message goes to the parent.
  • The service now speaks business terms ("resource not found", "duplicate resource").

Demo: without a dedicated handler, ResourceNotFoundException hits the RuntimeException handler → 500 "Student with id 100 not found". Right message, wrong status. Fix:

@ExceptionHandler(ResourceNotFoundException.class)
public ResponseEntity<…> handleResourceNotFoundException(ResourceNotFoundException ex, HttpServletRequest request) {
    … HttpStatus.NOT_FOUND …                  // 404
}

@ExceptionHandler(DuplicateResourceException.class)
public ResponseEntity<…> handleDuplicateResourceException(DuplicateResourceException ex, HttpServletRequest request) {
    … HttpStatus.CONFLICT …                   // 409
}

Now GET /api/students/100 → 404 "Student with id 100 not found". "A new handler per exception, because each needs its own status code."

Duplicate email → 409 Conflict

// service, inside createStudent
Student student = mapToEntity(createStudentRequestDto);      // also sets createdAt/updatedAt now
if (emailExists(student)) {
    throw new DuplicateResourceException("Student with email " + student.getEmail() + " already exists");
}

private boolean emailExists(Student student) {
    return studentRepository.existsByEmail(student.getEmail());   // derived query (Lecture 13)
}

// repository
boolean existsByEmail(String email);

Demo: "Mahesh" with taken saurav@gmail.com → 409 Conflict "Student with email saurav@gmail.com already exists".

Performance aside: email isn't the PK or indexed, so existsByEmail scans the table — fine for a demo, slow at millions of rows. Real apps index the column and often add a cache layer for hot data.

Step 5: controllers become one-liners

@GetMapping("/{id}")
public ResponseEntity<CreateStudentResponseDto> getStudent(@PathVariable Long id) {
    return ResponseEntity.ok(studentService.getStudentById(id));      // if we got here, it exists
}

@GetMapping
public ResponseEntity<List<CreateStudentResponseDto>> getAllStudents() {
    return ResponseEntity.ok(studentService.getAllStudents());       // empty list is still 200
}

@DeleteMapping("/{id}")
public ResponseEntity<Void> deleteStudent(@PathVariable Long id) {
    studentService.deleteStudent(id);
    return ResponseEntity.noContent().build();                        // 204, no body
}

Same pattern across the service:

  • update: findByIdAndDeletedIsFalse(id).orElseThrow(…ResourceNotFoundException…), copy fields onto existingStudent (one variable, not two), save, map; controller ok(…).
  • hard delete: findById(id).orElseThrow(…) + studentRepository.delete(studentToBeDeleted); now void (failure is an exception, so no "was it deleted?" boolean).
  • soft delete: findByIdAndDeletedIsFalse(id).orElseThrow(…), setDeleted(true), save, void; 204, same as hard delete to the client.
The payoff: no controller if/else; the service returns data or throws a named exception. The controller never sees entities, the repository never sees DTOs, and only the service knows both.

Step 6: one consistent error body

A plain String isn't structured. Every error shares one shape, modelled on Boot's default fields:

public class ExceptionResponseDto {
    private LocalDateTime timestamp;   // when
    private int statusCode;            // 404
    private String error;              // "Not Found"
    private String message;            // meaningful, safe message we chose
    private String path;               // which endpoint
    // all-args constructor + getters/setters
}

@ExceptionHandler(ResourceNotFoundException.class)
public ResponseEntity<ExceptionResponseDto> handleResourceNotFoundException(
        ResourceNotFoundException ex, HttpServletRequest request) {
    ExceptionResponseDto exceptionResponse = new ExceptionResponseDto(
            LocalDateTime.now(),
            HttpStatus.NOT_FOUND.value(),            // 404
            HttpStatus.NOT_FOUND.getReasonPhrase(),  // "Not Found"
            ex.getMessage(),
            request.getRequestURI());                // path, from the servlet request (Lecture 14)
    return ResponseEntity.status(HttpStatus.NOT_FOUND).body(exceptionResponse);
}
  • First named ExceptionDto, renamed ExceptionResponseDto for clarity.
  • Path comes from the HttpServletRequest the dispatcher already holds — just add it as a handler parameter.
  • Same builder in the duplicate (CONFLICT), runtime and generic (INTERNAL_SERVER_ERROR) handlers; only status changes.

Step 7: surfacing validation errors

A failed @Valid (Lecture 16) throws built-in MethodArgumentNotValidException; with no handler, the generic safety net answered. The DTO needs a field → message map, since several fields can fail:

public class ValidationExceptionResponseDto {
    private LocalDateTime timestamp;
    private int statusCode;
    private String error;
    private String message;
    private String path;
    private Map<String, String> fieldErrors;    // "age" → "Student must be at least 18 years old"
    // all-args constructor + getters/setters
}

@ExceptionHandler(MethodArgumentNotValidException.class)
public ResponseEntity<ValidationExceptionResponseDto> handleMethodArgumentNotValidException(
        MethodArgumentNotValidException ex, HttpServletRequest request) {
    Map<String, String> fieldErrors = new HashMap<>();
    ex.getBindingResult().getFieldErrors()
      .forEach(error -> fieldErrors.put(error.getField(), error.getDefaultMessage()));

    ValidationExceptionResponseDto response = new ValidationExceptionResponseDto(
            LocalDateTime.now(),
            HttpStatus.BAD_REQUEST.value(),           // 400 (the first draft said CONFLICT)
            HttpStatus.BAD_REQUEST.getReasonPhrase(),
            "Validation failed",                      // our own message, not ex.getMessage()
            request.getRequestURI(),
            fieldErrors);
    return ResponseEntity.status(HttpStatus.BAD_REQUEST).body(response);
}
  • getBindingResult().getFieldErrors() → list of FieldError, each with getField() and getDefaultMessage() (the annotation's message = "…").
  • "Validation failed" replaces ex.getMessage(), which is huge and full of internal detail.

Demo:

  • Too-long name, age 17, saurav.gmail.com → 400 with fieldErrors: name "Name cannot be null, empty or blank", email "Student email must be valid", age "Student must be at least 18 years old".
  • Valid Aliya (27, roll 114) → 201.
  • Reusing saurav@gmail.com → 409 with structured body and path.

Interview gaps

  • A catch-all Exception handler turns client errors into 500s (verified on Boot 4.1.0). With only @ExceptionHandler(Exception.class), Spring's own exceptions are swallowed:
    RequestWithout the catch-allWith the catch-all
    Unknown path404 (NoResourceFoundException)500
    Wrong HTTP method405 (HttpRequestMethodNotSupportedException)500
    /students/abc400 (MethodArgumentTypeMismatchException)500
    Malformed JSON400 (HttpMessageNotReadableException)500
    Fix: handle them explicitly, or extend ResponseEntityExceptionHandler (maps MVC exceptions correctly) and override its handleMethodArgumentNotValid — also declaring your own @ExceptionHandler for it is ambiguous.
  • Field-error map loses errors (verified): blank name fails @NotBlank + @Size(min = 2); 2 reported, map.put kept one. Use Map<String, List<String>> (computeIfAbsent(…).add(…)) or merge.
  • Log what you hide: generic handlers ("Something went wrong") log nothing, losing the stack trace. log.error("…", ex), ideally with a correlation/trace id in log and response.
  • Check-then-insert is racy: concurrent sign-ups both pass existsByEmail. Add a unique constraint (@Column(unique = true) or migration), map DataIntegrityViolationException → 409; the index also fixes the full scan.
  • Missing codes: 401 (not authenticated) vs 403 (authenticated, not allowed), via Spring Security; 422 Unprocessable Content sometimes for semantic validation.

Corrections & worth knowing

  • Standard format: Problem Details (RFC 9457, ex-7807). Spring 6+ ProblemDetail.forStatusAndDetail(HttpStatus.NOT_FOUND, "…") (type, title, status, detail, instance, custom props), throw ErrorResponseException, or spring.mvc.problemdetails.enabled=true. "Industry standard" alternative to a home-grown ExceptionResponseDto.
  • @ResponseStatus(HttpStatus.NOT_FOUND) class ResourceNotFoundException …: status without a handler, but no body control.
  • Handler choice: closest declared type wins (ExceptionDepthComparator), so order doesn't matter; across advices use @Order; a controller-local @ExceptionHandler applies only there and beats global advice.
  • /error: Boot registers a container error page forwarding to BasicErrorController; JSON vs Whitelabel by Accept header. Advice-handled exceptions never reach it.
  • Defaults (verified, include-message on): unknown path → 404 "No static resource nope."; wrong method → 405; /abc for Long → 400; bad JSON → 400 "JSON parse error…"; validation → 400 "Validation failed for object='req'. Error count: 2". Keep these statuses in your advice; don't flatten them.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"400 Not Found" → 404 Not Found (corrected in the lecture).
"2001 Created" → 201; "3001" → 301.
"field not found exception is a runtime exception" → Optional.get() throws NoSuchElementException.
"@ExceptionHandler takes the reflection of the class" → the class literal (RuntimeException.class).
Validation handler's first draft used CONFLICT; correct is BAD_REQUEST.

Lecture 18: Configuration & Profiles — .properties vs YAML, Profile-Specific Files, External Config & @Profile

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #18 · 51m · Code Army · builds on Lecture 10 (application.properties, @Value)

The problem: same code, different environments

CodeConfiguration
WhatControllers, services, repositoriesDB URL, username, password, server port, custom settings
Across environmentsDoesn't change; same build everywhere (features still added over time)Does change

Environments: Local (your machine, localhost:8080, unreachable outside) → Dev (shared, devs test) → QA / staging / pre-prod (names vary; prod-like, bug hunting before go-live) → Production (live users).

Local DB password 123; prod has a different URL and strong password. Configuration must vary; code must not — the problem profiles solve.

Setup

Initializr: profile-demo, Boot 4.1.0, Java 21, only Spring Web.

@RestController
@RequestMapping("/api/demo")
public class DemoController {

    @Value("${app.welcome.message}")      // read from configuration, not hard-coded
    private String message;

    @Value("${app.welcome.code}")
    private Integer code;

    @GetMapping
    public ResponseEntity<String> greet() {
        return ResponseEntity.ok(message + " : " + code);
    }
}
# application.properties
app.welcome.message=Hello, welcome to my application
app.welcome.code=200
# predefined Boot key; Tomcat default is 8080
server.port=9090

(In .properties, # is a comment only at line start; a trailing # … becomes part of the value.) With server.port=9090: "Tomcat started on port 9090", 8080 no longer answers.

Why not hard-code values?

  1. Every change = code change + commit, push, redeploy, even for a message.
  2. Per-environment values force commenting tricks (localDbUrl vs prodDbUrl, one commented out per deploy) — fragile.
  3. Security: code goes to Git; hard-coded DB URLs/usernames/passwords leak.
  4. Tight coupling: hard to change.
Precision note: application.properties is still internal config — in src/main/resources, packaged into the JAR. It fixes points 1 and 4, not per-environment or secrets. External config = values supplied from outside the JAR.

.properties vs YAML

Same purpose; @Value reads both identically. Only syntax differs:

application.propertiesapplication.yml
StructureFlat dotted key–value pairsTree by indentation (like Python), no dots/brackets
RepetitionPrefix repeated every lineCommon parent written once
ListsComma-separated, or indexed key[0]=…Comma string, or YAML sequence (- item)
PitfallLong repetitive keysIndentation errors; no tabs
 
# application.properties                       # application.yml
spring.application.name=profile-demo            spring:
server.port=9090                                  application:
app.welcome.message=Hello, welcome to my app        name: profile-demo
app.welcome.code=200                            server:
app.welcome.users=Aditya, Rohit, Rohan, Sohan     port: 9090
                                                app:
                                                  welcome:
                                                    message: Hello, welcome to my app
                                                    code: 200             # sibling of message
                                                    users: Aditya, Rohit, Rohan, Sohan
  • app → welcome → {message, code} yields app.welcome.message/.code; same indentation = siblings. Quotes optional.
  • Comma value binds to a list: @Value("${app.welcome.users}") List<String> names → [Aditya, Rohit, Rohan, Sohan]. Complex nesting: @ConfigurationProperties (Lecture 10).
  • Spring flattens YAML into the same dotted keys; format is preference.
Correction: both files load; .properties wins only on conflicts (verified on Boot 4.1.0): the lecture says Boot "reads the properties file" if both exist and deletes it to make YAML work. Actually both are loaded and merged; .properties wins for shared keys, yml-only keys still resolve (tested). Legal but confusing — pick one.
YAML sequences and @Value (verified): a real YAML list 
users:
  - Aditya
  - Rohit
flattens to app.welcome.users[0], [1]…, so ${app.welcome.users} doesn't exist (null in test) and @Value fails. Use the comma form with @Value, or @ConfigurationProperties for sequences.

Profiles: one file per environment

Name files application-{profile}.properties (or .yml):

src/main/resources/
├── application.properties            # COMMON to all environments (+ local defaults)
├── application-dev.properties        # dev only
├── application-staging.properties    # staging only
└── application-prod.properties       # prod only
Fileserver.portapp.welcome.message
application.properties(none, so 8080)Local: Hello, welcome…
application-dev.properties9090Dev: Hello, welcome…
application-staging.properties8080Staging: Hello, welcome…
application-prod.properties8191Prod: Hello, welcome…

Common file holds shared values: spring.application.name, app.welcome.code=200, users list. Staging file was first misnamed application.staging.properties — the hyphen is required.

Activating a profile

# in application.properties
spring.profiles.active=dev
  • Boot loads application.properties + application-dev.properties; profile values override common ones.
  • dev: port 9090, "Dev: Hello, welcome to my application : 200" (200 and users from common file).
  • staging: port 8080, "Staging: …"; 9090 now fails.

"Didn't we just move the problem?"

Yes. spring.profiles.active is inside the JAR, so each environment means editing and pushing — same problem as hard-coding. The value must come from outside the JAR.

External configuration

  • Command-line arguments.
  • Environment variables: from the OS terminal, a CI/CD pipeline, Kubernetes, or host server (covered with deployment later).

Demo, with spring.profiles.active removed from the file:

mvn spring-boot:run                                     # no profile → "No active profile set, falling back to default"
                                                        #   → port 8080, "Local: …"

mvn spring-boot:run -Dspring-boot.run.profiles=prod     # → "The following profiles are active: prod"
                                                        #   → port 8191, "Prod: Hello, welcome…"

The same JAR/code picks config at launch; source never changes per environment. Same for DB URL/username/password.

All ways to set the profile (added):
  • Maven plugin: mvn spring-boot:run -Dspring-boot.run.profiles=prod (lecture).
  • Packaged JAR: java -jar app.jar --spring.profiles.active=prod, or java -Dspring.profiles.active=prod -jar app.jar.
  • Env var: SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE=prod — usual in Docker/Kubernetes.
Relaxed binding: APP_WELCOME_MESSAGE overrides app.welcome.message; SPRING_DATASOURCE_PASSWORD overrides spring.datasource.password. Verified both overrides (command-line --app.welcome.message=… and env var APP_WELCOME_MESSAGE=…) on Boot 4.1.0.

@Profile: choosing beans per environment

Scenario: NotificationController (POST /api/notification) calls a NotificationService sending real email/SMS. Right in production; in dev, testing would send real messages — costs money (SMS more than email) and spams real people. Need a different implementation per environment, not just values. (First test 404'd: no mapping; adding @PostMapping fixed it.)

public interface NotificationService {               // no @Service: it's only a contract
    String send();
}

@Service
@Profile("prod")                                     // bean exists ONLY when prod is active
public class NotificationServiceImpl implements NotificationService {
    @Override
    public String send() {
        // real email / SMS sent here
        return "Here is a notification";
    }
}

@Service
@Profile({"dev", "default", "staging"})              // any of these profiles
public class DummyNotificationServiceImpl implements NotificationService {
    @Override
    public String send() {
        // no real notification is sent
        return "Here is a dummy notification";
    }
}

@RestController
@RequestMapping("/api/notification")
public class NotificationController {
    private final NotificationService notificationService;   // interface, injected by constructor

    public NotificationController(NotificationService notificationService) {
        this.notificationService = notificationService;
    }

    @PostMapping
    public ResponseEntity<String> sendNotification() {
        return ResponseEntity.ok(notificationService.send());
    }
}
  • One implementation: injected automatically.
  • Two without @Profile: startup fails (two candidates, one injection point).
  • @Primary/@Qualifier (Lecture 5) choose within one environment; @Profile chooses by environment.

Demo run

  1. Initially only @Profile("dev") / @Profile("prod"). -Dspring-boot.run.profiles=dev (9090) → "Here is a dummy notification".
  2. prod (8191) → "Here is a notification".
  3. No profile → startup fails: "Consider defining a bean of type NotificationService in your configuration" (nothing for default).
  4. Dummy changed to @Profile({"dev", "default", "staging"}) → plain run (8080) returns the dummy.

Profile-specific files vs @Profile

Profile-specific config files@Profile annotation
VariesValues: ports, URLs, credentials, messagesBeans / behaviour: which implementation exists
Howapplication-{profile}.properties / .yml over application.propertiesOn @Component/@Service, @Configuration classes or @Bean methods
ExampleProd vs local DB URLReal vs dummy notification sender
Selected bySame active profile(s): spring.profiles.active, command-line arg or env var

Interview gaps

  • Profile files don't keep secrets out of Git (application-prod.properties is committed). Use placeholders (spring.datasource.password=${DB_PASSWORD}) + env vars or a secrets manager (Vault, AWS Secrets Manager, Kubernetes Secrets).
  • Don't commit spring.profiles.active; set at deploy. spring.profiles.default changes the fallback.
  • Fail safe: a forgotten prod profile silently uses the dummy (via default). For critical beans make the real one default and the dummy explicit (@Profile("!prod")), or fail fast with no profile.
  • Feature flags: one toggle is often cleaner as @ConditionalOnProperty(name = "notifications.enabled", havingValue = "true") than a profile.

Corrections & worth knowing

  • Configuration precedence (simplified, highest first):
    1. Command-line arguments.
    2. Java system properties (-D).
    3. OS environment variables.
    4. Profile-specific files outside the JAR.
    5. Profile-specific files inside the JAR.
    6. application.properties outside the JAR (e.g. ./config/ next to it).
    7. application.properties inside the JAR.
    8. @PropertySource, then defaults.
    "Command line beats env vars, which beat packaged files" answers most interview questions.
  • External files: config/application.properties next to the JAR is auto-picked; --spring.config.location or spring.config.import point elsewhere. Config change without rebuild.
  • One YAML for all profiles: documents separated by ---, each with spring.config.activate.on-profile: dev. Compact, but large files get hard to read.
  • Several active profiles (spring.profiles.active=prod,metrics); later wins on conflicts. Profile groups (spring.profiles.group.prod=proddb,prodmq) bundle them.
  • @Value defaults: @Value("${app.welcome.message:Hello}") gives a fallback; otherwise a missing key fails startup.
  • default profile is active only when none is set — why the dummy needed "default" for a plain local run.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"real payment" (said a few times) → real notification.
"profiling" → the Spring feature is profiles. "Profiling" usually means performance profiling.

Lecture 19: Filters — Cross-Cutting Work Before the DispatcherServlet

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #19 · 1h06m · Code Army · logging, authentication, request IDs, timing and error responses with servlet filters

The problem: work that every request needs

Flow so far: client → DispatcherServlet → controller → service → repository, and back. New requirement, e.g. for GET localhost:8080/api/students:

  • Before business logic: log the request; authenticate (no logged-in user/token, no access); generate a unique request ID (1001, 1002, …).
  • After: add the request ID to the response (maybe other headers).
  • For every API.

Naive approach: do it in the controller

Attempt (50+ endpoints)Why it breaks
Logging/auth/ID code in every controller methodSame code repeated in 50+ places
Helper class every controller callsEach must remember to call it; controllers tightly coupled to infrastructure
Why the controller is the wrong place: logging, auth and request IDs are infrastructure / security concerns. By Single Responsibility (S of SOLID), a controller only receives the request and passes it to the service. The series keeps moving work out: business logic → services, errors → @RestControllerAdvice (Lecture 17). Cross-cutting request work → filters, later interceptors.

Where a filter sits

  • Runs before the DispatcherServlet (in) and after it (out).
  • Sees the raw request; HandlerMapping (Lecture 15) hasn't run, so a filter doesn't know which controller will handle it — suits generic per-request work.
  • Interceptors (next) sit after the dispatcher, before the controller — they know the handler.
  • Also sees requests that never reach a controller: static resources (/static/logo.png), /error, /favicon.ico.

What filters are used for

UseExample
LoggingMethod/URI, response status, maybe bodies; mask sensitive data like card numbers
Security / authenticationCheck auth header/token; block if missing
Modify the responseAdd headers like a request ID a client/third party can quote for logs
Modify the requestPossible but discouraged (alters original); use DTOs to shape data
EncodingForce UTF-8
TimingEnd-to-end request duration (turnaround time)
Early error responsesReject with status + JSON body without touching the dispatcher

A filter can inspect headers, cookies, auth info; add response headers; wrap request/response; block/allow; log; time.

Setup

  • Initializr: filter-demo, only Spring Web.
  • StudentController (@RequestMapping("/api/students")): @PostMapping createStudent calls StudentService.createStudent() (prints "Student created"), returns ResponseEntity.ok("Done").
  • Later a Student DTO (Long id, name, email) via @RequestBody; service prints name and email.

The Filter interface

package jakarta.servlet;                       // Servlet API, NOT Spring

public interface Filter {
    default void init(FilterConfig filterConfig) throws ServletException {}
    void doFilter(ServletRequest request, ServletResponse response, FilterChain chain)
            throws IOException, ServletException;          // the only abstract method
    default void destroy() {}
}
  • From the Servlet API, not Spring (jakarta.servlet). Boot 3+ uses jakarta.*; Boot 1/2 used javax.* — check imports in old code.
  • Lifecycle (like servlets, Lecture 14): init on creation, doFilter per request (the work), destroy on shutdown. init/destroy have defaults, rarely used; only doFilter is mandatory.
  • Why ServletRequest, not HttpServletRequest? The Servlet API is protocol-generic; HttpServletRequest/HttpServletResponse are HTTP subtypes. ~90% of work is HTTP, so you cast. Spring offers a base class that avoids it (see "Worth knowing").

The filter chain

  • chain.doFilter(request, response) = "done, next one's turn". FilterChain knows who's next; after the last filter it invokes the DispatcherServlet (doPost for our POST).
  • Code after chain.doFilter(…) runs on the way back, like recursion unwinding.
  • Returning without calling it stops the request; whatever was written to the response goes to the client.
  • FilterChain is an interface; Tomcat's ApplicationFilterChain is one implementation (also mock, pass-through).

Worked example 1: a logging filter

@Component                       // make it a bean → Spring Boot registers it for all requests
@Order(2)
public class LoggingFilter implements Filter {

    @Override
    public void doFilter(ServletRequest servletRequest, ServletResponse servletResponse,
                         FilterChain filterChain) throws IOException, ServletException {

        HttpServletRequest httpRequest = (HttpServletRequest) servletRequest;     // cast to HTTP
        HttpServletResponse httpResponse = (HttpServletResponse) servletResponse;

        long startTime = System.currentTimeMillis();
        String requestId = UUID.randomUUID().toString();
        httpResponse.setHeader("X-Request-ID", requestId);          // BEFORE the chain (see below)

        System.out.println("Incoming request: " + httpRequest.getMethod()
                           + " " + httpRequest.getRequestURI());    // e.g. POST /api/students
        try {
            filterChain.doFilter(servletRequest, servletResponse);  // pass the ORIGINAL objects on
        } finally {                                                 // runs even if an exception escapes
            long duration = System.currentTimeMillis() - startTime;
            System.out.println("Response status: " + httpResponse.getStatus());
            System.out.println("API response time: " + duration + " ms");
        }
    }
}
  1. Enter/exit prints: entered → "Student created" → exiting.
  2. Logging: "Incoming request: POST /api/students … Response status: 200" (real apps: logging library to file, later).
  3. Request ID: new UUID per call in X-Request-ID; client quotes it to fetch that call's logs.
  4. Timing: 43 ms; with Thread.sleep(2000) in the service (slow DB/Redis) ≈ 2 s.
  5. try/finally: post-processing runs even if a later layer throws.
Why the request-ID header is set before chain.doFilter (verified on Boot 4.1.0): on return, the controller has usually written the body, so the response is committed (headers sent); later headers are silently dropped. Test: X-Before reached the client; X-After (isCommitted() = true) didn't. Set response headers on the way in.

Worked example 2: an authentication filter

Simplified rule: every request needs header token: 12345 (real apps: JWT/bearer tokens + Spring Security, later). Second rule added at the end: header X-API-Key: secret123, else a JSON error body.

@Component
@Order(1)                                        // runs first
public class AuthenticationFilter implements Filter {

    @Override
    public void doFilter(ServletRequest servletRequest, ServletResponse servletResponse,
                         FilterChain filterChain) throws IOException, ServletException {

        HttpServletRequest httpRequest = (HttpServletRequest) servletRequest;
        HttpServletResponse httpResponse = (HttpServletResponse) servletResponse;

        String token = httpRequest.getHeader("token");
        if (!"12345".equals(token)) {                              // null-safe
            httpResponse.setStatus(HttpServletResponse.SC_UNAUTHORIZED);   // 401
            return;                                                // chain NOT called → blocked
        }

        String apiKey = httpRequest.getHeader("X-API-Key");        // header names are case-insensitive
        if (!"secret123".equals(apiKey)) {
            httpResponse.setStatus(HttpServletResponse.SC_UNAUTHORIZED);
            httpResponse.setContentType("application/json");
            httpResponse.getWriter().write("{\"message\": \"Invalid or missing API key\"}");
            return;
        }

        filterChain.doFilter(servletRequest, servletResponse);     // authenticated → continue
    }
}
  • HttpServletResponse constants like SC_UNAUTHORIZED = 401 read better than bare numbers.
  • First test returned 200 with a wrong token: no @Component, so not a bean, not registered. With it: 12345 → 200 "Done"; 1234 → 401 Unauthorized.
  • API key: missing → 401 + JSON message. A wrong comparison (equals for !equals) was fixed; then secret123 accepted.
Correction: write checks null-safe: the lecture's "token == null and token not equal to 12345" with && lets a null token pass and doesn't reject a present-but-wrong one. Intent is "missing or wrong": token == null || !token.equals("12345"). Simplest: !"12345".equals(token) (a literal's equals(null) is false).

Ordering filters with @Order

Auth should run first; logging only for authenticated requests:

@Component @Order(1) public class AuthenticationFilter implements Filter { … }
@Component @Order(2) public class LoggingFilter        implements Filter { … }
  • Lower number = earlier on the way in (later on the way out).
  • Correct token + key → 200 "Done" with timing printed; wrong key/token → 401, logging filter never runs.
  • Without @Order a chain still forms but Spring chooses the order, not guaranteed stable across machines/runs.
  • One responsibility per filter — no "master filter"; the chain exists to separate work.

Verified (Boot 4.1.0): auth IN → logging IN → controller → logging OUT → auth OUT.

Filter vs interceptor vs @RestControllerAdvice

Servlet FilterHandlerInterceptor (next)@RestControllerAdvice
Belongs toServlet API (Tomcat)Spring MVCSpring MVC
RunsBefore and after the DispatcherServletInside the dispatcher, around the controllerWhen a controller-layer exception is thrown
Knows the handler?NoYes (which controller method)Yes
Sees non-controller requestsYes: everything reaching the containerOnly those routed via the dispatcher: static resources (ResourceHttpRequestHandler) and the /error forward (BasicErrorController) do reach interceptors (verified in Lecture 21)No
Typical useAuth, logging, CORS, encoding, request IDsPer-handler checks, model tweaks, per-endpoint timingError responses

Interview gaps

  • Log before you reject: auth at @Order(1) leaves rejected requests (attacks, broken clients) unlogged and ID-less. Usually request-ID/logging is outermost, auth next.
  • Request ID in every log line: SLF4J MDC (MDC.put("requestId", id), cleared in finally); reuse an incoming gateway X-Request-ID.
  • Reading the body consumes it (one-shot stream). Use ContentCachingRequestWrapper/ContentCachingResponseWrapper; never log passwords/card data.
  • Secrets: keys in config/env (Lecture 18), constant-time compare (MessageDigest.isEqual), real auth via Spring Security (itself a filter chain).
  • Status in finally can lie (verified): with an escaped exception and no @RestControllerAdvice, finally sees 200; Boot sets 500 later. Without try/finally, post-processing was skipped entirely.

Worth knowing

  • OncePerRequestFilter — the Spring base class alluded to. Implement doFilterInternal(HttpServletRequest, HttpServletResponse, FilterChain): no casting, single execution per request even across forwards/async dispatches. Usual choice in Spring.
  • Registering filters. A @Component filter applies to all URLs (/*). To limit (e.g. /api/*) or set order/name without annotations, use FilterRegistrationBean: 
    @Bean
FilterRegistrationBean<LoggingFilter> loggingFilter() {
    var reg = new FilterRegistrationBean<>(new LoggingFilter());
    reg.addUrlPatterns("/api/*");
    reg.setOrder(2);
    return reg;
}
    Don't also add @Component (registered twice). Servlet-style @WebFilter works with @ServletComponentScan.
  • Dispatcher types (verified). Bean filters register for REQUEST dispatches; on the forward to /error our filters did not run again.
  • Built-in filters: CharacterEncodingFilter (Boot, UTF-8), CorsFilter, ForwardedHeaderFilter, Spring Security's SecurityFilterChain.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"Spring MVC gave a special servlet to avoid casting" → a special filter base class, OncePerRequestFilter.
The 2-second sleep's reading was said as "255 ms" → it's about 2000+ ms.

Lecture 20: Filters, Part 2 — Altering Requests & Responses, Wrappers, FilterRegistrationBean & OncePerRequestFilter

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #20 · 1h13m · Code Army · continues Lecture 19; what filters can change, what they shouldn't, and the "body disappeared" trap

Recap and goal

  • Chain: client → filter 1 → 2 → 3 → DispatcherServlet → controller → service → repository, then back through 3, 2, 1.
  • Filters are Servlet API, not Spring. Interceptors (between dispatcher and controller) come next.
  • Lecture 19 project, filters removed: Student DTO (id, name, email), POST /api/students, service just prints.

Question: a filter can observe; can it alter? What can vs should be changed?

Altering the response

Three parts: status code, headers, body.

1. Headers: easy, if set before the chain

@Component
public class ResponseHeaderFilter implements Filter {
    @Override
    public void doFilter(ServletRequest servletRequest, ServletResponse servletResponse,
                         FilterChain filterChain) throws IOException, ServletException {
        HttpServletResponse httpServletResponse = (HttpServletResponse) servletResponse;
        String requestId = UUID.randomUUID().toString();
        httpServletResponse.setHeader("X-Request-ID", requestId);   // key-value, BEFORE doFilter
        filterChain.doFilter(servletRequest, servletResponse);
    }
}
  • Only the response needs casting.
  • Postman showed a new random X-Request-ID per call.
  • Moving setHeader after doFilter made it disappear.
Why "after" doesn't work: the controller returns ResponseEntity.ok(…); the dispatcher writes the body into the HttpServletResponse via its writer (an output stream). Once written, the response is committed — too late for the filter (verified in Lecture 19). Strictly, Tomcat creates the HttpServletRequest/HttpServletResponse and passes them to the dispatcher; the lecture simplifies this as "the dispatcher builds them".

2. Status code and body, answering from the filter

A filter can end the request early with its own status and body (Lecture 19's auth filter):

@Component
public class AuthenticationFilter implements Filter {
    @Override
    public void doFilter(ServletRequest servletRequest, ServletResponse servletResponse,
                         FilterChain filterChain) throws IOException, ServletException {
        HttpServletRequest httpServletRequest = (HttpServletRequest) servletRequest;
        HttpServletResponse httpServletResponse = (HttpServletResponse) servletResponse;

        String token = httpServletRequest.getHeader("token");
        if (!"12345".equals(token)) {                                     // missing or wrong
            httpServletResponse.setStatus(HttpServletResponse.SC_UNAUTHORIZED);   // 401
            httpServletResponse.setContentType("application/json");
            httpServletResponse.getWriter().write("{\"message\": \"Authentication is required\"}");
            return;                                                       // never reaches the dispatcher
        }
        filterChain.doFilter(servletRequest, servletResponse);
    }
}

12345 → 200 "Done"; 123 → 401 {"message": "Authentication is required"}.

Worth knowing: this time the lecture phrases it correctly: "null or not 12345" (token == null || !token.equals("12345")); !"12345".equals(token) is the same, null-safe in one call.

3. Body: modifying the controller's output via a wrapper

Goal: controller returns {"name": "Aditya", "message": "Student is saved successfully"}; filter adds "appName": "Student Management System". After doFilter fails like headers: body already committed.

The trick: hand the chain a fake response: pass a wrapper around the real response. Dispatcher and controller treat it as the normal HttpServletResponse, but writes are buffered, not sent. Back in the filter: read the buffer, change it, copy the final version to the real response. Compared to the Decorator pattern (LLD notebook): a wrapper behaving like the object it wraps. 
@Component
public class ResponseBodyFilter implements Filter {
    @Override
    public void doFilter(ServletRequest servletRequest, ServletResponse servletResponse,
                         FilterChain filterChain) throws IOException, ServletException {
        HttpServletResponse httpServletResponse = (HttpServletResponse) servletResponse;
        ContentCachingResponseWrapper wrappedResponse =
                new ContentCachingResponseWrapper(httpServletResponse);

        filterChain.doFilter(servletRequest, wrappedResponse);      // downstream writes into the wrapper

        byte[] originalBodyBytes = wrappedResponse.getContentAsByteArray();   // body = stream of bytes
        String originalBody = new String(originalBodyBytes, StandardCharsets.UTF_8);

        String modifiedBody = """
                {"originalResponse": %s, "appName": "Student Management System"}"""
                .formatted(originalBody);

        wrappedResponse.resetBuffer();                              // drop the original body first!
        wrappedResponse.getWriter().write(modifiedBody);
        wrappedResponse.copyBodyToResponse();                       // push buffer into the real response
    }
}

Controller now returns a StudentResponseDto (name, message) built in the service: "Student is saved successfully".

  • Why nest the original? The JSON ends with }, so you can't just append , "appName": …. Wrapping as "originalResponse": {…} avoids editing JSON text. (Robust way: parse with Jackson, add a field.)
  • Text blocks ("""…""", standard since Java 15) with %s and .formatted(…) (also Java 15) avoid \" escapes and + concatenation.
  • Demo bug: first run returned both JSONs back-to-back — the wrapper still held the controller's body. resetBuffer() clears it first.

Verified (Boot 4.1.0): client got {"originalResponse": {"name":"Aditya","message":"Student is saved successfully"}, "appName": "Student Management System"} with correct Content-Length (set by copyBodyToResponse).

Possible ≠ advisable: changing headers is fine. Rewriting the controller's body is bad practice:
  • Client expects the controller's contract, gets something else.
  • Disabling/changing the filter silently changes the API.
  • Response shape belongs in controller/service, not infrastructure.
Your own body when rejecting (the 401 above) is fine.

Altering the request

A request carries method, URI, headers, parameters, body.

PartRead in a filterChange in a filter?
HTTP methodgetMethod()No setter
EndpointgetRequestURI()No setter
HeadersgetHeader("token") (null if absent)No setHeader: read-only
Request parametersgetParameter(…), getParameterMap()No setter: read-only
BodygetInputStream() / getReader(); no getBody()Only via a wrapper; reading consumes it
  • HttpServletRequest offers only setAttribute/setCharacterEncoding. Everything the client sent is read-only — sensibly: method, path, headers, parameters are the contract; a filter changing them is the wrong layer.
  • Hacky way: extend HttpServletRequestWrapper, override getHeader/getHeaders/getHeaderNames or getParameter/getParameterMap/getParameterNames/getParameterValues, pass the wrapper down. Works, but don't.

The trap: logging the request body makes it disappear

Only logging the body — learned "the hard way" at work by the lecturer.

@Component
public class RequestFilter implements Filter {
    @Override
