    public void doFilter(ServletRequest servletRequest, ServletResponse servletResponse,
                         FilterChain filterChain) throws IOException, ServletException {
        HttpServletRequest httpServletRequest = (HttpServletRequest) servletRequest;

        BufferedReader reader = httpServletRequest.getReader();
        StringBuilder body = new StringBuilder();
        String line = reader.readLine();
        while (line != null) {                   // read line by line until null
            body.append(line);
            line = reader.readLine();
        }
        System.out.println(body);                // logged fine…

        filterChain.doFilter(servletRequest, servletResponse);   // …but downstream gets nothing
    }
}

Result: body printed, then 500 Internal Server Error:

java.lang.IllegalStateException: getReader() has already been called for this request

Jackson (for @RequestBody) can't re-read the body to build Student.

Why the body is a stream, readable once

Tomcat (Lecture 14) parses method, URI, host, headers, parameters into easy getters. The body could be anything (JSON, HTML, PDF, video, a 2 GB file), so it's exposed as a byte stream: getInputStream() (bytes) or getReader() (BufferedReader). Not stored — reading moves a pointer ({, ", i, d…) with no rewind; the first reader (normally Jackson) consumes it.

Fixes

  1. Re-supply the body: store the bytes, pass an HttpServletRequestWrapper whose getInputStream()/getReader() serve them again.
  2. Spring's ready-made: ContentCachingRequestWrapper, caching bytes as they are read (see note).

The wrapper could also modify the body — possible, but never do it.

Precision note: ContentCachingRequestWrapper caches lazily (verified on Boot 4.1.0): it records bytes as downstream (Jackson) consumes them. getContentAsByteArray() before chain.doFilter → empty; after → {"name":"Aditya"}, controller unaffected. So log the request body after the chain. Spring 6.1+ also requires/offers a cache limit: new ContentCachingRequestWrapper(request, limit).

Registering a filter with FilterRegistrationBean

Instead of @Component, declare it as a bean in a config class for extra settings:

public class DummyFilter implements Filter {            // NO @Component
    @Override
    public void doFilter(ServletRequest servletRequest, ServletResponse servletResponse,
                         FilterChain filterChain) throws IOException, ServletException {
        System.out.println("Dummy filter called");
        filterChain.doFilter(servletRequest, servletResponse);
    }
}

@Configuration
public class FilterConfig {

    @Bean
    public FilterRegistrationBean<DummyFilter> getDummyFilterBean() {
        FilterRegistrationBean<DummyFilter> registrationBean = new FilterRegistrationBean<>();
        registrationBean.setFilter(new DummyFilter());
        registrationBean.setOrder(1);                            // same role as @Order
        registrationBean.addUrlPatterns("/api/*", "/admin/*");   // ONLY these paths
        registrationBean.setName("dummyFilter");                 // useful in logs
        return registrationBean;
    }
}
  • addUrlPatterns is the key extra: filter not even invoked elsewhere. /api/* matches /api/students, /api/teachers.
  • @Component alternative: if getRequestURI() doesn't start with /api/, call chain.doFilter and return. Works, but still runs per request; URL patterns are cleaner.
  • Demo: POST /api/students printed "Dummy filter called".
Worth flagging (added): don't combine @Component with a FilterRegistrationBean for the same filter — registered twice. Servlet URL patterns are simple: /api/* matches everything under /api/; Ant-style /api/** (Spring MVC/Security) isn't a servlet pattern.

Spring's own base class: OncePerRequestFilter

@Component
public class SpringDemoFilter extends OncePerRequestFilter {    // extends a class, not implements

    @Override
    protected void doFilterInternal(HttpServletRequest request,      // already HTTP-typed
                                    HttpServletResponse response,
                                    FilterChain filterChain) throws ServletException, IOException {
        // pre-work
        filterChain.doFilter(request, response);
        // post-work
    }

    @Override
    protected boolean shouldNotFilter(HttpServletRequest request) {  // default: false (filter everything)
        String uri = request.getRequestURI();
        return !uri.startsWith("/api/");                             // true → skip this filter
    }
}
  • Spring MVC class (org.springframework.web.filter) on top of Servlet Filter.
  • doFilterInternal gets HTTP types directly — no casting. shouldNotFilter holds the skip condition (true = skip).
  • One execution per request, even across forwards/includes/async dispatches where a plain filter could rerun.
  • Usual base for custom filters; Spring Security's extend it too.

What a filter may change: summary

TargetPossible?Should you?
Response headersYes, set before doFilterYes: request IDs, security headers, CORS
Response status + body when rejectingYes, then returnYes: 401/403/429 with an error message
Response body produced by the controllerVia ContentCachingResponseWrapperNo: breaks the API contract
Request method / URINo settersNo
Request headers / parametersOnly by overriding getters in HttpServletRequestWrapperNo: a hack
Request body: logVia caching wrapper; read after the chainCarefully: size limits, mask secrets
Request body: modifyVia a custom wrapperNo

Interview gaps

  • Memory: ContentCachingResponseWrapper buffers the whole response — breaks streaming, hurts large downloads. Skip such endpoints (shouldNotFilter); cap request wrappers.
  • Always copyBodyToResponse(), in finally — if an exception skips it, the client gets an empty body.
  • Charset: explicit (StandardCharsets.UTF_8 or the response's); platform default may garble non-ASCII.
  • Sensitive data: mask passwords, tokens, card numbers before logging; restrict log access.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"Java has a wrapper called ContentCachingResponseWrapper" → it's Spring's (org.springframework.web.util). HttpServletRequestWrapper is from the Servlet API.
"text blocks came in Java 15 or 16" → standard in Java 15 (previewed in 13/14).

Lecture 21: Interceptors — HandlerInterceptor, preHandle/postHandle/afterCompletion & Filters vs Interceptors

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #21 · 1h13m · Code Army · builds on Lectures 19–20 (filters) and 15 (DispatcherServlet internals)

The problem: filters are blind to the controller

  • Lecture 19: client → Tomcat → filter chain → DispatcherServlet → controller → service → repository, and back. Filters do shared infrastructure work: logging, authentication, response time, response headers.
  • Why a second concept? Filters are Servlet technology, between Tomcat and the dispatcher, with the raw request.
  • Only the DispatcherServlet, via HandlerMapping, decides which controller method runs (Lecture 15); a filter can't know.

The idea: hook in after the dispatcher, before the controller

  • Interceptors are Spring MVC. They chain like filters but run inside the dispatcher, after mapping is resolved.
  • All a filter does (log, auth, timing) plus handler-aware work: log which controller and method ran, per-endpoint authorisation, logic for specific controllers/paths only.
  • Interceptor timing covers just controller/service; filter timing includes the dispatcher.

The HandlerInterceptor interface

public interface HandlerInterceptor {                 // org.springframework.web.servlet
    default boolean preHandle(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response,
                              Object handler) throws Exception { return true; }
    default void postHandle(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response,
                            Object handler, @Nullable ModelAndView modelAndView) throws Exception {}
    default void afterCompletion(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response,
                                 Object handler, @Nullable Exception ex) throws Exception {}
}
  • All three are default; override what you need. A filter's single doFilter (code before/after chain.doFilter) is split into separate methods.
  • Request/response already HTTP-typed — no casting.
MethodWhenExtra parameterTypical use (REST APIs)
preHandleBefore the controller methodObject handlerLog request/handler, checks, start timer. true continues, false stops
postHandleAfter controller returns, before view render. Only if no exceptionModelAndView (null for REST)Add model data for server-rendered views. Rarely useful for REST
afterCompletionAfter the whole request, incl. view rendering, even on exceptionException ex (null if none or already handled)Log status, stop timer, log unhandled exceptions, clean up

What is a "handler"?

Generic word because a request isn't always served by a controller method (static resource, view…). For REST controllers it's a HandlerMethod wrapping the controller bean and the java.lang.reflect.Method. Interceptors wrap it in layers — the "onion-peel model".

Worked example: a logging interceptor

@Component
public class LoggingInterceptor implements HandlerInterceptor {

    @Override
    public boolean preHandle(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response,
                             Object handler) throws Exception {
        System.out.println("Incoming request");
        System.out.println("HTTP method: " + request.getMethod());               // POST
        System.out.println("Request URI: " + request.getRequestURI());           // /api/students
        System.out.println("Request parameters: " + request.getQueryString());   // name=Aditya&age=28 (or null)
        System.out.println("Client IP: " + request.getRemoteAddr());
        System.out.println("Token header: " + request.getHeader("token"));

        if (handler instanceof HandlerMethod handlerMethod) {                    // safe cast (pattern matching)
            System.out.println("Controller called: " + handlerMethod.getBeanType().getName());
            System.out.println("Controller method: " + handlerMethod.getMethod().getName());
        }
        return true;                               // continue to next interceptor / controller
    }

    @Override
    public void afterCompletion(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response,
                                Object handler, Exception ex) throws Exception {
        System.out.println("Response status: " + response.getStatus());
        if (ex != null) {
            System.out.println("Exception: " + ex.getMessage());
        }
    }
}
  • Safe cast: first a direct (HandlerMethod) handler, then instanceof — a direct cast throws for non-controller handlers (static resources).
  • Output: POST, /api/students, parameters null (or name=Aditya&age=28); loopback IP; token 123; in.strikes…StudentController / createStudent, then "Controller called", then "Response status: 200".
  • ex in afterCompletion is non-null only for escaped exceptions; if @RestControllerAdvice (Lecture 17) handled it, null.
  • postHandle not overridden, as usual for REST.

Registering it: @Component isn't enough

With only @Component, nothing from the interceptor printed. Unlike auto-registered filters, interceptors must be added to the registry via a WebMvcConfigurer:

@Configuration
public class WebConfig implements WebMvcConfigurer {

    private final LoggingInterceptor loggingInterceptor;
    private final AuthenticationInterceptor authenticationInterceptor;
    private final AuthorizationInterceptor authorizationInterceptor;

    public WebConfig(LoggingInterceptor loggingInterceptor,                 // constructor injection
                     AuthenticationInterceptor authenticationInterceptor,
                     AuthorizationInterceptor authorizationInterceptor) {
        this.loggingInterceptor = loggingInterceptor;
        this.authenticationInterceptor = authenticationInterceptor;
        this.authorizationInterceptor = authorizationInterceptor;
    }

    @Override
    public void addInterceptors(InterceptorRegistry registry) {       // note: addInterceptorS
        registry.addInterceptor(authenticationInterceptor)
                .addPathPatterns("/api/**")
                .excludePathPatterns("/api/auth/login", "/api/public/**")
                .order(1);
        registry.addInterceptor(authorizationInterceptor)
                .addPathPatterns("/api/**")
                .order(2);
        registry.addInterceptor(loggingInterceptor)
                .order(3);
    }
}
  • First attempt overrode misspelled addInterceptor; @Override flagged it. It's addInterceptors.
  • After registration: preHandle → "Controller called" → postHandle → afterCompletion.

Path patterns (Spring MVC, not servlet)

PatternMatchesExample
/apiExactly that path/api only
/api/*Exactly one more segment/api/students, /api/teachers, not /api/students/1
/api/**Any depth below/api/students, /api/students/1/courses
  • Servlet filter patterns (Lecture 20) are cruder: /api/* = everything under /api.
  • excludePathPatterns carves out login or public pages viewable without login (X/Twitter example); otherwise auth would block the login request itself.

Authentication and authorisation interceptors (homework, sketched)

@Component
public class AuthenticationInterceptor implements HandlerInterceptor {
    @Override
    public boolean preHandle(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response,
                             Object handler) throws Exception {
        String apiKey = request.getHeader("x-api-key");            // header names are case-insensitive
        if (!"secret123".equals(apiKey)) {
            response.setStatus(HttpServletResponse.SC_UNAUTHORIZED);  // 401: who are you?
            return false;                                          // stop here
        }
        return true;
    }
}

@Component
public class AuthorizationInterceptor implements HandlerInterceptor {
    @Override
    public boolean preHandle(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response,
                             Object handler) throws Exception {
        String userRole = request.getHeader("x-user-role");
        if (!"ADMIN".equals(userRole)) {
            response.setStatus(HttpServletResponse.SC_FORBIDDEN);  // 403: known, but not allowed
            response.setContentType("application/json");
            response.getWriter().write("{\"message\": \"You are not authorized to perform this action\"}");
            return false;
        }
        return true;
    }
}
401 vs 403: Authentication = "who are you / logged in?" → 401 Unauthorized (misleading name for "unauthenticated"). Authorisation = "are you allowed to do this?" (e.g. only admins delete) → 403 Forbidden.

Demo:

  • x-api-key: secret123 + x-user-role: ADMIN → 200, full logs.
  • Role user → 403 with JSON message (plain text until setContentType("application/json") added).
  • Wrong key → empty response (only return false, no status set in demo) and no logging — logging is order 3, never ran.

Further homework: timing interceptor (start in preHandle, stop in afterCompletion), port Lecture 19's filter examples, add admin-only delete/soft-delete to the CRUD app.

How it really executes: the dispatcher drives each phase

The three methods run at different moments, each called by the DispatcherServlet:

Correction: "after" methods run in reverse order (verified on Boot 4.1.0): the lecture's diagram shows postHandle/afterCompletion 1 → 2. With A (order 1) and B (order 2):
A.preHandle → B.preHandle → controller → B.postHandle → A.postHandle → B.afterCompletion → A.afterCompletion
A true onion: first in, last out, like filters.
More verified behaviour:
  • preHandle returns false: controller and later interceptors skipped, no postHandle. afterCompletion still runs for interceptors whose preHandle returned true (B denied → only A.afterCompletion).
  • postHandle can't change a REST response. For @ResponseBody/ResponseEntity the body is written inside the handler call, so it's already committed (isCommitted() = true, modelAndView = null); a header set there never arrived. Set headers in preHandle (or use ResponseBodyAdvice).
  • Exceptions: controller threw → postHandle skipped, afterCompletion got the exception.
  • Non-controller requests through the dispatcher are seen too. Static file → handler = ResourceHttpRequestHandler. After an exception, the /error forward ran every interceptor again with BasicErrorController — hence the instanceof HandlerMethod check, and possible double-logging of errors.

Filters vs interceptors

FilterInterceptor
Belongs toServlet API (jakarta.servlet.Filter)Spring MVC (HandlerInterceptor)
PositionAround the DispatcherServletInside it, around the handler
Knows controller/method?NoYes (HandlerMethod)
HooksOne doFilter (before/after chain.doFilter)preHandle, postHandle, afterCompletion
Stop a requestDon't call chain.doFilterReturn false from preHandle
Registration@Component (auto) or FilterRegistrationBeanWebMvcConfigurer.addInterceptors (required)
Path matchingServlet patterns (/api/*)Spring patterns (/api/* vs /api/**), plus excludes
Wrap/replace request or response objectsYes (wrappers, Lecture 20)No, only uses the objects given
Best forGlobal: request IDs, encoding, CORS, security, global loggingHandler-aware: per-endpoint logging/metrics, per-controller rules, custom annotations
The lecture's guidance:
  • Generic, global logic → filters; controller-specific → interceptors. Real apps use both.
  • Don't do authentication/authorisation in interceptors — worst place, the request has passed the dispatcher. Reject in the filter layer; in practice use Spring Security (a filter chain, later). The auth interceptors above are exercises.
  • Changing bodies is possible (e.g. in preHandle) but not good practice.
  • Pairs well with custom annotations, e.g. handlerMethod.hasMethodAnnotation(AdminOnly.class) (later project).

Worth knowing

  • Order: .order(n), lower first; else registration order (more deterministic than filters, but be explicit).
  • Client IP behind proxies: getRemoteAddr() returns the proxy/LB; read X-Forwarded-For or set server.forward-headers-strategy=framework. Locally: 0:0:0:0:0:0:0:1.
  • getBeanType().getName() = fully qualified name; getSimpleName() gives StudentController.
  • Async: for async handlers, postHandle/afterCompletion aren't called on the first dispatch; AsyncHandlerInterceptor.afterConcurrentHandlingStarted runs instead.
  • Don't add @EnableWebMvc to the WebMvcConfigurer in Boot (Lecture 15); implementing the interface suffices.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"AuthorizationInterceptor implements HandlerMapping" → HandlerInterceptor.
"addInterceptor" override → addInterceptors (fixed in the lecture).
"client IP 0.0.0.1" → the loopback address.

Lecture 22: Introduction to AOP — Cross-Cutting Concerns, Scattering & Tangling, and the Decorator Road to Aspects

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #22 · 49m · Code Army · the motivation for Aspect-Oriented Programming; the mechanics come next lecture

From filters and interceptors to "any method"

MechanismWhat it wrapsLecture
FilterThe raw incoming request, before the DispatcherServlet19–20
InterceptorA controller (handler)21
AOPAny method of any Spring bean: services, repositories, utilities…22+

Services, repositories and helpers also need logic before, after or around methods. Aspect-Oriented Programming is a separate Spring Framework module for that. Often found hard at first; the lecture builds up from the problem.

Setup

  • aop-introduction-demo, Spring Web only.
  • Student DTO: name, age, rollNumber, subject.
  • StudentController: POST /api/students → studentService.createStudent(student), returns "Done".
  • StudentService.createStudent prints "Student is saved successfully" (real app: studentRepository.save(student) + response DTO).

The problem: production code buries the business logic

The business logic of createStudent is one line: save the student. Production needs much more around it:

public StudentResponseDto createStudent(Student student) {
    securityService.checkPermission("CREATE_STUDENT");          // permission check
    log.info("StudentService : createStudent called");          // logging
    long start = System.currentTimeMillis();                    // execution time
    try {
        studentRepository.save(student);                        // ← the ONLY business logic
        auditService.record("CREATE_STUDENT", student);         // auditing
        StudentResponseDto result = mapToDto(student);
        cache.put(student.getRollNumber(), result);             // caching
        return result;
    } catch (Exception ex) {
        log.error("createStudent failed", ex);                  // error logging
        throw ex;
    } finally {
        long duration = System.currentTimeMillis() - start;     // execution time
        log.info("createStudent took {} ms", duration);
    }
}
  • Permission checks can't rely only on filters/interceptors: not every call starts at an API. A service can call another internally (e.g. PrincipalService calling createStudent), bypassing the web layer.
  • Times 50 methods per class and hundreds/thousands of classes: "you spend half your time reading infrastructure code before you find the business logic".

Two kinds of logic, two kinds of concern

Business logicInfrastructure / supporting logic
ExamplesCreate a student, place an order, verify a paymentLogging, security, execution timing, auditing, caching, exception handling
Varies byDomain: each app differsHardly varies: same boilerplate everywhere, different keys/values
Shape of the concernVertical: all student logic in StudentService, payment in PaymentServiceHorizontal: logging needed in every class
NameCore / business concernsCross-cutting concerns
Definition: concern and cross-cutting concern: a concern is an area of interest (student, payment, order management). A cross-cutting concern cuts across many classes/modules instead of living in one — logging, security, timing. Spring AOP's stated purpose is handling these, so know the term precisely.

The two symptoms

SymptomMeaningSeen in
ScatteringOne concern spread across many classesLogging in StudentService, OrderService, PaymentService, TeacherService…
TanglingOne method mixes many concernscreateStudent interleaving security, logging, timing, auditing, caching with one save

Attempt 1: helper / utility classes

public class LoggingServiceUtil {                    // no @Component: static helpers
    public static void logStart(String className, String methodName) {
        System.out.println("Executing " + className + " : " + methodName);
    }
    public static void logEnd(String className, String methodName) {
        System.out.println("Finishing " + className + " : " + methodName);
    }
}

// in StudentService.createStudent:
LoggingServiceUtil.logStart("StudentService", "createStudent");
studentRepository.save(student);
LoggingServiceUtil.logEnd("StudentService", "createStudent");

Why it fails:

  • Logic reusable, but the calls remain in every method: still scattered and tangled.
  • Developers can forget the call; audit logs silently go missing.
  • Success/failure paths need try/catch with separate logs; the method keeps growing.

Attempt 2: inheritance

Every service extends a base LoggingService. Same failure: each method still calls logStart()/logEnd(). Plus Java's single inheritance means one base class can't supply logging, security and timing together.

Attempt 3: wrappers, the Decorator pattern

Keep the business class clean; wrap it in an object implementing the same interface that adds behaviour then delegates — the Decorator pattern (LLD notebook; wrappers also in Lecture 20).

public interface StudentService {
    void createStudent(Student student);
}

@Service
public class StudentServiceImpl implements StudentService {        // business logic ONLY
    @Override
    public void createStudent(Student student) {
        studentRepository.save(student);
        System.out.println("Student saved");
    }
}

@Component
public class LoggingDecorator implements StudentService {           // is-a StudentService
    private final StudentServiceImpl studentServiceImpl;            // has-a StudentService

    public LoggingDecorator(StudentServiceImpl studentServiceImpl) {
        this.studentServiceImpl = studentServiceImpl;
    }

    @Override
    public void createStudent(Student student) {
        LoggingServiceUtil.logStart("StudentServiceImpl", "createStudent");   // before
        studentServiceImpl.createStudent(student);                           // delegate
        LoggingServiceUtil.logEnd("StudentServiceImpl", "createStudent");     // after
    }
}

@Component
@Primary                                     // controller gets the OUTERMOST wrapper
public class ExecutionTimeService implements StudentService {
    private final LoggingDecorator loggingDecorator;

    public ExecutionTimeService(LoggingDecorator loggingDecorator) {
        this.loggingDecorator = loggingDecorator;
    }

    @Override
    public void createStudent(Student student) {
        long start = System.currentTimeMillis();
        loggingDecorator.createStudent(student);
        long duration = System.currentTimeMillis() - start;
        System.out.println("Duration: " + duration);
    }
}

// StudentController now depends on the interface:
private final StudentService studentService;     // Spring injects the @Primary one
  • Injection conflict: with two, then three StudentService beans, injection is ambiguous ("more than one bean of StudentService"). @Primary on the outermost wrapper fixes it (Lecture 5); @Qualifier works too.
  • Demo: "Executing …" → "Student saved" → "Finishing …". Duration showed 0 ms until Thread.sleep(2000) simulated slow work → ~2000 ms.
  • StudentServiceImpl holds only business logic. Decorators stack indefinitely (decorator 3 = authentication…).

Verified (Boot 4.1.0): injected bean was ExecutionTimeDecorator; output "Executing …" → "Student saved: Aditya" → "Finishing …" → "Duration: 204 ms" with a 200 ms sleep.

Why decorators still aren't the answer:
  • A wrapper per service per concern: classes × concerns. A 20-method service needs every method re-implemented in every decorator.
  • Hand-wiring is error-prone: give decorator 3 decorator 1 by mistake and decorator 2 silently never runs; forget one and a concern is missing.
Right idea, wrong to write by hand. AOP generates these wrappers for you.

AOP: declare what, where and when once

QuestionExampleAOP term (added)
What should happen?LogAdvice: the code to run
Where?All methods of all service classesPointcut: expression selecting join points (method executions)
When?At method start and endAdvice type: @Before, @After, @AfterReturning, @AfterThrowing, @Around
  • An aspect = a class bundling advice and pointcuts for one concern.
  • Spring wraps matching beans automatically, "somewhere in the background, where we don't see the code". As IoC freed us from managing objects, AOP lifts infrastructure code into its own module.
OOP vs AOP (the lecture's comparison)
OOPAOP
Unit of modularityClass (real entity: Student, StudentService)Aspect (a concern: logging, security)
OrganisesBusiness logic, verticallyCross-cutting concerns, horizontally
RelationshipComplementary: AOP works on top of OOP code without changing it
One-liner from the lecture: "Just as Spring gave us beans so we don't manage objects, it gave us AOP so we don't manage infrastructure logic in every class." Writing aspects and internals: next lecture.

Worth knowing (preview, added)

  • Spring AOP creates proxies — auto-generated wrappers, Attempt 3's decorators written for you: JDK dynamic proxy when proxying via an interface; CGLIB subclass otherwise (Boot's default). Strictly the Proxy pattern (same interface, control access), but structurally identical to Decorator.
  • You already use AOP: @Transactional, @Cacheable, @Async, @PreAuthorize, @Retryable are aspects/proxies.
  • Proxy-based limitations: only Spring beans and method execution; self-invocation (this.otherMethod()) bypasses the proxy, so no advice; private/final methods can't be intercepted. AspectJ (compile-/load-time weaving) lifts these.
  • Design note: the demo's decorators depend on concrete classes (StudentServiceImpl, LoggingDecorator). Depending on the StudentService interface with @Qualifier makes them reorderable/reusable — Decorator as usually taught.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"What, how and when" → the lecturer corrects it to what, where and when.
"the monolith / microservices both have concerns" was said as "agolith" → monolith.

Lecture 23: Writing Your First Aspect — @Aspect, Pointcuts and the Five Advice Types

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #23 · 1h28m · Code Army · continues Lecture 22; pointcut syntax and JoinPoint methods come next lecture

Recap: Proxy or Decorator?

Controller calls the outermost wrapper; each wrapper works and hands on; the result returns the same way.

Spring calls the wrappers proxies. Interview: does Spring AOP use Proxy or Decorator?

The lecture's answer:
  • Structurally the same (near-identical UML; Decorator usually adds an abstract decorator layer for stacking).
  • Differ in intent: Decorator enhances (logging, auth… layered); Proxy controls access (remote, virtual, protection proxy…).
  • Both defensible: Spring says "proxy"; adding behaviour = decorator. Explain shared structure, different intent.
Worth adding: one Spring proxy runs a chain of advice (interceptor chain), not one proxy per aspect. Patterns: see LLD notebook.

Setup: AOP dependency (renamed in Boot 4)

aop-demo starts with spring-boot-starter-webmvc (+ test starter). Maven Central lists spring-aop and spring-boot-starter-aop, but the old starter doesn't work with Boot 4:

<dependency>
    <groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
    <artifactId>spring-boot-starter-aspectj</artifactId>
</dependency>
  • Boot 3.x: spring-boot-starter-aop; Boot 4.x: spring-boot-starter-aspectj (verified, 4.1.0).
  • Drop copied <version>/<scope>; Boot parent manages it.
  • AspectJ = separate full AOP library; Spring AOP borrows its annotations and pointcut language.
Worth knowing: Boot needs no @EnableAspectJAutoProxy (auto-config); plain Spring adds it on a @Configuration class. (Added.)

The target: controller and service

Packages controller, service only:

@RestController
@RequestMapping("/api/students")
public class StudentController {
    private final StudentService studentService;

    public StudentController(StudentService studentService) {
        this.studentService = studentService;
    }

    @PostMapping
    public ResponseEntity<String> createStudent() {
        String s = studentService.createStudent();
        return ResponseEntity.ok(s);
    }
}

@Service
public class StudentService {
    public String createStudent() {
        System.out.println("Student saved");
        return "Student saved";
    }
}

Goal: print "A student is going to be saved" before the service runs, without editing createStudent() (no infrastructure in business logic, Lecture 22). AOP can intercept any bean method; services are typical (controllers already have filters/interceptors).

Writing the aspect: @Component + @Aspect

Aspects go in package aspect:

package in.strikes.demo.aspect;

import org.aspectj.lang.annotation.Aspect;
import org.aspectj.lang.annotation.Before;
import org.springframework.stereotype.Component;

@Aspect
@Component
public class LoggingAspect {

    @Before("execution(String in.strikes.demo.service.StudentService.createStudent())")
    public void logBeforeMethod() {
        System.out.println("A student is going to be saved");
    }
}
  • @Component — aspect must be a bean, else ignored.
  • @Aspect — its methods intercept others. Not a stereotype → need both.
  • Each method = one interception; class name = kind (logging).
QuestionFor this exampleAnswered byAOP term
What?Print the log lineBody of logBeforeMethod()Advice (method + timing annotation)
When?Before createStudent@Before
Where?StudentService.createStudent()Expression in @Before(...)Pointcut

Pointcut parts:

  • execution(...) — intercept method execution; String — return type.
  • in.strikes.demo.service.StudentService.createStudent — fully qualified class + method.
  • () — no params.
A student is going to be saved
Student saved

Pointcut syntax next lecture; this one is about advice.

The vocabulary

  • Aspect — class (LoggingAspect) for one cross-cutting concern.
  • Advice — method + timing annotation (@Before + logBeforeMethod()): what + when.
  • Pointcut — expression selecting where.
  • Target — real intercepted object (StudentService bean).
  • Proxy — wrapper through which the target is reached.
Precision note (added): lecture calls the aspect method "the proxy"; the proxy is the Spring-generated object (CGLIB subclass like StudentService$$SpringCGLIB$$0, verified); the method is the advice. Join point = where advice can run (Spring AOP: always a method execution); pointcut = predicate selecting join points. Weaving = linking aspects to targets; Spring does it at runtime via proxies.

Inside: proxy between controller and service

At injection, Spring sees StudentService is intercepted and injects (conceptually) a StudentProxy:

  • is-a StudentService (subclass or shared interface) — controller can't tell.
  • Overrides createStudent(): run LoggingAspect.logBeforeMethod(), then the real method via a held reference.
You never touch the proxy: two objects internally, one bean for you (StudentService).

The five advice types: one try/catch/finally

All answer when:

// what the proxy conceptually does around the target method
beforeLogic();                          // @Before
try {
    Object result = targetMethod();     // the intercepted method
    afterReturningLogic(result);        // @AfterReturning  (success only)
    return result;
} catch (Exception ex) {
    afterThrowingLogic(ex);             // @AfterThrowing   (failure only)
    throw ex;                           // rethrown to the caller (controller)
} finally {
    afterLogic();                       // @After           (always)
}
// @Around = you write this whole block yourself
AdviceRunsPositionTypical uses
@BeforeBefore targetAbove tryEntry logging, auth, preconditions
@AfterReturningOnly on normal returnIn tryLog/audit result
@AfterThrowingOnly if threwcatchLog/monitor failures
@AfterAlwaysfinallyRelease locks/resources, clear caches
@AroundWraps the callWhole blockTiming, retries, change args/result, swallow exceptions
Confusion settled: without exceptions @After = @AfterReturning in effect. With one: @AfterReturning skipped, @AfterThrowing runs, @After runs "just like a finally block".

@Before: blocks only by throwing

Start logging, auth, preconditions (real auth: Spring Security; fine for small apps, like Lectures 19–21 filters).

Can @Before stop the target?

  • Not by returning — return; ends only the advice (verified).
  • Yes, by throwing — proxy never reaches the target:
@Before("execution(String in.strikes.demo.service.StudentService.createStudent())")
public void logBeforeMethod() {
    System.out.println("A student is going to be saved");
    boolean allowed = false;               // stand-in for a real auth check
    if (!allowed) {
        throw new RuntimeException("Method execution not allowed");
    }
}

Postman 500; "Student saved" never prints.

Reading arguments with JoinPoint

@Before("execution(String in.strikes.demo.service.StudentService.createStudent())")
public void logBeforeMethod(JoinPoint joinPoint) {
    Object[] arr = joinPoint.getArgs();   // arguments, as an Object array
    System.out.println("A student is going to be saved");
}

Any advice may take JoinPoint (call info; API next lecture). Non-around advice can read args, not modify; that's @Around's job.

Precision note (verified, Boot 4.1.0): getArgs() returns a copy: args[0] = x has no effect. Elements are shared references, so mutating a mutable arg (((Student) args[0]).setName(...)) does reach the target — same bad hack as the @AfterReturning one below.

@AfterReturning: success only, can't change result

@AfterReturning("execution(String in.strikes.demo.service.StudentService.createStudent())")
public void logAfterReturningMethod() {
    System.out.println("logAfterReturningMethod called");
}

Prints after "Student saved". (@Before commented out from here.)

When the target throws

  • Service throws new RuntimeException("Some error occurred") → propagates to controller; advice skipped; 500.
  • Service catches its own exception (try { throw … } catch (RuntimeException e) {}) → returns normally; advice runs.
  • Advice can't catch target exceptions (can't call the target) → needs @Around.

Capturing the return value: returning

@AfterReturning(
    value = "execution(String in.strikes.demo.service.StudentService.createStudent())",
    returning = "result")
public void logAfterReturningMethod(String result) {
    System.out.println("Target method returned: " + result);
}
  • returning = "result" must equal the parameter name.
  • Parameter type = expected return (Object if unknown).
  • With a second attribute, name the pointcut (value = / pointcut =).

Output: "Student saved", "Target method returned: Student saved".

Experiment: return a modified string

Advice returning result + ...: Postman still gets Student saved (verified). Return is ignored → declare void.

Experiment: mutate a returned object (possible, don't)

DTO Student (private String name; private int age;). Controller takes @RequestBody Student student → studentService.createStudent(student) returns it. Postman sends {"name": "Aditya", "age": 28}.

  1. Advice param Student result; result.setName("Rohit"); result.setAge(21);.
  2. First run: nothing — pointcut still says String …createStudent(), no match. (A String-typed returning param also silently never fires for a Student return.)
  3. Fix: fully qualified in.strikes.demo.dto.Student return and (in.strikes.demo.dto.Student) param (only java.lang types may be short).
  4. Postman gets {"name": "Rohit", "age": 21} (verified) — same object mutated.
  5. Shortcut (next lecture): execution(* in.strikes.demo.service.StudentService.createStudent(..)) — * any return, (..) any params.
The lecture's verdict: "Possible, but a very wrong practice." Works only on mutable objects; can't replace the result or change a String (immutable). Use @Around.

@AfterThrowing: failure only, observe not handle

@AfterThrowing(
    value = "execution(* in.strikes.demo.service.StudentService.createStudent(..))",
    throwing = "exception")
public void logAfterThrowingMethod(Throwable exception) {
    System.out.println("Exception type: " + exception.getClass().getName());
    System.out.println("Exception message: " + exception.getMessage());
}
  • No throw → advice silent.
  • Service throws new RuntimeException("Some error happened") → "Student saved", advice prints java.lang.RuntimeException / "Some error happened", exception reaches controller (500) — the model's catch { log; throw ex; }.
  • throwing = "exception" must match the parameter name.

Parameter type filters exceptions

  • Throwable — any exception.
  • RuntimeException — matches the demo.
  • NullPointerException — not fired for a plain RuntimeException (verified): NPE is a subclass, not a parent.

What it cannot do

  • Consume the exception — no try possible (can't invoke the target), and catch alone won't compile.
  • Replace the outcome — returning "done" changes nothing (verified). Keep void.

Use: monitoring, auditing, alerting.

@After: always, like finally

@After("execution(* in.strikes.demo.service.StudentService.createStudent(..))")
public void logAfterMethod() {
    System.out.println("logAfterMethod executed");
}
  • Throws: "Student saved", "logAfterMethod executed", exception propagates.
  • Returns: same two lines, normal result.

Can't change the return or consume the exception. Use for finally work: release locks/resources, clear caches.

Summary from the lecture: @AfterReturning = success, @AfterThrowing = failure, @After = "whatever happens". None can modify the result or handle the exception — monitor only.

@Around: the most powerful advice

Runs before and after; you decide if/when the target runs. Can change args and return, consume exceptions, call the target repeatedly. The target runs via ProceedingJoinPoint (child of JoinPoint that can execute it):

@Around("execution(* in.strikes.demo.service.StudentService.createStudent(..))")
public void logAroundMethod(ProceedingJoinPoint joinPoint) throws Throwable {
    System.out.println("Before target method");
    joinPoint.proceed();                    // run the target now
    System.out.println("After target method");
}
  • proceed() throws Throwable → advice declares it or uses try/catch.
  • Three lines print but Postman body is empty: void advice drops the result → proxy returns null → ResponseEntity.ok(null) (200, no body).
Worth knowing (verified): a void @Around on a primitive-returning method (int) fails: AopInvocationException: Null return value from advice does not match primitive return type. Around advice should return Object from proceed().

Fix: return the result (proceed() gives Object; cast if needed):

@Around("execution(* in.strikes.demo.service.StudentService.createStudent(..))")
public Student logAroundMethod(ProceedingJoinPoint joinPoint) throws Throwable {
    System.out.println("Before target method");
    Student student = (Student) joinPoint.proceed();
    student.setAge(31);          // around may legitimately change the result
    student.setName("Rohit");
    System.out.println("After target method");
    return student;
}

Unlike @AfterReturning, the advice's return is what the caller gets (could be a different object or changed String).

Not calling proceed()

Only the before/after lines print; "Student saved" never does; empty body. Advice controls whether the target runs. Classic use: timing around proceed().

Exceptions inside @Around

"Just like a Java method calling another method": uncaught target exception skips the rest and reaches the controller (console: "Before target method", "Student saved").

Worked example: generic around = all four others

@Around("execution(* in.strikes.demo.service.StudentService.createStudent(..))")
public Object logAroundMethod(ProceedingJoinPoint joinPoint) throws Throwable {
    System.out.println("Starting " + joinPoint.getSignature().getName()); // like @Before
    try {
        Object result = joinPoint.proceed();
        System.out.println("Execution successful");                     // like @AfterReturning
        return result;
    } catch (Exception e) {
        System.out.println("Execution failed: " + e.getMessage());      // like @AfterThrowing
        throw e;                                                        // rethrow to the controller
    } finally {
        System.out.println("Execution completed");                      // like @After
    }
}
  • joinPoint.getSignature().getName() = createStudent.
  • Returns Object → works for any target without casting.

Demo run

CaseConsole outputPostman
Service throws "Some error happened"Starting createStudent → Student saved → Execution failed: Some error happened → Execution completed500
Service returns normallyStarting createStudent → Student saved → Execution successful → Execution completedThe student JSON

throw e; → return null; = exception consumed.

Don't use @Around for everything: use the most specific advice — @Before for before; @AfterReturning/@AfterThrowing/@After for success/failure/both. (Added, Spring docs: narrower advice can't forget proceed(), drop the result, or swallow exceptions.)
Worth flagging (added): catching Exception misses Errors (OutOfMemoryError) and sneaky-thrown checked Throwables; catch Throwable to log everything (rethrown anyway).

Around's full power: change args/return, call twice

// StudentController
@GetMapping
public ResponseEntity<String> dummyMethod() {
    String s = "Aditya";
    return ResponseEntity.ok(studentService.dummyMethod(s));
}

// StudentService
public String dummyMethod(String s) {
    System.out.println("dummy method called");
    return s;
}

GET /api/students returns Aditya. Aspect: execution(* in.strikes.demo.service.StudentService.dummyMethod(..)). Plain proceed() passes original args through.

Changing arguments: proceed(Object[] args)

@Around("execution(* in.strikes.demo.service.StudentService.dummyMethod(..))")
public Object logAroundMethod(ProceedingJoinPoint joinPoint) throws Throwable {
    Object[] arr = joinPoint.getArgs();
    String originalString = (String) arr[0];               // one String parameter
    String modifiedString = originalString.toUpperCase();
    Object[] modifiedArr = { modifiedString };
    String returnType = (String) joinPoint.proceed(modifiedArr); // target gets "ADITYA"
    returnType = returnType + " intercepted";                    // change the result too
    return returnType;
}
  • Target gets ADITYA; Postman: ADITYA intercepted.
  • Args only as a fresh Object[].
  • (returnType holds a value, not a type.)
Worth knowing (verified): arg count must match: two for a one-arg method → IllegalArgumentException: Expecting 1 arguments to proceed, but was passed 2 arguments; types must be compatible. proceed(newArgs) replaces the join point's args — a later plain proceed() also sees "ADITYA".

Calling the target more than once

Object return1 = joinPoint.proceed();
System.out.println("Intercepted request, calling again");
Object return2 = joinPoint.proceed();
return return2;

"dummy method called" prints twice. Loops (retries) possible.

Why this is dangerous: only for idempotent ops — a payment/email would happen twice. The retry-aspect concern.

Advice types compared

Capability@Before@AfterReturning@AfterThrowing@After@Around
Parameter typeJoinPointJoinPoint + resultJoinPoint + exceptionJoinPointProceedingJoinPoint (required)
Read argumentsYesYesYesYesYes
Replace argumentsNoNoNoNoYes, proceed(args)
See return valueNoYes (returning)NoNoYes
Replace return valueNoNo (ignored)NoNoYes
See exception—NoYes (throwing)NoYes
Swallow exception—NoNoNoYes
Prevent target runningOnly by throwingNo (already ran)NoNoYes, skip proceed()
Run target several timesNoNoNoNoYes

Anticipated question: order of all five in one aspect?

(Not in lecture.) Since Spring 5.2.7, same-aspect precedence: @Around, @Before, @After, @AfterReturning, @AfterThrowing; @After acts as the outer finally (AspectJ semantics). Observed on Boot 4.1.0:

SuccessFailure
@Around (before part) → @Before → target → @AfterReturning → @After → @Around (after part)@Around (before part) → @Before → target → @AfterThrowing → @After → @Around (finally part) → exception to caller

Matches the try/catch/finally model. Two same-type advices in one aspect: undefined order. Across aspects: @Order/Ordered on the class; lower = outer.

Corrections & worth knowing

  • @Aspect is AspectJ's in Boot 3 too. Lecture implies an "aop.lang.annotation" package under Boot 3; both Boot 3 (spring-boot-starter-aop) and 4 (spring-boot-starter-aspectj) use org.aspectj.lang.annotation.Aspect (aspectjweaver jar). Only the starter name changed.
  • JoinPoint/ProceedingJoinPoint are interfaces (org.aspectj.lang); ProceedingJoinPoint extends JoinPoint, adds proceed()/proceed(Object[]); @Around only.
  • Aspect method = advice, not proxy (proxy is Spring-generated CGLIB by default in Boot).
  • Spring AOP uses AspectJ's annotations/pointcut language, not its weaver. Proxy-based, runtime: only public (CGLIB: also protected/package non-final) bean methods called from outside. AspectJ weaves bytecode (compile/load time): constructors, fields, private methods, self-calls (Lecture 22's limits).
  • Silent non-matching = most common AOP bug. Seen 3×: stale pointcut after refactor, wrong returning type, @AfterThrowing param a subclass of the thrown exception. No error, advice never runs. Use broad pointcuts (*, (..)) or custom annotations.
  • Non-around advice return values ignored (verified) — make them void.
  • Real uses: @Transactional, @Cacheable = around (begin/commit/rollback; return cached value without proceeding); @Retryable = proceed() in a loop (idempotency!); Micrometer @Timed = timing around proceed().
Transcript slips to ignore:
"prog-ji" / "prog-gee" = proxy.
"login aspect" / "login logic" = logging.
"in.stries.dm" = in.strikes.demo.
"LED video" = LLD series.
"String is non-immutable" → immutable (why @AfterReturning couldn't change it).
"when and what together we call an advice" / "it answers what and when" — advice = what + when.

Lecture 24: Pointcut Expressions — Wildcards, Designators, Named Pointcuts and this vs target

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #24 · 1h19m · Code Army · answers AOP's "where?" question (Lecture 23 answered "what" and "when")

Setup: same demo, extended

  • StudentController — POST /api/students → createStudent(@RequestBody Student); GET → getStudent().
  • StudentService — createStudent(Student) prints "Student saved", returns it; getStudent(...) returns "All student data".
  • Student DTO — name, age.
  • LoggingAspect — each experiment = @Before printing "Method intercepted".

Anatomy of an execution pointcut

First word = designator (execution, within, bean…). Inside execution(...):

execution( [access-modifier]  return-type  [declaring-type.]method-name(params)  [throws exception] )
execution( public             String       in.strikes.aopdemo.service.StudentService.getStudent(String) )
PartRequired?Example
Access modifierOptional (omitted = any)public, protected
Return typeRequiredString, *
Declaring class + method nameMethod required; class optional (see Corrections)in.strikes.aopdemo.service.StudentService.getStudent
ParametersRequired (even ())(String), (), (..)
ExceptionOptionalthrows java.io.IOException
  • Modifier usually omitted; protected to target those (proxies reach only public/protected/package methods).
  • @Before("execution(String in.strikes.aopdemo.service.StudentService.getStudent())") → "Method intercepted", "All student data".
  • When it becomes getStudent(String s), pointcut must say getStudent(String) or it stops matching.

Exact createStudent(Student) needs fully qualified types (only java.lang may be short):

@Before("execution(in.strikes.aopdemo.dto.Student " +
        "in.strikes.aopdemo.service.StudentService.createStudent(" +
        "in.strikes.aopdemo.dto.Student))")
public void logBeforeMethod2() { System.out.println("Method intercepted"); }

Why exact pointcuts defeat AOP

Three lines per method; 20 methods = 20 pointcuts — the scattering AOP removes (Lecture 22). Fix: wildcards (patterns).

Wildcard 1: * — exactly one "anything"

PointcutMatches
execution(* …StudentService.getStudent(String))Any return type (String, Integer, Long, void, object…)
execution(* …StudentService.getStudent(*))Exactly one param, any type
execution(* …StudentService.getStudent(*, *))Exactly two params
execution(* …StudentService.getStudent(*, int))Two params, second int
execution(* …StudentService.*(*))Any method with one param: createStudent + getStudent
execution(* …StudentService.get*(*))Names starting with get
execution(* …StudentService.*Student(*))Names ending with Student
execution(* in.strikes.aopdemo.service.*.*(*))Any method of any class directly in service (1st * class, 2nd method)
execution(* *(*))Any one-arg method anywhere (too broad)

Verified: service.*.*(*) hit createStudent, getStudent, not zero-arg dummyMethod(). Package * = one segment; service.subservice needs ...

Wildcard 2: .. — "zero or more"

public int dummyMethod() { return 0; } escapes (*); (..) = any number of params. In a package, .. = package + all sub-packages.

Where*..
Return typeAny typeNot allowed
PackageOne segmentAny depth
Class / method nameAny name or part (get*)Not meaningful
ParametersExactly oneZero or more
  • execution(* in.strikes.aopdemo.service.StudentService.*(..)) — every StudentService method.
  • execution(* in.strikes.aopdemo.service..*(..)) — whole service tree (verified: hit service.sub.Dummy.hello()); docs' service..*.*(..) identical.

"Match everything" crashes startup

execution(* *(..)):

Error creating bean with name '…TomcatServletWebServerAutoConfiguration':
Could not generate CGLIB subclass of class …TomcatServletWebServerAutoConfiguration
Caused by: java.lang.IllegalArgumentException: Cannot subclass final class …

It matches Spring's own beans; some can't be proxied (verified, Boot 4.1.0: Tomcat auto-config class is final; CGLIB proxies are subclasses). Fix: limit to base package:

@Before("execution(* in.strikes.aopdemo..*(..))")

POST now prints "Method intercepted" twice (controller + service), then "Student saved".

Worth knowing (verified): base-package pointcuts also catch @Bean methods of your @Configuration classes (hit the main class's CommandLineRunner bean at startup). Prefer layer pointcuts (within(…service..*)).

Designator 2: within — whole classes

execution = method level; within = class level (all methods, no signature):

@Before("within(in.strikes.aopdemo.service.StudentService)")  // all methods of one class
@Before("within(in.strikes.aopdemo.service.*)")               // classes directly in service
@Before("within(in.strikes.aopdemo.service..*)")              // whole service layer, sub-packages too

Same power as execution, reads better for "whole class/layer".

Designator 3: @annotation — annotated methods

Demo: jdk.jfr.Timestamp on createStudent:

@Before("@annotation(jdk.jfr.Timestamp)")
public void logBeforeMethod() { System.out.println("Method intercepted"); }

Only createStudent hit (verified). Fully qualified name; RUNTIME retention.

Why it's the most useful

With your own annotations (later lecture) the method declares needs; logic lives in an aspect:

  • @Authenticate → AuthenticationAspect checks token/key (real apps: Spring Security).
  • @Audit → logs/records metrics.
  • @EvaluateTime → around advice times the call.
  • Stackable: @Audit @EvaluateTime.
@Around("@annotation(in.strikes.aopdemo.annotation.EvaluateTime)")
public Object evaluateTimeMethod(ProceedingJoinPoint joinPoint) throws Throwable {
    long startTime = System.currentTimeMillis();
    Object result = joinPoint.proceed();
    long endTime = System.currentTimeMillis();
    System.out.println("Took " + (endTime - startTime) + " ms");
    return result;       // the lecture's sketch omits this; see Lecture 23
}

How @Transactional, @Cacheable, @Async work.

Designator 4: bean — by bean name

@Before("bean(studentService)")
  • All methods of bean studentService (default name = class name, lower-case first letter).
  • Works for @Bean-method beans by name.
  • Spring-only (not AspectJ); wildcards: bean(*Service). (Added.)
The four you'll use 95% of the time: execution, within, @annotation, bean.

Combining: &&, ||, !

// every method of StudentService OR StudentController
@Before("bean(studentService) || bean(studentController)")

// every PUBLIC method in the service layer
@Before("within(in.strikes.aopdemo.service..*) && execution(public * *(..))")

bean(studentService) && bean(studentController) matches nothing. XML: and, or, not (added).

Named pointcuts: @Pointcut

Name a reused expression once on an empty void method; method name = pointcut name:

@Pointcut("within(in.strikes.aopdemo.service..*) && execution(public * *(..))")
public void logPublicServiceMethod() {
    // empty body: this method only carries the pointcut
}

@Before("logPublicServiceMethod()")
public void logBefore() { … }

@Around("logPublicServiceMethod()")
public Object logAround(ProceedingJoinPoint jp) throws Throwable { return jp.proceed(); }

Name it for its scope. Never called at runtime.

Industry practice: one ApplicationPointcuts class

Reusable pointcuts in one class (aspect package), referenced by fully qualified name from every aspect. Broad first, then combined:

public class ApplicationPointcuts {

    @Pointcut("within(in.strikes.aopdemo.controller..*)")
    public void controllerLayer() {}

    @Pointcut("within(in.strikes.aopdemo.service..*)")
    public void serviceLayer() {}

    @Pointcut("execution(public * *(..))")
    public void publicMethod() {}

    @Pointcut("serviceLayer() && publicMethod()")
    public void publicServiceMethod() {}

    @Pointcut("execution(* get*(..)) && publicMethod()")
    public void getterMethod() {}
}

// in LoggingAspect
@Before("in.strikes.aopdemo.aspect.ApplicationPointcuts.publicServiceMethod()")
public void logBeforeMethod() { System.out.println("Method intercepted"); }
  • Add repository layer, public controller methods, setters… likewise.
  • Inline only narrow, single-use pointcuts.
  • Verified: holder class needs no annotations.
Worth flagging: getterMethod() matches every public get* on every bean (Spring's too) → combine with a layer (serviceLayer() && getterMethod()) or risk the startup crash. Name-based matching is fragile: any getX opts in.

Rarer: @within and @target

Take an annotation, class level: all methods of annotated classes.

@Before("@within(org.springframework.stereotype.Service)")   // all methods of @Service classes
@Before("@target(org.springframework.stereotype.Service)")

within = class name; @within/@target = class annotation; @annotation = method annotation.

The difference shows with inheritance

  • @within = declaring class → BaseService → not intercepted.
  • @target = runtime object → StudentService → intercepted.
  • Same without inheritance. Verified, Boot 4.1.0.
Worth knowing (verified): @target(...) alone crashes startup ("Cannot subclass final class") — runtime-decided, so every bean that might match gets proxied. Scope it: @target(…Service) && within(in.strikes.aopdemo..*). within(StudentService) also skips inherited saveStudent.

Rarer: args and @args

Method level, by runtime arguments:

  • args(in.strikes.aopdemo.dto.Student) — called with a single Student.
  • @args(jdk.jfr.Timestamp) — single arg whose class has @Timestamp (put on Student).

Alone, args(Student) also crashes: countless framework methods take Object that could be a Student. Scope it:

@Before("args(in.strikes.aopdemo.dto.Student) && within(in.strikes.aopdemo.service..*)")
@Before("@args(jdk.jfr.Timestamp) && within(in.strikes.aopdemo.service..*)")

Both hit only createStudent (verified). @args: "interesting, not very useful".

Pattern in the @ designators:
  • @annotation — on the method.
  • @within / @target — on the class (declaring / runtime).
  • @args — on the argument's class.
Worth adding: args mostly binds: @Before("serviceLayer() && args(student)") public void log(Student student) = typed arg, no joinPoint.getArgs()[0] cast. @annotation(evaluateTime) binds the annotation likewise.

Background: CGLIB vs JDK dynamic proxies

Real object = target; proxy = this. Two proxy kinds:

JDK dynamic proxyCGLIB proxy
HowImplements the same interface (like Lecture 22's decorators)Extends the class, overrides, runs advice, calls super.method()
NeedsAn interfaceNon-final class and methods
Proxy instanceof StudentService?No (verified: jdk.proxy2.$Proxy63)Yes (verified: StudentService$$SpringCGLIB$$0)
DefaultPlain Spring (@EnableAspectJAutoProxy) with an interfaceSpring Boot (since 2.0), even with an interface

Switching Boot to JDK proxies: two things

  1. Interface StudentServiceInterface (createStudent, getStudent); StudentService implements it. (Usually StudentService / StudentServiceImpl.)
  2. spring.aop.proxy-target-class=false (default true).
  • Property without interface → still CGLIB.
  • Interface without property → still CGLIB.
  • Then startup failed: "expected a bean of type …StudentService" — controller injected the concrete class. Fix: inject StudentServiceInterface (loose coupling, V1/V2/V3 implementations).

Rarer: this vs target

  • target(in.strikes.aopdemo.service.StudentService) — is the real object a StudentService?
  • this(in.strikes.aopdemo.service.StudentService) — is the proxy?
Proxy typetarget(StudentService)this(StudentService)
CGLIB (Boot default)MatchesMatches (proxy extends it)
JDK dynamic proxyMatchesNo match (only the interface)

Verified, Boot 4.1.0: JDK proxies → target still intercepts, this nothing.

The lecturer's point: "this = target" only under Boot's CGLIB default (where within behaves alike too); JDK proxies expose the difference.
Worth flagging (verified): even with CGLIB, this(StudentService) missed inherited BaseService.saveStudent(); target hit it. Prefer target.

Designator cheat sheet

DesignatorLevelTakesMatchesUse
executionMethodSignature patternMatching methodsVery common
withinClassType / package patternMethods declared in those typesVery common
@annotationMethodAnnotationMethods carrying itVery common
beanBeanBean name (wildcards OK)All methods of that beanCommon (Spring-only)
@withinClassAnnotationMethods declared in annotated classesRare
@targetClassAnnotationCalls on objects whose runtime class is annotatedRare; scope it
argsMethodTypesCalls with those runtime arg typesMostly binding; scope it
@argsMethodAnnotationCalls whose argument's class is annotatedRare; scope it
thisClassTypeProxy is that typeRare
targetClassTypeTarget is that typeRare

Corrections & worth knowing

  • Declaring type in execution is optional (lecture: mandatory). execution(* dummyMethod(..)) matches in any class (verified). Grammar: execution(modifiers? ret-type declaring-type? name(params) throws?).
  • Named pointcuts resolve at context startup (lecture: "compile time"). javac sees a plain string; Spring (AspectJ parser) resolves logPublicServiceMethod() when building proxies → typos fail at startup. (AspectJ ajc checks at compile time.)
  • "Too broad" crashes = proxying unproxyable beans. Static designators (execution, within, @annotation, bean, @within) proxy only matching beans; runtime ones (args, @args, @target, this, target) proxy every bean that might match — always add within(your.base.package..*). Broad pointcuts also slow calls.
  • Plain Spring: JDK proxy if interface, else CGLIB (lecture: JDK by default). Boot sets spring.aop.proxy-target-class=true so injecting concrete classes works.
  • CGLIB appears in errors even for "JDK" setups: interface-less classes always get CGLIB, which can't subclass final classes or override final/private methods. Kotlin classes are final → kotlin-spring (all-open) plugin.
  • Precedence: ! > && > ||; parenthesise when mixing.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"the first star represents the class name and the second star represents the package name" → in service.*.*(..): 1st * = class, 2nd = method.
".. means zero or one argument" → corrected: zero or more.
"wild guards" = wildcards; "prog-ji" = proxy; "SGLIB" / "CG LIB" = CGLIB.
"login aspect" = logging aspect; "post run" / "post one" = Postman.
"log bifore service method" = named pointcut logPublicServiceMethod().
"StudentService overrides the interface" → implements.

Lecture 25: Custom Annotations with AOP — @TrackExecutionTime, Meta-Annotations, and Where Proxies Are Born

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #25 · 43m · Code Army · the last AOP lecture: building your own annotations and how BeanPostProcessor creates proxies

Starting point: @annotation with a borrowed annotation

Same aop-demo: StudentController (POST/GET /api/students), StudentService (createStudent(Student) returns the student; getStudent(String) prints/returns "All student data"), Student DTO, empty SimpleAspect.

  1. @Before("execution(String in.strikes.aopdemo.service.StudentService.getStudent(String))") prints "Method intercepted" (as in Lecture 24).
  2. @Before("@annotation(jdk.jfr.Timestamp)") + @Timestamp on getStudent: still works.

Real power: your own annotation (@PerformLog, @Authenticate…) on exactly the methods needing the behaviour; logic in an aspect.

Why custom annotations are a "superpower": they're declarative. The method only states "authenticate me first" / "time me"; infrastructure logic sits in an aspect, separated from business logic (Lecture 22's goal).

Creating an annotation: @interface

Package annotation, type TrackExecutionTime (first named EvaluateTime): times any annotated method (as earlier with filters/interceptors).

package in.strikes.aopdemo.annotation;

public @interface TrackExecutionTime {
}
  • Declared with @interface, not class or plain interface.
  • Already complete and usable. No elements = marker annotation: it only marks; the aspect gives meaning.

The aspect: time any method marked @TrackExecutionTime

Code before and after → @Around with ProceedingJoinPoint (Lecture 23):

@Around("@annotation(in.strikes.aopdemo.annotation.TrackExecutionTime)")
public Object measureExecutionTime(ProceedingJoinPoint joinPoint) throws Throwable {
    long startTime = System.currentTimeMillis();
    try {
        return joinPoint.proceed();
    } finally {
        long endTime = System.currentTimeMillis();
        long duration = endTime - startTime;
        String methodName = joinPoint.getSignature().getName();
        System.out.println("Time taken by " + methodName + ": " + duration + " ms");
    }
}
  • Returns Object = proceed()'s result. Targets return String, Student, long…; void would swallow it and the client gets null.
  • try without catch. Handling target exceptions isn't this aspect's job (single responsibility); let them reach a global exception handler (Lecture 17) or another aspect.
  • finally. End time recorded even when the target throws.
  • throws Throwable. proceed() can throw anything; not handled here.

Demo run

  1. @TrackExecutionTime on getStudent → "Time taken by getStudent: 0 ms".
  2. Simulated slowness: try { Thread.sleep(2000); } catch (InterruptedException e) {}. Postman waits ~2 s; log ~2000 ms (verified: 2002 ms).
  3. Also on createStudent → "Student saved", "Time taken by createStudent: 0 ms". One annotation times any method.
Worth adding: empty-catch InterruptedException is fine for a demo; real code restores the flag: Thread.currentThread().interrupt();. For durations prefer monotonic System.nanoTime(); currentTimeMillis() jumps if the wall clock is adjusted.

Meta-annotations: where and how long

Meta-annotations (annotations on annotations) answer:

  1. Where can it be applied? → @Target
  2. How long is it kept? → @Retention

@Target(ElementType...): where

@Target(ElementType.METHOD)                        // methods only
@Target({ElementType.METHOD, ElementType.TYPE})    // several: methods and classes
ElementTypeAllowed on
TYPEClasses, interfaces (incl. annotation interfaces), enums, records
METHODMethods
FIELD, PARAMETER, CONSTRUCTOR, LOCAL_VARIABLEWhat the names say
ANNOTATION_TYPEOther annotations — makes a meta-annotation (@Target itself uses it)
PACKAGE, TYPE_PARAMETER, TYPE_USE, MODULE, RECORD_COMPONENTRarer targets
  • @Target(ElementType.TYPE) on a method = compile error: "'@TrackExecutionTime' not applicable to method".
  • No @Target = allowed on (almost) any declaration. Restrict anyway: timing a whole class makes no sense → METHOD only.

@Retention(RetentionPolicy...): how long

PolicyKept inTypical use
SOURCESource only; compiler discards@Override, Lombok, compile-time checks
CLASS.class bytecode, not visible via runtime reflectionBytecode tools
RUNTIMEBytecode and runtime reflectionAnything a framework reads while running: Spring, JPA, validation, AOP

AOP matching happens at runtime (context start, bean creation) → RUNTIME.

@Documented

Javadoc only: annotation appears in generated docs of annotated elements. Harmless.

@Target(ElementType.METHOD)
@Retention(RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME)
@Documented
public @interface TrackExecutionTime {
}

Open @RestController or @Service: @Target(ElementType.TYPE), @Retention(RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME), @Documented (+ @Controller, @ResponseBody for @RestController, Lecture 11).

Worth knowing (added): @Inherited (class-level annotation inherited by subclasses; no effect on methods/interfaces) and @Repeatable (same annotation multiple times). Spring annotations are composable: @RestController = @Controller + @ResponseBody; build your own composed annotations likewise.

Two ways to reference the annotation in the pointcut

FormPointcutAdvice signature
Type-matching@annotation(in.strikes.aopdemo.annotation.TrackExecutionTime)(ProceedingJoinPoint joinPoint)
Binding@annotation(trackExecutionTime)(ProceedingJoinPoint joinPoint, TrackExecutionTime trackExecutionTime)

Binding: name inside @annotation(...) must match the parameter name; parameter type tells Spring which annotation, so no fully qualified name. Bonus: advice receives the annotation instance (needed next).

Configured annotations: adding elements

To carry extra info, declare elements (method-like declarations, optional default). Lecture adds a slow-method threshold and an operation name:

@Target(ElementType.METHOD)
@Retention(RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME)
@Documented
public @interface TrackExecutionTime {
    long warnAfter() default 2000;   // ms; warn if the method is slower
    String operation() default "";   // human-readable label
}
  • With defaults, existing bare @TrackExecutionTime uses keep working.
  • Element without default = mandatory at every use site.
  • Why a threshold: audit trail of slow methods guides optimisation (1 s is slow; 2 s very slow).
@TrackExecutionTime(warnAfter = 1500, operation = "Get student data")
public String getStudent(String s) { … }       // sleeps 2 s, so it should warn

@TrackExecutionTime(warnAfter = 2000, operation = "Creating new student")
public Student createStudent(Student student) { … }

Worked example: the aspect reads the annotation's values

@Around("@annotation(trackExecutionTime)")
public Object measureExecutionTime(ProceedingJoinPoint joinPoint,
                                   TrackExecutionTime trackExecutionTime) throws Throwable {
    long startTime = System.currentTimeMillis();
    try {
        return joinPoint.proceed();
    } finally {
        long duration = System.currentTimeMillis() - startTime;

        String operation = trackExecutionTime.operation();
        if (operation.isBlank()) {
            operation = joinPoint.getSignature().getName();   // fall back to the method name
        }
        long warningThreshold = trackExecutionTime.warnAfter();

        if (duration >= warningThreshold) {
            System.out.println("SLOW OPERATION ALERT: Time taken by " + operation + ": " + duration + " ms");
        } else {
            System.out.println("Time taken by " + operation + ": " + duration + " ms");
        }
    }
}

Demo run

  • GET → "All student data", "SLOW OPERATION ALERT: Time taken by Get student data: 2002 ms" (verified).
  • POST → "Student saved", "Time taken by Creating new student: 0 ms".
  • Bare @TrackExecutionTime logs the method name (verified: "Time taken by dummyMethod: 0 ms").
Worth adding: real apps use a logger (log.warn(...) for slow, log.info/debug otherwise) or a metric, not System.out. Micrometer's @Timed is the production-grade version of this annotation + aspect.

Where proxies are created: BeanPostProcessor in the bean lifecycle

Two lifecycle steps skipped earlier: the BeanPostProcessor callbacks around initialization.

  1. Read bean definitions.
  2. Instantiate the bean.
  3. Inject dependencies.
  4. *Aware callbacks.
  5. BeanPostProcessor.postProcessBeforeInitialization
  6. Initialization callbacks (@PostConstruct, afterPropertiesSet, init-method).
  7. BeanPostProcessor.postProcessAfterInitialization ← proxies are created here
  8. Bean ready for use … destruction callbacks at shutdown.
public interface BeanPostProcessor {
    default Object postProcessBeforeInitialization(Object bean, String beanName) throws BeansException { return bean; }
    default Object postProcessAfterInitialization(Object bean, String beanName)  throws BeansException { return bean; }
}

Both receive bean + name and return a bean (same or replacement); the container stores and injects whatever is returned.

  • Before initialization — light prep: initial configuration, validation.
  • After initialization — bean fully ready; Spring checks if any aspect's pointcut applies. StudentService has @TrackExecutionTime methods → SimpleAspect applies → proxy returned and injected into StudentController. No applicable aspect → plain target.

Verified on Boot 4.1.0 with a logging BeanPostProcessor:

BPP before-init: StudentService
@PostConstruct on StudentService
BPP after-init (ours): StudentService$$SpringCGLIB$$0

Proxy exists only after @PostConstruct → calls inside @PostConstruct run on the raw object; aspects don't apply.

Anticipated question: "Does Spring scan every aspect for every bean and call?"

No. Lecture: at startup Spring collects all aspects/pointcuts once into an efficient structure; no re-reading per bean or per call.

Precision note (added):
  • Once at startup: @Aspect beans parsed into advisors (advice + pointcut), cached.
  • Once per bean, at creation: auto-proxy creator (AnnotationAwareAspectJAutoProxyCreator, itself a BeanPostProcessor) checks advisors' pointcuts against the bean's class/methods → proxy or not.
  • Per method, first call: proxy computes and caches the interceptor chain; later calls reuse it.
  • Only runtime designators (args, this, target, @target, @args, Lecture 24) need a per-call check — another reason to prefer static ones.

AOP wrap-up (Lectures 22–25)

LectureQuestion answeredKey ideas
22Why AOP?Cross-cutting concerns, scattering/tangling, decorator road, what/where/when
23What & when@Aspect, five advice types, JoinPoint/ProceedingJoinPoint
24WhereWildcards, designators, named pointcuts, CGLIB vs JDK proxies, this vs target
25Real-world usage & internalsCustom annotations, meta-annotations, binding form, BeanPostProcessor creates proxies

Corrections & worth knowing

  • Default retention is CLASS, not RUNTIME. Lecture says RUNTIME is default ("why it worked before we added @Retention"). Per the JLS it's CLASS (verified with javap: stored as RuntimeInvisibleAnnotations). It "worked" because Spring's AspectJ matcher reads bytecode. Verified on Boot 4.1.0 with @NoRet (no @Retention): type-matching @annotation(…NoRet) still fired, but binding form @annotation(n) silently stopped matching, and method.getAnnotation(...) returns null. Always declare @Retention(RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME).
  • Annotation "methods" are called elements. Types limited to primitives, String, Class, enums, annotations, and 1-D arrays of those; defaults must be compile-time constants (no null, hence operation() default ""). An element named value can be set unnamed: @MyAnno("x").
  • Binding form relies on parameter names — needs the -parameters compiler flag (Boot parent POM enables it; see Lecture 15 note on @PathVariable). Otherwise use argNames or the type-matching form.
  • @PostConstruct is itself run by a BeanPostProcessor: CommonAnnotationBeanPostProcessor's postProcessBeforeInitialization; several *Aware callbacks (e.g. ApplicationContextAware) also come from a before-init post-processor. "Aware → BPP-before → init → BPP-after" is a simplification.
  • Proxies can be created earlier for circular references: if another bean needs StudentService mid-creation, the auto-proxy creator hands out an early proxy via getEarlyBeanReference.
  • Same mechanism powers Spring's own annotations: @Transactional, @Cacheable, @Async, @Validated, @PreAuthorize = annotation + advisor + BPP-created proxy. So Lecture 22's limits apply to all: no self-invocation, no private/final methods, Spring beans only.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"Time taken by getStudent is 255 milliseconds" after a 2-second sleep → garbled; about 2005 ms.
"System.getCurrentTimeMillis" → System.currentTimeMillis().
"inspection logic" → interception logic; "cash block" → catch block.
"Student aspect is applying" → corrected to SimpleAspect.
"BeanPostProcessor is a special class" → an interface.
"@PostConstruct" captioned variously; "IntelliJ" as "intelligent"; "Postman" as "post run".

Lecture 26: JDBC from Scratch — Drivers, Connection, Statement, PreparedStatement, ResultSet and Transactions

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #26 · 1h32m · Code Army · plain Java + MySQL, no Spring; the foundation for Spring JDBC, Hibernate and JPA in later lectures

The problem: a Java app talking to a DB on another server

Plain Java, no Spring Boot. A Java app (Demo.java) on a JVM on Server A; a relational DB (MySQL, Oracle, SQL Server, H2, PostgreSQL…) on Server B (same machine or across a network). A query like SELECT * FROM student is just a String; to run it:

  1. Open a connection via some protocol (client–server).
  2. Send the query.
  3. DB evaluates and responds.
  4. Translate: Java has objects, DBs have rows and columns.

Why the app can't talk to the DB directly

Each vendor differs in:

  • connecting and authenticating;
  • packaging SQL queries;
  • SQL data types (MySQL VARCHAR vs others);
  • returning results and errors;
  • transactions.

Java would need per-DB classes, and code would change per DB.

The idea: JDBC = standard interfaces + vendor drivers

JDBC (Java Database Connectivity): Java's standard API for relational DBs (like Collections, Reflection), in java.sql.

Analogy: Collections Framework

  • Collection declares add, remove…
  • ArrayList, LinkedList, HashSet override them differently (append; traverse to last node; hash to bucket).
  • Client calls add on a Collection without knowing the class — polymorphism.

JDBC likewise:

  • JDBC defines Connection, Statement, PreparedStatement, ResultSet…
  • A driver per DB implements them and speaks its protocol.
  • Switching MySQL → PostgreSQL = new driver + URL, same code.
DatabaseDriver (Maven artifact)URL prefix
MySQLMySQL Connector/J (com.mysql:mysql-connector-j)jdbc:mysql://
PostgreSQLPostgreSQL JDBC Driver (org.postgresql:postgresql)jdbc:postgresql://
OracleOracle JDBC driver (com.oracle.database.jdbc:ojdbc11)jdbc:oracle:thin:@
H2com.h2database:h2jdbc:h2:

(Table added for reference.)

Setup: MySQL, a client, a Maven project

  • MySQL server locally (port 3306), credentials set at install.
  • GUI client: DBeaver (open source, cross-platform); MySQL Workbench, SQLyog also fine.
  • IntelliJ project jdbc-demo, Maven (pom.xml); main class under src/main/java/in/strikes/….

JDBC ships with the JDK; the driver is external (9.6.0 from mvnrepository.com):

<dependencies>
    <dependency>
        <groupId>com.mysql</groupId>
        <artifactId>mysql-connector-j</artifactId>
        <version>9.6.0</version>
    </dependency>
</dependencies>

External Libraries then shows mysql-connector-j + protobuf-java (transitive dependency).

Database and table (DBeaver)

New connection → MySQL, localhost, 3306, credentials → Test Connection → Finish. Database student_db:

USE student_db;

CREATE TABLE students (
    id    BIGINT PRIMARY KEY AUTO_INCREMENT,
    name  VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL,
    email VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL UNIQUE,
    age   INT
);

Empty; rows come from Java.

The model class

POJO in model:

public class Student {
    private Long id;
    private String name;
    private String email;
    private int age;

    public Student() {}                                    // needed by mapRow
    public Student(String name, String email, int age) {  // id is auto-generated
        this.name = name; this.email = email; this.age = age;
    }
    // getters, setters, toString()
}

Opening a connection: DriverManager

String url      = "jdbc:mysql://localhost:3306/student_db";
String username = "root";
String password = "123456";
