  • jdbc:mysql: — sub-protocol; picks the driver.
  • localhost:3306 — host:port (remote IP if elsewhere).
  • /student_db — database.

Connection is an interface (java.sql.Connection); get the driver's implementation from DriverManager:

try {
    Connection connection = DriverManager.getConnection(url, username, password);
    System.out.println("Database connected successfully");
    connection.close();
} catch (SQLException e) {
    System.out.println("Database connection failed");
    e.printStackTrace();
}
What getConnection does:
  1. DriverManager passes the URL to the registered MySQL driver.
  2. Driver accepts jdbc:mysql:, reads host, port, DB.
  3. Opens a network connection, authenticates.
  4. Returns its Connection, or throws SQLException (checked).
  • Connections are expensive: always close().
  • Verified (added): no MySQL running → Connector/J 9.6.0 throws com.mysql.cj.jdbc.exceptions.CommunicationsException (SQLState 08S01, "communications link failure").

Sending queries: Statement

A Statement wraps SQL, created from (tied to) a connection:

Statement statement = connection.createStatement();
MethodUse forReturns
executeUpdate(sql)INSERT, UPDATE, DELETEint: rows affected
executeQuery(sql)SELECTResultSet
execute(sql)Anyboolean (below)

DB code lives in repository: StudentRepository (URL/credentials, one method per operation); Main calls new StudentRepository().createUser() etc.

Worked example: CRUD with Statement (hard-coded)

Create

public void createUser() {
    try {
        Connection connection = DriverManager.getConnection(url, username, password);
        Statement statement = connection.createStatement();
        String sql = "INSERT INTO students (name, email, age) " +
                     "VALUES ('Aditya', 'ad@gmail.com', 28)";
        int result = statement.executeUpdate(sql);
        if (result == 1) System.out.println("Create operation successful");
        else             System.out.println("Creation failed");
        connection.close();
    } catch (SQLException e) {
        System.out.println("Database connection failed");
        e.printStackTrace();
    }
}
  • SQL literals use single quotes inside the Java string.
  • result = rows affected (1).
  • First run: "Table 'student_db.student' doesn't exist" (it's students); fixed → success, row visible in DBeaver.

Update and delete

String sql = "UPDATE students SET age = 30 WHERE id = 1";   // updateUser(): age 28 → 30
String sql = "DELETE FROM students WHERE id = 1";           // deleteUser()

Same skeleton; "successful" when one row affected. Main runs one operation per run (like one API call).

Reading: executeQuery and the ResultSet cursor

Rows are logically Students / a List<Student>, but you map them yourself.

A ResultSet for SELECT id, name, email, age FROM students
(cursor)idnameemailage
→ starts before row 1
after 1st next()1Adityaa@gmail.com28
after 2nd next()2………
after 3rd next()3………
4th next() → falseno more rows: exit the loop
  • Cursor starts before row 1; call next() first (verified: else "No data is available").
  • next() returns false when done → while (rs.next()).
  • By label (rs.getLong("id"), rs.getString("name"), rs.getInt("age")) or index (rs.getLong(1), rs.getString(2)…).
  • Indexes are 1-based.
Tip: don't write SELECT *: name columns (SELECT id, name, email, age FROM students) so readers see what's fetched and you fetch only what's needed. (Added: avoids silent breakage/over-fetching when columns are added.)

Mapping a row to an object

private Student mapRow(ResultSet resultSet) throws SQLException {
    Student student = new Student();
    student.setId(resultSet.getLong("id"));
    student.setName(resultSet.getString("name"));
    student.setEmail(resultSet.getString("email"));
    student.setAge(resultSet.getInt("age"));
    return student;
}

// getUserById()
ResultSet resultSet = statement.executeQuery(
        "SELECT id, name, email, age FROM students WHERE id = 7");
resultSet.next();                        // move the cursor onto the first row
Student student = mapRow(resultSet);
System.out.println(student);             // println calls toString()

Prints Student{id=7, name='Aditya', email='…', age=28}; id 7 because auto-increment never reuses 1–6.

execute(): a boolean saying what came back

boolean result = statement.execute(sql);
if (result) {                                          // true  → the result is a ResultSet
    ResultSet resultSet = statement.getResultSet();
    // read rows as before
} else {                                               // false → it was an update count
    int rowsAffected = statement.getUpdateCount();
}
  • Not success/failure — failure throws SQLException.
  • Verified: SELECT → true (update count -1); DELETE → false, count 0.
  • For SQL known only at runtime; else use the specific methods.

Removing hard-coded values: why formatting is wrong

createStudent(Student student) via text block + formatted():

String sql = """
        INSERT INTO students (name, email, age)
        VALUES ('%s', '%s', %d)
        """.formatted(student.getName(), student.getEmail(), student.getAge());

Works (Rohit inserted), but:

  • No proper NULL handling.
  • Binary data, SQL dates awkward.
  • Poor readability.
  • Must know format types (%s, %d).
The real failure mode (added): SQL injection. Input becomes SQL. Verified on H2, email x' OR '1'='1: formatted … WHERE email = 'x' OR '1'='1' returned every row; PreparedStatement returned 0. O'Brien breaks the formatted INSERT. Never format user input into SQL.

The fix: PreparedStatement with ?

? placeholders (no types/quotes), bound by 1-based index with typed setters:

public void createStudent(Student student) {
    String sql = """
            INSERT INTO students (name, email, age)
            VALUES (?, ?, ?)
            """;
    try (Connection connection = DriverManager.getConnection(url, username, password);
         PreparedStatement preparedStatement = connection.prepareStatement(sql)) {

        preparedStatement.setString(1, student.getName());
        preparedStatement.setString(2, student.getEmail());
        preparedStatement.setInt(3, student.getAge());

        int rowsAffected = preparedStatement.executeUpdate();   // NO sql argument here
        if (rowsAffected == 1) System.out.println("Create student successful");
        else                   System.out.println("Create student failed");
    } catch (SQLException e) {
        e.printStackTrace();
    }
}
  • SQL given at creation (prepareStatement(sql)); runs on execute.
  • Setters: setInt, setString, setLong, setBoolean, setBigDecimal, setDate/setObject, setArray, setBinaryStream…, setNull.
  • Demo: new Student("Rohan", "rohan@gmail.com", 21) → success.
Why PreparedStatement wins:
  • Values sent separately from SQL → no injection.
  • Driver handles quoting, escaping, NULL, dates, binary.
  • Readable; no %s/%d.
  • Parsed statement/plan reusable (added).
The bug hit in the lecture: update/get methods failed with "You have an error in your SQL syntax": code called preparedStatement.executeUpdate(sql) / executeQuery(sql). Those Statement overloads run raw text (literal ?s), ignoring bindings; spec says they must throw on a PreparedStatement — H2: "This method is not allowed for a prepared statement" (verified); MySQL: syntax error. Use the no-arg executeUpdate() / executeQuery().

Closing resources: finally, then try-with-resources

close() at the end of try leaks the connection if executeUpdate throws.

  1. close() in finally: forces Connection connection = null; (and PreparedStatement) outside try; close() throws SQLException → nested try/catch; close statement before connection. Bloated.
  2. Try-with-resources: resources in try ( … ) auto-close in reverse order, even on exceptions. SQL string built outside try.

ResultSet must close too (before its statement); params are set before executeQuery(), so nest:

public void getStudentById(long id) {
    String sql = """
            SELECT id, name, email, age
            FROM students
            WHERE id = ?
            """;
    try (Connection connection = DriverManager.getConnection(url, username, password);
         PreparedStatement preparedStatement = connection.prepareStatement(sql)) {

        preparedStatement.setLong(1, id);
        try (ResultSet resultSet = preparedStatement.executeQuery()) {
            if (resultSet.next()) {                 // the id might not exist
                Student student = mapRow(resultSet);
                System.out.println(student);
            }
        }
    } catch (SQLException e) {
        e.printStackTrace();
    }
}

Rest of the refactored CRUD

  • updateStudent(Student student, long id) — UPDATE students SET name = ?, email = ?, age = ? WHERE id = ?, setLong(4, id). Demo: 8L, "Rohit" → "Rohit Negi", 27 → 26.
  • deleteStudent(long id) — DELETE FROM students WHERE id = ?; id 9.
  • getStudents() — while (resultSet.next()) into new ArrayList<>(). First "Column 'id' not found" (SELECT omitted id, mapRow reads it); fixed.
  • Renamed to createStudent, updateStudent, deleteStudent, getStudentById.
Anticipated question: SELECT before UPDATE to check existence? No — two round trips. Missing id → rowsAffected 0 (verified, WHERE id = 10). Added: check-then-act is also a race.

Transactions: setAutoCommit(false)

Transfer ₹1000 A→B = debit + credit; both or neither. All-or-nothing unit = transaction. JDBC defaults to auto-commit (each statement saved immediately); turn it off and commit/roll back:

try (Connection connection = DriverManager.getConnection(url, username, password)) {
    connection.setAutoCommit(false);                 // don't save after each statement
    try (PreparedStatement debit  = connection.prepareStatement(
                 "UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - ? WHERE id = ?");
         PreparedStatement credit = connection.prepareStatement(
                 "UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance + ? WHERE id = ?")) {

        debit.setInt(1, 1000);  debit.setLong(2, aId);  debit.executeUpdate();
        credit.setInt(1, 1000); credit.setLong(2, bId); credit.executeUpdate();

        connection.commit();                         // both succeeded: make them permanent
    } catch (SQLException e) {
        connection.rollback();                       // anything failed: undo everything since the last commit
        throw e;
    }
}

Verified on H2 (1000 each): 300 transfer committed (700 / 1300); a transfer failing after the debit rolled back, still 700 / 1300. (Lecture only sketches this.)

Where this is heading: Spring @Transactional = around advice (Lectures 22–25): auto-commit off, commit on success, rollback on runtime exception. Deep-dive later.

Pain points motivating Spring JDBC / JPA

  • Connect, create statement, bind, close — every method.
  • Manual row mapping (mapRow).
  • Checked SQLException; vendor error codes.
  • Manual setAutoCommit / commit / rollback.

Next: Spring JDBC (JdbcTemplate), Hibernate, JPA.

Statement vs PreparedStatement

StatementPreparedStatement
Created withconnection.createStatement()connection.prepareStatement(sql)
SQL passedAt execution: executeQuery(sql)At creation; execute with no argument
ParametersConcatenated/formatted? + typed setters (1-based)
SQL injectionVulnerableSafe
NULLs, dates, binaryManual, error-pronesetNull, setObject, setBinaryStream…
Repeated executionRe-parsedReusable plan
Use whenStatic SQL, DDL, scriptsAlmost always

Corrections & worth knowing

  • Drivers come from vendors, not JDBC (lecture: "JDBC has given different drivers"). JDBC = interfaces only; hence Maven dependencies.
  • No Class.forName("com.mysql.cj.jdbc.Driver") since JDBC 4.0 (Java 6): drivers self-register via META-INF/services/java.sql.Driver (ServiceLoader). Verified: DriverManager.drivers() listed com.mysql.cj.jdbc.Driver from the classpath jar alone.
  • executeUpdate = rows affected, not a 0/1 flag. UPDATE without WHERE returns all touched (verified: 2); DDL (CREATE TABLE) returns 0 on success. Failures = SQLException.
  • getInt maps SQL NULL to 0 (verified). Use rs.wasNull() or rs.getObject("age", Integer.class) (returned null); int age can't say "unknown".
  • Text blocks and String.formatted() standard since Java 15 (preview 13–14); lecture says "15 or 16".
  • Connection per operation is slow (TCP + auth). Use a pool behind a DataSource (Boot default HikariCP); close() returns it to the pool.
  • No hard-coded credentials — config/env vars (Lecture 18).
  • Restore auto-commit on pooled connections, else autoCommit(false) leaks to the next user; frameworks reset it, hand-written code: setAutoCommit(true) in finally.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"executeUpdate is used for create, insert, delete" → corrected: create/insert, update, delete.
"result = statement.executeQuery" in the refactored update → executeUpdate().
"getRoll number" in mapRow → age.
"MySQL connector J" / "Mys sequel" / "SQL" = MySQL; "DBVer" / "DB" = DBeaver; "intelligent" = IntelliJ; "pm.xml" = pom.xml.
"CRED" / "curd" = CRUD; "cash exception" = catch; "percentile s / percentile d" = %s, %d.

Lecture 27: Spring JDBC — Template Method Pattern, DataSource, HikariCP, JdbcTemplate, RowMapper and Exception Translation

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #27 · 1h05m · Code Army · rewrites Lecture 26's raw-JDBC repository with Spring JDBC

Starting point: Lecture 26's raw JDBC in Spring Boot

Project spring-jdbc-demo:

  • StudentController (/api/students):
    • POST create → "done".
    • GET all, GET /{id}.
    • PUT update, DELETE.
  • StudentService — constructor-injects StudentRepository, delegates.
  • StudentRepository — unchanged raw JDBC:
    • Hard-coded URL/username/password.
    • PreparedStatements in try-with-resources.
    • executeUpdate / executeQuery, private mapRow.
  • Student — id, name, email, age.

Works from Postman (add "Sohan", get /10, get all).

The problem: repeated boilerplate

createStudent, updateStudent, deleteStudent… all repeat:

Same in every method (boilerplate)Different in every method
Open connection (DriverManager.getConnection)The SQL query
Create (prepared) statementThe parameter values
Close statement and connectionThe result mapping (row → Student, Teacher, Order…)
Catch SQLException (checked: catch or throws)

Spring does the fixed part, you supply the varying part: Spring JDBC, an enhancement on top of raw JDBC (still uses Connection, PreparedStatement, SQLException internally).

Design smell: the raw repository breaks Single Responsibility — querying plus connection/statement infrastructure.

The pattern behind it: Template Method

A job must always run m1() → m2() → m3() → m4() in fixed order (steps may pass results along).

  1. Naive: client calls Demo's m1…m4 in order → tightly coupled; adding m5 between m2/m3 breaks every client.
  2. Template method: template() calls m1–m4; clients call only it.
  3. Variable steps (m2, m4 vary): abstract Demo defines m1, m3, template(), declares m2, m4; subclasses DemoConcrete1, DemoConcrete2 implement them. New variation = new subclass.
Standard definition (GoF):
"Define the skeleton of an algorithm in an operation, deferring some steps to subclasses. Template Method lets subclasses redefine certain steps of an algorithm without changing the algorithm's structure." (See LLD notebook.)

JdbcTemplate: the template for database access

StepWho does it
Get a connectionJdbcTemplate
Create the PreparedStatementJdbcTemplate
SQL + parameter valuesYou (the "m2/m4")
Execute, iterate ResultSetJdbcTemplate
Row → objectYou (a RowMapper)
Close statement, then connectionJdbcTemplate
Catch and translate SQLExceptionJdbcTemplate
Precision note: you don't subclass JdbcTemplate; varying steps are callback objects (RowMapper, PreparedStatementSetter, ResultSetExtractor, often lambdas) — Template Method idea via callbacks, a Template Method/Strategy mix. Same style: RestTemplate, JmsTemplate, TransactionTemplate.

DataSource: where connections come from

javax.sql.DataSource is an interface whose main job is to hand out a Connection.

// raw JDBC (Lecture 26)
Connection c = DriverManager.getConnection(url, username, password);

// with a DataSource
Connection c = dataSource.getConnection();

URL/credentials configured once in application.properties.

Connection pooling

A new connection per call is expensive:

  1. Network connection to the DB server (usually another machine).
  2. Driver sends credentials; DB authenticates.
  3. Session created; only then data flows.

Fine for a demo; at 10,000 req/s you can't open 10,000 connections (slow; DB throughput is limited).

The connection pool

Like a thread pool: a container of already-open connections (say 5) lent out.

  • Borrow: createUser takes an idle connection; "close" (connection.close(), manual or try-with-resources) returns it to the pool.
  • Exhaustion: all 5 held → next request waits or times out (configurable).
  • Pool size also caps concurrent DB queries.

HikariCP: Spring Boot's default pool

  • Production-grade; pulled in by spring-boot-starter-jdbc. Swap by adding another pool's dependency.
  • Config: spring.datasource.hikari.* — maximum-pool-size, connection-timeout, idle-timeout, connection-test-query…
  • Bigger is not better (each connection loads the DB); size via load testing.
  • Verified on Boot 4.1.0: default 10 (maximumPoolSize=10).
Worth knowing: a DataSource need not pool. DriverManagerDataSource opens a fresh connection per getConnection() — OK for tests/tiny apps; Boot defaults to Hikari.

Setup: dependency and properties

Initializr: tick "JDBC API". Existing project: add from mvnrepository.com (no version/scope; Boot parent manages them):

<dependency>
    <groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
    <artifactId>spring-boot-starter-jdbc</artifactId>
</dependency>

Alongside spring-boot-starter-webmvc and mysql-connector-j. Use spring-boot-starter-jdbc, not plain spring-jdbc. Move credentials out of the repository:

spring.datasource.url=jdbc:mysql://localhost:3306/student_db
spring.datasource.username=root
spring.datasource.password=123456
# optional pool tuning, e.g.
# spring.datasource.hikari.maximum-pool-size=10

(No quotes around .properties values.)

How Boot builds the DataSource and JdbcTemplate beans

  1. Detects Spring JDBC on the classpath.
  2. Detects a JDBC driver (MySQL Connector/J).
  3. Reads spring.datasource.*.
  4. Prefers HikariCP as pool.
  5. Registers a DataSource bean.
  6. Creates a JdbcTemplate bean wired to it (JdbcTemplateAutoConfiguration).

Proof: inject DataSource into the repository and print it in @PostConstruct:

@PostConstruct
public void init() {
    System.out.println(dataSource.getClass());
}
// class com.zaxxer.hikari.HikariDataSource

Verified on Boot 4.1.0: com.zaxxer.hikari.HikariDataSource, same instance used by the auto-configured JdbcTemplate. You talk to JdbcTemplate; JdbcTemplate talks to the DataSource.

The three main JdbcTemplate methods

JdbcTemplateRaw JDBC equivalentUse forReturns
update(sql, args...)executeUpdateINSERT, UPDATE, DELETEint rows affected
query(sql, rowMapper, args...)executeQuery + loopSELECT, many rows (0 OK)List<T>; empty if none
queryForObject(sql, rowMapper, args...)executeQuery + one next()SELECT expecting exactly one rowT; throws if 0 or >1

Worked example: rewriting the repository

@Repository
public class StudentRepository {

    private final JdbcTemplate jdbcTemplate;

    public StudentRepository(JdbcTemplate jdbcTemplate) {
        this.jdbcTemplate = jdbcTemplate;
    }

Create, update, delete: update(...)

public void createStudent(Student student) {
    String sql = """
            INSERT INTO students (name, email, age)
            VALUES (?, ?, ?)
            """;
    int rowsAffected = jdbcTemplate.update(sql,
            student.getName(),
            student.getEmail(),
            student.getAge());
    if (rowsAffected == 1) System.out.println("Create student successful");
    else                   System.out.println("Create student failed");
}

public void updateStudent(Student student, long id) {
    String sql = """
            UPDATE students SET name = ?, email = ?, age = ?
            WHERE id = ?
            """;
    int rowsAffected = jdbcTemplate.update(sql,
            student.getName(), student.getEmail(), student.getAge(), id);
    // print success / failure as before
}

public void deleteStudent(long id) {
    int rowsAffected = jdbcTemplate.update("DELETE FROM students WHERE id = ?", id);
    // print success / failure as before
}
  • No connection, PreparedStatement, try/catch or close(). ? values passed as varargs in placeholder order.
  • Demo: POST "Raman", 19 → "done" → row in DBeaver.

Reading: mapping one row with RowMapper<T>

Spring executes, loops with next() and collects; it can't know row → Student (no ORM here — JPA/Hibernate later), so you supply it. RowMapper<T> is a functional interface:

T mapRow(ResultSet rs, int rowNum) throws SQLException;

Same logic as Lecture 26's private mapRow, in a class Spring calls:

public class StudentRowMapper implements RowMapper<Student> {
    @Override
    public Student mapRow(ResultSet rs, int rowNum) throws SQLException {
        Student student = new Student();
        student.setId(rs.getLong("id"));
        student.setName(rs.getString("name"));
        student.setEmail(rs.getString("email"));
        student.setAge(rs.getInt("age"));
        return student;
    }
}
private final StudentRowMapper studentRowMapper = new StudentRowMapper(); // stateless: create once

public List<Student> getStudents() {
    String sql = "SELECT id, name, email, age FROM students";
    return jdbcTemplate.query(sql, studentRowMapper);
}

public Student getStudentById(long id) {
    String sql = "SELECT id, name, email, age FROM students WHERE id = ?";
    return jdbcTemplate.queryForObject(sql, studentRowMapper, id);   // mapper BEFORE the args
}
What query(...) does internally: the Lecture 26 loop: 
ResultSet rs = ps.executeQuery();
List<T> list = new ArrayList<>();
int rowNum = 0;
while (rs.next()) {
    list.add(rowMapper.mapRow(rs, rowNum++));
}
return list;

Demo: GET /api/students/12 → Sohan; /13 → Raman; GET /api/students → all; DELETE 13 → gone.

Q: why queryForObject instead of query + first?

  • query on a missing id returns an empty list.
  • queryForObject enforces exactly one row:
    • 0 rows → EmptyResultDataAccessException.
    • >1 row → IncorrectResultSizeDataAccessException.
    Map to 404 in a global exception handler (Lecture 17).
  • Verified on Boot 4.1.0: missing id 99 → [] with query, EmptyResultDataAccessException with queryForObject; two rows → IncorrectResultSizeDataAccessException.

Skipping the mapper class: BeanPropertyRowMapper

When column names match property names (id, name, email, age):

private final RowMapper<Student> rowMapper = new BeanPropertyRowMapper<>(Student.class);
  • Ready-made RowMapper; uses no-arg constructor + setters, matching by name.
  • Maps snake_case to camelCase: full_name → fullName (verified).
  • StudentRowMapper can then be deleted; GET /10 still works.
  • Write your own when names differ a lot or you need custom conversion.
Worth adding: a lambda can replace the class: (rs, rowNum) -> new Student(rs.getLong("id"), rs.getString("name"), …). BeanPropertyRowMapper uses reflection → slower (matters only for large result sets). Spring 5.3+ DataClassRowMapper maps via constructor (e.g. records).

Exception translation: SQLException → DataAccessException

The rewritten repository has no catch. Where did the checked SQLException go?

The problem with SQLException

  • Generic: DB unreachable, bad grammar, constraint violation, no connection → same type.
  • Distinguishing needs getSQLState(), getErrorCode(), getMessage() — codes differ by vendor; branching couples you to one DB.
  • Checked → forces try/catch or throws everywhere.

What Spring does

JdbcTemplate catches it and rethrows a specific, unchecked DataAccessException subclass chosen from vendor error codes. Catch only if you want.

ExceptionWhen
DuplicateKeyExceptionUnique/PK violation, e.g. existing email (unique)
DataIntegrityViolationExceptionOther integrity violations: NOT NULL, FK…
BadSqlGrammarExceptionSyntax errors, unknown table/column
EmptyResultDataAccessExceptionqueryForObject found 0 rows
IncorrectResultSizeDataAccessExceptionqueryForObject found >1 row
CannotGetJdbcConnectionExceptionNo connection (DB down, wrong credentials)

Verified on Boot 4.1.0 (H2 MySQL mode; unreachable MySQL via Connector/J), real hierarchy:

(Omitted: DataAccessException extends NestedRuntimeException; CannotGetJdbcConnectionException has an extra level, NonTransientDataAccessResourceException.)

Where to handle them

Not in the repository (its job is querying). Let them propagate to the service or a @RestControllerAdvice (Lecture 17), or translate to a domain exception:

try {
    jdbcTemplate.update(sql, student.getName(), student.getEmail(), student.getAge());
} catch (DuplicateKeyException e) {
    throw new EmailAlreadyExistsException("Email exists");   // your own exception, mapped to 409 by the advice
}

Spring JDBC vs JPA/Hibernate

  • Still hand-written SQL; for SQL-free CRUD use JPA / Hibernate (method-name queries, lectures 11–13; next).
  • Spring JDBC suits data-intensive apps with complex queries (joins, reporting, tuned SQL) where ORM mapping gets messy.
  • Raw JDBC is rarely used directly.

Raw JDBC vs Spring JDBC

Raw JDBC (Lecture 26)Spring JDBC
ConnectionsDriverManager.getConnection per callPooled DataSource (HikariCP)
ConfigurationURL/credentials in codespring.datasource.*
Statements and closingYou, try-with-resourcesJdbcTemplate
ParameterssetString(1, …)…Varargs to update/query
Row mapping loopYou write while (rs.next())You supply a RowMapper; Spring loops
ErrorsChecked, generic SQLExceptionUnchecked, specific DataAccessException subclasses
TransactionsManual setAutoCommit/commit/rollback@Transactional (same pooled connection)

Corrections & worth knowing

  • Exceptions form a hierarchy, not the lecture's flat list: DuplicateKeyException extends DataIntegrityViolationException; EmptyResultDataAccessException extends IncorrectResultSizeDataAccessException (verified). Parent handler catches child — order specific first, or rely on @ExceptionHandler closest-match.
  • HikariCP is Boot's default, not "Java's" — JDK has no pool; JDBC/JPA starters bring Hikari.
  • JdbcTemplate is thread-safe — inject the shared bean, no new JdbcTemplate(dataSource) per repository; same for stateless RowMappers.
  • Named params / JdbcClient: NamedParameterJdbcTemplate → WHERE id = :id. Spring 6.1+ (Boot 3.2+): jdbcClient.sql("SELECT … WHERE id = :id").param("id", id).query(Student.class).optional() → Optional, no EmptyResultDataAccessException. Both auto-configured.
  • Generated id: update() returns rows affected only; use update(PreparedStatementCreator, KeyHolder) or SimpleJdbcInsert for the AUTO_INCREMENT id.
  • Transactions: @Transactional service method → all JdbcTemplate calls share one connection and commit/roll back together (replaces manual setAutoCommit(false)/commit/rollback); DataSourceTransactionManager auto-configured.
  • Pool sizing: HikariCP says a small pool (~cores × 2 + disks) beats a large one; too many connections thrash the DB.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"JdbcTemplate follows the JDBC design pattern" → the Template design pattern.
"SQL result has to be caught" → corrected to catching SQLException.
"Hari CP" / "hikari CB" = HikariCP; "Spring Initt" = Spring Initializr; "JDVC" = JDBC.
"curd" = CRUD; "IntelliJ" as "intelligent"/"intelligence".

Lecture 28: Hibernate & JPA Basics — ORM, EntityManager CRUD and Entity Mapping (@Column, Enums, Converters, @Embedded, @ElementCollection)

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #28 · 1h12m · Code Army · no more hand-written SQL; internals (why update needs no method) come next lecture

Setup: same layers, empty repository

Project hibernate-demo:

  • StudentController (/api/students) — create, get-by-id, update, delete.
  • StudentService — delegates to StudentRepository (stubs return null).
  • Student — id, name, email, age.
  • StudentRepository — empty, to be filled with Hibernate.

vs Spring JDBC (Lecture 27): hand-written SQL per method, manual params (jdbcTemplate.update(sql, name, email, age)), a StudentRowMapper per ResultSet row.

The problem: object–relational impedance mismatch

Java sideDatabase side
Class Student (blueprint)Table students
Fields id, name, email, ageColumns id, name, email, age
One objectOne row
Classes, inheritance, polymorphism, object referencesTables, rows, columns, primary/foreign keys

Same student, described differently: the object–relational impedance mismatch. Hence JDBC's manual work (queries, binding, mapping rows ↔ objects), forcing every developer to write SQL and coupling code tightly to the DB.

The idea: ORM (Object–Relational Mapping)

Map class and table once:

  • class name → table name;
  • fields → columns;
  • each saved object → a new row.

The framework reuses that mapping for all CRUD.

Responsibilities of an ORM framework

  • Metadata mapping (class ↔ table, property ↔ column); SQL generation (fixed syntax like INSERT INTO … VALUES, UPDATE … SET … WHERE; names/values from the mapping).
  • Parameter binding; result-set mapping (no RowMapper).
  • Relationship management (e.g. Student → Address); caching; transaction integration.

JPA vs Hibernate vs JDBC

  • JPA = Jakarta Persistence API (formerly Java Persistence API): only a specification; stores nothing itself.
  • Hibernate = Spring's default JPA implementation; swappable (e.g. EclipseLink). Native APIs rarely used.
  • JDBC still carries Hibernate's generated SQL (connections, prepared statements, driver).

spring-boot-starter-data-jpa (only data dependency, plus webmvc) brings JPA + Hibernate. Verified: Boot 4.1.0 ships Hibernate 7.4.1. Also add mysql-connector-j; the "driver class not found" error in application.properties disappears once it's in the POM.

Configuration: data source + Hibernate properties

Old students table dropped first (DROP TABLE students;) so Hibernate creates its own.

spring.datasource.url=jdbc:mysql://localhost:3306/student_db
spring.datasource.username=root
spring.datasource.password=123456

spring.jpa.hibernate.ddl-auto=update
spring.jpa.show-sql=true
spring.jpa.properties.hibernate.format_sql=true

ddl-auto: who manages the schema (DDL)

ValueAt startup
createDrop and recreate tables from entities: all data lost each restart
create-dropLike create, plus drop at shutdown (Boot default for embedded DBs like H2)
updateCompare entities to schema, add missing tables/columns (lecture's choice: data survives)
validateOnly check schema matches; fail at startup if not
noneNothing; you create tables (Boot default for MySQL etc.)
Production rule: don't let ddl-auto manage production schemas (learning/staging only). Use migrations: Flyway or Liquibase (versioned SQL scripts).
  • show-sql=true prints every generated SQL statement.
  • format_sql=true pretty-prints them across lines.

Making Student an entity

@Entity
public class Student {
    @Id
    @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY)
    private Long id;
    private String name;
    private String email;
    private int age;

    public Student() {}                              // JPA needs a no-arg constructor
    public Student(String name, String email, int age) { … }
    // getters and setters
}
  • @Entity — map this class to a table.
  • @Id — primary key; without it the IDE errors "Persistent entity 'Student' should have primary key".
  • @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY) — DB auto-increments (1, 2, 3…), as in CRUD lectures 11–13.

Worked example: CRUD with EntityManager

@Repository
public class StudentRepository {

    @PersistenceContext
    private EntityManager entityManager;

    public void save(Student student) {
        entityManager.persist(student);                 // INSERT
    }

    public Student findById(Long id) {
        return entityManager.find(Student.class, id);   // SELECT by primary key; null if absent
    }

    public void remove(Student student) {
        entityManager.remove(student);                  // DELETE
    }
    // no update() method at all
}
  • EntityManager = the JPA interface for everything; @PersistenceContext injects it (both next lecture).
  • One line per operation vs Lecture 27's SQL string + update(...) + row mapper.
  • find takes the entity class (Student.class, so it knows what to build) and id.
  • remove takes the entity object, not an id (lecture's remove(id) gave a type error; fix: service finds, then removes).
@Service
public class StudentService {
    private final StudentRepository studentRepository;
    // constructor injection

    @Transactional
    public Student createStudent(Student student) {
        studentRepository.save(student);
        return student;
    }

    @Transactional
    public Student getStudentById(Long id) {
        return studentRepository.findById(id);
    }

    @Transactional
    public void updateStudent(Long id, Student studentRequest) {
        Student student1 = studentRepository.findById(id);
        if (student1 == null) throw new RuntimeException("Student not found");
        student1.setName(studentRequest.getName());
        student1.setAge(studentRequest.getAge());
        student1.setEmail(studentRequest.getEmail());
        // no save / update call!
    }

    @Transactional
    public void deleteStudent(Long id) {
        Student student = studentRepository.findById(id);
        if (student == null) throw new RuntimeException("Student not found");
        studentRepository.remove(student);
    }
}
  • @Transactional wraps the method in a transaction: all or nothing.
  • Update takes id via @PathVariable: @PutMapping("/{id}").
  • "Not found" RuntimeException normally mapped by the global handler (Lecture 17).

Demo run

  1. Startup — Hibernate DDL from the entity: create table student (id …, age …, email …, name …, primary key (id)).
  2. POST Raman (19), Aditya (29) → ids 1, 2; GET /1, /2 return them.
  3. PUT /2 age 29 → 28: 405 Method Not Allowed (@PutMapping lacked "/{id}").
  4. PUT again → "done" but age still 29, only SELECT logged: no @Transactional on updateStudent. Added (and to delete).
  5. Restart (update: data survives). PUT age 25 → SELECT + UPDATE. DELETE /2 → gone.
Why update needs no method (preview, verified): in a transaction, a find-loaded entity is managed; Hibernate snapshots it and at commit issues UPDATE for changed fields — dirty checking. Without a transaction it's detached right after find. Verified on Boot 4.1.0: non-transactional change lost; @Transactional → select + update, no save. Details next lecture.

Entity mapping in depth

@Table: naming the table

@Entity
@Table(name = "students")
public class Student { … }
  • @Entity (required) = persistent; @Table (optional) = which table.
  • Without @Table: class name in lower snake case (Student → student); with it, students is created on restart.

@Column: per-column settings

@Column(name = "student_name", nullable = false, length = 100)
private String name;

@Column(unique = true, nullable = false, length = 150)
private String email;
AttributeMeaningDefault
nameDB column name (Java field stays name; SQL uses snake_case)derived from field
nullablefalse → NOT NULLtrue
lengthMax length → varchar(100)255
uniqueNo duplicate values (email, not name)false
insertable / updatableIncluded in generated INSERT / UPDATE?true
precision / scaleTotal digits / digits after point (BigDecimal)—

Restart DDL: student_name varchar(100) not null, email varchar(150) not null + unique key (confirmed in DBeaver).

More types: BigDecimal, dates, enums, LOBs, transient fields

@Column(precision = 5, scale = 2)
private BigDecimal percentage;          // DECIMAL(5,2): up to 999.99; 100.00 fits

private LocalDate dateOfBirth;          // DATE
private LocalDateTime createdAt;        // DATETIME(6) on MySQL
// private LocalTime someTime;          // TIME

@Enumerated(EnumType.STRING)
private StudentStatus status;           // enum StudentStatus { ACTIVE, INACTIVE, SUSPENDED }

@Lob
private String profileDescription;      // LONGTEXT on MySQL

@Transient
private String displayName;             // NOT a column; Java-only field
  • @Enumerated(EnumType.STRING) stores the name (MySQL enum('ACTIVE','INACTIVE','SUSPENDED')); ORDINAL stores the unreadable position.
  • @Lob: large text/binary. Don't store big blobs (images, files) in the DB — use object storage (S3-style), store the URL.
  • @Transient: no column (verified: no display_name).

Restart: alter table student add column created_at …, date_of_birth, percentage, profile_description, status enum(…) (add getters/setters, update constructor).

AttributeConverter: store true/false as "yes"/"no"

boolean isMonitor defaults to a bit (0/1), easy to misread. Keep boolean in Java, store yes/no:

public class BooleanToStringConverter implements AttributeConverter<Boolean, String> {

    @Override
    public String convertToDatabaseColumn(Boolean aBoolean) {   // Java → DB
        if (aBoolean == null) return null;
        return aBoolean ? "yes" : "no";
    }

    @Override
    public Boolean convertToEntityAttribute(String s) {         // DB → Java
        if (s == null) return null;
        return "yes".equals(s);
    }
}

// in Student
@Convert(converter = BooleanToStringConverter.class)
private boolean isMonitor;

Demo: POST "isMonitor": true → DBeaver shows yes; Hibernate added check (is_monitor in ('no','yes')) (verified). displayName still not stored.

Fix applied: the lecture's return s.equals("yes"); NPEs on a NULL column — use "yes".equals(s) or null-check first. Hibernate also ships org.hibernate.type.YesNoConverter ('Y'/'N') and TrueFalseConverter ('T'/'F').

Value objects: @Embeddable + @Embedded

Address (houseNumber, street, city, state, pinCode) only makes sense inside a Student — composition in LLD terms. So no @Entity/own table; its fields are embedded as student-table columns.

@Embeddable
public class Address {
    private String houseNumber;
    private String street;
    private String city;
    private String state;
    private String pinCode;
    public Address() {}
    // all-args constructor, getters, setters
}

// in Student
@Embedded
private Address address;

Restart: alter table student add column city …, house_number, pin_code, state, street. Old (commented-out) columns like percentage were not removed — update only adds. Table dropped in DBeaver and recreated cleanly: id, age, email, student_name, city, house_number, pin_code, state, street.

Embed vs separate entity: a Department can exist independently (deleting a student mustn't delete it) → own table + foreign key (relationship lectures). Embed only what lives and dies with the owner.

Two addresses of the same type: @AttributeOverrides

currentAddress and permanentAddress would both produce house_number, city… (collision). Rename columns per field:

@Embedded
@AttributeOverrides({
    @AttributeOverride(name = "houseNumber", column = @Column(name = "current_house_number")),
    @AttributeOverride(name = "street",      column = @Column(name = "current_street")),
    @AttributeOverride(name = "city",        column = @Column(name = "current_city")),
    @AttributeOverride(name = "state",       column = @Column(name = "current_state")),
    @AttributeOverride(name = "pinCode",     column = @Column(name = "current_pin_code"))
})
private Address currentAddress;

// same block with "permanent_..." names on permanentAddress

name = Java property in Address; @Column(name) = new DB column. Result: current_city, current_house_number, current_pin_code, current_state, current_street, permanent_city, ….

Collections: @ElementCollection + @CollectionTable

Set<String> skills fits no SQL column type → its own table with the student id as foreign key, one row per skill:

student_skills (one student → many rows)
student_id (FK)skills
1Spring Boot
1Java
1Python
 
@ElementCollection
@CollectionTable(name = "student_skills",
                 joinColumns = @JoinColumn(name = "student_id"))
private Set<String> skills;
  • @ElementCollection — collection of basic values/embeddables in a separate table.
  • @CollectionTable(name) — that table's name.
  • @JoinColumn(name = "student_id") — FK column back to students.id.

Startup runs two create tables: students (id, age, email, student_name; no skills column) and student_skills (student_id, skills). No own id (dependent on student); for a row PK, make it a separate @Entity.

A collection of embeddables

@ElementCollection
@CollectionTable(name = "student_address",
                 joinColumns = @JoinColumn(name = "student_id"))
private Set<Address> addresses;

Creates student_address(student_id, city, house_number, pin_code, state, street), one row per address. Any collection type (List, Set…) of primitives/wrappers, strings or embeddables.

Mapping cheat sheet

JavaAnnotationDatabase result
Class@Entity (+ @Table)Table
Primary key@Id + @GeneratedValue(IDENTITY)bigint auto_increment primary key
Field(none) or @Column(…)Column
BigDecimal@Column(precision, scale)decimal(p,s)
LocalDate / LocalTime / LocalDateTime(none)date / time / datetime(6)
Enum@Enumerated(EnumType.STRING)Name text (MySQL enum(…))
Big text/binary@Loblongtext / longblob
Java-only field@TransientNo column
Custom conversion@Convert(converter = …)Whatever the converter returns
Value object@Embeddable / @Embedded (+ @AttributeOverrides)Columns in owner's table
Collection of values@ElementCollection + @CollectionTableSeparate table, FK to owner

Corrections & worth knowing

  • Enum ordinals start at 0, not 1. Verified: INACTIVE (2nd) → 1, check (ordinal_status between 0 and 2). Real danger of ORDINAL (default for bare @Enumerated): reordering/inserting constants silently remaps rows. Use STRING.
  • LocalDateTime → datetime(6) on MySQL, not timestamp. (Instant/OffsetDateTime are zone-aware.)
  • Prefer Spring's @Transactional over the lecture's jakarta.transaction.Transactional (honoured): org.springframework.transaction.annotation.Transactional adds readOnly, propagation, isolation, rollbackFor, timeout. AOP proxy (Lectures 22–25): no self-invocation or private methods.
  • ddl-auto=update never drops/alters columns and treats renames as new columns — use Flyway/Liquibase.
  • Deleting an owner deletes its element-collection rows (verified: delete from student_skills where student_id=? first).
  • @ElementCollection is lazy and costly to modify: changing one element of a List may delete and re-insert all rows; for large/volatile collections use a @OneToMany entity. (Verified: Set<String> → Hibernate 7 adds unique (student_id, skills).)
  • Injected EntityManager is a thread-safe proxy routing to the current transaction's (non-thread-safe) EntityManager — safe in a singleton repository.
  • Logging SQL: show-sql uses System.out; prefer logging.level.org.hibernate.SQL=debug (+ org.hibernate.orm.jdbc.bind=trace for values).
  • Next: Spring Data JPA's JpaRepository<Student, Long> (Lectures 11–13) generates this, same EntityManager underneath.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"Hibernation logic" / "hibernet" = Hibernate; "hybrid demo" = hibernate-demo.
"jakarta.transactional" → jakarta.transaction.Transactional.
"ElementCollection makes a column" → corrected: a new table.
"Y or N" for the converter → code stores "yes"/"no".
"I've set insertable false" → both left at default true.
"Spring Unit" = Spring Initializr; "DBVer" = DBeaver; "IntelliJ" as "intelligent"/"intelligence".

Lecture 29: Hibernate Internals — Persistence Context, EntityManager(Factory), Transactions, Dirty Checking, Entity States and Flush

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #29 · 1h13m · Code Army · explains why Lecture 28's update worked with no update method

Why study the internals

Spring Data JPA → JPA → Hibernate: internals explain every repository call (interviews; "why didn't my query run?").

Demo hibernate-internals-demo = Lecture 28's app:

  • StudentController (POST/GET/PUT/DELETE); StudentService, all @Transactional (create, get, update-by-setters, delete-after-find).
  • StudentRepository: @PersistenceContext EntityManager; save (persist), findById (find), remove.
  • Student (id, name, email, age); ddl-auto=update, SQL logging on.

POST Aditya (29) → row 1. Four objects explain everything:

  1. the persistence context
  2. the EntityManager
  3. the EntityManagerFactory
  4. database transactions

1. The persistence context: a tracked cache of entities

On find or persist, Hibernate puts the entity into a container, the persistence context, where it is:

  • Cached, keyed by type + id (Student#1, Student#2…).
  • Tracked: field changes are noticed and written to the DB by Hibernate.

Proof: two finds, one SELECT

public Student findById(Long id) {
    Student s1 = entityManager.find(Student.class, id);
    Student s2 = entityManager.find(Student.class, id);   // same transaction
    return s2;
}

GET /api/students/1 → one select … from student … where id=?. Verified on Boot 4.1.0: s1 == s2 is true (same instance). In separate transactions: two SELECTs (fresh context each).

The name for it: the persistence context is the first-level cache (L1), scoped to one context. A second-level cache, shared across contexts, comes later.

2. EntityManager: the API to the persistence context

You never touch the context directly; EntityManager methods talk to the persistence context, not straight to the DB:

MethodWhat it does to the context
persist(e)New entity becomes managed; INSERT scheduled
find(Class, id)Returns from context, or SELECTs and adds
remove(e)Marks managed entity for deletion; DELETE scheduled
flush()Sends pending SQL now
clear()Detaches all entities
detach(e) / merge(e) / refresh(e) / contains(e)Covered below

3. EntityManagerFactory: makes EntityManagers

  • Factory pattern: each unit of work asks it for an EntityManager.
  • Lifetime: factory lives as long as the application; EntityManagers are created/destroyed many times.
  • Each new EntityManager has its own new persistence context, which ends with it.

JPA interfaces vs Hibernate's own types

Hibernate predates JPA; JPA later standardised its concepts as interfaces, and Hibernate extended them:

JPA (what you use)Hibernate interfaceHibernate implementation class
EntityManagerSession (extends EntityManager)SessionImpl
EntityManagerFactorySessionFactory (extends EntityManagerFactory)SessionFactoryImpl

"Session" in Hibernate docs = an EntityManager. Swapping EntityManager for Session in the repository still works (persist, find, remove unchanged). Stick to JPA anyway.

@PersistenceContext: why not plain constructor injection?

  • StudentRepository is a singleton bean.
  • A real EntityManager is not thread-safe; one shared instance breaks under concurrent persist/find/remove.
  • @PersistenceContext injects a proxy (like Lectures 22–25's AOP proxies) that routes each call to the EntityManager bound to the current transaction, creating one via the factory when a transaction starts.
Don't confuse the two "persistence contexts": @PersistenceContext (annotation) only gets you the right EntityManager (the factory creates it); the persistence context (concept) is the entity container that EntityManager manages.

Chain for one service call:

  1. @Transactional method starts.
  2. New transaction begins.
  3. Factory creates a new EntityManager + persistence context.
  4. Repository calls go via the proxy to it.
  5. Method ends: commit; EntityManager and context closed.

4. Transactions: all or nothing

Transfer ₹1000 from A (id 1) to B (id 2):

UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - 1000 WHERE id = 1;
UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance + 1000 WHERE id = 2;

If the DB fails between them, A loses money and B gets nothing. A transaction makes them succeed or fail together:

BEGIN;
  UPDATE ... A;          -- if anything fails here or below:
  UPDATE ... B;          --   ROLLBACK (A's debit is undone)
COMMIT;                  -- otherwise make both permanent

BEGIN … COMMIT/ROLLBACK = one unit of work: 0 or 1, never between.

Raw Hibernate vs Spring

// raw Hibernate
Session session = sessionFactory.openSession();
Transaction tx = session.beginTransaction();       // BEGIN
try {
    session.persist(student);
    tx.commit();                                   // COMMIT
} catch (Exception e) {
    tx.rollback();                                 // ROLLBACK
}

// Spring
@Transactional
public void createStudent(Student student) {
    studentRepository.save(student);
}

@Transactional = Lecture 25's AOP pattern: a pointcut matches the annotation; an around advice:

  • Before: begin transaction.
  • proceed() into your method.
  • Returns → commit; throws → roll back.

One annotation replaces the try/commit/catch/rollback block.

Where to put @Transactional: on the service layer. Repository methods work but are too narrow (a transaction should span a whole business operation, e.g. both halves of a transfer) and mix responsibilities. Optional for pure reads, but "adding it does no harm" (better: @Transactional(readOnly = true); see Corrections).

Why update needs no method: dirty checking + flush

updateStudent(1, {name: "Rohit", email: "rohit@gmail.com", age: 25}):

  1. @Transactional → new transaction, EntityManager, context.
  2. findById(1): context empty → SELECT … WHERE id = 1 via DataSource/JDBC; Student on the heap is tracked with a snapshot.
  3. Setters change only the heap object; no SQL.
  4. Before commit: compare with snapshot → dirty.
  5. Flush: UPDATE student SET name = 'Rohit', email = …, age = 25 WHERE id = 1; commit; all three end.

Demo: PUT /api/students/1 → select then update; DBeaver shows Rohit / 25. Also why Lecture 28's first PUT failed: no transaction → no managed entity, no flush.

Why the persistence context exists:
  • Fewer queries — repeat find served from memory.
  • Automatic updates — change the object; Hibernate writes SQL.
  • Batched writes (write-behind) — changes sent together at flush, not one round trip per setter.

The four entity states

StateMeaningHow you get there
TransientPlain heap object; no context knows itnew Student(…)
ManagedIn a context, tracked; changes auto-flushed (the important one)persist, find (or merge)
DetachedWas managed, no longer tracked; changes ignoredMethod/transaction ends, or detach(e) / clear()
RemovedScheduled for deletion; DELETE at flushremove(e) on a managed entity

Demos

  • Detach: findById + entityManager.detach(s1); PUT Aditya → Rohan / 21 → "done", DB unchanged, only SELECT (verified: contains(s) false). clear() detaches all.
  • Merge: repository attachAgain(Student) calls entityManager.merge; service: detach, then student1 = studentRepository.attachAgain(student1) → PUT "Mohit" saved (select + update). Without merge, "Arun" isn't saved.
  • Remove: find (managed) then remove; DELETE runs at flush, not necessarily at that line.
  • contains(e) — is it managed? (rarely needed).
Worth knowing (verified on Boot 4.1.0):
  • merge doesn't re-attach the passed object — copies its state onto a managed instance (SELECT if needed) and returns that copy. Verified: merge(s) == s false; contains false for original, true for returned; extra SELECT before UPDATE. Hence student1 = …attachAgain(student1).
  • Removed comes only from Managed. remove on a detached entity → IllegalArgumentException ("Given entity is not associated with the persistence context"). Hence find-then-remove.

Flush: write-behind, and flushing manually

Flush synchronises the context with the DB. Hibernate is write-behind: flushes at transaction end, not per change — three setters → one UPDATE.

Forcing it (repository flush() after each setter) → select + three updates (Smita, age 25, new email). Pointless here: it defeats write-behind.

Where manual flush is useful: batch processing

Persisting a million students in one loop keeps a million managed objects (memory + dirty-checking cost) and nothing reaches the DB until the end. Flush and clear every N:

@Transactional
public void saveAll(List<Student> students) {
    int count = 0;
    for (Student student : students) {
        entityManager.persist(student);
        count++;
        if (count % 100 == 0) {       // every 100 entities
            entityManager.flush();    // send the pending INSERTs
            entityManager.clear();    // free the context
        }
    }
}   // the remainder is flushed at commit

Context and DB work in batches of 100.

Fix applied: the lecture checks counter % 100 == 0 before persisting/incrementing → flushes an empty context at iteration 0 and batch boundaries are off by one. Increment first, then test.

refresh: DB changed behind Hibernate's back

A direct DB edit (e.g. Smita → Arun in DBeaver) is invisible to the context (it tracks the heap object, not the table). entityManager.refresh(student) re-reads the row and overwrites entity state. Verified: an unsaved local change was discarded, fresh SELECT issued. Rarely needed (nobody hand-edits JPA-managed tables).

Corrections & worth knowing

  • "Dirty checking", not "dirty read" — a dirty read is an isolation anomaly (reading another transaction's uncommitted data). "Dirty entity" is correct.
  • Flush ≠ commit. Flush sends SQL inside the open transaction, which can still roll back. Verified: persist + flush() (INSERT ran) + exception → 0 rows. Only commit is permanent.
  • Flush also precedes queries: FlushMode.AUTO flushes before a JPQL/HQL query that could see pending changes. Verified: name change + select count(…) → UPDATE ran first. Write-behind-until-end isn't guaranteed.
  • Boot keeps the EntityManager open per web request: Open Session In View (spring.jpa.open-in-view=true, startup warning). Verified: entity returned from a @Transactional service still managed (contains == true) in the controller, though controller changes aren't flushed (no transaction). Many set open-in-view=false to surface lazy-loading bugs early.
  • IDENTITY ids aren't JDBC-batched. Flush/clear bounds memory; batching needs spring.jpa.properties.hibernate.jdbc.batch_size=100, and Hibernate disables insert batching for GenerationType.IDENTITY (each INSERT runs to get its id). Bulk: SEQUENCE (PostgreSQL/Oracle) or JdbcTemplate.batchUpdate (Lecture 27).
  • @Transactional(readOnly = true) for reads — manual flush mode, no dirty checking/snapshots. Verified: name change in a readOnly method not saved. (Spring's org.springframework.transaction.annotation.Transactional; jakarta's lacks readOnly.)
  • Rollback rule: unchecked (RuntimeException, Error) → rollback; checked → commit unless rollbackFor = Exception.class. AOP-based: not for self-invocation or private methods (Lecture 22).
  • persist/find/remove are JPA (EntityManager, inherited by Session), not "Hibernate's methods". Native save/update/saveOrUpdate deprecated since Hibernate 6.
  • ACID: Atomicity (all-or-nothing), Consistency, Isolation (dirty reads live here), Durability — standard interview follow-up.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"JPA has its own class SessionFactoryImpl" → SessionImpl / SessionFactoryImpl are Hibernate's.
"DataStore object" for the DB connection → DataSource (Lecture 27).
"sirf 1000 rupees ... +100" in the second UPDATE → both move ₹1000.
"atrocity" → inconsistency; "hyper net" / "hibernation" = Hibernate; "S1 dot ID" = alias s1_0.id in Hibernate's SQL.
"Postman" as "post run"/"post one"; "IntelliJ" as "intelligent"; "DBVer" = DBeaver.

Lecture 30: JPA Relationships — @ManyToOne/@OneToMany, @OneToOne, @ManyToMany, Owning vs Inverse Side (plus Lombok)

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #30 · 1h23m · Code Army · builds on the Hibernate CRUD lecture (28)

Setup: empty project + Lombok

jpa-relationship-demo: empty controller, service, repository (with @PersistenceContext EntityManager), Student entity with id, name. Deps: spring-boot-starter-data-jpa, webmvc, mysql-connector-j.

Lombok

Instead of IDE-generated getters/setters/constructors, add Lombok (org.projectlombok:lombok; version managed by Boot parent):

@Getter @Setter              // getId/setId, getName/setName, ... (standard naming)
@NoArgsConstructor           // JPA needs it
@AllArgsConstructor
@Entity
public class Student { … }
  • @Data — getters, setters, toString, equals/hashCode at once (see warning below).
  • @Builder — Builder-pattern API.
  • Accept IntelliJ's "Enable annotation processing" prompt.
Worth knowing (verified on Boot 4.1.0, JDK 23): the dependency alone isn't enough on JDK 23+ — the JDK no longer runs annotation processors implicitly, so mvn compile failed ("cannot find symbol: method setName"). Declare it as a processor (as Initializr does): 
<plugin>
    <groupId>org.apache.maven.plugins</groupId>
    <artifactId>maven-compiler-plugin</artifactId>
    <configuration>
        <annotationProcessorPaths>
            <path><groupId>org.projectlombok</groupId><artifactId>lombok</artifactId></path>
        </annotationProcessorPaths>
    </configuration>
</plugin>
Also mark Lombok <optional>true</optional> (compile-time only). Boot 4.1 manages Lombok 1.18.46.

The problem: an object inside an object

@Entity
public class Student {
    @Id @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY) private Long id;
    private String name;
    private Department department;      // error: "Basic attribute should not be 'Department'"
}

public class Department {
    private Long id;
    private String name;                // more fields later
}

A column can't hold an object. Relational way:

  • separate department table (id, name);
  • student stores only dept_id, a foreign key to department.id.
student
idnamedept_id (FK)
1Aditya1001
2Rohit1002
department
id (PK)name
1001CSC
1002Physics

Java: student.getDepartment(). SQL: a join (… WHERE students.dept_id = departments.id). Raw JDBC: store deptId, fetch student, then query a DepartmentRepository. JPA relationships keep the object model and let Hibernate generate joins and FKs.

Relationship vocabulary

Cardinality

CardinalityExampleReads as
Many-to-one (N:1)Student → DepartmentMany students, one department
One-to-many (1:N)Department → StudentOne department, many students (same relationship, other end)
One-to-one (1:1)User ↔ ProfileOne user, one profile, and vice versa
Many-to-many (N:M)Student ↔ CourseStudent takes many courses; course has many students

Direction

  • Unidirectional — one side references the other (Student has department; Department knows nothing). Drawn as an arrow.
  • Bidirectional — both reference each other (Department also has List<Student> students). "Many" end holds one object; "one" end holds a collection.

Owning vs inverse side

  • Owning side — table holds the FK (Student owns dept_id); carries @JoinColumn.
  • Inverse side — the other end (Department); in bidirectional mappings says mappedBy = "…" ("the other side manages this").

Many-to-one (unidirectional): Student → Department

@Getter @Setter @NoArgsConstructor @AllArgsConstructor
@Entity
public class Student {
    @Id @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY)
    private Long id;
    private String name;

    @ManyToOne
    @JoinColumn(name = "dept_id")          // FK column in the student table
    private Department department;
}

@Getter @Setter @NoArgsConstructor
@Entity
public class Department {
    @Id @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY)
    private Long id;
    private String name;
}
  • @ManyToOne — cardinality.
  • @JoinColumn(name = "dept_id") — FK column name; it references Department's primary key (an FK always points at the other table's PK).
  • Department must be an @Entity with @Id — fixes the "Basic attribute" error.

Startup DDL (ddl-auto=update): create table department (id, name), create table student (id, name, dept_id), alter table student add constraint … foreign key (dept_id) references department. DBeaver's Diagram tab shows the link.

Nullable or mandatory?

@ManyToOne(optional = false)                        // Java/JPA side: association required
@JoinColumn(name = "dept_id", nullable = false)     // DDL side: dept_id NOT NULL
private Department department;

Default: dept_id = NULL allowed (e.g. Raman, no department). optional = entity-level rule; nullable = generated schema.

Worked example: saving students with departments

Added DepartmentController (POST /api/department), DepartmentService (@Transactional), DepartmentRepository (save = persist, getDepartmentById = find). {"name": "CSC"} first stored null: no Lombok @Setter on Department, so JSON couldn't bind name. Fixed (tables dropped); CSC, Physics, History created.

Into an existing department

// StudentController: POST /api/student?deptId=1   body {"name": "Aditya"}
@PostMapping
public ResponseEntity<String> createStudent(@RequestBody Student student,
                                            @RequestParam Long deptId) { … }

// StudentService
@Transactional
public void createStudent(Student student, Long deptId) {
    Department department = departmentRepository.getDepartmentById(deptId);  // SELECT department
    student.setDepartment(department);
    studentRepository.save(student);                                        // INSERT student
}
  • Service may use several repositories (business logic); repository only saves.
  • /{deptId} path variable gave 404 when omitted → switched to @RequestParam deptId.
  • Log: select department, insert student. Aditya, Rohit → dept_id = 1; Sohan → 2.

Creating the department on the fly

// POST /api/student/with-department?deptName=Political Science   body {"name": "Monika"}
@Transactional
public void createStudent(Student student, String deptName) {
    Department department = new Department();
    department.setName(deptName);
    departmentRepository.save(department);     // INSERT department
    student.setDepartment(department);
    studentRepository.save(student);           // INSERT student with the new FK
}

Two overloaded @PostMappings clashed ("ambiguous mapping": same path, differing only by param) → path /with-department. Result: department id 4, Monika linked; two inserts logged.

Worth knowing (verified): no cascading by default. Persisting a student referencing an unsaved department throws TransientPropertyValueException: … references an unsaved transient instance of 'Department' (persist the transient instance before flushing). Persist the parent first (as here) or add cascade = CascadeType.PERSIST.

One-to-many (bidirectional): Department → List<Student>

Could department hold student_id? No: two CSC students would need two rows with id 1001, violating the PK. The FK always lives on the "many" side; one-to-many is the same layout as many-to-one, seen from the other end.

// in Department
@OneToMany(mappedBy = "department")      // = the field name in Student
private List<Student> students = new ArrayList<>();
  • mappedBy = "department": "Student.department (with its @JoinColumn) owns this." It names the Java field, not a table/column.
  • Schema unchanged after restart: department(id, name), student(id, name, dept_id).
  • Gain: navigate both ways (department.getStudents(), student.getDepartment()).
  • Initialise the list (= new ArrayList<>()) — lecture hit a 500 "department.getStudents() is null" until it did.

The cost: sync both sides yourself

student.setDepartment(dept) must be paired with dept.getStudents().add(student); otherwise Rohan says CSC but CSC's list lacks Rohan (inconsistent in-memory model). Both service methods now update both sides:

student.setDepartment(department);
department.getStudents().add(student);
studentRepository.save(student);
// no departmentRepository.save(department) needed: it is managed (Lecture 29)

Reverse endpoint POST /api/department/with-student?studentName=Anamika (body {"name": "Physics"}) creates both and links both ways; Anamika gets dept_id = 4. Log: insert student, insert department, update student.

Why insert, insert, update (verified): student persisted before department → inserted with dept_id = NULL (no dept id yet), department inserted, then UPDATE fills the FK. Persist the parent first → just two inserts.

Helper methods: centralise the syncing

// in Student (instead of Lombok's setter)
public void addDepartment(Department department) {
    this.department = department;
    department.getStudents().add(this);
}

public void removeDepartment() {
    if (this.department != null) {
        this.department.getStudents().remove(this);   // remove FIRST...
        this.department = null;                        // ...then clear the reference
    }
}
Fix applied: the lecture's helper is private (unusable from the service) — must be public. Its remove sets department = null before this.department.getStudents().remove(this) → NullPointerException. Reverse the order, as above.

One-to-one: User ↔ Profile

Either table may hold the FK (user.profile_id or profile.user_id) without PK violation. Lecture puts it in profile:

@Entity
public class Profile {
    @Id @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY) private Long id;
    private String bio;

    @OneToOne
    @JoinColumn(name = "user_id")        // Profile is the owning side
    private User user;
}

@Entity
public class User {
    @Id @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY) private Long id;
    private String name;

    @OneToOne(mappedBy = "user")         // optional: makes it bidirectional
    private Profile profile;
}

Two constraints keep it one-to-one:

  • FK: profile.user_id → user.id.
  • Unique on profile.user_id; without it two profiles could point at user 1 (silently one-to-many).

Verified on Boot 4.1.0: Hibernate adds the unique constraint itself for @OneToOne (user_id bigint unique). mappedBy on User doesn't change the schema.

On direction: "Don't assume bidirectional is always better." It adds sync work and complexity; if you only use profile.getUser(), stay unidirectional.

Many-to-many: Student ↔ Course, and the join table

Neither table can hold the FK:

  • student.course_id needs two "1, Aditya" rows (Spring, MERN) → duplicate student PK.
  • course.student_id needs two "1001, Spring" rows (Aditya, Rohit) → duplicate course PK.

Solution: a third join table of FK pairs:

student_course (join table)
student_id (FK)course_id (FK)
11001
11002
21001
21002
 
@Entity
public class Student2 {
    @Id @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY) private Long id;
    private String name;

    @ManyToMany
    @JoinTable(name = "student_course",
               joinColumns        = @JoinColumn(name = "student_id"),   // owning side's FK
               inverseJoinColumns = @JoinColumn(name = "course_id"))    // other side's FK
    private List<Course> courses = new ArrayList<>();
}

@Entity
public class Course {
    @Id @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY) private Long id;
    private String name;

    @ManyToMany(mappedBy = "courses")      // optional inverse side
    private List<Student2> students = new ArrayList<>();
}

Startup: student2(id, name), course(id, name), student_course(student_id, course_id). No new columns in entity tables; links live only in the join table, queried both ways.

Summary: where the foreign key goes

MappingFK locationOwning side annotationInverse side
Many-to-one / one-to-manyAlways the many side@ManyToOne + @JoinColumn@OneToMany(mappedBy = "…")
One-to-oneEither table (+ unique constraint)@OneToOne + @JoinColumn@OneToOne(mappedBy = "…")
Many-to-manyNeither: join table@ManyToMany + @JoinTable@ManyToMany(mappedBy = "…")

Homework

  • CRUD endpoints for User/Profile (1:1) and Student/Course (N:M), as for Student/Department.
  • Update a student's department and check both tables; add helpers for bidirectional sides.

Corrections & worth knowing

  • Only the owning side is written to the DB. Syncing both sides matters for the in-memory model, but Hibernate reads only Student.department for the FK. Verified: adding only to department.getStudents() saved dept_id = NULL; student.setDepartment(d) saved dept_id = 1. Also, the department isn't auto-saved "because student is the owning side" — it's saved because it's managed (found/persisted in the transaction), not via ownership or cascade.
  • List in @ManyToMany: no PK, slow to modify. Lecture says the two FKs form the join table's PK. Verified on Hibernate 7.4: List<Course> → no PK; Set<Course> → primary key (course_id, student_id). Removing one course from the List → delete all the student's rows and re-insert the rest (a "bag"). Use Set.
  • No Lombok @Data (or @ToString/@EqualsAndHashCode) on bidirectional entities — generated toString/hashCode recurse forever (verified: StackOverflowError). Same loop when Jackson serialises a bidirectional entity. Use @Getter/@Setter, exclude associations, return DTOs (Lecture 16).
  • Fetch types and N+1 (announced, not covered). Defaults: @ManyToOne/@OneToOne EAGER; @OneToMany/@ManyToMany LAZY. N students each touching a lazy association → 1 + N queries. Fix: @ManyToOne(fetch = FetchType.LAZY) + JOIN FETCH or @EntityGraph where needed.
  • Cascade / orphan removal: @OneToMany(mappedBy = "department", cascade = CascadeType.ALL, orphanRemoval = true) propagates persist/remove to children and deletes a child removed from the collection. Only for true parent–child ownership; never on @ManyToMany (removing a student would delete shared courses).
  • Join table with extra data (enrolment date, grade): replace @ManyToMany with an Enrollment entity holding two @ManyToOnes.
  • Reserved table names: entity User → table user, reserved in some DBs (H2, PostgreSQL). Use @Table(name = "app_user").
Transcript slips to ignore:
"between them is a one-to-many relationship" (Student → Department) → many-to-one from the student side, as then drawn.
"mern" = MERN; "lombak" / "LMB" = Lombok; "JPI relationship demo" = jpa-relationship-demo.
"the value of mappedBy is the other table" → it's the field name on the owning entity.
"cascade delete" in DBeaver = dropping tables with dependent constraints, not JPA cascading.
"Postman" as "post one", "IntelliJ" as "intelligent", "DBVer" = DBeaver.

Lecture 31: Cascading, Lazy vs Eager Loading, the N+1 Problem and Entity Graphs

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #31 · 57m · Code Army · the last JPA-relationships lecture; Spring Data JPA starts next

Starting point: Lecture 30's Student ↔ Department

  • Student (id, name) — @ManyToOne @JoinColumn(name = "dept_id") Department department; owning side (dept_id in student).
  • Department (id, name) — @OneToMany(mappedBy = "department") List<Student> students; inverse side, no column.
  • Controllers/services for both, EntityManager repositories, Lombok getters/setters.

Startup: department(id, name), student(id, name, dept_id) + FK. POST /api/department {"name": "CSC"} stores a department, no students.

The problem: saving every child by hand

New department (Physics) with four new students, simulated in DepartmentService.createDepartment:

@Transactional
public void createDepartment(Department department) {
    Student s1 = new Student(); s1.setName("Aditya");
    Student s2 = new Student(); s2.setName("Rohit");
    Student s3 = new Student(); s3.setName("Rohan");
    Student s4 = new Student(); s4.setName("Sonika");
    for (Student s : List.of(s1, s2, s3, s4)) s.setDepartment(department);  // owning side
    department.getStudents().addAll(List.of(s1, s2, s3, s4));                // inverse side

    departmentRepository.save(department);    // persist department
    studentRepository.save(s1);               // ...and each student, one by one
    studentRepository.save(s2);
    studentRepository.save(s3);
    studentRepository.save(s4);
}

Works: five INSERTs, all with dept_id = 2. But each child needs its own save; forget one and it's silently lost (new objects aren't in the persistence context).

Some children only make sense with their parent (department → students, Order → OrderItems): saving/deleting the parent should do the same to children.

The idea: cascading from parent to children

// in Department
@OneToMany(mappedBy = "department", cascade = CascadeType.PERSIST)
private List<Student> students = new ArrayList<>();

"persist a department → also persist every student in students." With the four studentRepository.save calls commented out (tables recreated), Physics and Aditya, Rohit, Rohan, Sonika are stored.

CascadeType.REMOVE: delete parent → delete children

// DepartmentController
@DeleteMapping("/{id}")
public ResponseEntity<String> deleteDepartment(@PathVariable Long id) { … }

// DepartmentService
@Transactional
public void removeDepartment(Long deptId) {
    Department department = departmentRepository.findById(deptId);   // em.find
    departmentRepository.removeDepartment(department);               // em.remove(department)
}
  • With REMOVE: DELETE /api/department/1 removes the department and its students. Log: department SELECT, students SELECT, one DELETE per student, department DELETE. No manual cleanup or helpers.
  • Without cascade: fails with "Cannot delete or update a parent row: a foreign key constraint fails" — DB won't delete a still-referenced department.
Q: "What if a student belongs to several departments?" Not in this model: Student → Department is many-to-one (exactly one department). Multiple departments = many-to-many, where cascading removal would be wrong. Cascade follows cardinality.

Cascade types

CascadeTypePropagates
PERSISTpersist(parent) → persist new children
REMOVEremove(parent) → remove children
MERGEmerge(parent) → merge children (added)
REFRESH / DETACHrefresh / detach of parent → children (added)
ALLAll of the above

Advice: prefer listing exactly what you mean (e.g. {PERSIST, REMOVE}) over ALL.

The problem: loading the whole object graph

A real Student may hold:

  • Department (many-to-one) with Teachers;
  • Profile (one-to-one);
  • List<Course> (many-to-many), each with Instructors.

Naively, entityManager.find(Student.class, 1001) queries all of it — many queries for one student. This is the reachable object graph.

The idea: lazy vs eager loading

  • Eager: load the association with the entity (typically a join).
  • Lazy: load only basic columns (id, name…); each association field holds a proxy, queried on first use.
@ManyToOne(fetch = FetchType.LAZY)
@JoinColumn(name = "department_id")
private Department department;

@OneToOne(fetch = FetchType.LAZY)
@JoinColumn(name = "profile_id")
private Profile profile;

Demo run

Adds Profile (id, bio) + ProfileRepository. createStudent saves a new department (CSC) and profile ("simple bio"), sets them, saves the student. GET /api/student/{id} returns it.

  1. First GET: infinite JSON. Department still had students; Jackson loops Student → Department → students → … (Lecture 30 warning). Fix: remove the back-reference, recreate tables.
  2. Without LAZY: full student; one SELECT with LEFT JOIN department and LEFT JOIN profile (eager).
  3. With LAZY on both: JSON shows "hibernateLazyInitializer" inside department and profile (Hibernate proxies). Log: three separate SELECTs (student, department, profile), triggered when Jackson read the proxies.
  4. Proof of laziness in fetchStudentById: 
    Student s1 = studentRepository.findById(id);
System.out.println("lazily fetched student");
Department d1 = s1.getDepartment();
System.out.println("lazily fetched department");
Profile p1 = s1.getProfile();
System.out.println("lazily fetched profile");
return s1;
    All three print before any department/profile SELECT. A proxy loads only when its data is touched (e.g. d1.getName()), not on getDepartment().
When to use which: lazy saves a lot when the graph is deep and often unused (you may never look at the department). Eager only where the data is always needed.

The N+1 problem

List<Student> students = …;                 // SELECT * FROM students   (1 query, e.g. 100 rows)
for (Student s : students) {
    System.out.println(s.getDepartment().getName());   // lazy proxy: 1 query per student
}
  • 1 root query returns N = 100 students.
  • Loop fires N more (one per department): 1 + 100 = 101 round trips.
  • Looks harmless (list "already in memory") — classic mistake by developers unaware of internals.

Spotting it: slow endpoint; SQL log repeats SELECT … WHERE id = ? per row.

The first-level cache softens it

Students share departments (many-to-one); a department loaded into the persistence context (Lecture 29) is reused without a query. 100 students in 3 departments → 1 + 3; 100 distinct departments → 1 + 100. Verified on Boot 4.1.0: 12 students, 3 departments, loop over getDepartment().getName() → 1 student SELECT + 3 department SELECTs.

Lazy loading isn't the villain: N+1 appears when lazy loading is used without understanding it. Lazy still solves the bigger whole-graph problem; know when a path touches the association and fetch it up front there.

The fix: fetch specific associations per query

Keep associations LAZY globally; for queries that need them, fetch in the same SQL. The lecture uses an entity graph:

public interface StudentRepository extends JpaRepository<Student, Long> {

    @EntityGraph(attributePaths = "department")          // this query fetches department too
    @Query("select s from Student s")
    List<Student> findAllWithDepartment();

    @EntityGraph(attributePaths = {"department", "profile"})
    Optional<Student> findById(Long id);
}
  • Rest of the app stays lazy; only these methods fetch eagerly (their callers loop over it).
  • List several paths — any association callers will touch.
  • Verified: @EntityGraph(attributePaths = "department") on the Spring Data method → one SELECT … LEFT JOIN department instead of 1 + 3.
Correction: @EntityGraph is a Spring Data annotation. The lecture puts it on a method of its hand-written EntityManager-based @Repository class, but org.springframework.data.jpa.repository.EntityGraph is honoured only on Spring Data repository interface methods (jakarta.persistence.EntityGraph is an interface, not an annotation). Verified: silently ignored there, still 1 + 3. With plain EntityManager:
  • JOIN FETCH: em.createQuery("select s from Student s join fetch s.department", Student.class) — verified: 1 query.
  • JPA entity graph as a hint: EntityGraph<Student> g = em.createEntityGraph(Student.class); g.addAttributeNodes("department"); then em.find(Student.class, id, Map.of("jakarta.persistence.fetchgraph", g)), or query.setHint(…). Or @NamedEntityGraph on the entity.

N+1 fixes compared

ApproachQueries for 12 students / 3 deptsNotes
LAZY + loop (the problem)1 + 3 (1 + N if departments distinct)Verified
Make it EAGER1 + 3Does not fix N+1 for queries; see Corrections
JOIN FETCH in JPQL1Works with plain EntityManager (verified)
@EntityGraph on a Spring Data method1Lecture's approach (verified)
Batch fetching: hibernate.default_batch_fetch_size=50 or @BatchSize1 + 1 (… WHERE id IN (…))Stays lazy, loads proxies in batches (added)
DTO projection (select new …(s.name, d.name) from Student s join s.department d)1No entities/proxies; best for read-only views (added)

Corrections & worth knowing

  • Eager doesn't prevent N+1 for queries. @ManyToOne/@OneToOne are EAGER by default (first GET showed the join), but EAGER guarantees data is loaded, not how: find() uses a join; JPQL select s from Student s runs, then one SELECT per distinct department. Verified: EAGER @ManyToOne + plain JPQL → 1 + 3 even without touching the department. Hence: make @ManyToOne/@OneToOne LAZY, fetch explicitly per query. (@OneToMany/@ManyToMany default LAZY.)
