  • Cascade REMOVE is chatty and covers only the mapped association. Verified: loads students, one DELETE each, then the department. For large child sets use a bulk JPQL DELETE or DB-level ON DELETE CASCADE. Another table referencing the department (not mapped with cascade) still caused an FK violation.
  • Where cascade belongs: the parent's @OneToMany (or a truly owned one-to-one). Never cascade REMOVE from @ManyToOne (deleting a student would delete the department) or on @ManyToMany (shared courses vanish).
  • orphanRemoval = true ≠ REMOVE. REMOVE deletes children when the parent is removed; orphanRemoval also deletes a child merely removed from the collection.
  • Returning entities with lazy proxies as JSON is fragile. "hibernateLazyInitializer" leaks Hibernate internals; it only worked because Open Session In View (Lecture 29) kept the EntityManager open during serialisation. With spring.jpa.open-in-view=false → LazyInitializationException. Map to DTOs (Lecture 16) inside the transaction.
  • Also available: Hibernate fetch profiles and @Fetch(FetchMode.SUBSELECT); but JOIN FETCH, entity graphs, batch size and DTO projections cover almost every case.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"escaping" / "cascade tape" = cascading / CascadeType.
"relationship is one-to-one" (Student → Department, after removing cascade) → many-to-one.
"remove calls refresh" in the repository → it calls entityManager.remove.
"n + 1 is not bad" → means lazy loading is not bad.
"lazy initializer" / "hibernate lazy initializer" in the JSON = the proxy's hibernateLazyInitializer field.

Lecture 32: Spring Data JPA Masterclass — Repository Interfaces, Runtime Proxies, Derived Queries, @Query/JPQL, Sorting and Pagination

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #32 · 1h47m · Code Army · ties together JDBC (26), Spring JDBC (27) and Hibernate/JPA (28–31)

The road so far

LayerWhat it removedWhat was still manual
Raw JDBC (26)—Connections, statements, SQL, mapping, closing
Spring JDBC (27)Connections, statements, closing (Template pattern)SQL strings, row mappers
Hibernate / JPA (28–31)SQL: just persist/find/removeThe repository class itself
Spring Data JPAThe repository implementationOnly an interface

Every hand-written JPA repository looks the same:

@Repository
public class StudentRepository {
    @PersistenceContext private EntityManager entityManager;
    public void save(Student student)       { entityManager.persist(student); }
    public Student findById(Long id)        { return entityManager.find(Student.class, id); }
    public void delete(Student student)     { entityManager.remove(student); }
}

DepartmentRepository/UserRepository differ only in entity type and id type; the logic never changes. Not hard, just repetitive.

The idea: declare an interface, Spring supplies the implementation

  1. Spring provides base interfaces (Repository, JpaRepository) with the standard methods.
  2. Your interface extends one (no implementing, no bodies); inject it and call it.
  3. Spring creates the implementation at runtime — the "magic" from CRUD lectures 11–13.

Spring Data vs Spring Data JPA

  • Spring Data = umbrella project: one repository model (interfaces, method naming, paging/sorting) regardless of store.
  • Per-store modules: Spring Data JPA (relational, via JPA/Hibernate), MongoDB, Redis, Cassandra, Neo4j…
  • Benefit (polymorphism): same method names (save, findById…) — SQL via Hibernate for JPA, document ops for MongoDB.
  • JPA (spec: EntityManager, @Entity, @Column…) ≠ Spring Data JPA (repository abstraction on top).

Worked example: minimal Spring Data repository

Project spring-data-jpa-demo: StudentController (/api/student: POST create, GET /{id}); Student entity (auto id, name, email, age; Lombok); empty service and repository package.

public interface StudentRepository extends JpaRepository<Student, Long> {
    // nothing, not even @Repository
}

@Service
public class StudentService {
    private final StudentRepository studentRepository;
    public StudentService(StudentRepository studentRepository) {
        this.studentRepository = studentRepository;
    }

    public void createStudent(Student student) {
        studentRepository.save(student);
    }

    public Student fetchStudentById(Long id) {
        Optional<Student> studentOptional = studentRepository.findById(id);
        return studentOptional.get();       // happy path only; see Corrections
    }
}
  • JpaRepository<Student, Long> — the two generics are exactly what varies: entity and primary-key type.
  • findById returns Optional<Student> (id may not exist).
  • Demo: startup creates student table; POST {"name": "Aditya", "email": "ad@gmail.com", "age": 29} → "done" + INSERT; GET /api/student/1 → student + SELECT. No implementation written.

The repository interface hierarchy

InterfaceWhat you get
Repository<T, ID>Nothing. Marker naming entity + id type; declare only methods you want.
CrudRepositorysave, saveAll, findById, existsById, findAll, findAllById, count, delete, deleteById, deleteAll… Bulk methods return Iterable.
ListCrudRepositorySame, but saveAll/findAll/findAllById return List
PagingAndSortingRepositoryfindAll(Sort) (Iterable), findAll(Pageable) (Page)
ListPagingAndSortingRepositoryfindAll(Sort) returning List
JpaRepositoryAll above + flush, saveAndFlush, saveAllAndFlush, deleteAllInBatch, deleteAllByIdInBatch…

Marker Repository: declare only what you need

public interface StudentRepository extends Repository<Student, Long> {
    void save(Student student);
    Optional<Student> findById(Long id);
}

With an empty Repository, save(…) won't compile. Declare the two methods (no bodies) and POST/GET work (INSERT/SELECT logged): Spring matched the names save/findById to its standard implementations. Benefit: control — e.g. omit delete methods so services can't delete students.

Runtime: proxy → SimpleJpaRepository or query engine

  1. At startup Spring creates a runtime proxy implementing your interface; that's what's autowired. Verified: bean is jdk.proxy2.$Proxy… targeting org.springframework.data.jpa.repository.support.SimpleJpaRepository.
  2. Standard methods (save, findById…) delegate to SimpleJpaRepository<T, ID> — an ordinary pre-written class implementing JpaRepository generically via EntityManager. Nothing generated; same logic for every entity: 
    // conceptually, inside SimpleJpaRepository<T, ID>
public <S extends T> S save(S entity) {
    if (entityInformation.isNew(entity)) { entityManager.persist(entity); return entity; }
    else                                  { return entityManager.merge(entity); }
}
public Optional<T> findById(ID id) {
    return Optional.ofNullable(entityManager.find(domainClass, id));
}
  3. Custom methods (findByEmail) go to the query-method infrastructure, which parses the name (or reads @Query) into a query, run through EntityManager.

All still via EntityManager, so Lecture 29 applies: persistence context, dirty checking, flush, entity states.

What the standard methods really do

  • save doesn't write immediately: persist (new) or merge (existing/detached) into the persistence context; INSERT/UPDATE at flush/commit (or now with saveAndFlush).
  • saveAll(list) isn't one bulk SQL: loops calling save; writes flushed together (Lecture 29 write-behind).
  • findById = entityManager.find in an Optional; findAll = all rows.
  • existsById loads no entity, only a count: select count(*) from student s1_0 where s1_0.id=? (verified).
  • count, delete, deleteById, deleteAll, plus JpaRepository's flush, saveAndFlush (save + flush), saveAllAndFlush, deleteAllInBatch (older deleteInBatch deprecated).

Update without an update method

@Transactional                     // required: see below
public void updateStudent(Student studentRequest, Long id) {
    Student student = studentRepository.findById(id).get();   // now managed
    student.setAge(studentRequest.getAge());
    student.setName(studentRequest.getName());
    // no save needed: dirty checking flushes an UPDATE at commit (Lecture 29)
}
Correction: needs @Transactional on the service method: the lecture omits it. Without an outer transaction, findById runs in its own short transaction (repository methods are transactional), so the entity is already detached at the setters. Verified: without it age stayed 29, no UPDATE; with it, select + update, age 77. Alternative: call studentRepository.save(student).

Derived queries

Split at By: before = subject (action); after = predicate (conditions on properties).

Optional<Student> findByEmail(String email);
//      subject: find    predicate: Email
//  → select ... from student where email = ?
Subject keywordsPredicate keywords
find…By, exists…By, count…By, delete…By, findTop3By, findDistinctByAnd, Or, Not, Like, Containing, StartingWith, EndingWith, GreaterThan, LessThan, Between, In, NotIn, IsNull, IsNotNull, True, False, IgnoreCase, OrderBy…Asc/Desc

findByEmailAndAge(String email, int age) → … where email = ? and age = ? (params in order); findByEmailOrderByNameAsc(String email) → … order by name asc; findByEmailLike(String pattern) → … where email like ?. IntelliJ auto-completes: findBy → Email, Age, AgeAfter…

Worth knowing (verified):
  • Like adds no wildcards; Containing does. findByEmailLike("a1@gmail") → [] (pass "%a1@gmail%" yourself); findByEmailContaining("a1@gmail") found it. Same for StartingWith/EndingWith.
  • Bad names fail at startup. Typo findByEmial → "No property 'emial' found for type 'Student'". The name is the query, validated when the proxy is built.

When names get out of hand

findTop10ByNameContainingIgnoreCaseAndAgeGreaterThanAndActiveTrueAndDepartmentNameOrderByAdmissionDateDesc(…)

Works (top-10, case-insensitive contains, age >, active = true, department name, ordering) but unreadable and error-prone; DepartmentName is ambiguous: field departmentName or Student.department.name? (Added: Spring prefers the longest matching property; underscore forces traversal: Department_Name.) For complex logic, write the query.

Custom queries with @Query

Native SQL

@Query(value = """
        SELECT * FROM student WHERE email = :email
        """, nativeQuery = true)
Optional<Student> findByEmail(@Param("email") String email);

// positional alternative
@Query(value = "SELECT * FROM student WHERE email = ?1 AND name = ?2", nativeQuery = true)
Optional<Student> findByEmailAndName(String email, String name);
  • nativeQuery = true = plain SQL passed unchanged; uses table and column names.
  • Named: :email bound via @Param("email"), order irrelevant. Positional: ?1, ?2 follow param order.
  • With @Query, the method name doesn't drive the query — keep it short and meaningful.
  • Best for complex SQL: multiple joins, DB-specific features.

JPQL (Jakarta Persistence Query Language)

SQL-like but against entities and properties, not tables/columns. Useful when thinking in the entity model or not relying on the physical schema. Hibernate translates it to SQL (its dialect, HQL, is essentially the same).

SQL (native)JPQL
SELECT * FROM student WHERE email = :emailSELECT s FROM Student s WHERE s.email = :email
Table student, column user_emailEntity Student (case-sensitive), property email
JOIN department d ON s.dept_id = d.idJOIN s.department d (navigate the association)

Joins in JPQL: explicit vs implicit

-- explicit join
SELECT s FROM Student s JOIN s.department d WHERE d.active = true

-- implicit join (path navigation, no JOIN keyword)
SELECT s FROM Student s WHERE s.department.active = true

-- both become SQL like:
-- select s.id, s.name, s.dept_id from student s
--   join department d on s.dept_id = d.id where d.active = true

No dept_id needed: the mapping (Lecture 30) supplies the join condition. LEFT JOIN, inner joins etc. work as in SQL.

Sorting with Sort

Sort in the DB (ORDER BY), not in Java. Sort generates the clause:

Sort sort = Sort.by("age").descending();                        // ORDER BY age DESC
Sort sort = Sort.by(Sort.Direction.ASC, "name");                 // ORDER BY name ASC
Sort sort = Sort.by("name").ascending()
                .and(Sort.by("age").descending());               // ORDER BY name ASC, age DESC
List<Student> students = studentRepository.findAll(sort);

Default ascending. Demo: four Adityas (29, 27, 25, 24) → 24→29 asc, 29→24 with .descending(). GET /api/student/name/Aditya needed its own path: /{name} clashed with /{id} (IllegalStateException: Ambiguous handler methods).

Correction: the lecture's endpoint ignores the name: fetchAll(String name) calls studentRepository.findAll(sort), sorting all students; name unused. Looked right only because all rows were Adityas; verified: adding Rohit, he appeared too. Filter and sort with a derived query taking a Sort: 
List<Student> findByName(String name, Sort sort);   // where name = ? order by age
// verified: returned only the four Adityas, sorted

Pagination with Pageable, Page and Slice

Fetching 2 million rows at once overloads DB, network and API. Fetch pages (10,000 rows, then the next 10,000…).

Pageable pageable = PageRequest.of(0, 2);          // page number (0-based), page size
Page<Student> page = studentRepository.findAll(pageable);
List<Student> students = page.getContent();

Pageable = interface; PageRequest.of(page, size) (optional Sort) implements it. 0-based: PageRequest.of(4, 10) skips 40 rows → rows 41–50. Demo: page 0 size 2 → ages 29, 27; page 1 → 25, 24. In practice page/size come from query params.

Page methodReturns
getContent()Rows of this page
getNumber() / getSize() / getNumberOfElements()Page index / requested size / rows actually here
getTotalElements() / getTotalPages()Totals across pages (need a count query)
hasNext() / hasPrevious() / isFirst() / isLast()Navigation

Page vs Slice

  • Page extends Slice, adding getTotalElements()/getTotalPages() via a second, count query.
  • Verified: findAll(PageRequest.of(0, 2)) ran … offset ? rows fetch first ? rows only plus select count(s1_0.id) from student → totalElements = 5, totalPages = 3.
  • Verified: Slice<Student> findSliceByName(String name, Pageable p) ran one query yet knew hasNext() (fetches size + 1 rows).
  • Use Slice for infinite-scroll / "load more" without totals; counting millions of rows is expensive.

Spring Data JPA's five responsibilities

(1) Common CRUD — SimpleJpaRepository; (2) runtime repository implementation — proxy; (3) queries from method names — query-method infrastructure; (4) custom queries — @Query, native/JPQL; (5) pagination & sorting — Sort, Pageable, Page/Slice.

Corrections & worth knowing

  • save picks persist vs merge by "is the entity new?", not "is it detached?". With a generated id, new = id == null (or @Version null). A manually set id counts as not new → merge, which SELECTs first. Verified: saving Manual with id = 5 ran select … where id=? then insert. Implement Persistable<ID> to control isNew().
  • Don't call Optional.get() blindly — NoSuchElementException on missing id. Use orElseThrow(() -> new StudentNotFoundException(id)), mapped to 404 in @RestControllerAdvice (Lecture 17).
  • Repository methods are already transactional: SimpleJpaRepository is @Transactional(readOnly = true), read-write on modifying methods. A service calling several still needs its own @Transactional for one unit (and for dirty-checking updates).
  • Always sort when paginating. Without ORDER BY row order is arbitrary, so pages can overlap/skip. Use PageRequest.of(page, size, Sort.by("id")).
  • Modifying queries need @Modifying: @Query("update Student s set s.age = :age where s.id = :id") + @Modifying, in a transaction. Bypasses the persistence context — add clearAutomatically = true if managed entities could go stale.
  • Projections: return an interface/record (interface StudentNameOnly { String getName(); }) to fetch only some columns — often better than loading whole entities.
  • Returning Page from a controller: since Spring Data 3.3, serialising PageImpl logs a warning (unstable JSON shape). Return page.getContent() + your own metadata DTO, or @EnableSpringDataWebSupport(pageSerializationMode = VIA_DTO). Controllers can take a Pageable param directly (?page=0&size=20&sort=age,desc).
  • Hierarchy change (Spring Data 3.0): PagingAndSortingRepository no longer extends CrudRepository; List… variants added. Older tutorials show the old chain. getById/getOne deprecated for getReferenceById.
  • JPQL = "Jakarta" Persistence Query Language (lecture self-corrects); JPQL uses entity names (@Entity(name = …), default simple class name), not table names.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"the @Query clause WHERE email = email" → named params need a colon: WHERE email = :email.
"saveAll calls entityManager.save" → it calls the repository's save (persist/merge); EntityManager has no save.
"the proxy decides between SimpleJpaRepository or something else" → "something else" = query-method (derivation/@Query) infrastructure.

Lecture 33: Transactions — ACID, Begin/Commit/Rollback and @Transactional

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #33 · 49 min · Code Army · builds on Hibernate's persistence context (29), AOP around advice (23) and Spring Data JPA (32)

The problem: a half-done money transfer

Transfer ₹10,000 from account A to B = two statements: (1) UPDATE A: balance − 10,000; (2) UPDATE B: balance + 10,000. If (1) succeeds and the DB crashes / (2) fails, A lost ₹10,000 and B got nothing: the sum dropped by ₹10,000, breaking "conservation of money".

Definition (transaction):
A group of operations executed as one block: all succeed or none take effect — never "first succeeded, second failed". 2 or 50 operations, same transaction. Use for critical resources (money, inventory, bookings) where a partial result is unacceptable.

ACID

PropertyMeaningLecture example
AtomicityIndivisible like an atom: all or nothingDebit A and credit B both happen, or neither
ConsistencySuccess moves the DB from one valid state to anotherA has ₹5,000, sends ₹10,000; without a balance check A ends at −₹5,000 (invalid)
IsolationConcurrent transactions don't corrupt each other's reads/writes; each sees correct valuesTwo withdrawals on account C, below
DurabilityOnce committed, survives crash/restart; never lost or rolled backTransfer completes, DB crashes; after restart B still has the ₹10,000
Consistency is partly your job: the DB can't know "balance must not go negative" unless told. The app enforces it via validations (debitAccount below); DB constraints (CHECK (balance >= 0), foreign keys, NOT NULL) are a second line of defence (added).

Isolation: two-withdrawal example

Account C has ₹6,000; T1 withdraws ₹2,000 and T2 ₹3,000 concurrently:

Interleaving that breaks isolation (a "lost update")
StepT1T2Balance in DB
1reads 6,0006,000
2reads 6,0006,000
3computes 6,000 − 2,000, writes 4,0004,000
4computes 6,000 − 3,000, writes 3,0003,000 — wrong

Should be ₹1,000; T1's update was lost (both read the same start). Correct: T1 writes 4,000, then T2 reads 4,000 and writes 1,000.

Nuance: isolation doesn't mean "one transaction at a time" — both progress; the question is what T2 may read while T1 runs (always the correct value). Isolation levels: next lecture.

ACID is a relational-DB property; transactions are a database concept, not a Spring invention.

Transactions in SQL; the physical transaction

BEGIN;                                                    -- MySQL: START TRANSACTION also works
UPDATE account SET balance = balance - 10000 WHERE id = 1;
UPDATE account SET balance = balance + 10000 WHERE id = 2;
COMMIT;                                                   -- or ROLLBACK; if something went wrong
  • BEGIN starts the boundary; COMMIT = all done, make permanent; ROLLBACK = something failed, restore the pre-BEGIN state.
  • Rollback example: A 15,000 → 5,000 succeeded, crediting B throws → A back at 15,000.
  • Physical transaction = the actual DB transaction; Spring Boot just uses it. Lecturer mentions "virtual" transactions for later (Spring's term: logical transaction — several @Transactional methods sharing one physical transaction; that's propagation, next lecture).
Rollback isn't a reverse UPDATE issued by you or Spring: nobody runs "add ₹10,000 back"; the DB tracks and undoes the changes (lecturer: like Cmd+Z / popping a stack). Precision (added): MySQL InnoDB applies an undo log of before-images (big rollbacks take time); PostgreSQL MVCC marks the transaction aborted so its row versions are never visible. No application-level compensating SQL either way.

The same transfer at each layer

Stack: Spring Data JPA → JPA/Hibernate → JDBC. Higher layer = more abstracted:

LayerHow you demarcate a transaction
JDBC (26)conn.setAutoCommit(false) … conn.commit() / conn.rollback() (added: JDBC exposes BEGIN/COMMIT/ROLLBACK via Connection)
Hibernate / JPA (29)session.beginTransaction() / tx.commit() / tx.rollback(); JPA: em.getTransaction().begin()…
SpringOne annotation: @Transactional

By hand in Hibernate:

Transaction tx = session.beginTransaction();
try {
    op1();
    op2();
    tx.commit();
} catch (Exception e) {
    tx.rollback();
    throw e;
}

How @Transactional works: around advice on a proxy

AOP (Lectures 23–25): Spring wraps @Transactional methods in the code above as an around advice — begin before proceed(), commit on normal return, roll back on throw.

Under the hood (added): advice = TransactionInterceptor → PlatformTransactionManager; with Spring Data JPA, the auto-configured JpaTransactionManager opens the EntityManager and JDBC connection. You could build it yourself: @Around on a custom annotation with Hibernate begin/commit/rollback.

Correction: class-level @Transactional isn't "one transaction for the class": it applies to every public method; each call gets its own transaction (unless one is active and propagation joins it). Method-level overrides class-level.

Worked example: transaction-demo

Entities

  • Account (@Entity, Lombok @Getter @Setter @NoArgsConstructor): Long id (@Id @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY)), String name, BigDecimal balance; domain ops debitAccount(BigDecimal amount)/creditAccount(BigDecimal amount) with validation.
  • TransferRecord (audit): id, Long fromAccountId, Long toAccountId, BigDecimal amount, LocalDate transferredAt.
    • Plain Long ids, not @ManyToOne Account: keeps the audit table simple; only ids needed.
    • @AllArgsConstructor included id ("Cannot resolve constructor"), so an id-less constructor was generated in IntelliJ.
public void debitAccount(BigDecimal amount) {
    if (amount == null || amount.signum() <= 0) {
        throw new IllegalArgumentException("Amount must be positive");
    }
    if (balance.compareTo(amount) < 0) {
        throw new RuntimeException("Insufficient balance");   // lecture: a custom exception would be better
    }
    balance = balance.subtract(amount);
}

public void creditAccount(BigDecimal amount) {
    if (amount == null || amount.signum() <= 0) {
        throw new IllegalArgumentException("Amount must be positive");
    }
    balance = balance.add(amount);
}
Why signum(), compareTo(), subtract(): BigDecimal is an object; <, -, + don't work. signum() → −1/0/1; compareTo() compares values. Never equals() for money: new BigDecimal("5.0").equals(new BigDecimal("5.00")) is false (scales differ), compareTo gives 0. BigDecimal over double: binary floating point can't represent 0.1 exactly.

Repositories and the transactional service

public interface AccountRepository  extends JpaRepository<Account, Long> {}
public interface TransferRepository extends JpaRepository<TransferRecord, Long> {}

@Service
public class TransferService {
    private final AccountRepository accountRepository;
    private final TransferRepository transferRepository;
    // constructor injection

    @Transactional
    public void transfer(Long fromAccountId, Long toAccountId, BigDecimal amount) {
        Account fromAccount = accountRepository.findById(fromAccountId)
                .orElseThrow(() -> new RuntimeException("Account not found"));
        Account toAccount = accountRepository.findById(toAccountId)
                .orElseThrow(() -> new RuntimeException("Account not found"));

        fromAccount.debitAccount(amount);
        toAccount.creditAccount(amount);

        transferRepository.save(
            new TransferRecord(fromAccountId, toAccountId, amount, LocalDate.now()));
    }
}
  • Debit, credit, audit record in one transaction: all or none.
  • Lecture fixes: raw JpaRepository didn't compile → add <Account, Long>/<TransferRecord, Long>; LocalDateTime.now() into a LocalDate field → LocalDate.now(); params renamed fromTransactionId → fromAccountId.
  • Controllers (return "done"; entities as request bodies, no DTOs):
    • AccountController: POST /api/account {name, balance} → AccountService.createAccount → accountRepository.save.
    • TransferController: POST /api/transfer {fromAccountId, toAccountId, amount} bound to TransferRecord → transferService.transfer(…).

Demo run

  1. Startup creates account and transfer_record (MySQL, DBeaver).
  2. POST /api/account {"name": "Aditya", "balance": 15000} and {"name": "Rohit", "balance": 5000} → done.
  3. POST /api/transfer {"fromAccountId": 1, "toAccountId": 2, "amount": 10000} → Aditya 5,000, Rohit 15,000, one transfer_record row.

Q: "Balances changed without save on the accounts. Why?"

Dirty checking (Lecture 29): findById loads both accounts as managed entities in the transaction's persistence context; at commit Hibernate compares with snapshots and issues the UPDATEs. No accountRepository.save(fromAccount) needed.

Flush is not commit: demonstrating rollback

Force a flush after every step, then throw:

@Transactional
public void transfer(Long fromAccountId, Long toAccountId, BigDecimal amount) {
    // ... load both accounts ...
    fromAccount.debitAccount(amount);
    accountRepository.saveAndFlush(fromAccount);     // UPDATE sent to DB now
    toAccount.creditAccount(amount);
    accountRepository.saveAndFlush(toAccount);       // UPDATE sent to DB now
    transferRepository.save(new TransferRecord(fromAccountId, toAccountId, amount, LocalDate.now()));
    transferRepository.flush();                      // INSERT sent to DB now
    throw new RuntimeException("Some error occurred");
}
Rohit sends ₹10,000 back (2 → 1); start: Aditya 5,000, Rohit 15,000
RunResult in DBWhy
With @TransactionalUnchanged: 5,000 / 15,000, no new recordFlushed SQL ran inside the open transaction; exception rolled it all back
@Transactional commented outChanged: 15,000 / 5,000 + new record, despite the exceptionNo outer transaction: each repository call committed on its own

Verified (Boot 4.1.0 + H2): with annotation 5000/15000, 1 record; without, 15000/5000, 2 records.

The real failure mode without a transaction: here the exception came last. The scarier case: exception after debit, before credit — money leaves and never arrives (the opening problem). @Transactional prevents it.
Flush vs commit: flush sends pending SQL inside the current transaction — invisible to others (normal isolation), still rollback-able. Commit ends the transaction, making changes permanent and visible (durability).

Q: "What if I catch the exception inside the method?"

The transaction commits: rollback triggers only when the exception propagates out through the proxy; swallowed = normal return. Verified: catching a RuntimeException inside committed the transfer. Rethrow, or mark rollback: TransactionAspectSupport.currentTransactionStatus().setRollbackOnly().

Corrections & worth knowing

  • By default only unchecked exceptions roll back (RuntimeException, Error); checked exceptions commit. Verified: checked exception after debit+credit committed both; @Transactional(rollbackFor = Exception.class) rolled back. Lecture only throws RuntimeExceptions; classic interview question.
  • Self-invocation bypasses the transaction (proxy-based, same as AOP in Lecture 23): this.transfer(…) from the same class skips the proxy. Verified: non-transactional selfCall() invoking transferFailTx() left partial writes committed. Fixes: call from another bean, restructure, or TransactionTemplate.
  • Visibility: use public methods. Private never proxied; since Spring 6.0 protected/package-private work with CGLIB proxies, but public is the safe convention.
  • Where: service layer around a whole use case (transfer), not controllers. Repository methods are individually transactional (Lecture 32) — why each call committed alone in the no-annotation run.
  • Programmatic: TransactionTemplate.execute(status -> { … }) — explicit boundaries, e.g. around part of a method.
  • Durability in practice: write-ahead log (InnoDB redo log, PostgreSQL WAL) flushed to disk at commit; data pages written later, replayable after a crash.
  • @Transactional(readOnly = true) for reads: Hibernate skips dirty checking/flushing; some drivers/DBs optimise.
  • Audit timestamp: use LocalDateTime/Instant (lecture's LocalDate records only the day), or let the DB fill it (@CreationTimestamp, column default CURRENT_TIMESTAMP), as mentioned.
  • No throws Throwable needed for orElseThrow(() -> new RuntimeException(…)) (lecture briefly added one); unchecked needs no declaration.

Gaps worth raising in an interview

  • @Transactional alone doesn't stop the lost update. transfer reads (findById) → modifies in Java → writes: the T1/T2 pattern. Under MySQL REPEATABLE READ (or PostgreSQL READ COMMITTED) two concurrent transfers can both read 15,000, losing a debit. Fixes:
    • Pessimistic lock: @Lock(LockModeType.PESSIMISTIC_WRITE) on a finder (SELECT … FOR UPDATE).
    • Optimistic lock: @Version column + retry on OptimisticLockException.
    • Atomic SQL: UPDATE account SET balance = balance - :amt WHERE id = :id AND balance >= :amt, check affected rows.
  • Deadlocks: 1→2 and concurrent 2→1 each hold one row lock, wait for the other. Lock in a consistent order (lower id first).
  • Missing validation: fromAccountId == toAccountId; "Account not found" should be a custom exception → 404 (Lecture 17), "Insufficient balance" → 400/422.
  • Entities as request bodies: client could send id/transferredAt. Use a TransferRequest DTO with @NotNull/@Positive.
  • Idempotency: a retried POST /api/transfer transfers twice; real payment APIs take an idempotency key.
  • Cross-service/DB: one @Transactional = one database; use saga or outbox patterns.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"account A decreases by ₹1,000" → ₹10,000.
"its balance wasn't 55,000" (durability) → ₹5,000.
"Aditya ₹15,000 … ₹1,000" and "Rohit has ₹1,000" → ₹15,000 both.
"updated because of dirty reads" → dirty checking; a dirty read is an isolation anomaly (reading uncommitted data).
"@Transaction" → @Transactional (org.springframework.transaction.annotation.Transactional; jakarta.transaction.Transactional works but lacks readOnly, isolation).
"in JDBC we'd write BEGIN/COMMIT SQL" → JDBC uses Connection.setAutoCommit(false), commit(), rollback().

Next lecture: transaction propagation and isolation levels.

Lecture 34: @Transactional Deep Dive — rollbackFor, Propagation Levels and Isolation Levels

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #34 · 1h00m · Code Army · continues Lecture 33 (transactions, ACID)

The five attributes of @Transactional

@Transactional(
    propagation = Propagation.REQUIRED,
    isolation   = Isolation.READ_COMMITTED,
    timeout     = 5,
    readOnly    = false,
    rollbackFor = IOException.class
)
public void createOrder(…) { … }
AttributeMeaningActual default (added)
propagationBehaviour when called from another method: join caller's transaction, start new, run without…REQUIRED
isolationHow much concurrent transactions see of each other's changesIsolation.DEFAULT = DB's default
timeoutSeconds until timeout-1 = none (DB/driver default)
readOnlyRead-only or read-writefalse
rollbackForException types that trigger rollbackEmpty: RuntimeException and Error only
Precision note (added): the lecture calls the slide's values defaults; only REQUIRED and readOnly = false are. READ_COMMITTED/timeout = 5 are examples; real defaults Isolation.DEFAULT, -1.

Most important: propagation, isolation, rollbackFor.

rollbackFor

  • Unchecked (RuntimeException + subclasses) roll back by default, with or without rollbackFor.
  • Checked exceptions don't: the transaction commits (verified in Lecture 33's notes).
  • rollbackFor = SomeCheckedException.class makes it (and subclasses) roll back; a parent works too, e.g. rollbackFor = Exception.class.
Worth adding: "runtime exceptions always roll back" holds only by default: noRollbackFor = SomeRuntimeException.class makes one commit. Checked commit by default per the EJB convention: checked = expected business outcome the caller handles; unchecked = unexpected failure.

Setup: orders and payment audits

Project transaction-demo, reset:

  • Order: id, productName, amount.
  • PaymentAudit: id, amount, orderId (acts as FK), new boolean success.
  • OrderController: POST /api/order → orderService.placeOrder(order) → "done". No audit controller (nobody audits directly).
  • OrderRepository / PaymentAuditRepository extend JpaRepository<…, Long>.
@Service
public class OrderService {
    private final OrderRepository orderRepository;
    private final PaymentAuditService paymentAuditService;
    // constructor injection

    @Transactional                                   // OUTER method
    public void placeOrder(Order order) {
        orderRepository.save(order);
        paymentAuditService.audit(order);            // calls into ANOTHER bean
    }
}

@Service
public class PaymentAuditService {
    private final PaymentAuditRepository paymentAuditRepository;

    @Transactional(propagation = Propagation.REQUIRED)   // INNER method
    public void audit(Order order) {
        PaymentAudit paymentAudit = new PaymentAudit(order.getAmount(), order.getId(), true);
        paymentAuditRepository.save(paymentAudit);
    }
}
Setup fixes:
  • Import Spring's org.springframework.transaction.annotation.Transactional, not jakarta.transaction.Transactional (no isolation, timeout, readOnly; its TxType lacks NESTED).
  • ORDER is SQL-reserved: startup failed "You have an error in your SQL syntax … near 'order'" → @Table(name = "orders").

Propagation: outer and inner methods

placeOrder = outer, audit = inner. Propagation is set on the inner method: if a transaction may already exist, join it, start my own, run without, or refuse? Seen already: a @Transactional service's repository calls join its transaction.

Physical transaction: if both run in the same DB transaction, that's one physical transaction — shared EntityManager/persistence context (Lecture 29); either rolls back → both do. Each method's scope inside it = a logical transaction; many logical → one physical (added).

Propagation applies only via the Spring proxy (another bean) — hence a separate PaymentAuditService. this.audit() ignores the inner annotation (self-invocation, Lecture 33).

REQUIRED (default): join if exists, else create

Rule: transaction exists → use it; else create one. Either way it requires one.

Q: "Inner sets its own isolation/timeout — which wins?"

The outer. Lecture: isolation = READ_COMMITTED, timeout = 30 on placeOrder, isolation = SERIALIZABLE, timeout = 5 on audit; audit joins, inheriting the existing settings; its own are ignored.

Verified: inner REQUIRED with isolation = SERIALIZABLE, readOnly = true reported outer's READ_COMMITTED, readOnly = false — silently. validateExistingTransaction = true on the transaction manager makes mismatches throw (added).

Demo: four REQUIRED experiments

#ScenarioResultWhy
1Happy path: POST {"productName": "iPhone 17 Pro Max", "amount": …}"done"; two INSERTs; rows in orders and payment_audit (success = 1)One physical transaction commits both
2Outer throws RuntimeException("Some error occurred") after audit() (Speaker, ₹2,000)Both INSERTs ran, neither row existsShared rollback undoes the audit too
3Inner throws RuntimeException("Something went wrong"), inner catches it"done"; both rows savedException never crossed the proxy
4Inner throws, outer catches with empty catch (Laptop, ₹50,000)Client gets error; neither row savedUnexpectedRollbackException — below

Experiment 4: "silently rolled back because it has been marked as rollback-only"

  1. audit() throws; its proxy sees the runtime exception.
  2. Inner scope only joined, so it can't roll back directly — marks the shared transaction rollback-only.
  3. placeOrder catches and returns normally; its proxy tries to commit.
  4. Manager sees the flag, rolls back, and throws UnexpectedRollbackException so the caller isn't misled.

Verified message: "Transaction silently rolled back because it has been marked as rollback-only". Catching an inner exception doesn't save a joined transaction.

When to use REQUIRED: inner work depends on outer (audit only if the order exists) — the usual case, hence default. No outer transaction → audit creates its own.

REQUIRES_NEW: always a fresh, independent transaction

Rule: none exists → create (like REQUIRED). Exists → suspend it, create a new physical transaction with separate EntityManager, persistence context and (added) JDBC connection.

Suspend = pause (not commit/rollback): outer waits, inner completes, outer resumes.

ScenarioResult (lecture demo; verified)
Normal flow (Laptop)Both rows saved; looks like REQUIRED
Outer throws after audit() (Camera, ₹90,000)Audit saved, order gone — inconsistent: audit for a nonexistent order
Inner throws, outer catchesOrder saved, audit gone, no UnexpectedRollbackException; inner rolled back alone, outer committed
When to use REQUIRES_NEW (and when not): only for genuinely independent work; dangerous for order + payment (Camera demo). Good uses (added): audit/failure log that must survive a main rollback; sequence-number generation; an outbox record that must persist.
Worth flagging (added): second connection held while the first is suspended — with a small HikariCP pool under load, all threads hold one and wait for another: exhaustion/deadlock. A real FK would block: inner can't see outer's uncommitted INSERT; demo works only because orderId is a plain column (real FK → inner waits on outer's lock or fails).

Remaining propagation levels

SUPPORTS: transaction welcome, not required

  • Exists → join (like REQUIRED, shared rollback). None → run without (verified: isActualTransactionActive() false).
  • Use: neutral helpers following the caller. Rare.

MANDATORY: must be called inside a transaction

  • Exists → join. None → throw.
  • Demo: remove @Transactional from placeOrder, POST "Bottle" (₹200) → IllegalTransactionStateException: No existing transaction found for transaction marked with propagation 'mandatory' (verified).
  • Use: sensitive inner ops (payment audit) safe only within a caller's transaction. Lecturer: fits this case better than REQUIRED — a non-transactional order service makes the audit refuse rather than risk an unnoticed inconsistency.
Worth flagging (verified): in that failure the order row was still saved — orderRepository.save committed in its own repository transaction before audit() threw. MANDATORY is a loud guard, not a rollback mechanism.

NOT_SUPPORTED: always run without a transaction

  • Exists → suspend, run without. None → just run. Verified: inner saw isActualTransactionActive() == false.
  • Use: operations that must not be transactional.

NEVER: transaction must NOT exist

  • Exists → throw; none → run without. Stricter than NOT_SUPPORTED (which suspends).
  • Verified: IllegalTransactionStateException: Existing transaction found for transaction marked with propagation 'never'.

NESTED: join with a savepoint

A savepoint = marker you can roll back to without rolling back everything:

BEGIN;
op1; op2;
SAVEPOINT sp1;
op3; op4;
ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT sp1;   -- undoes op3, op4; op1, op2 survive; transaction still open
COMMIT;
  • Exists → same physical transaction + savepoint at inner start. None → create (like REQUIRED).
  • Inner fails → only its work rolled back to the savepoint; outer can catch and commit.
  • Outer rolls back → inner too (same transaction) — the difference from REQUIRES_NEW.
Correction (verified, Boot 4.1.0): NESTED doesn't work with Spring Data JPA out of the box: auto-configured JpaTransactionManager threw NestedTransactionNotSupportedException: Transaction manager does not allow nested transactions by default; with nestedTransactionAllowed = true: "JpaDialect does not support savepoints". Reason: a savepoint would roll back DB rows but not entities cached in the persistence context. NESTED is for JDBC access (JdbcTemplate + DataSourceTransactionManager, Lecture 27).
Propagation cheat-sheet
PropagationCaller HAS a transactionCaller has NO transactionTypical use
REQUIRED (default)Join itCreate newDependent work (order + audit)
REQUIRES_NEWSuspend it, create newCreate newIndependent work that must commit regardless (logs)
SUPPORTSJoin itRun withoutNeutral helpers
MANDATORYJoin itThrowOnly inside a caller's transaction
NOT_SUPPORTEDSuspend it, run withoutRun withoutMust not be transactional
NEVERThrowRun withoutAssert "never inside a transaction"
NESTEDSame transaction + savepointCreate newPartial rollback (JDBC only)

REQUIRED vs REQUIRES_NEW vs NESTED

QuestionREQUIREDREQUIRES_NEWNESTED
Physical transactionsOne (shared)Two (independent)One, with a savepoint
Inner fails, outer catchesEverything rolls back + UnexpectedRollbackExceptionInner rolled back, outer commitsInner rolled back to savepoint, outer commits
Outer fails after inner succeededBoth roll backInner stays committedBoth roll back
Inner's own isolation/timeoutIgnoredAppliedIgnored

Isolation: the three read anomalies

Relevant when transactions run concurrently. Most to least severe:

1. Dirty read: reading uncommitted changes

TimeT1T2
1BEGIN
2UPDATE balance = 20,000 (was 10,000), not committed
3BEGIN; reads balance = 20,000
4ROLLBACK → back to 10,000Works with 20,000, a value that never officially existed

2. Non-repeatable read: same row, different value

TimeT1T2
1BEGIN; reads balance = 10,000
2UPDATE balance = 8,000; COMMIT
3Reads same row → 8,000

Also: row 1001, Aditya at time 1 becomes 1001, Rohit at time 2. Less severe than dirty read (new value was committed).

Correction: why it's a problem: not "T1 may re-read forever" (lecture's framing) but two reads of the same row in one transaction disagree — a decision on the first ("balance 10,000, allow 9,000 withdrawal") may be wrong when T1 acts; reports reading twice contradict themselves.

3. Phantom read: same query, different row set

-- T1
SELECT * FROM employee WHERE salary > 100000;   -- A, B, C
-- T2: INSERT INTO employee (… salary 150000 …); COMMIT;
-- T1, same query again
SELECT * FROM employee WHERE salary > 100000;   -- A, B, C, D  ← a "phantom" row

Non-repeatable = existing row's value changed (UPDATE). Phantom = set/count of matching rows changed (INSERT/DELETE).

The four isolation levels

isolation = Isolation.… (enum). Each level picks accepted anomalies: concurrency vs consistency.

Stricter going down (SQL standard)
Isolation levelDirty readNon-repeatable readPhantom read
READ_UNCOMMITTEDPossiblePossiblePossible
READ_COMMITTEDPreventedPossiblePossible
REPEATABLE_READPreventedPreventedPossible (some DBs prevent it)
SERIALIZABLEPreventedPreventedPrevented
  • READ_UNCOMMITTED: max concurrency, even dirty reads. Not used in production.
  • READ_COMMITTED: no dirty reads — "concurrency, but not reading uncommitted data".
  • REPEATABLE_READ: also no non-repeatable reads; only phantoms remain.
  • SERIALIZABLE: none; result as if run one after another. Nuance: not literally one at a time — locks let them overlap without interfering; they only look serial.
Why not always SERIALIZABLE: concurrency collapses (T1, then T2, then T3…). As with multithreading, transactions that can safely run together should. Pick the weakest tolerable level; lecture recommends READ_COMMITTED or REPEATABLE_READ.

Isolation.DEFAULT

Fifth enum value: the DB's default — MySQL (InnoDB): REPEATABLE_READ; PostgreSQL: READ_COMMITTED (added: Oracle, SQL Server also READ_COMMITTED).

Closing: isolation = Isolation.REPEATABLE_READ on audit is ignored since it joins placeOrder's transaction (no isolation there → DB default). Put isolation on the outer method.

Corrections & worth knowing

  • "Repeatable read is the most used" depends on the DB: MySQL's default; READ_COMMITTED is default (most common) on PostgreSQL, Oracle, SQL Server.
  • Real databases differ from the textbook table:
    • InnoDB REPEATABLE_READ: MVCC snapshots → plain SELECTs see no phantoms; locking reads use next-key (gap) locks to block range inserts.
    • PostgreSQL REPEATABLE_READ: snapshot isolation — no phantoms, but "write skew" possible.
    • PostgreSQL SERIALIZABLE (SSI) doesn't block; aborts one transaction with a serialization failure → app must retry. MySQL's uses shared locks.
  • Isolation doesn't fix the lost update (Lecture 33's read-modify-write): at READ_COMMITTED last writer silently wins. Use SELECT … FOR UPDATE (@Lock(PESSIMISTIC_WRITE)), @Version, or atomic UPDATEs. The anomalies above concern reads.
  • readOnly = true isn't just documentation: Hibernate flush mode manual (no dirty checking/flush), JDBC connection read-only → PostgreSQL rejects writes; replicas/routing data sources can use it.
  • timeout (seconds): with JPA a query timeout checked when statements run; doesn't interrupt hanging Java code. Exceeding → TransactionTimedOutException/QueryTimeoutException + rollback.
  • Runtime check: TransactionSynchronizationManager.isActualTransactionActive() / getCurrentTransactionIsolationLevel() — how the above was verified.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"the slide shows the default values" → only REQUIRED and readOnly=false are defaults.
"@Transaction" → @Transactional.
"Propagation.REQUIRED_NEW" / "required new" → REQUIRES_NEW.
"payment order's audit" / "payment order service" → PaymentAuditService.
"non-repeatable read: balance ₹1,000" → ₹10,000.
"the inner method would implement without transaction" (SUPPORTS) → execute without.
"NESTED: the operations done so far won't be rolled back" → only inner work after the savepoint is rolled back; outer failure rolls back everything.

Next: transactions done; next topic Spring Security.

Lecture 35: Spring Security Introduction — Security Vocabulary, the Authentication Object, Stateful vs Stateless and Boot's Default Security

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #35 · 1h19m · Code Army · first lecture of the Spring Security module

The problem: every endpoint is public

Starting project spring-security-demo: only spring-boot-starter-webmvc and one controller:

@RestController
@RequestMapping("/api/students")
public class StudentController {
    @GetMapping
    public String getStudent() { return "Student retrieved successfully"; }
}

GET localhost:8080/api/students returns the message to anyone. The model so far (request → controller → service → repository → DB) never asked: is this caller allowed? is it public? did we authenticate/authorize at all? Anyone could fetch/create/update/delete. Goal: a security layer demanding username/password first.

Security vocabulary

Assets: anything valuable

Asset typeExamples
DataEmail, password, username, bank details: any user data held
OperationalEndpoint operations: create/delete user, transfer money
SecuritySession IDs, JWT tokens
InfrastructureDatabase, application, servers
AvailabilityUptime; if down, users can't transact or store data

Threat, vulnerability, attack

  • Threat — anyone/anything intending to harm an asset.
  • Vulnerability — an exploitable weak point in your app.
  • Attack — a threat exploiting a vulnerability.
One-liner from the lecture: "Threat + vulnerability = attack."

Example: attacker wants admin access (threat), opens /admin; app allows unlimited password attempts (vulnerability); a script tries combinations until one works — brute-force attack, app compromised.

Trust boundary

Data moving between levels (browser → internet → app → DB) crosses a trust boundary and mustn't be trusted — it may have been altered.

Why the client can never be trusted: front-end code runs in the user's browser. Hiding an "Admin panel" button isn't security: users can edit HTML/JS, or just hit /api/admin with curl/Postman. Access checks must happen on the server.

Authentication vs authorization

AuthenticationAuthorization
QuestionWho are you? Verify a claimed identity ("I am Aditya") via credentialsWhat may you do? Accessible endpoints/parts, typically by role (admin, user, manager)
Security-guard analogyGuard checks your employee card at the buildingBeing inside doesn't get you into the CEO's room
Instagram analogyLogging inDelete your own posts, not a friend's
OrderFirstSecond: roles matter only after verification

A public API is still an authorization decision (rule: "everyone"). Every endpoint needs some access control; tighten later.

Authentication factors and MFA

FactorExamples
Something you knowPassword, PIN, security answer
Something you haveSmart card, authenticator app (Google/Microsoft), cryptographic key, phone receiving OTP
Something you areFingerprint, iris, face
What makes MFA strong: proofs from different factors (password + authenticator OTP, password + fingerprint). Password + PIN = same factor, not real MFA.

Brute force vs honest mistakes

  • Allow a few retries for typos; locking out after one miss is bad UX.
  • Repeated attempts suggest brute force; unlimited attempts is the vulnerability above.
  • Don't reveal which part was wrong: "Invalid username or password", never "wrong password" (leaks valid usernames). Log the exact reason internally only.

Added: Spring Security doesn't lock accounts or rate-limit out of the box. Defences: per-IP/account rate limiting, back-off/temporary lockout, CAPTCHA after N failures.

Spring Security's core terms

TermMeaning
IdentityAny entity whose actions the system must recognise: user, customer, scheduled job…
CredentialsProof for a claimed identity: username + password, OTP…
PrincipalThe current user of this request
AuthoritiesPermissions of the principal, e.g. COURSE_READ, USER_DELETE; each a GrantedAuthority
RolesGroup bundling authorities: ADMIN (may delete users), USER, MANAGER
PermissionsFiner-grained rights (exact APIs); in practice same as authorities
AuthenticationInterface holding principal, credentials, authorities, authenticated status
SecurityContextContainer holding the current Authentication
SecurityContextHolderGives the current SecurityContext from anywhere
Identity isn't a database row: the users row 1001, Aditya, 29 is one representation; one person can have several accounts. Identity = the claim ("I am Aditya") verified via credentials.
Precision note (added): roles are authorities with a prefix: a role is a GrantedAuthority named ROLE_…. hasRole("ADMIN") checks ROLE_ADMIN; hasAuthority("COURSE_READ") checks a plain authority.

The Authentication object: before and after login

One interface for both the login attempt ("authentication request") and the verified user ("authentication principal"):

FieldAuthentication request (before)Authentication principal (after)
principal"aditya": claimed nameFull user details loaded from DB
credentialsRaw passwordnull: cleared
authoritiesEmptye.g. COURSE_READ, COURSE_UPDATE
authenticatedfalsetrue
 
public interface Authentication extends Principal, Serializable {
    Collection<? extends GrantedAuthority> getAuthorities();
    Object getCredentials();
    Object getDetails();        // extra info: remote IP, session id…
    Object getPrincipal();
    boolean isAuthenticated();
    void setAuthenticated(boolean isAuthenticated);
    // getName() comes from java.security.Principal
}

Stored passwords compared hashed (salt/pepper later).

Worth knowing (verified, Boot 4.1.0 / Spring Security 7):
  • Concrete class: UsernamePasswordAuthenticationToken; factories unauthenticated(principal, credentials), authenticated(principal, credentials, authorities).
  • After Basic login the controller saw UsernamePasswordAuthenticationToken, principal of type User (a UserDetails), credentials=null — ProviderManager erases credentials on success, matching the table.
  • Spring Security 7 adds a factor authority (FACTOR_PASSWORD) recording how the user authenticated (new built-in MFA support).

SecurityContext and SecurityContextHolder

"Context" in Spring = container: ApplicationContext holds beans, persistence context holds entities, SecurityContext holds the current Authentication. Central so you needn't pass Authentication into every method:

SecurityContext context = SecurityContextHolder.getContext();
Authentication authentication = context.getAuthentication();
Object principal = authentication.getPrincipal();
// also: getCredentials(), getAuthorities(), getDetails(), isAuthenticated(), getName()
How the holder works: ThreadLocal: servlet app = one thread per request; the context sits in that thread's ThreadLocal, readable by any code on it. On request end it's cleared before the thread returns to the pool, so the next user can't inherit it. Added: default strategy MODE_THREADLOCAL; other threads (e.g. @Async) don't see it unless propagated (DelegatingSecurityContextExecutor or MODE_INHERITABLETHREADLOCAL).

HTTP is stateless: how does the server remember a login?

After /login, how does the server know /getUser is authenticated? Re-asking the password on every like/comment/upload is absurd. Two architectures:

Stateful authentication (sessions)

  1. Browser sends /login?username=aditya&password=secret123 (illustration only; never put passwords in a URL).
  2. Server authenticates, builds Authentication, stores it server-side under a session ID.
  3. Response: Set-Cookie: JSESSIONID=abc123; browser stores it.
  4. GET /getUser with Cookie: JSESSIONID=abc123 → server looks up abc123, sees authenticated=true, allows if authorities permit.

Only the ID is in the browser; session state lives server-side (memory, DB, or cache like Redis).

AdvantagesDisadvantages
Authenticate once; later requests look up the sessionStorage for every user's session
Full server control: invalidate any time (bank re-auth every 15 min), track last accessScaling: with replicas behind a load balancer, the session lives on only one
  • Problem: login on replica 1; next request hits replica 2, which knows nothing → "please log in again".
  • Sticky sessions (hash user/IP to one replica) work but break uniform load; lecturer calls it the wrong approach.
  • Central session store (e.g. Redis) queried by all replicas = standard fix. Added: Spring's Spring Session (spring-session-data-redis).
  • Session theft: whoever holds your session ID is you; protect the cookie.
Correction: what session fixation actually is: not "session ID stays fixed so the user is never re-authenticated" (lecture). Attack: attacker plants a known session ID in the victim's browser before login (crafted link); victim logs in, server marks that session authenticated, attacker uses it.
  • Real fix (lecturer's second suggestion): new session ID at login — Spring Security default (changeSessionId).
  • Expiring sessions (24-hour invalidation) limits theft damage but isn't the fixation fix.
  • Protect the cookie: HttpOnly (no JS), Secure (HTTPS only), SameSite.

Stateless authentication (tokens)

Server stores nothing between requests. Each request carries a token; the server builds a fresh Authentication each time into that request's SecurityContext. Any replica handles any request; no shared store.

Self-contained (signed) tokenOpaque token
ContentsEverything: subject, authorities, issuer, expiry + a signature against tamperingRandom identifier, e.g. x7Fq9LmP…, meaningless alone
How the server gets the AuthenticationVerify signature, read claims; no lookupAsks a token service (auth server) — close to a session ID
ExampleJWT (own lecture later)Many OAuth2 access tokens

Opaque + token service looks almost stateful (lecturer admits). Called stateless from the app's view since the token service is a separate system, whereas a Redis session store is part of your app — "a matter of definition".

The real distinction (key point): not where the credential travels (cookie or header; a token in a cookie can be stateless) but whether the server reuses a stored authentication (stateful) or recreates it from each request (stateless).

Bearer tokens

Authorization: Bearer eyJhbGciOi…: obtained at login, sent in a header on every request.

Correction: "bearer" = possession is enough, not proven ownership: the lecture says it tells the server "the sender is the owner"; the opposite — whoever holds it can use it; a stolen token is indistinguishable. Hence HTTPS only, short lifetimes, careful storage.
  • "Bearer" doesn't imply self-contained: JWT or opaque.
  • Signed isn't encrypted: a JWT payload is readable by anyone; the signature only prevents tampering.

Request lifecycles side by side

StepStateful (session)Stateless (token)
1POST /login with username/password; extract and authenticateGET /profile with Authorization: Bearer … (token from an earlier login)
2Create Authentication → SecurityContext → save in HttpSession, return session cookieExtract token, create an Authentication
3Later GET /profile with Cookie: JSESSIONID=abc123 → load session → SecurityContext → holderNew SecurityContext → holder
4Authorization check → controller → service → repository
5Clear SecurityContextHolder (ThreadLocal)Clear holder; nothing saved. Next request repeats all.

Authn/authz happen before the controller (before DispatcherServlet) — the filter layer.

Worked example: adding spring-boot-starter-security

<dependency>
    <groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
    <artifactId>spring-boot-starter-security</artifactId>
</dependency>   <!-- no version: managed by the starter parent -->

No classes or config — just the dependency and a Maven reload.

Demo run

  1. Startup logs Using generated security password: 56… + "This generated password is for development use only. Your security configuration must be updated before running your application in production." New random password each restart.
  2. Postman GET /api/students → 401 Unauthorized.
  3. Postman Basic Auth, user + generated password → "Student retrieved successfully".
  4. Browser → redirected to generated /login page; sign in → back to the original URL with data.
  5. Every endpoint (GET, POST, PUT, DELETE) now protected.

Verified (Boot 4.1.0): no credentials → 401 + WWW-Authenticate: Basic realm="Realm"; with Accept: text/html → 302 to /login. Entry point chosen by request type.

Where Spring Security sits: the filter chain

The starter adds classes to the classpath; auto-configuration creates beans (incl. these filters). Two auto-configurations:

Auto-configurationAnswersDefault
SecurityAutoConfiguration (+ servlet web-security config)Which requests need authentication, by which mechanism?All; form login for browsers, HTTP Basic for API clients
UserDetailsServiceAutoConfigurationWhich users exist, loaded from where?One in-memory user with random password (no DB)

Default filter chain bean, roughly:

@Bean
SecurityFilterChain defaultSecurityFilterChain(HttpSecurity http) throws Exception {
    http.authorizeHttpRequests(requests -> requests.anyRequest().authenticated());
    http.formLogin(withDefaults());
    http.httpBasic(withDefaults());
    return http.build();
}

Default user via InMemoryUserDetailsManager (user, generated password, enabled). Both defaults back off when you define your own SecurityFilterChain/user-details bean (next lectures). The generated user only exists to test that security works.

Worth knowing (verified, Boot 4.1.0):
  • Class locations: Boot 4 moves them to the spring-boot-security module (org.springframework.boot.security.autoconfigure…); default chain in ServletWebSecurityAutoConfiguration (Boot 3: SpringBootWebSecurityConfiguration).
  • Debug log default chain: SecurityContextHolderFilter, HeaderWriterFilter, CsrfFilter, LogoutFilter, UsernamePasswordAuthenticationFilter, DefaultLoginPageGeneratingFilter, BasicAuthenticationFilter, …, AnonymousAuthenticationFilter, ExceptionTranslationFilter, AuthorizationFilter.
  • Lecture says the default user has ROLE_USER; actually no roles unless spring.security.user.roles is set.

Overriding the default user via properties

# application.properties
spring.security.user.name=aditya
spring.security.user.password=secret123
spring.security.user.roles=USER,ADMIN

No generated password after restart. aditya/secret123 → 200; secret12 → 401. Verified authorities: ROLE_ADMIN, ROLE_USER (Spring adds ROLE_) + FACTOR_PASSWORD.

The CSRF surprise: correct credentials, still 401

Added @PostMapping createStudent, @PutMapping updateStudent, @DeleteMapping deleteStudent: 401 without credentials as expected, but with correct aditya/secret123 POST still 401; GET works.

Cause: CSRF protection (Cross-Site Request Forgery; detail later), on by default for state-changing methods (POST, PUT, PATCH, DELETE); GET exempt (shouldn't change anything). Lecture workaround — an endpoint handing out the token:

@GetMapping("/csrf")                       // own path: a second bare @GetMapping clashed at startup
public CsrfToken getToken(CsrfToken csrfToken) {
    return csrfToken;                      // Spring injects the current token
}
  1. GET /api/students/csrf (Basic auth) → {"headerName": "X-CSRF-TOKEN", "parameterName": "_csrf", "token": "…"}.
  2. Add header X-CSRF-TOKEN: <token> to POST/PUT/DELETE.
  3. Now "Student created / updated / deleted successfully".
Worth knowing (verified, Boot 4.1.0):
  • Why 401, not 403: log shows CsrfFilter: Invalid CSRF token found (access denied, 403); Spring forwards to /error, itself secured and dispatched as anonymous, so the Basic entry point answers 401. Misleading status; real cause is the missing token.
  • Token tied to the session (HttpSession, JSESSIONID). Postman keeps cookies, so the flow works; token without the session cookie → 401 again.
  • Token changes on every read: XOR-masked since Spring Security 6 (BREACH protection); all values valid for the session.
Correction: what CSRF really is: not "using another site's session ID here" (lecture). Attack: logged in to bank.com (cookie in browser), you visit evil.com, whose page auto-submits a form to bank.com/transfer; the browser attaches bank.com's cookie automatically, so it runs as you. The token defeats this since evil.com can't read it.
  • Matters for credentials the browser sends automatically: cookies, browser-cached Basic auth.
  • Stateless APIs using Authorization: Bearer typically disable CSRF (done later in our own config).

Corrections & worth knowing

  • 401 vs 403: 401 Unauthorized really means unauthenticated (no/invalid credentials; with WWW-Authenticate); 403 Forbidden = authenticated but not allowed. Classic interview question.
  • HTTP Basic is not a bearer token (separate Postman options; lecture blurs them):
    • Basic: Authorization: Basic base64(username:password) every request.
    • Bearer: Authorization: Bearer <token>.
    • Base64 is encoding, not encryption — Basic only over HTTPS.
  • HTTPS = HTTP over TLS ("HTTP Secure"), not "secured transfer protocol".
  • Filter origin: the container knows one filter, DelegatingFilterProxy → Spring's FilterChainProxy → matching SecurityFilterChain (can be several, e.g. /api/** vs web UI).
  • Stored passwords: slow salted hash (BCrypt/Argon2 via PasswordEncoder), never encrypted/plain. Default in-memory user uses {noop} (plain) — another reason it's dev-only.
  • JWT revocation is the price of statelessness: valid until expiry; real systems use short-lived access + refresh tokens, or a deny-list (partly stateful again).
Transcript slips to ignore:
"grand authorities" → GrantedAuthority.
"Spring context is a container that stores the authentication" → SecurityContext.
"spring.security.username" → spring.security.user.name.
"update mapping" → @PutMapping (self-corrected).
"HTTP Basics / bearer token are the same" → see Corrections.
"deployment use only" in the log → "development use only" (self-corrected).

Next: our own security configuration and filters; creating/authenticating our own users.

Lecture 36: Spring Security Hands-On (Part 1) — User/Role Entities, Password Hashing with BCrypt, Salt & Pepper, Registration

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #36 · 1h26m · Code Army · continues Lecture 35; login via Spring Security is promised for the next lecture

Goal and project setup

Store real users in a DB and authenticate by username/password instead of Boot's generated user. New project (package in.strikes, "spring-practical-demo") from Spring Initializr: Spring Data JPA, Spring Web, MySQL Driver, Spring Security, Lombok (added later).

application.properties: datasource URL/username/password, spring.jpa.hibernate.ddl-auto=update, show-sql, format_sql (first start failed without them: a JPA starter requires a datasource). Test endpoint:

@RestController
@RequestMapping("/api/users")
public class UserController {
    @GetMapping("/hello")
    public String hello() { return "hello"; }
}

401 without auth; Basic user + generated password → hello. Spring already built an in-memory user details object, stored its password and matched the incoming one.

What authentication boils down to

  1. Client sends an identity claim (username/email/phone — must be unique, hence no login by name) + a credential (password).
  2. Spring builds an unauthenticated Authentication (principal = "aditya", credentials = password, no authorities, authenticated=false).
  3. App loads the stored password for that username (DB, memory, third party…).
  4. Compares with the incoming password; on match builds an authenticated Authentication (principal = user details, credentials = null, authorities e.g. ROLE_USER, authenticated=true) into the SecurityContext (Lecture 35).

Two types behind the default user, where we plug in our own:

  • UserDetails — interface: Spring's view of "a user".
  • UserDetailsService — loads a UserDetails by username.

Define your own user source and Boot's default backs off (next lecture).

The UserDetails contract

public interface UserDetails extends Serializable {
    Collection<? extends GrantedAuthority> getAuthorities();
    String getPassword();
    String getUsername();
    default boolean isAccountNonExpired()     { return true; }
    default boolean isAccountNonLocked()      { return true; }
    default boolean isCredentialsNonExpired() { return true; }
    default boolean isEnabled()               { return true; }
}
FlagMeaning (lecture's examples)
isEnabled()Disabled, not deleted (e.g. policy violation)
isAccountNonLocked()Temporarily locked, e.g. too many wrong passwords ("locked for 30 minutes")
isAccountNonExpired()Account expires, e.g. all on 31 December
isCredentialsNonExpired()Password expired ("change every month")

Negative phrasing so true = all good. Rule: your entity (Customer, Student, User…) can have any fields but must have a unique login key and a password; flags/authorities optional. Spring Security works with UserDetails, so the entity must be mapped/wrapped into a UserDetails (getUsername() → entity username, getPassword() → entity password). (Added, verified on Spring Security 7.1: the four flags are default methods returning true, so only the first three need implementing.)

Worked example: User and Role entities

Why no role column? A user has several roles (Aditya = USER + ADMIN, Rohit = MANAGER, Sohan = ADMIN + MANAGER) and a role has many users: many-to-many. A column would need row 1, Aditya twice, which PK id forbids. Hence a role table + join table (Lecture 30).

@Entity @Getter @Setter @NoArgsConstructor
public class User {
    @Id @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY)
    private Long id;

    @Column(nullable = false, unique = true)     // the identity claim must be unique
    private String username;

    @Column(nullable = false)                    // passwords may repeat, so no unique
    private String password;

    private boolean enabled = true;              // the only UserDetails flag this app needs

    @ManyToMany
    @JoinTable(name = "user_roles",
        joinColumns = @JoinColumn(name = "user_id"),
        inverseJoinColumns = @JoinColumn(name = "role_id"))
    private Set<Role> roles = new HashSet<>();
}

@Entity @Getter @Setter @NoArgsConstructor
public class Role {
    @Id @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY)
    private Long id;

    @Column(nullable = false, unique = true)
    private String name;                         // e.g. ROLE_USER, ROLE_ADMIN
}
  • Unidirectional: no Set<User> in Role — we only go user → roles; bidirectional needs both sides synced.
  • Startup: DBeaver shows role (id, name), user (id, enabled, password, username), user_roles (user_id, role_id).
  • Import fix: class first named role; @ManyToMany complained ("attribute value type should not be role") as IntelliJ picked another Role. Rename to Role, import ours.
Worth flagging (added):
  • @ManyToMany is LAZY by default: getAuthorities() outside a transaction may throw LazyInitializationException. Use fetch = FetchType.EAGER for roles, or a fetch join / entity graph (Lecture 31).
  • user is reserved in PostgreSQL, H2, SQL Server (fine on MySQL). Prefer @Table(name = "users"), like orders in Lecture 34.

Repositories

public interface UserRepository extends JpaRepository<User, Long> {
    Optional<User> findByUsername(String username);   // login always looks users up by username
}
public interface RoleRepository extends JpaRepository<Role, Long> {
    Optional<Role> findByName(String name);
}

The service layer here will be Spring Security's UserDetailsService (next lecture).

Never store raw passwords: hash them

Storing aditya | secret123 lets anyone with table access (or a leaked dump) log in as Aditya.

EncryptionHashing
DirectionTwo-way: a key decrypts itOne-way: no way back to the password
Key managementKey must be stored; can leak tooNo key
Fits passwords?No: plain password never neededYes: store hash(password); at login hash input and compare
The key insight: we never need to know the password, only check equality — comparing hashes does that. Even developers can't see passwords, provided you never log raw passwords.

Why a deliberately slow hash

SHA-256 is very fast — exactly wrong for passwords.

  • How attackers guess: huge lists (GBs, millions) of common passwords (password, aditya123, 12345); humans pick patterns (name + @123, DOB); a bot tries every entry.
  • Why speed matters: fast hash = nanoseconds per guess; milliseconds per hash makes the list take years, while a real user barely notices at login.
  • Standard slow algorithm: BCrypt (used by Spring Security).
Verified (Boot 4.1.0, this laptop): one BCryptPasswordEncoder hash (default strength 10) ~58 ms; strength 12 ~242 ms (+1 doubles work). SHA-256: 100,000 hashes in ~75 ms (<0.001 ms each). BCrypt ~75,000× slower per guess — the point.
Precision note (added): which attack slow hashing stops: the lecture shows a bot hammering the login form, but online guessing is mainly stopped by rate limiting / lockout (Lecture 35; network latency already slows it). Slow hashing matters most for offline cracking: a leaked table hashed on attacker GPUs at unlimited speed — only a slow, salted hash makes that impractical.

Rainbow tables and salting

Algorithms are public, so attackers precompute common password → hash per algorithm (e.g. secret123 → ER34@12…). If your hashed table leaks (hack, insider), a bot looks up each hash to get the plain password. That precomputed lookup = rainbow table.

  • Salt — random string generated by the app (not user), different per password, appended before hashing: hash("secret123" + "RE4567_…").
  • Defeats rainbow tables: no precomputed table contains password + random salt.
  • Storage: salt must be available at login to recompute; BCrypt handles it.
  • Pepper — a fixed secret (lecture: qwerty), same for all: hash(password + salt + pepper). Kept in an env variable/restricted file, not the DB — a second line of defence: a DB leak alone can't crack hashes.
Worth knowing (verified): salt lives inside the BCrypt string: $2a$10$t7kovpeHrgy25SOC2iIkC.C9WDy7nyDYLXSNgKlPapI4QnIt9Z9BO (60 chars):
  • $2a$ — BCrypt version.
  • 10$ — cost: 210 rounds.
  • Next 22 chars = salt: 16 random bytes (128 bits); lecture's "16-bit" is off by 8×.
  • Remaining 31 chars = hash.
No salt column needed: matches() reads salt and cost from the string. BCryptPasswordEncoder adds no pepper; implement it yourself (or keyed hash/HSM).

PasswordEncoder and BCryptPasswordEncoder

MethodDoes
String encode(CharSequence rawPassword)Salt + hash; when storing (registration, password change)
boolean matches(CharSequence rawPassword, String encodedPassword)Re-hash input with stored salt and compare; at login. Order: raw first, encoded second.
default boolean upgradeEncoding(String encoded)"Re-hash this?", e.g. after raising cost
 
@Configuration
public class SecurityConfig {
    @Bean
    public PasswordEncoder passwordEncoder() {
        return new BCryptPasswordEncoder();
    }
}

Demo: same password, different hashes

  1. passwordEncoder.encode("secret123") twice → different strings, same only up to $2a$10$ (salt differs).
  2. passwordEncoder.matches("secret123", thatHash) → true. Verified: true for secret123, false for aditya123.

Q: "Why did adding the PasswordEncoder bean break the generated password?"

Once the bean existed, Basic auth with user + generated password → 401. Lecturer's workaround: comment out the bean, use new BCryptPasswordEncoder() in the controller/service. Actual reason (verified: log "Encoded password does not look like BCrypt", 401):

  • Boot's default user stores its password as plain text, prefixed {noop} ("compare as plain") when no encoder bean exists.
  • With a PasswordEncoder bean, Spring uses your BCrypt encoder; a plain UUID isn't a BCrypt hash, so nothing matches.

Workaround is interim only (hard-coding bypasses DI). Proper fix next lecture: own UserDetailsService with BCrypt-hashed users, bean restored.

Worked example: the registration endpoint

POST /api/users/register with {"username": "aditya", "password": "secret123"} (front end confirms password twice; back end gets it once). DTOs, not the entity: the response must never include the password, even hashed.

@Getter @Setter
public class UserRegisterRequestDto { private String username; private String password; }

@Getter @Setter
public class UserRegisterResponseDto { private String username; private String message; }

@Service
public class AuthService {                     // named for its job; a controller can use several services
    private final UserRepository userRepository;
    private final PasswordEncoder passwordEncoder;
    // constructor injection

    public UserRegisterResponseDto register(UserRegisterRequestDto dto) {
        User user = new User();
        user.setUsername(dto.getUsername());
        // NOT user.setPassword(dto.getPassword()) — that stores the raw password
        String encodedPassword = passwordEncoder.encode(dto.getPassword());
        user.setPassword(encodedPassword);
        user.setEnabled(true);
        // roles: left empty for now (a role service comes later)
        userRepository.save(user);

        UserRegisterResponseDto response = new UserRegisterResponseDto();
        response.setUsername(user.getUsername());
        response.setMessage("User saved successfully");
        return response;
    }
}

@PostMapping("/register")
public ResponseEntity<UserRegisterResponseDto> register(@RequestBody UserRegisterRequestDto dto) {
    return ResponseEntity.ok(authService.register(dto));
}
  • Validation (@NotBlank, @Size(min = 8)…) could go on the DTO (Lecture 16).
  • Once encoded, the raw password is unrecoverable unless logged or seen in a debugger.

Demo run

  1. POST /api/users/register → 401: sign-up is protected too; must be made public (next lecture).
  2. Basic auth with generated user → still 401 (CSRF, Lecture 35). Added @GetMapping("/token") returning CsrfToken; sent it as X-CSRF-TOKEN.
  3. Result: {"username": "aditya", "message": "User saved successfully"}; INSERT logged; DBeaver shows aditya, enabled=1, a $2a$10$… hash instead of secret123.

Absurdity noted: we logged in as Boot's user to register. Fix: declare public endpoints and plug in our users (next lecture).

A hand-written login, and why we won't keep it

// AuthService — illustration only
public boolean login(UserRegisterRequestDto dto) {
    Optional<User> userOptional = userRepository.findByUsername(dto.getUsername());
    User user = userOptional.get();                          // assumes the user exists
    String encodedPassword = user.getPassword();
    return passwordEncoder.matches(dto.getPassword(), encodedPassword);
}

Demo: POST /api/users/login (Basic auth + fresh CSRF token; old one invalid after restart) → true; aditya123 → false.

Why hand-rolling login isn't enough: it checks the password once — no remembered login, no session/token, no per-request check that the caller is logged in and allowed.
  • Spring Security's filter chain does that for every request.
  • Your job: register users and wrap User in a UserDetails.
One-liner from the lecture: "Focus on your business code." AOP takes infrastructure code, validation takes validation code, exception handling takes exception code, and Spring Security says "leave the security code to me".

Gaps worth raising in an interview

  • Duplicate usernames: no findByUsername check; the unique constraint throws DataIntegrityViolationException (500). Check first, return 409 Conflict, keep the constraint (check-then-insert race).
  • Optional.get() in login: 500 for unknown usernames, revealing which exist. Return the same "invalid username or password", with similar timing (dummy matches) to prevent user enumeration.
  • No roles assigned at registration: give ROLE_USER (via roleRepository.findByName), seeded at startup.
  • Password policy: no min length or breached-password check. BCrypt uses only the first 72 bytes; longer passphrases silently truncated.
  • Field injection (@Autowired private PasswordEncoder) and new BCryptPasswordEncoder() in services were demo shortcuts; constructor-inject the single PasswordEncoder bean.

Corrections & worth knowing

  • Prefer the delegating encoder for new apps: PasswordEncoderFactories.createDelegatingPasswordEncoder() stores {bcrypt}$2a$10$… (verified). The {id} prefix allows switching to Argon2/scrypt with old hashes still working; upgradeEncoding signals re-hash (verified: strength-12 encoder → true for a strength-10 hash).
  • Modern alternatives: OWASP ranks Argon2id, then scrypt, then BCrypt (cost ≥ 10). Spring has Argon2PasswordEncoder, SCryptPasswordEncoder (need Bouncy Castle), Pbkdf2PasswordEncoder. BCrypt remains a solid default.
  • Tune the cost to ~100 ms+ per hash on your server: new BCryptPasswordEncoder(12). Higher cost = more work per login (DoS consideration).
  • Lombok + boolean enabled generates isEnabled(), matching UserDetails.isEnabled() later.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"if you decrypt this password" (rainbow table) → you hash it; hashes aren't decrypted.
"shar 256" → SHA-256.
"16-bit salt" → 16 bytes (128 bits).
"rest mapping" → @RequestMapping; "@Setter" said for @Service (self-corrected).
"the user's sign-up happened" after login matching → login succeeded.
"getName()" on the DTO → getUsername() (Lombok getters missing at first).
