Next: Spring Security handles login — wrap User in UserDetails, implement UserDetailsService, make /register public.

Lecture 37: Spring Security Hands-On (Part 2) — UserDetails, UserDetailsService, the Filter-Chain Architecture, AuthenticationManager & DaoAuthenticationProvider

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #37 · 1h32m · Code Army · continues the Lecture 36 project

Where we left off

  • So far:
    • UserController: /hello, /register, /token CSRF helper, dummy /login.
    • Request/response DTOs, User/Role entities + repositories.
    • AuthService.register: encodes password via PasswordEncoder, sets enabled = true.
  • Today: delete hand-written login (service + controller); Spring Security's filters do login.
  • Missing: new users get no roles.

Assigning roles: never trust the client

Accepting "roles": ["ROLE_USER", "ROLE_ADMIN"] in the register body + user.setRoles(…) is wrong: everyone would claim admin. The application decides the few admins/managers. Client sends username + password; server attaches roles from the DB.

Seeding roles via an internal endpoint

@RestController
@RequestMapping("/api/roles")
public class RoleController {
    private final RoleService roleService;
    // constructor injection
    @PostMapping
    public ResponseEntity<String> addRole(@RequestBody Role role) {   // entity is tiny (just name), so no DTO
        roleService.addRole(role);
        return ResponseEntity.ok("done");
    }
}

@Service
public class RoleService {
    private final RoleRepository roleRepository;
    public void addRole(Role role) { roleRepository.save(role); }   // no validation, for now
}

Internal API (Postman only; DBeaver inserts also work). Demo (CSRF token + Basic): POST /api/roles {"name": "ROLE_USER"} → "done", then ROLE_ADMIN.

Registration attaches ROLE_USER

// AuthService.register, before userRepository.save(user)
Role role = roleRepository.findByName("ROLE_USER").get();   // returns Optional; lecture assumes it exists
user.getRoles().add(role);

Every new user gets a user_roles row.

Wrapping User in UserDetails

Spring Security speaks only UserDetails (username, password, authorities, four status flags). Bridge to our User entity: a class that implements UserDetails and delegates to User (adapter/wrapper).

public class CustomUserDetails implements UserDetails {
    private final User user;
    public CustomUserDetails(User user) { this.user = user; }

    @Override public String getUsername() { return user.getUsername(); }
    @Override public String getPassword() { return user.getPassword(); }   // the BCrypt hash
    @Override public boolean isEnabled()  { return user.isEnabled(); }

    @Override
    public Collection<? extends GrantedAuthority> getAuthorities() {
        return user.getRoles().stream()
                .map(role -> new SimpleGrantedAuthority(role.getName()))
                .toList();
    }
    // isAccountNonExpired / isAccountNonLocked / isCredentialsNonExpired:
    // not overridden — the interface defaults return true
}
  • GrantedAuthority is an interface; SimpleGrantedAuthority is the usual implementation, wrapping e.g. "ROLE_USER".
  • Overrides: IntelliJ "implement methods" offers the three abstract ones; we also override isEnabled(), the only flag our entity stores. (Lecture put CustomUserDetails in the entity package.)

UserDetailsService: one method

A single-method (functional) interface: UserDetails loadUserByUsername(String username) — "given a username, give me a UserDetails".

@Service
public class CustomUserDetailsService implements UserDetailsService {
    private final UserRepository userRepository;
    // constructor injection

    @Override
    public UserDetails loadUserByUsername(String username) throws UsernameNotFoundException {
        User user = userRepository.findByUsername(username)
                .orElseThrow(() -> new UsernameNotFoundException("User not found"));
        return new CustomUserDetails(user);          // return the wrapper, not our entity
    }
}
  • UsernameNotFoundException: Spring Security's ready-made exception; Spring handles it and answers the client.
  • No-wrapper alternative: Spring's org.springframework.security.core.userdetails.User builder: User.withUsername(user.getUsername()).password(user.getPassword()).authorities(…).disabled(!user.isEnabled()).build(). Lecturer prefers the wrapper.

Who does the actual checking?

We have UserDetailsService → repository → UserDetails, and the PasswordEncoder bean. Something must connect them:

  1. Receive the unauthenticated Authentication (username + raw password).
  2. loadUserByUsername(username) → UserDetails.
  3. passwordEncoder.matches(rawPassword, encodedPassword) against the stored hash.
  4. Check status flags (isEnabled, non-locked, non-expired, credentials non-expired).
  5. Success → authenticated Authentication (principal = UserDetails, credentials = null, authorities = roles). Failure → exception back to client.

That class is DaoAuthenticationProvider. How requests reach it:

Architecture: Tomcat to the filter chains

  • DelegatingFilterProxy: the only filter Tomcat knows; just forwards into Spring.
  • FilterChainProxy holds several SecurityFilterChains, tried in order like an if / else-if ladder; first matching one handles the request, rest skipped.
  • No chain matches (no catch-all /**)? Spring Security ignores the request; it goes straight to DispatcherServlet.
  • Boot's default (none defined): one chain matching any request — why every endpoint needed login.
public interface SecurityFilterChain {
    boolean matches(HttpServletRequest request);   // e.g. does the path match /api/**?
    List<Filter> getFilters();                      // the filters of this chain, called one after another
}

HttpSecurity is a builder (Builder pattern); build() returns a SecurityFilterChain with our matcher and filters. Never implemented by hand.

Filter order inside a chain

#CategoryExamples
1Exploit protectionCsrfFilter, security headers
2AuthenticationUsernamePasswordAuthenticationFilter, BasicAuthenticationFilter
3Exception-handling boundaryExceptionTranslationFilter (security exceptions → 401/403 or redirect)
4AuthorizationAuthorizationFilter

Authentication always before authorization. Learn the flow + a few key filters, not every name. Boot's default chain: SecurityContextHolderFilter, CsrfFilter, LogoutFilter, UsernamePasswordAuthenticationFilter, BasicAuthenticationFilter, AnonymousAuthenticationFilter, ExceptionTranslationFilter, AuthorizationFilter, etc. (full list verified in Lecture 35).

Form login vs HTTP Basic

Form loginHTTP Basic
ClientBrowser HTML form (username, password, Submit)API client, e.g. Postman (Authorization → Basic Auth)
RequestPOST /login, Content-Type: application/x-www-form-urlencoded, body username=aditya&password=secret123Any request with Authorization: Basic base64(username:password)
FilterUsernamePasswordAuthenticationFilterBasicAuthenticationFilter
Enabled byhttp.formLogin(…)http.httpBasic(…)
What an auth filter does: only turns the request into an unauthenticated Authentication — both filters build a UsernamePasswordAuthenticationToken (principal "aditya", credentials "secret123", no authorities, authenticated=false) — and hands it to the AuthenticationManager. Verifies nothing itself.

AuthenticationManager, ProviderManager, providers

public interface AuthenticationManager {
    Authentication authenticate(Authentication authentication) throws AuthenticationException;
    // in: unauthenticated request token; out: authenticated token, or an exception
}

Different login styles need different checks:

  • Username/password — load hash from DB, compare.
  • JWT — verify token, read claims.
  • OTP — e.g. look up code in Redis.

So the manager stays generic and delegates to providers, one per Authentication type:

  1. Filter calls authenticationManager.authenticate(token); implementation is ProviderManager.
  2. ProviderManager asks each provider supports(token.getClass()): for UsernamePasswordAuthenticationToken, DaoAuthenticationProvider says yes; JWT/OTP say no.
  3. DaoAuthenticationProvider.authenticate(token) runs the five steps above with CustomUserDetailsService + BCrypt PasswordEncoder.
  4. Authenticated Authentication goes into the SecurityContext via SecurityContextHolder (Lecture 35).
Correction: providers don't "override" AuthenticationManager (lecture's claim). They implement a separate AuthenticationProvider (authenticate + supports); only ProviderManager implements AuthenticationManager, holding a list of providers (verified via javap, Spring Security 7.1). JwtAuthenticationProvider is in the OAuth2 resource-server module; an OTP provider would be your own.

Worked example: SecurityConfig

@Configuration
public class SecurityConfig {

    @Bean
    public PasswordEncoder passwordEncoder() {             // re-enabled (Lecture 36 had it commented out)
        return new BCryptPasswordEncoder();
    }

    @Bean                                                  // optional: Spring would build this itself
    public DaoAuthenticationProvider authenticationProvider(CustomUserDetailsService userDetailsService,
                                                            PasswordEncoder passwordEncoder) {
        DaoAuthenticationProvider provider = new DaoAuthenticationProvider(userDetailsService);
        provider.setPasswordEncoder(passwordEncoder);
        return provider;
    }

    @Bean                                                  // replaces Boot's default "authenticate everything" chain
    public SecurityFilterChain securityFilterChain(HttpSecurity http,
                                                   DaoAuthenticationProvider provider) throws Exception {
        http
            .csrf(csrf -> csrf.disable())                  // so POSTs work without the X-CSRF-TOKEN dance
            .authenticationProvider(provider)
            .formLogin(Customizer.withDefaults())          // adds UsernamePasswordAuthenticationFilter
            .httpBasic(Customizer.withDefaults())          // adds BasicAuthenticationFilter
            .authorizeHttpRequests(auth -> auth
                .requestMatchers("/api/users/register").permitAll()     // sign-up must be public
                // .requestMatchers("/admin/**").hasRole("ADMIN")       // example: needs ROLE_ADMIN
                // .requestMatchers("/api/public/**").permitAll()       // e.g. contact page, public feed
                .anyRequest().authenticated());            // everything else requires login
        return http.build();                               // Builder pattern → SecurityFilterChain
    }
}
  • AuthService gets PasswordEncoder via constructor injection again; Lecture 36's hard-coded new BCryptPasswordEncoder() removed.
  • Customizer.withDefaults() = "standard configuration" — almost always what you want.
  • CSRF disabled → /token no longer needed; controller keeps /register and /hello.
  • No /login endpoint written — Spring Security provides it. Unauthenticated browser access redirects there; a front-end login page would POST to it.
  • requestMatchers needs the full path /api/users/register (lecture first wrote /register, then corrected; controller is under /api/users).

Demo run

  1. Startup: no generated password in the log.
  2. Delete old Aditya row, restart. POST /api/users/register {"username": "aditya", "password": "secret123"} with No Auth → "User saved successfully"; user_roles row links to ROLE_USER.
  3. GET /api/users/hello no auth → 401. Basic aditya/secret123 → still 401; log: LazyInitializationException: cannot lazily initialize a collection … roles … no session.
  4. After fix + restart: Basic aditya/secret123 → hello.
  5. Unknown rohit → 401. Register rohit/secret1234; both users can call /hello.

Q: "Why LazyInitializationException, and why an entity graph?"

@ManyToMany is LAZY: loadUserByUsername returns User without roles; later getAuthorities() → user.getRoles() runs after the persistence context closed. Fix: entity graph (Lecture 31):

public interface UserRepository extends JpaRepository<User, Long> {
    @EntityGraph(attributePaths = "roles")
    Optional<User> findByUsername(String username);
}

Knowing internals means understanding the error instead of pasting a ChatGPT fix blindly.

Verified (Boot 4.1.0, H2, same classes):
  • Without entity graph: Basic auth 401, LazyInitializationException in log.
  • With it: 200; authorities ROLE_USER (+ Spring Security 7's FACTOR_PASSWORD).
  • Wrong password / unknown user: both 401.
  • Why open-in-view didn't help (added): it's a Spring MVC interceptor, starting after the security filters — no open persistence context during authentication.

The remaining flaw (per the lecturer)

Basic auth sends credentials on every request (like Instagram asking your password per like); no remembered login. Fix next: sessions (stateful) or JWT (stateless).

Precision note (verified): form login already remembers you. True only for HTTP Basic (cookie-only follow-up → 401; no context saved in session). Form login is stateful by default: POST /login (form) → 302 to / + JSESSIONID; cookie-only follow-up → 200 (SecurityContext in HttpSession, Lecture 35).

Corrections & worth knowing

  • Filters aren't chosen by content type (lecture's claim). Each filter checks what it handles:
    • UsernamePasswordAuthenticationFilter: only POST /login with form params; no JSON body (JSON login needs a custom filter/endpoint).
    • BasicAuthenticationFilter: any request with Authorization: Basic, not just /login.
  • Why the generated password disappeared: not CustomUserDetails (plain class) but the UserDetailsService bean (our @Service). UserDetailsServiceAutoConfiguration backs off when a UserDetailsService, AuthenticationProvider or AuthenticationManager bean exists.
  • DaoAuthenticationProvider bean is optional: with exactly one UserDetailsService + one PasswordEncoder bean, Spring builds it; lecturer writes it out for illustration.
  • Version note (verified, Spring Security 7.1): DaoAuthenticationProvider's only constructor takes the UserDetailsService. Old no-arg constructor + setUserDetailsService(…) (deprecated in 6.x) is gone — such tutorials won't compile on Boot 4.
  • Unknown user = wrong password: DaoAuthenticationProvider hides UsernameNotFoundException as BadCredentialsException (Lecture 35: don't reveal which part was wrong) and runs a dummy password match for unknown users (timing).
  • Disabling CSRF is only safe for stateless APIs. This config has form login with sessions — CSRF's target (Lecture 35). OK for Basic/JWT-only APIs, not browser form-login apps.
  • /api/roles is a privilege-escalation hole: with anyRequest().authenticated(), any logged-in user can create roles. Protect: .requestMatchers("/api/roles/**").hasRole("ADMIN"). hasRole("ADMIN") checks authority ROLE_ADMIN — hence the stored prefix.
  • findByName("ROLE_USER").get() throws NoSuchElementException (500) if roles weren't seeded. Seed at startup (CommandLineRunner or data.sql) or orElseThrow with a clear message.
  • Sessions store the principal: form login puts CustomUserDetails (+ User) in the HttpSession → make it Serializable if sessions are persisted/replicated (Spring Session/Redis); keep the hash out or let Spring erase credentials.
  • CustomUserDetails location: a security package beats entity — it's an adapter, not a JPA entity.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"grand authority / simple grand authority" → GrantedAuthority / SimpleGrantedAuthority.
"DAO authentication filter" (bean creation, and "calls getAuthorities") → DaoAuthenticationProvider.
"loadUserByUserName" / "load yuser by username" → loadUserByUsername.
"lazily initialise the roles" as the entity graph's goal → it loads them eagerly for that query (lecturer says both).
"Setter" for @Service (self-corrected).

Next: remembering the logged-in user — stateful sessions, then JWT.

Lecture 38: Sessions & JWT — Stateful Session Cookies, SessionCreationPolicy, Building JWT from First Principles, and Stateless Login with Spring Security

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #38 · 1h22m · Code Army · continues the Lecture 36–37 project; stateful/stateless theory from Lecture 35

Recap: project and problem

  • So far:
    • User–Role (many-to-many), RoleController, UserController (/register, /hello), AuthService.register (BCrypt + ROLE_USER).
    • CustomUserDetails/CustomUserDetailsService.
    • SecurityConfig: PasswordEncoder, DaoAuthenticationProvider, chain permitting /api/users/register, everything else authenticated.
  • /hello reports who's logged in: takes an Authentication param, returns "Hello, you are logged in as " + authentication.getName(). Basic aditya/secret123 → "Hello, you are logged in as aditya".
  • Problem: switch to No Auth → 401. HTTP is stateless: every request is new. Server builds the unauthenticated Authentication, authenticates, builds the authenticated one (principal = user details, credentials = null, authorities = ROLE_USER, authenticated = true), serves, forgets.

Stateful: the server already keeps sessions

Stateful idea (Lecture 35): store the authenticated object server-side under a random session ID (e.g. asdf123), sent to the client in a cookie; client returns it each request; server reuses the stored Authentication — no re-login.

Spring Security does this by default; we just weren't using it:

  1. Postman cookies for the Basic request show a JSESSIONID.
  2. Browser: localhost:8080/api/users/hello → redirect to generated /login; log in aditya/secret123 → "Hello, you are logged in as aditya".
  3. New tab, same URL → no login. DevTools → Application → Cookies → localhost:8080: JSESSIONID, a random string carrying no info; server maps it to the stored authentication.
  4. Delete cookie, refresh → login page; log in again → new JSESSIONID.
  5. /logout (Spring-provided) → redirect to login; old session invalid, fresh one created on next login.
Precision note (verified in Lecture 37): form login saves the SecurityContext in HttpSession (hence the browser demo); HTTP Basic doesn't (since Spring Security 6) — its JSESSIONID holds no authentication, so cookie-only → 401 (Postman auto-resends cookies, yet No Auth failed). "We just weren't sending the session ID back" applies to form login only. After /logout the old cookie may remain but the server has invalidated its session.

SessionCreationPolicy

http.sessionManagement(session ->
        session.sessionCreationPolicy(SessionCreationPolicy.IF_REQUIRED));   // the default
PolicyMeaning
IF_REQUIRED (default)Create a session when authentication needs one
ALWAYSAlways create one
NEVERNever creates one (added: uses one if the app created it)
STATELESSNever create or use a session — the JWT choice

Stateful downside: replicas don't share sessions (sticky sessions or central store, Lecture 35). But stateful is fast (no per-request rebuild) — both have trade-offs.

Stateless: a self-describing token

After login the server gives the client a token holding everything to rebuild the Authentication (identity, roles…) and discards it; each request's header token is used to recreate it. Nothing stored → any replica serves any request. Best-known format: JWT (JSON Web Token).

JWT from first principles: why naive ideas fail

Server hands the client this JSON, later rebuilding Authentication (principal = sub, authorities = role, authenticated = true):

{ "sub": "aditya", "role": "ROLE_USER" }
Attempt → how the client breaks it
#AttemptAttackResult
1Base64URL-encode JSON into a compact URL/header-safe string (qwe…)Reversible by anyone: decode, ROLE_USER → ROLE_ADMIN, re-encode (wert456…)Server believes user is admin. Broken.
2Send encoded.hash(json); server re-hashes decoded JSON, comparesHashes are public and unkeyed: modify, re-encode and recompute hash (try SHA-256 etc. until it matches)Hashes match, server fooled. Broken.
3Encrypt with server-held keyClient can't read/change without the keyWorks — JWE (JSON Web Encryption), but overkill here.
4Sign with server-held secret: encoded.signature(encoded, key)Client can decode/edit payload but can't produce a valid signatureServer re-signs payload, sees mismatch, rejects. Standard JWT.
Core idea: encoding and plain hashes give no protection (anyone recomputes). Only a secret the client lacks stops forgery. Signed JWT payload is readable by anyone but tamper-evident. Added: HS256's "signature" is an HMAC (keyed hash) — exactly why attempt 2 fails and 4 works.
Verified (Boot 4.1.0): decoded a real token, set authorities to ["ROLE_ADMIN"], re-encoded, kept the original signature → /admin/panel returned 401 with WWW-Authenticate: Bearer error="invalid_token" … Signed JWT rejected: Invalid signature. Genuine ROLE_USER token got 403 (authenticated, not admin).

Anatomy of a JWT

base64url(header) . base64url(payload) . base64url(signature) — three Base64URL parts joined by dots.

PartContents
Headeralg (e.g. HS256), typ (JWT), optional kid (key ID)
Payload (claims)
  • Registered claims:
    • sub (subject, e.g. aditya), iss (issuer, e.g. coderarmy-api), aud (audience, e.g. course-api).
    • iat (issued at), exp (expiry), epoch seconds.
  • Custom claims, e.g. roles/authorities.
All optional. Never put passwords/sensitive data here — only encoded.
Signaturesign(base64url(header) + "." + base64url(payload), key) (the "signing input")

Symmetric vs asymmetric signing

Symmetric (e.g. HS256)Asymmetric (e.g. RS256, ES256)
KeysOne secret key signs and verifiesPrivate key signs; public key verifies
ProSimple, fastVerifiers can't forge; public key shareable
RiskEvery verifier holds the secret; one compromised (say S3) can mint valid tokensOnly issuer (S1) holds the private key
Typical useSingle app (this lecture)Microservices; most production systems

In microservices S1 issues, S2/S3 only verify. A symmetric key lets all of them sign; asymmetric keeps signing power in one place.

Worked example: stateless JWT login

Dependency and properties

<dependency>
    <groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
    <artifactId>spring-boot-starter-oauth2-resource-server</artifactId>
</dependency>   <!-- OAuth itself comes in a later lecture; this brings the JWT support -->

# application.properties
jwt.secret=<output of: openssl rand -base64 32>   # real apps: env var / secret manager, never committed
jwt.issuer=coderarmy-api
jwt.expiry=900                                    # seconds = 15 minutes

Beans

Boilerplate, same in every app — know each one's purpose, don't memorise.

@Bean
public SecretKey jwtSecretKey(@Value("${jwt.secret}") String secret) {
    byte[] decodedKey = Base64.getDecoder().decode(secret);
    return new SecretKeySpec(decodedKey, "HmacSHA256");
}

@Bean
public JwtEncoder jwtEncoder(SecretKey key) {        // signs tokens
    return new NimbusJwtEncoder(new ImmutableSecret<>(key));
}

@Bean
public JwtDecoder jwtDecoder(SecretKey key) {        // verifies signature + expiry, parses claims
    return NimbusJwtDecoder.withSecretKey(key).build();
}

@Bean
public AuthenticationManager authenticationManager(DaoAuthenticationProvider provider) {
    return new ProviderManager(provider);            // needed because we call authenticate() ourselves
}

@Bean
public JwtAuthenticationConverter jwtAuthenticationConverter() {
    JwtGrantedAuthoritiesConverter authorities = new JwtGrantedAuthoritiesConverter();
    authorities.setAuthoritiesClaimName("authorities");   // read roles from our claim
    authorities.setAuthorityPrefix("");                   // our values already start with ROLE_
    JwtAuthenticationConverter converter = new JwtAuthenticationConverter();
    converter.setJwtGrantedAuthoritiesConverter(authorities);
    return converter;
}

The lecture's AuthenticationManager bean snippet is unreadable on screen; ProviderManager is one standard way, authenticationConfiguration.getAuthenticationManager() another.

The filter chain

@Bean
public SecurityFilterChain securityFilterChain(HttpSecurity http,
                                               JwtAuthenticationConverter converter) throws Exception {
    http
        .csrf(csrf -> csrf.disable())                                  // fine now: no cookies, Bearer header only
        .authorizeHttpRequests(auth -> auth
            .requestMatchers("/api/users/register", "/auth/login").permitAll()
            .anyRequest().authenticated())
        .sessionManagement(s -> s.sessionCreationPolicy(SessionCreationPolicy.STATELESS))
        .oauth2ResourceServer(oauth -> oauth.jwt(jwt -> jwt.jwtAuthenticationConverter(converter)));
    // formLogin / httpBasic / .authenticationProvider(...) removed: requests now authenticate via Bearer JWT
    return http.build();
}

Login endpoint and token service

@RestController
@RequestMapping("/auth")
public class AuthController {                        // separate from UserController for clarity
    // AuthenticationManager and JwtService injected

    @PostMapping("/login")
    public LoginResponseDto login(@RequestBody LoginRequestDto dto) {
        Authentication authenticationRequest =
            UsernamePasswordAuthenticationToken.unauthenticated(dto.getUsername(), dto.getPassword());
        Authentication authentication = authenticationManager.authenticate(authenticationRequest);
        String token = jwtService.generateToken(authentication);
        return new LoginResponseDto(token);          // { "accessToken": "…" }
    }
}

@Service
public class JwtService {
    // JwtEncoder injected; @Value("${jwt.issuer}") String issuer; @Value("${jwt.expiry}") long expiry;

    public String generateToken(Authentication authentication) {
        Instant now = Instant.now();
        List<String> authorities = authentication.getAuthorities().stream()
                .map(GrantedAuthority::getAuthority).toList();
        JwtClaimsSet claims = JwtClaimsSet.builder()
                .issuer(issuer)
                .issuedAt(now)
                .expiresAt(now.plusSeconds(expiry))
                .subject(authentication.getName())
                .claim("authorities", authorities)
                .build();
        JwsHeader header = JwsHeader.with(MacAlgorithm.HS256).build();   // see note below
        return jwtEncoder.encode(JwtEncoderParameters.from(header, claims)).getTokenValue();
    }
}
  • We write /auth/login ourselves, doing by hand what form login did: build the unauthenticated token, ask the AuthenticationManager (→ DaoAuthenticationProvider), issue a JWT.
  • LoginRequestDto (username, password) separate from UserRegisterRequestDto for clear naming; LoginResponseDto has accessToken + @AllArgsConstructor.
Worth knowing (verified, Spring Security 7.1): specify HS256 when encoding. Lecture says the encoder bean sets it, but new NimbusJwtEncoder(new ImmutableSecret<>(key)) + JwtEncoderParameters.from(claims) (no header) failed: JwtEncodingException: … Failed to select a JWK signing key (default header alg RS256; HMAC secret can't satisfy). Pass JwsHeader.with(MacAlgorithm.HS256). Spring Security 7 also has NimbusJwtEncoder.withSecretKey(key).

Demo run

  1. POST /auth/login (public, No Auth), body {"username": "aditya", "password": "secret123"} → {"accessToken": "eyJ…"}.
  2. GET /api/users/hello with Authorization → Bearer Token → "Hello, you are logged in as aditya".
  3. Same with manual header Authorization: Bearer <token> (all Postman's tab does).
  4. Unlimited calls without re-login until expiry (15 min).
Verified (Boot 4.1.0, H2, same setup):
  • Decoded token: header {"alg": "HS256"}; payload {"iss": "coderarmy-api", "sub": "aditya", "exp": …, "iat": …, "authorities": ["ROLE_USER", "FACTOR_PASSWORD"]}.
  • /hello with Bearer → 200 with ROLE_USER, no Set-Cookie (STATELESS).
  • 401: no token, wrong password on /auth/login, forged token.

Q: "Why the JwtAuthenticationConverter?"

Lecture: else ROLE_ADMIN → ROLE_ROLE_ADMIN. Actually worse: the default JwtGrantedAuthoritiesConverter reads scope/scp with prefix SCOPE_, never authorities. Verified: default converter → no ROLE_USER, only Spring Security 7's FACTOR_BEARER, so every hasRole(…) fails. A JwtAuthenticationConverter bean is auto-picked by oauth2ResourceServer().jwt() even if not passed.

Session vs JWT

Session (stateful)JWT (stateless)
Client holdsOpaque JSESSIONID cookieSelf-contained signed token (usually a header)
Server holdsSecurityContext per sessionOnly the key
Per requestSession lookupVerify signature, rebuild Authentication
ScalingSticky sessions or shared storeAny replica
Logout / revokeEasy: invalidate sessionHard: valid until exp
CSRFRelevant (cookies auto-sent)Not with Authorization headers

Interview gaps

  • No revocation/logout: stolen/old token works until exp. Short access tokens (5–15 min) + refresh tokens (server-side, rotated, revocable). True logout needs a deny-list (partly stateful again).
  • Client token storage: localStorage is readable by XSS; HttpOnly Secure cookie resists XSS but brings back CSRF. Choose deliberately.
  • Secret handling: move the HMAC secret from application.properties to env var/secret manager. ≥256 bits randomness: openssl rand -base64 32 = 32 random bytes, not "a 32-bit integer". Support key rotation (kid header).
  • Validate more claims: NimbusJwtDecoder checks signature + exp/nbf by default (60 s clock skew), not iss/aud unless you add validators (e.g. JwtValidators.createDefaultWithIssuer(…)).
  • Stale authorities: roles baked into the token; a demoted user keeps old roles until expiry.
  • Login errors: wrong password → authenticate() throws BadCredentialsException (401 here); map explicitly (e.g. @RestControllerAdvice) to clean JSON, and rate-limit /auth/login (Lecture 35 brute-force).

Corrections & worth knowing

  • Base64URL is encoding, not encryption (lecture is right) — any decoder shows the payload.
  • JWS vs JWE: a normal JWT is a JWS (signed); JWE (encrypted) also hides the payload — only when claims must be confidential.
  • Why "OAuth2 resource server": our API is a resource server accepting Bearer tokens; here the same app also issues them (its own authorization server). OAuth next lecture.
  • Field injection (@Autowired private AuthenticationManager / JwtEncoder) only to save time (admitted); prefer constructor injection.
  • FACTOR_PASSWORD claim: copying getAuthorities() also copies Spring Security 7's factor marker; filter to ROLE_ for a clean claim.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"sign up with JWT" → authenticate (log in) with JWT.
"JWT authentication token / JWT authentication provider are used here" → resource server's BearerTokenAuthenticationFilter + JwtAuthenticationProvider produce a JwtAuthenticationToken.
"SHA 256" read as "shar 256"; "HMAC SHA 256" → HmacSHA256.
"a 32-bit integer" → 32 random bytes.
"the client created this token" (hashing attempt) → the server creates it.

Next: OAuth.

Lecture 39: OAuth 2.0 & OpenID Connect — Roles, Tokens, the Authorization Code Flow with PKCE, and "Sign in with Google" in Spring Boot

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #39 · 1h27m · Code Army · builds on sessions/JWT (Lectures 35 & 38)

The problem: login without owning the credentials

So far our app authenticated users itself (username + password to /login → session ID or JWT). Two cases don't fit:

  1. "Log in with Google" (authentication): no new password; browsers are already signed in to Google. A POST /loginWithGoogle taking the Google password is absurd — it unlocks Drive, Photos, Calendar.
  2. Google data on the user's behalf (authorization): a Coder Army "Productivity App" reads the user's Google Calendar — limited access (read only) with explicit consent, no password.

Google must authenticate users and authorize our app for narrow data, without the password reaching us. OAuth 2.0 solves authorization; OpenID Connect adds authentication on top.

OAuth 2.0: idea and the four roles

Conceptual flow:

  1. User tells Google "I allow Coder Army to read my calendar".
  2. Google gives the app a token.
  3. App presents it to the Calendar API.
RoleMeaningIn our example
Resource ownerOwns the protected dataThe user (their Google Calendar)
ClientApp requesting access — not necessarily a browser; whoever makes the requestOur Spring Boot app (Productivity App)
Authorization serverAuthenticates user, collects consent, issues tokensGoogle's auth system
Resource serverHosts protected data, accepts tokensGoogle Calendar API
  • Scope — what the client asks for, conceptually calendar.read (read, not write). Added: Google's real string is https://www.googleapis.com/auth/calendar.readonly.
  • Consent — Google's screen: "Productivity App wants permission to: read your Google Calendar, view your profile". Deny → no token.
  • Access token — what the client receives. Format not mandated: JWT (resource server verifies signature) or opaque string (resource server looks it up).

OpenID Connect: authentication on top of OAuth

One-liner from the lecture:
OAuth 2.0 answers "what can this application access?"; OpenID Connect answers "who logged in?"

The access token describes permissions for a resource server, not identity for the client. OIDC adds the ID token: a JWT the client decodes to learn who the user is.

// decoded ID token payload (illustrative)
{
  "iss": "https://accounts.google.com",   // issuer (the lecture writes "google.com")
  "sub": "1098765432101234567890",         // Google's stable, unique id for this user
  "aud": "<your client id>",              // the token is meant for YOUR app
  "name": "Aditya",
  "email": "aditya@gmail.com",
  "iat": 1790000000, "exp": 1790003600
}

"Sign in with Google" flow (lecture's version):

  1. User clicks the button.
  2. We 302-redirect to Google.
  3. User logs in there (if not already).
  4. Google gives our app an ID token.
  5. We decode it and register/log in the user in our DB.

No password touches our site.

The three tokens

TokenPurposeConsumed byLifetime / format
ID token (OIDC)Authentication: who the user isThe clientShort; always a JWT (OIDC spec)
Access token (OAuth)Authorization: call an API within granted scopesThe resource server (client just carries it)Short (minutes to an hour); JWT or opaque (Google's are opaque)
Refresh tokenNew access token on expiry without bothering the userThe authorization server (client sends it)Long-lived; kept secret by the client

Why refresh tokens: access tokens expire fast (leaks short-lived); refresh token → fresh access token silently, instead of re-consent each expiry. Re-consent only when the refresh token expires/is revoked.

Precision note (added):
  • Refresh-token lifetime varies by provider (lecture's "1–2 days" is just an example). Google's typically live until revoked, issued only when the client requests offline access (access_type=offline).
  • Never send an ID token to an API as an access token — its aud is your client, not the API.

Why tokens must not travel through the browser

A 302 → our-app/callback?token=… exposes the token to history, logs, extensions, interceptors; a stolen access token works exactly like ours (bearer token, Lecture 35). Rule: tokens are exchanged over the back channel (server to server), never the front channel (browser redirects). Added: "token in the redirect" is the old implicit flow, deprecated for this reason.

Authorization Code flow, step by step

  1. Authorization request. App 302-redirects to Google with:
    • client_id — issued (with a client_secret) when you register the app with Google.
    • redirect_uri — where Google sends the user back.
    • scope.
    • response_type=code — "give me a code, not a token".
    • state — random string Google echoes back, matching each callback to its request.
  2. Google authenticates the user (email, password, maybe 2FA).
  3. Consent ("Coder Army wants access to …").
  4. Authorization code: redirect to redirect_uri?state=abc123&code=TAYFGV456…. The code is not a token, just a one-time random string. Spring Security's default callback: /login/oauth2/code/google.
  5. Exchange code for tokens: back end calls Google's token endpoint with the code → ID, access, refresh tokens. Server to server, never the browser.

PKCE: Proof Key for Code Exchange

Worry: the code passes through the browser; a hacker could grab it and call the token endpoint. PKCE ("pixie") binds the code to whoever started the flow:

  1. Before redirecting, client creates a long random secret code_verifier.
  2. Computes code_challenge = SHA-256(code_verifier); sends only the challenge in step 1; Google stores it with the request.
  3. At token exchange (step 5) client sends code + code_verifier.
  4. Google hashes the verifier, compares to stored challenge; tokens only on match.

A code thief never saw the verifier (only its irreversible hash crossed the browser) → exchange rejected.

Precision note (added):
  • Client secret already protects confidential clients: a server-side app's token request carries the client_secret (never leaves the server), so a stolen code alone is useless.
  • PKCE was designed for public clients (SPAs, mobile) that can't keep a secret; also defends against code injection. OAuth 2.1 recommends it for all clients.
  • Exact encoding: code_challenge = BASE64URL(SHA256(code_verifier)), code_challenge_method=S256; verifier is 43–128 chars.
  • state's main job is CSRF protection of the callback (attacker can't make your browser complete their login). For OIDC, a nonce in the ID token prevents replay.
Verified (Boot 4.1.0 / Spring Security 7, dummy Google client):
  • Browser-style GET /profile → 302 to /oauth2/authorization/google (single provider: Spring skips its login page).
  • That endpoint → 302 to https://accounts.google.com/o/oauth2/v2/auth with response_type=code, client_id, scope=openid profile email, state, redirect_uri=http://localhost:8099/login/oauth2/code/google, nonce, code_challenge=…, code_challenge_method=S256.
  • Spring does all of steps 1–5 (state, nonce, PKCE) even for a client with a secret.

Registering the app with Google

Google only talks to known apps: create an OAuth client in Google Cloud Console (steps in lecturer's notes) → client ID + client secret. Added: also register the exact redirect URI http://localhost:8080/login/oauth2/code/google, else redirect_uri_mismatch.

App plan:

  1. Google authenticates the user.
  2. Spring receives the ID token, gives us an OidcUser.
  3. User in our DB? Yes → load/update. No → create.

Worked example: oauth-demo

Spring Initializr dependencies:

  • Spring Web, Spring Data JPA, MySQL Driver, Lombok (added later).
  • OAuth2 Client (brings Spring Security). Not Resource Server or Authorization Server — our app is the client.

Entity and repository

@Entity @Getter @Setter @NoArgsConstructor
public class User {
    @Id @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY)
    private Long id;
    private String name;
    private String email;
    private String provider;          // "google"
    private String providerSubject;   // the ID token's "sub": Google's stable id for this user
    // no password field: users never log in with a password here
    public User(String name, String email, String provider, String providerSubject) { … }
}

public interface UserRepository extends JpaRepository<User, Long> {
    Optional<User> findByProviderAndProviderSubject(String provider, String providerSubject);
}

provider + providerSubject uniquely identify a user — how we recognise the same Google account next time.

UserService: register or update

@Service
public class UserService {
    private final UserRepository userRepository;

    public User registerOrUpdate(String provider, OidcUser oidcUser) {
        String providerSubject = oidcUser.getSubject();
        String name  = oidcUser.getClaimAsString("name");
        String email = oidcUser.getClaimAsString("email");
        Optional<User> existingUser =
            userRepository.findByProviderAndProviderSubject(provider, providerSubject);
        if (existingUser.isPresent()) {             // known user: refresh details
            User user = existingUser.get();
            user.setName(name);
            user.setEmail(email);
            return user;                            // (see Gaps: needs a save/transaction to persist)
        }
        User newUser = new User(name, email, provider, providerSubject);
        return userRepository.save(newUser);        // first login: create
    }

    public Optional<User> findByProviderAndSubject(String provider, String subject) {
        return userRepository.findByProviderAndProviderSubject(provider, subject);
    }
}

CustomOidcUserService: hook into OIDC login

@Service
public class CustomOidcUserService implements OAuth2UserService<OidcUserRequest, OidcUser> {
    private final OidcUserService delegate = new OidcUserService();   // Spring's default implementation
    private final UserService userService;

    @Override
    public OidcUser loadUser(OidcUserRequest userRequest) throws OAuth2AuthenticationException {
        OidcUser oidcUser = delegate.loadUser(userRequest);           // "let Spring do the real OIDC work"
        String provider = userRequest.getClientRegistration().getRegistrationId();   // "google"
        userService.registerOrUpdate(provider, oidcUser);
        return oidcUser;
    }
}

By loadUser, Spring has done the whole sequence diagram (redirect, callback, state check, code + verifier exchange). delegate.loadUser validates the ID token (maybe calls userinfo) and builds the OidcUser; we add persistence. Delegate-then-customise is Spring's documented pattern.

Security config, controller, properties

@Configuration
public class SecurityConfig {
    @Bean
    public SecurityFilterChain securityFilterChain(HttpSecurity http,
                                                   CustomOidcUserService customOidcUserService) throws Exception {
        http
            .authorizeHttpRequests(auth -> auth
                .requestMatchers("/").permitAll()          // exactly "/", not "everything starting with /"
                .anyRequest().authenticated())
            .oauth2Login(oauth -> oauth
                .userInfoEndpoint(u -> u.oidcUserService(customOidcUserService))
                .defaultSuccessUrl("/profile", true));
        return http.build();
    }
}

@RestController
public class UserController {
    @GetMapping("/")
    public String home() {
        return "Public home. Login using http://localhost:8080/oauth2/authorization/google";
    }

    @GetMapping("/profile")
    public Map<String, Object> profile(@AuthenticationPrincipal OidcUser oidcUser) {
        User user = userService.findByProviderAndSubject("google", oidcUser.getSubject()).orElseThrow();
        Map<String, Object> response = new HashMap<>();
        response.put("internalUserId", user.getId());           // our own primary key
        response.put("provider", user.getProvider());
        response.put("subject", oidcUser.getSubject());
        response.put("name", oidcUser.getClaimAsString("name"));
        response.put("email", oidcUser.getClaimAsString("email"));
        return response;
    }
}

# application.properties (Google's endpoints are pre-configured by Spring Boot)
spring.security.oauth2.client.registration.google.client-id=${GOOGLE_CLIENT_ID}
spring.security.oauth2.client.registration.google.client-secret=${GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET}
spring.security.oauth2.client.registration.google.scope=openid,profile,email
  • /profile returns OidcUser details to show what Google actually sends.
  • No callback endpoint or token call written: Spring provides /oauth2/authorization/google and /login/oauth2/code/google.
  • Lecture hard-codes ID/secret in application.properties for the demo but warns: never commit the client secret — use env vars (as above) or a secret manager. (application.yml reads better for these long keys.)

Demo run

  1. Startup creates user table (id, email, name, provider, provider_subject).
  2. Postman GET / → public message with login URL.
  3. Browser localhost:8080/profile → accounts.google.com → choose account → "Sign in to Coder Army OAuth Demo. Google will allow access to your name, profile picture, email" → Continue.
  4. Back at /profile: provider, subject, name, internal user ID, email.
  5. /profile again → no Google prompt; DevTools shows JSESSIONID — after the OAuth dance Spring keeps login in a normal stateful session (Lecture 38). Issue your own JWT to go stateless.
  6. /oauth2/authorization/google directly also starts Google login.

Homework: offer both own username/password + JWT login (Lecture 38) and Sign in with Google.

OAuth 2.0 vs OpenID Connect

OAuth 2.0OpenID Connect
AnswersWhat may this app access?Who is the user?
Key tokenAccess token (+ refresh token)ID token (JWT), on top of OAuth
Token audienceResource server (API)Client (your app)
TriggerAny scopes, e.g. calendar.readonlyThe openid scope
Spring Boot useCall Google APIs with the OAuth2AuthorizedClient's access tokenoauth2Login() → OidcUser

Interview gaps

  • Update branch doesn't persist: registerOrUpdate sets name/email but never calls save and isn't @Transactional → changes lost (Lecture 33 dirty checking). Add @Transactional or save(user).
  • Race on first login: simultaneous first logins can insert duplicates; add a unique constraint on (provider, provider_subject) and handle the conflict.
  • Identify by sub, not email (lecture does this right): emails change/get recycled. If linking by email, check email_verified.
  • Hard-coded "google" in /profile: use the registration ID from OAuth2AuthenticationToken when adding providers (GitHub, Microsoft…).
  • Roles: Google users get only default OIDC authorities; map to your own ROLE_* from the DB in loadUser (return an OidcUser with your authorities) to use hasRole.
  • Calendar-style access needs the Calendar scope, access_type=offline for a refresh token, and secure refresh-token storage. Demo only does login.

Corrections & worth knowing

  • Refresh tokens go to the authorization server's token endpoint, not the resource server (lecture draws this correctly).
  • Why the OAuth2 client starter (added): spring-boot-starter-oauth2-client = oauth2Login(), client registrations, authorized-client management. Resource-server starter (Lecture 38) = APIs that accept Bearer tokens. Authorization-server project = issuing tokens yourself.
  • "Resource owner password" grant (app collects the password, sends it to the provider) is what OAuth exists to avoid; removed in OAuth 2.1.
  • Post-login session is Spring's: Google tokens live in OAuth2AuthorizedClientService (in memory by default). Logging out of your app doesn't log out of Google.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"the user sent us the token… sorry, the server" → self-corrected: the server issues it.
"iss: google.com" → https://accounts.google.com.
"code_challenge = SHA-256 of the verifier" → Base64URL of the SHA-256 digest.
"request_uri" (in places) → redirect_uri.
"@RestController... I mean @RequestMapping" → self-corrected.
"permit everything that starts with slash" → requestMatchers("/") permits only the root path.

Lecture 40: Unit Testing in Spring Boot — JUnit, Mockito, @WebMvcTest, @DataJpaTest (and Course Wrap-Up)

Source: Code Army Spring Boot series, video #40 · 1h22m · Code Army · final lecture of the Spring Framework series

The problem: "it works in Postman" isn't testing

Project spring-testing-demo: Product entity (id, name, price, stock) and:

  • ProductController — GET /api/product/{id} → 200 + product; POST /api/product → 201 Created.
  • ProductService:
    • getProductById — findById(id).orElseThrow(() -> new RuntimeException("Product not found " + id)).
    • createProduct — throws IllegalArgumentException("Product already exists") if existsByName(name), else save.
  • ProductRepository — JpaRepository + boolean existsByName(String name).

Demo: POST {"name": "laptop", "price": 50000, "stock": 10} → 201, row in MySQL; again → 500, log IllegalArgumentException: Product already exists (expected).

That's manual testing: one path, repeated by hand after each change, misses edge cases in real logic. Industry expects you to write unit tests.

Motivating example: PriceCalculator edge cases

@Service
public class PriceCalculator {
    public double calculatePrice(double price, double discount) {
        if (price < 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("Price cannot be negative");
        if (discount < 0 || discount > 100)
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("Discount should be within 0 to 100");
        return price - (price * discount / 100);
    }
}

Happy path: 1000 at 20% → 800. Edge cases (price 0, negative price/discount, discount > 100), as in DSA, produced the guard clauses. Automated tests check them every time:

  1. Start from a known input.
  2. Execute to get the actual output.
  3. Compare with the expected result: equal = pass, else fail.
Anatomy of a test: Arrange, Act, Assert:
  • Arrange — initial conditions/inputs (price 1000, discount 20).
  • Act — call the method under test (calculatePrice).
  • Assert — compare actual with expected (800).

Kinds of tests

LevelScopeExample
UnitOne class/method in isolationPriceCalculator.calculatePrice, ProductService with mocked repository
SliceBetween unit and integration: one layerWeb layer via @WebMvcTest, JPA layer via @DataJpaTest
IntegrationSeveral components togetherRepository + Hibernate + DB; controller → service → repository
End-to-endWhole flow, front end to DBButton click → filters/security → DispatcherServlet → controller → … → DB

Concentric circles: unit innermost, E2E outermost. Developers mostly write unit tests; all must pass before a feature branch merges.

Precision note (added): "slice" in Spring: Boot's "test slices" are annotations loading part of the context. The lecture's @WebMvcTest and @DataJpaTest tests are slice tests, though filed under unit testing.

What to test, and what not to

TestDon't test
  • Business logic
  • Conditional branches (if/else ladders, switch)
  • Validation rules
  • Exception handling (deliberate exceptions are behaviour)
  • Security checks
  • Anything that could break in production
  • Library/framework code (tested upstream)
  • Plain getters/setters
  • Built-in JPA methods
  • Code without meaningful decisions
AI-generated suites often include these; review before pushing — they only slow the build.

Where tests live and what you need

  • src/test/java mirrors src/main/java (Lecture 3): service/PriceCalculator → src/test/java/…/service/PriceCalculatorTest.
  • Initializr adds one test starter per main starter: spring-boot-starter-data-jpa-test, spring-boot-starter-webmvc-test. They bring JUnit (write/run tests) and Mockito (mocks); nothing extra needed.
  • Run via mvn test (Maven test phase), IntelliJ Maven panel, or the green run icon.
    • Failures print "There are test failures"; details in target/surefire-reports/.
    • A test failure fails the build.
Version note (verified on Boot 4.1.0):
  • Libraries: JUnit Jupiter 6.0.3, Mockito 5.23.0.
  • Test starters: Boot 4's per-technology starters (spring-boot-starter-webmvc-test, spring-boot-starter-data-jpa-test) replace Boot 3's single spring-boot-starter-test.
  • Slice annotations moved: org.springframework.boot.webmvc.test.autoconfigure.WebMvcTest, org.springframework.boot.data.jpa.test.autoconfigure.DataJpaTest (Boot 3: org.springframework.boot.test.autoconfigure.web.servlet / …orm.jpa).

Worked example 1: PriceCalculator with plain JUnit

class PriceCalculatorTest {
    private PriceCalculator priceCalculator;

    @BeforeEach                                  // runs before every test
    void setUp() { priceCalculator = new PriceCalculator(); }   // plain `new`, no Spring, no @Autowired

    @Test
    void shouldApplyDiscountToPrice() {
        // Arrange
        double price = 1000;
        double discount = 20;
        // Act
        double actualPrice = priceCalculator.calculatePrice(price, discount);
        // Assert
        assertEquals(800, actualPrice);          // (expected, actual)
    }

    @Test
    void shouldRejectDiscountAbove100() {
        IllegalArgumentException exception = assertThrows(IllegalArgumentException.class,
                () -> priceCalculator.calculatePrice(1000, 120));
        assertEquals("Discount should be within 0 to 100", exception.getMessage());
    }
}
  • No Spring, on purpose: don't start the context, Tomcat or a port to test a Java method; keep the test phase fast. Think "plain Java".
  • @Test (org.junit.jupiter.api) marks methods JUnit runs; they return void.
  • Name by behaviour (shouldApplyDiscountToPrice), not test1; long names help others.
  • Many tests per method, one per edge case (above 100, below 0, negative price…).
  • Failure demo: expecting 700 → "expected: <700.0> but was: <800.0>". Dropping "should" from the expected message also fails.
  • @AfterEach runs after every test, like finally (e.g. release resources).
  • Lecture deletes the generated …ApplicationTests (empty @SpringBootTest that just starts the context) so only its own tests run.
Common JUnit assertions (org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions)
AssertionPasses when
assertEquals(expected, actual) / assertNotEqualsValues are (not) equal
assertTrue(condition) / assertFalse(condition)Condition true / false
assertNull(x) / assertNotNull(x)x is (not) null
assertThrows(Type.class, () -> …)Lambda throws that type; returns it for further checks
Worth knowing (verified): compare doubles with a tolerance: assertEquals(800, actualPrice) passes (800.0 exactly representable), but assertEquals(0.3, 0.1 + 0.2) fails (0.30000000000000004); assertEquals(0.3, 0.1 + 0.2, 1e-9) passes. Use the delta overload, or better BigDecimal for money (Lecture 33).

Why the service test needs Mockito

new ProductService() won't compile (constructor needs a ProductRepository); @Autowired real repository drags in the context, Hibernate and real MySQL. Replace the dependency with a test double.

Test doubles
DoubleWhat it isExample
DummyPassed only to satisfy a signature; never usednull as the repository — NPE as soon as the service calls it
StubReturns predefined answersfindById(1) always returns Laptop / 50000 / 10
FakeLightweight working implementationIn-memory "database": a HashMap, or H2
MockConfigurable as dummy, stub or fake, and verifies interactionsMockito's @Mock ProductRepository

Added: fifth classic double is the spy (@Spy) — real object, calls recorded, partially stubbable. Textbook split: stubs → state verification (check result); mocks → behaviour verification (check calls made).

Worked example 2: ProductService with @Mock and @InjectMocks

@ExtendWith(MockitoExtension.class)              // lets JUnit understand Mockito's annotations
class ProductServiceTest {

    @Mock                                        // fake repository, managed by Mockito, NOT a Spring bean
    private ProductRepository productRepository;

    @InjectMocks                                 // real ProductService built with the mock injected
    private ProductService productService;       // (conceptually: new ProductService(productRepository))

    @Test
    void shouldReturnProductWhenProductExists() {
        // Arrange: stub the repository call the service will make
        Product product = new Product(1L, "Laptop", 50000, 10);
        when(productRepository.findById(1L)).thenReturn(Optional.of(product));
        // Act
        Product actualResult = productService.getProductById(1L);
        // Assert
        assertEquals(1L, actualResult.getId());
        assertEquals("Laptop", actualResult.getName());
        verify(productRepository).findById(1L);  // the repository really was called (once) with 1
    }

    @Test
    void shouldThrowExceptionWhenProductDoesNotExist() {
        when(productRepository.findById(99L)).thenReturn(Optional.empty());
        RuntimeException exception = assertThrows(RuntimeException.class,
                () -> productService.getProductById(99L));
        assertEquals("Product not found 99", exception.getMessage());
        verify(productRepository).findById(99L);
    }

    @Test
    void shouldCreateProductWhenNameIsUnique() {
        Product request = new Product(null, "Keyboard", 200, 5);     // incoming: no id yet
        Product savedProduct = new Product(10L, "Keyboard", 200, 5); // what save() returns
        when(productRepository.existsByName("Keyboard")).thenReturn(false);
        when(productRepository.save(request)).thenReturn(savedProduct);
        Product result = productService.createProduct(request);
        assertEquals(10L, result.getId());
        assertEquals("Keyboard", result.getName());
        verify(productRepository).existsByName("Keyboard");
        verify(productRepository).save(request);
    }

    @Test
    void shouldRejectProductWhenNameAlreadyExists() {
        Product request = new Product(null, "Keyboard", 200, 5);
        when(productRepository.existsByName("Keyboard")).thenReturn(true);
        assertThrows(IllegalArgumentException.class,               // act + assert in one step
                () -> productService.createProduct(request));
        verify(productRepository).existsByName("Keyboard");
        verify(productRepository, never()).save(any(Product.class)); // save must NOT be called, for any product
    }
}
  • when(…).thenReturn(…): "when the service calls findById(1), return this product". No real DB.
  • verify(mock).method(args): optional; proves a call happened (e.g. which branch ran).
  • verify(mock, never()) + any(Product.class) = "never called with any product"; passing request would check only that object.
  • These tests mostly re-check a thin JPA wrapper, contradicting "don't test framework code" — admitted, used only to teach mechanics; real services have mapping/validation logic worth testing.
Worth knowing (verified): Mockito strict stubs: with MockitoExtension, an unused stub fails the test with UnnecessaryStubbingException ("Please remove unnecessary stubbings or use 'lenient' strictness") — catches stale copy-pasted setup. Remove it, or lenient().when(…) if intentionally shared.

Worked example 3: controller with @WebMvcTest and MockMvc

Logic lives in the service, but controller tests check web behaviour:

  • URL and HTTP-method mappings.
  • @PathVariable / @RequestParam / @RequestBody binding.
  • Jackson JSON (de)serialisation.
  • Status codes (200, 201…).

A plain Java call skips all that; the request must go through DispatcherServlet.

@WebMvcTest(ProductController.class)             // small context: MVC infrastructure + this controller only
class ProductControllerTest {

    @Autowired
    private MockMvc mockMvc;                     // perform HTTP requests without starting Tomcat or a port

    @MockitoBean                                 // a Mockito mock registered AS A BEAN in that context
    private ProductService productService;

    @Test
    void shouldReturnProductIfProductExists() throws Exception {
        // Arrange
        when(productService.getProductById(1L)).thenReturn(new Product(1L, "Laptop", 50000, 10));
        // Act + Assert
        mockMvc.perform(get("/api/product/{id}", 1L).accept(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON))
               .andExpect(status().isOk())
               .andExpect(content().contentTypeCompatibleWith(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON))
               .andExpect(jsonPath("$.id").value(1))
               .andExpect(jsonPath("$.name").value("Laptop"))
               .andExpect(jsonPath("$.price").value(50000.0))
               .andExpect(jsonPath("$.stock").value(10));
        verify(productService).getProductById(1L);
    }
}
  • @WebMvcTest: limited context — controllers, MVC infrastructure, Jackson, filters; no services, repositories or other beans.
  • MockMvc simulates the servlet request through the filter chain and DispatcherServlet to the handler; otherwise you'd need a running server.
  • Why @MockitoBean, not @Mock: the controller bean needs a ProductService bean; a plain @Mock lives outside the context → startup fails. @MockitoBean puts the mock into the IoC container.
  • POST likewise: post("/api/product").contentType(APPLICATION_JSON).content("{…}") + status().isCreated(). Verified: GET and POST controller tests pass on Boot 4.1.
Correction: @MockBean is gone in Boot 4: deprecated in Boot 3.4, removed in Boot 4 (verified: no MockBean class in spring-boot-test 4.1.0). Replacement: Spring Framework's @MockitoBean (and @MockitoSpyBean) in org.springframework.test.context.bean.override.mockito, since Framework 6.2.

Secured endpoints: @WithMockUser

With Spring Security on the classpath, @WebMvcTest applies it. @WithMockUser(username = "aditya", roles = "ADMIN") (from spring-security-test, via the security starter's test starter) runs the request as an authenticated user with those roles.

Worked example 4: repository with @DataJpaTest

Test only your queries (derived, @Query, native), against a real DB. @DataJpaTest loads only the JPA slice (entity manager, Hibernate, DataSource, repositories); H2 on the test classpath = zero-config in-memory DB.

@DataJpaTest
class ProductRepositoryTest {
    @Autowired private ProductRepository productRepository;

    @Test
    void existsByNameFindsSavedProduct() {
        productRepository.save(new Product(null, "Mouse", 500, 3));
        assertTrue(productRepository.existsByName("Mouse"));
        assertFalse(productRepository.existsByName("Monitor"));
    }
}

Verified on Boot 4.1 with H2: passes. Added: each @DataJpaTest test runs in a transaction rolled back afterwards — no data leaks between tests. Teams rarely test repositories: native SQL is usually tried against the real server first.

Which tool for which test

TestSpring context?DependenciesKey annotationsSpeed
Plain unit (PriceCalculator)NoneCreate with new@Test, @BeforeEachMilliseconds
Unit with mocks (ProductService)NoneMockito mocks@ExtendWith(MockitoExtension.class), @Mock, @InjectMocksMilliseconds
Web slice (ProductController)MVC slice@MockitoBean beans@WebMvcTest, MockMvcSeconds
JPA slice (ProductRepository)JPA sliceEmbedded DB (H2)@DataJpaTestSeconds
Full integrationWhole appReal beans (DB via Testcontainers)@SpringBootTestSlowest

Corrections & worth knowing

  • @SpringBootTest needs everything the app needs: contextLoads() starts the full context incl. MySQL — fails on CI without the DB. Use slices, embedded DB, or Testcontainers (real MySQL in Docker, added).
  • H2 isn't MySQL: dialect differences (reserved words, functions, native SQL) → H2-green queries can fail in prod. Testcontainers gives real-DB fidelity.
  • The 500 for a duplicate product is an unhandled IllegalArgumentException; with @RestControllerAdvice (Lecture 17) it should be 409 Conflict — exactly what a @WebMvcTest should assert.
  • double for price is a teaching simplification; use BigDecimal for money (Lectures 33, 36).
  • AssertJ (assertThat(actual).isEqualTo(800)) ships with Boot's test starters; more readable failures, preferred by many teams.
  • Coverage isn't correctness: JaCoCo reports lines run, not whether you asserted the right things. Aim for meaningful branch/edge-case tests, not a percentage.
Transcript slips to ignore:
"mock it bean" → @MockitoBean.
"@BeforeEach is like post-processing" → runs before each test (setup); @AfterEach is the after-hook.
"@JpaTest" → @DataJpaTest (self-corrected).
"2001 Created" → 201 Created.
"test the security layer with unit tests" → done with slice/integration tests + @WithMockUser.
"the lecture is repeated" near the end: closing advice spoken twice.

Course wrap-up

Lecturer revisits the Lecture 1 module map: Spring Core, Spring Boot, MVC, Data (JDBC, JPA, Hibernate, Spring Data), AOP, Security, testing. Advice: rewatch gaps, revise notes, and build one project using everything:

  • Filters, interceptors, AOP.
  • Exception handling, DTOs, entities.
  • Spring Security.
  • Unit tests.

Microservices, API gateway and a full front-end project announced for members-only videos.
